Build 33 — honest depths, seamarks and depths by themselves, the map during an alarm (depths, loading on demand and map controls in every tier; AIS with Pro):

  • Depths against the map’s water (C3): Contours and soundings now only appear where the depth model and the map’s water agree; rivers and harbour basins narrower than about 800 m stay without contours, in the demo for example the shipping channel at Warnemünde. Does a contour still run over land in your area? Is one missing in a wide basin where you expect it?
  • “≈” and “Quality unknown” (C3): Dashed lines marked “≈” are approximate — close to the shore, on a coarse grid and where EMODnet rates the survey as low or has not rated it; larger unrated areas carry “Quality unknown”, the area row in Offline maps names quality and source. In the demo off Gedser. Do you read dashed as “approximate” at a glance, at night too? Does the quality the area row gives for your area match what you know of it?
  • Loading online (C3): With “Load map online”, the map fetches the seamarks, harbours and depths of the visible area by itself, underway too, as soon as you are not looking at an overview; for this, Offline maps has the row “Auto-downloaded”. The demo does not load anything. Sail with a connection into an area you have never saved: do buoys and depths appear by themselves, and are they still there without a connection? Switched off again for now — see Build 34.
  • Button column during the MOB alarm (B2, C13): The buttons on the right start below the banner; at large text size + and − give way first, during the alarm then the round button — the location button stays. Quiet hints pause while the alarm is running. Trigger MOB in the demo at large text size: does a button lie on the banner? Do you hit “End” and the radio button with one tap?
  • Map in landscape (B2): Banners only move in when there is enough width, the data card takes up at most just under half the height and sits next to or above the left row of buttons. In landscape, tap a harbour: is the data card clear, without covering a button?
  • Stale AIS targets (C4): If no position comes from a target for 3 minutes, it is shown dashed without a vector (in the overview as a hollow dot), CPA and TCPA are no longer calculated, the point card names the last report — in the demo the freighter after five minutes. Can you recognise “stale” by the shape, at night too?
  • CPA warning next to the Go-To (C4): The warning is never shortened; if it doesn’t fit next to the Go-To, the Go-To chip gives way. Is “CPA … nm” still fully readable above the MOB banner too?
  • Go-To dashed (C4): The course line to the target is dashed, the COG vector solid, by day as at night. Can you tell the two apart at a glance, even with a small course error?
  • “Start / finish” (C6): If start and finish line are the same line, one label is shown there instead of two on top of each other. Is that right on your windward-leeward course?

Build 32 — the anchor watch overnight, the second morning of a multi-day voyage and the trim workbench (safety and logbook in every tier; the workbench too, its key figures tiered as in training):

  • Anchor watch overnight (C13): As long as an anchor is down, Etmar registers the watch with iOS as ongoing background work — even without a recording running, the normal case after mooring. Set the anchor without an open voyage, lock the phone, leave it overnight; in the morning, still locked, walk out of the circle with the phone. Does the alarm come immediately? How much battery did the night cost? You share the log for this under Tools → Settings → Tracking diagnostics → Share diagnostics log.
  • The second morning of a multi-day voyage (C1): If yesterday’s destination is still in the voyage and you cast off right there, it only counts after you have left its radius — the first fix no longer stops the recording. The form of a new cast-off entry says below the destination that you are within its radius. Does the recording keep running? And on a round trip: when you return to this harbour, does “Mooring (stage)” come?
  • 300 m for new destinations and stages (C2, C12): Whatever is created without its own radius when casting off, in voyage planning or by a long press on the map gets 300 m instead of 1000 m. Does the recording end at the breakwater and not half a mile before it? Destinations already saved keep their radius.
  • Trim workbench (C9): At the top of the trim list, the “Workbench” row leads to “Rated” (each trim from a measured run next to the performance of the run), to “Wind axis” (all entries per point of sail over the wind, with the gaps) and to “Compare” (two entries side by side) — in the demo with the “Forestay trim training”. At your desk, can you see which trim paid off, where you are missing one and what was different on a good day? Set the demo tier to Plus and Free as well: does every row say what it measures and what is missing?
  • Night mode (C16): At night, locked main buttons carry muted light-red text instead of black on dark, green confirmations such as “Position recorded”, the check marks and the “Cast off” tile are amber. Can you read both at a glance at night, and does a locked button look locked?
  • Demo anchor (A3): An anchor set in the demo goes with the demo — as soon as the app goes into the background. Demo, set the anchor, end the demo: does it stay quiet, without an anchor banner? Known up to Build 33: if a real anchor was already down when the demo started, the demo assessed it against the position in the demo area. Now the demo does not start and says that “Anchor up” has to be tapped first.

Build 31 — Workshop and maintenance, for everyone planning the winter on the boat (everything in every tier; in the demo everything is already set up):

  • Workshop home page (C14): at the top the winter at a glance — photo, completed tasks with a ring, days to the next event, what the winter has cost —, below it the shortcuts “This week” and shopping list and the “Maintenance” row. Do the figures match what is in your projects? Do you hit the shortcuts with gloves?
  • Project header: photo, progress ring and a timeline with start, today, the events as flags and the end; the costs are shown as bars, also per category. Do you hit a flag when two events are close together?
  • Adding tasks quickly: “New task” at the end of each category — title, Return, next; the categories carry a symbol derived from their name. Does the keyboard stay open between two tasks, also with an iPad keyboard?
  • Album and before/after: “All photos” in the project opens the album; a task with two photos shows before and after — in the demo on the sacrificial anode. Is the right photo under “Before”? Do the images stay sharp when you rotate the iPhone or iPad?
  • Choosing photos: “Choose from photos” now responds to the whole row, and at night the red filter pauses while the picker is open. Does a tap next to the text open the picker? Can you choose a photo or tap “Cancel” at night, and is the red back and the screen dimmed afterwards? Known up to Build 33: the share sheet could not be operated at night. It responds at night too (see above).
  • Maintenance cockpit (C10): The equipment is sorted by due date, a bar shows for each part how much of the interval is used up, due is shown in orange instead of red, and “Send to Workshop” (swipe left, long press or button in the detail) puts a part into a project as a task. Does the task land in the category you expect? Does the swipe work with gloves?
  • Maintenance log: “Serviced” in a part’s detail records date, engine hours reading, note and amount; the Workshop records it too when you confirm the service while checking off a task. Does the prefilled reading match the hour meter on your engine? If you have two devices: does the entry arrive on the other one?
  • Inventory as PDF: The share symbol in the equipment list makes a PDF with photos, serial numbers, maintenance status and the latest entries of the maintenance log. Email it to yourself: is it readable and handy?

Build 20 (as of 21 September 2026) — four things first:

  • Pre-departure checklist: Before casting off, it shows how long the free storage will still hold the raw recording — under six hours in orange, “estimated” without a measurement of its own. Does the figure roughly match what your phone has free? And: the course row now opens Offline maps directly on the course — is the map section right?
  • Raw log archive: Next to the share symbol there is a bin with a confirmation, the swipe left stays. Can you hit it with wet fingers without sharing by accident?
  • Map: If a track stays single-coloured, the legend says why (no wind from the bus or missing tier) — where there is no legend, the voyage detail says it. The map bar no longer collapses while a hint is over the map, and in landscape a new user can find the navigation too. Are you missing the explanation anywhere?
  • Analysis and calendar: In the full-screen chart a swipe moves the time axis, before it could only be zoomed; a tap in the chart shows the spot on the map — in every chart with a time axis, including the speed build-up around the gun. “Start” on the calendar event is now easier to hit. Does the swipe pan, or does it still zoom?
  • Also: “What’s new” under Settings → Help lists what has changed for each build. The race editor checks the course like the template editor, and Undo in the course editor keeps a mark’s pinged accuracy.

From Build 19, still valid:

  • Demo mode: If a recording is running on a real voyage, the demo no longer starts. Is the message understandable, and can the demo be started again after you finish?
  • “Reopen” in the voyage detail brings back end date, race timer and recording; an automatically set finish stays as “Finish (discarded)”. Does the undo bar appear at the bottom, and does it take everything back?
  • NMEA raw recording: Below 500 MB of free storage it stops by itself, with a notification; the voyage recording keeps running. Only those with little storage will see this.
  • Regatta — only with a course that knows an extension (time rule or marked extension leg): The finish ends the race, not the voyage. The recording keeps running until mooring, at mooring the race gets its departure port as destination, two hours stationary end it if need be; after the scored finish the logbook shows the voyage controls (Mooring) instead of “Finish race”. Without such a course nothing changes; the course editor warns if there are two finish lines and none is marked. The NSV Wednesday race from the community library (version 3) is such a course, joining is enough; if you already have it, you see “New version available” and choose “Switch — delete the old version” or “Keep both”.
  • Also: After restarting the app, the phone’s last known location no longer ends up as a point in the voyage (it could be hours old and far away). If you quit the app during a voyage and open it again later: the track should not show a jump.
  • Workshop (formerly “Winter storage”), for everyone already planning: the tool is now called Workshop, because work on the boat doesn’t only come up in winter. Photos are now also available on the project, progress counts the same everywhere (tasks including subtasks, without materials). New: tasks can have a due date, events such as crane or yard are shown at the top of the project and in the calendar, and “This week” (at the top of the Workshop list) gathers across all projects what is due by Sunday — this replaces the weekend planning per project. Reminders come at most three times a week (Friday 5 pm, the evening before an event, the morning after a missed due date), and can be switched off under Settings → Notifications. Plus amounts on materials (“Bought” asks for the actual amount), a cost row with budget in the project and a shopping list across all projects with parts as text. Also “Concerns”: tasks are linked to a piece of equipment, checking off asks about the service, the winterising/recommissioning templates suggest services that are due, and projects can be completed (history per winter). And who does it: per task Myself, Boatyard or Helper with a name, a yard filter in the checklist and “Share work list” under Planning — the tasks of one assignee as a PDF with tick boxes, photos and boat name. Questions: do the figures in the Friday notification match “This week”? Is anything missing in “This week” that the old weekend view had? Does the total in the project match what you entered? Does the work list arrive at your yard as a readable PDF?

Since Build 19: your race analyses from before show different figures. This is intentional. After the first fully recorded race, every analysis could be recalculated against real measured data instead of just being read against the code — and that turned up errors that could not be seen at the desk. The analysis is calculated afresh from the track every time it is opened, so the corrections apply retroactively to all your races. What changes:

  • Polar performance rises by about three percentage points, more upwind. The ORC grid ends at 52° and 150°; anything tighter or deeper was previously measured against the edge value — in a real race that affected 60 % of all points. Now the beat and run optima of your polar are used as additional support points, and between the columns the interpolation is curved instead of linear.
  • Tacks and gybes are counted differently. The type of manoeuvre now comes from the wind angle at the change of tack instead of the classification of the whole leg. In a test race “6 tacks, 1 gybe, 4 course changes” became the actual 7 tacks and 4 gybes.
  • The start loss in metres disappears for races that don’t start upwind. It compared windward progress against a boat that would have beaten for the same time — on a reaching start a meaningless figure. Instead, the progress to the first mark is shown.
  • Extra distance counts differently. Instead of “sailed ÷ straight line” (on the beat never under 130 %) it is now the excess over what is typically needed for the point of sail — 141 % becomes +8 %.
  • OCS is claimed less often. On a drawn line, 20 m is drawing error, not a premature start.
  • Mark times can change. Marks that only have to be passed now get their time abeam instead of from the detection radius — where there used to be a dash there is now a time, and where the buoy happened to lie within the radius, it shifts by seconds. The same applies to a rounding mark whose radius you missed: if it lay in the narrow corridor around the buoy, the moment abeam counts. Leg times and the time-loss waterfall follow suit.

If one of your figures shifts more than seems plausible to you: write to me. That is exactly what the beta is for.

  1. The map is the workspace. The green button on the map starts a voyage, race or training session — directly from there, without a detour via the logbook. You plan routes on the main map too. The “Contrast” map style is the default: land and roads toned down so that track and course stand out. It is the only one — the “Chart” option under Settings → Map is gone. Can you find everything you need underway from the map — or are you missing the way back anywhere?
  2. Courses from the community. Below your own courses there are now some that come with the update, with organiser, area, start procedure and dates. “Join” creates your own copy and adds the dates to the calendar. The other way round, you submit your own course, a harbour entry or a route via the share menu (“Send to Etmar”). Is the way understandable?
  3. Destinations from the chart data. When choosing a destination, harbours, marinas and anchorages from the chart data also appear — offline, sorted by distance. Do you find your home harbours? Are the VHF channel and number of berths that are carried over to the harbour guide correct?
  4. Polar without typing. Polar diagrams are completely free; new is reading in the ORC certificate as a PDF — polar, boat data and sails in one step. If you have a certificate: does everything arrive correctly?
  5. Wind suggestion in the logbook: Without an onboard network, wind speed and direction from the nearest station or the model are shown in grey in the empty fields; only “Apply” enters them; the app no longer prefills gusts, pressure and weather conditions. With NMEA on board, your own instruments take priority. Does the suggestion match the wind outside? Empty fields mean: no connection, no station within range or “Wind from open sources” is off.
  6. The map in landscape. In landscape the map screen now looks different: the tab bar is a capsule with four symbols, hides straight away when you rotate and comes back via the handle at the bottom edge; the instrument column starts narrow with SOG and COG. Plus the map image in the race analysis: a tap on a manoeuvre, a leg, a mark or the start shows the spot in the track. Is the capsule there when you need it, and gone when you need the map? Do you recognise in the map image where you were?
  7. The logbook in harbour and underway. As long as no voyage is running, the logbook no longer shows the map of the last voyage, but what counts at the pontoon: berth, age of the GPS fix, the open items of the checklist and the choice of engine or sail — under sail straight away with the sail combination. Cast off, anchor and MOB sit fixed at the bottom edge. In return, the voyage maps have moved into the list under “All voyages”, each voyage with the shape of its track. When a voyage is running, the buttons are no longer nine equal-sized tiles in nine colours: Quick Log, Sails and Engine are large and coloured at the top — those are the controls you use minute by minute —, below them at half height and only outlined Trim, Anchor, Mooring, Entry and Radio. Colour now only says something about the state: Engine yellow while it is running, Anchor green when it is down, MOB red. The reason is the night filter and the sun — there green, orange and purple become three similar shades, while a tile’s position stays. At the pontoon, is what you need there? Underway, do you hit the three large buttons blind, and do you find the small ones when you need them? Do you recognise your voyages by their shape — or do you miss the large map on the start page?
  8. Readability on deck. Most important: can you read the values in sunshine, when wet and at night? Do you hit the buttons one-handed, with wet fingers, in a seaway? I want to know everything that sticks there.

If you have NMEA on board: four new fields from the bus — pressure, pressure trend, log and trip. With pressure, the trend matters, not the number: a fall of more than 1 hPa per hour means wind.

↑ Back to contents

A6Giving feedback

Three ways, all of them reach me. The first is the best.

From within TestFlight — with a screenshot

At the moment the problem occurs, take a screenshot (side button + volume up). A preview briefly appears at the bottom left; tap it, then tap Share → “Share Beta Feedback”. Add a sentence. TestFlight automatically sends the screenshot, device, iOS version and build along with it — by far the most useful kind of feedback. Alternatively, in the TestFlight app: Etmar → Send feedback.

From within the app

Tools → Help & demo → Send feedback: choose the type (Error / Idea / Question), write, “Next” — the app opens a prepared email. Technical details (version, device, iOS, language, tier, demo on/off) are attached if you wish and are shown in plain text on screen beforehand. No position, no voyage or boat data. The email is sent by you, never by the app.

Directly by email

support@etmar.app — for anything longer than a screenshot: suggestions, technical sailing points, “that’s the wrong way round, seamanship-wise”.

What helps me most What did you do, what happened, what did you expect? Plus the situation: ashore or at sea, in hand or in a mount, sun or night, with or without onboard electronics. A “button too small in a swell” is a complete bug report.
↑ Back to contents

A7Your data during the beta

What goes where — and how to protect yourself.

  • No server, no account. Voyages, trim, harbours and images are stored on the device. If iCloud is on, the app syncs to your private CloudKit container — I have no access to it.
  • What the app sends out: map tiles and seamarks/depths when loading an area (OpenFreeMap, the seamark files via Cloudflare or GitHub Pages, EMODnet — (from the next build) the depths from EMODnet, delivered as ready-made files via Cloudflare, and EMODnet itself only if that source cannot be reached) and, while “Load map online” is on, for the section you are looking at (only the map tiles for that, no seamarks and depths), place names during geocoding, wind without a wind sensor from the Deutscher Wetterdienst, from DMI and from NOAA (in the USA also from the NDBC buoys and coastal stations, with the station ID only, and in Sweden from SMHI, with the ID and the setting “last hour”, “last day” or “last months”) — “Wind now” on the map, the wind suggestion in the entry and the hourly wind after the session (station ID or the 1° × 1° grid square only, no coordinate; for the map layers under “Wind”, “Stations” queries up to ten station IDs, “Model” sends the bounds of the map section instead of the grid square, rounded to quarter degrees, at most 4° × 4°, more coarsely rounded when zoomed far out, at most 64° × 64°; the app has not queried Apple Weather since Build 44). All without position history and only when you use the respective feature.
  • TestFlight feedback reaches me via Apple: text, screenshot, device model, iOS version, build. Apple additionally logs crashes.
  • iCloud is not a backup — an error gets synced along with everything else. Before larger experiments, make a backup file: Settings → Data backup → Save / share backup (one file, all data including images, see C16).
Try it without risk MOB, anchor alarm and SOS are real features (the next build hides the anchor alarm), but they alert no one outside your device — no distress call, no DSC transmission, no phone call. You can safely run through them in the harbour. New for the anchor alarm: the sound breaks through the silent switch and Sleep focus. For this, the app asks once for Critical Alerts when you drop anchor — allow it, otherwise with the phone locked it stays at vibration; with the app open it sounds even without. Try the alarm once in the harbour before you rely on it at night.
↑ Back to contents

Part B · Basics

Three things that explain everything else: where things are, why the map is at the centre and what your tier unlocks.

B1Layout: four tabs

The position never moves — wet, at night and in a swell, muscle memory is the only thing that works reliably.

  • Map — where the app opens at launch and your workspace (B2).
  • Context slot — one place, three faces: without an open voyage “Planning” (how many items are still open before casting off — the checklist itself is in the logbook —, plan a voyage; if an event is coming up in the next seven days, “Next event” at the very bottom — a tap opens it; calendar, courses, harbour guide and routes are now only under Tools), after casting off “Voyage” (next stage, destination, stage plan, voyage details; during a training session “Measured runs” appears instead of the stage plan, after the scored finish of a race “Race analysis”), with a race created “Regatta” (course, analysis, in the race four tiles with the next mark). The switch depends on events — casting off, creating a race, closing —, never on a countdown: the app never switches the tab by itself while you are looking.
  • Logbook — voyages, entries, quick buttons, countdown.
  • Tools — everything else, grouped by situation; the order follows usefulness without a subscription: “Safety” (SOS across the full width; below it up to version 1.0 “Anchoring”, hidden from the next build, C13), “On board” (Trim, Harbour guide), “Prepare” (Calendar, Courses & waypoints, Routes, Offline area), “Boat” (Boat, Workshop), “Instruments & analysis” (Instruments, Analyses) and “App” (Settings, Help & demo). Every tile always stays in the same place, whether a voyage or a race is running; the situation or focus only adds a frame: “Courses & waypoints” during a race, “Routes” during a voyage. Instruments and Analyses open in every tier. The dashboard needs no tier, so the “Instruments” tile carries no badge — what may be missing there is data from the onboard network, and the tiles say so themselves. “Analyses” carries no badge either: without a subscription they show the key figures and the facts of every session; what Pro adds is stated by a collapsed row at the end of the list. The tiles are single-coloured; colour only shows danger and state — SOS always red, Anchoring depending on the watch, “Help & demo” while the demo is running, “Routes” while a route is running.

Switching boats: the boat name is the title above the logbook and, from two boats on, is also the switcher — tap it, choose a boat; you will find the same switch under Tools → Boat, section “Switch boat”. You may switch at any time, even underway. If a recording is still running on another boat, the app asks when you cast off instead of quietly taking it over.

Profile: under Settings → Profile you choose what you use Etmar for — Racing & training, Cruising, Workshop (the page itself: C16). Whatever you deselect loses its entry points for creating new items. In Tools the tile disappears (“Courses & waypoints”, “Routes” or “Workshop”), the others move up in the same order, and below the grid it says “Hidden in your profile: … — Settings › Profile”; a tap on it opens the Profile page. Without Racing & training, “New race” and “New training” are also missing from the logbook menu, and a race event in the calendar shows the sentence “Racing is hidden in your profile” instead of “Start”; likewise “Regatta” and “Training” are missing while moored, and a training event says “Training is hidden in your profile”. What is already there stays: your races in the logbook, their analysis and the race record, workshop events in the calendar. Anything running always stays — if you deselect in the middle of the pre-start, you lose nothing until the race is over, and a running route keeps its tile. Logbook, map and safety can never be switched off; the demo and the help show everything.

iPad: the same destinations appear as a sidebar instead of a tab bar at the bottom — in landscape at the chart table the full area stays free for the map. The Instruments button places the values there beside the map instead of over it.

Night mode: as a moon item in the map’s layers menu and in the menu (⋯) of the logbook and dashboard, or as a switch in Settings — puts a red filter over the whole app. The “from sunset” automatic switches on according to the sun’s position at your location. On the SOS page the filter is deliberately suspended. Under the filter, tier badges (“Pro”) and caveats appear at full brightness instead of grey, and warnings — “All good — widen circle” on the anchor screen, the “Location denied” chip, “No GPS fix — ping not possible” — are black areas with an orange border so that they stand out from the red alarm (MOB, “ANCHOR DRAGGING”). In the dark appearance without night mode, the text on blue areas (Go-To line, chips, buttons) is black.

↑ Back to contents

B2The map as your workspace

Underway you are on the map. So that is where the tools are, too.

The map works without an open voyage too: destinations from the harbour guide as orange dots, seamarks and depth contours from the loaded areas, your boat as a blue arrow with a COG vector (six minutes ahead, plotter convention), grey as soon as the position is more than two minutes old. Then “Fix … min ago” appears at the arrow, and the Go-To line strikes through bearing and distance. The point card, the card of a tapped harbour and the expanded Go-To tile give the same minute and keep counting without a new fix.

The COG vector is bolder and ends in a cross-bar: that is where you will be on the same course and at the same speed once the time has run. You set the time under Tools → Settings → Map → COG vector to 3, 6 (default) or 12 minutes; the vectors of the AIS targets use the same. At the bottom left, above the map buttons, there is a scale bar — a round distance in your distance unit (nm or km, under Settings → Units) that recalculates when zooming and is amber at night; it is absent in the voyage story.

With a compass on the NMEA network, the boat arrow turns to the heading (HDG), and underway a thin line in the direction of the bow lies under the COG vector, reaching beyond its cross-bar — so the bar belongs to the vector alone. The angle between the two is leeway plus current. If the compass sends magnetic, the map needs the variation (from the bus or under Settings → NMEA connection → Advanced); if it is missing or the compass is silent for more than ten seconds, the arrow shows COG as it does without a compass. In demo mode a simulated compass steers against a simulated current — line and vector diverge by a few degrees.

The most important moves

  • Touch and hold on an empty spot opens a round menu: Harbour, Anchorage, Waypoint, Go To, Route — within the radius of a harbour also POI, with an open voyage with a stage plan also “Stage”.
  • “Map centre” (under the logbook actions, with an open voyage): the same menu for the centre of the screen — move the map until the target is under the centre, then underway tap the round button at the bottom right: the logbook actions → “Map centre”. With wet hands a more reliable move than the long press. Without a voyage this route does not exist: the green button is then called “Cast off” and casts off immediately.
  • Location button (bottom right, 60 pt): centres on the boat and switches follow mode on; any map gesture of your own releases it again. Underway it is already on when the map opens — provided a voyage is running and the position (no more than two minutes old) shows at least 1 kn of speed, on the first opening after app launch and for the map of this voyage from the logbook. The boat is then in the centre, with a good nautical mile across the iPhone — close enough for all seamarks. If the first position only arrives after opening (GPS still without a fix), the map catches up within the first minute, as long as you have not moved it yourself. In the harbour, while planning and in the voyage story the map opens with the overview as before; if you then switch between tabs, the section stays as it was. During an MOB alarm the map does not follow the boat alone: the button brings boat and MOB point into view together (C13).
  • Zoom buttons: with gloves, pinching is unreliable. The map always stays north-up — a two-finger move with a wet hand does not rotate it, so boat arrow, AIS triangles and their vectors are always correctly aligned with the map. If a banner at the top reaches down to the button column — such as the MOB banner with the line “Bearing out of date” at large text sizes —, + and − are dropped and the column starts below it; zooming then works with two fingers, in also by double-tap, out with a short tap with two fingers at once. The banner is never shortened for this. If there is still not enough room during an MOB alarm, the round button (Logbook or Cast off) also gives way — with an open voyage Etmar writes the MOB entry itself. The location button always stays: during the alarm it brings boat and MOB point into view together.
  • Instruments button: places the values next to the map instead of over it — in portrait as a data strip at the bottom, in landscape and on the iPad as a column on the right. The column starts narrow (SOG and COG, two tiles one above the other); you make it wider in the layers menu (“Change column width”, C3), and the sailing compass now only has room in full screen — in a 210 pt wide column it was smaller than the dial of a wristwatch.

What is at the top and bottom

At the top the context bar: underway destination harbour and stage (the Go-To line carries remaining distance and arrival time to the Go-To destination, see below); in the pre-start and race instead countdown, distance to the start line, time-to-burn and line bias — the same figures as in the logbook, just where your boat can be seen relative to the line. A tap opens the Context tab.

At the top there are at most two bars. Priority goes to the route planning band, in the race the race bar, then the Go-To line, the “Reverse course?” offer, the quiet notices (course shown, offline package, missing seamarks) and finally the voyage’s destination bar — whatever has no room appears as soon as a bar goes. If you are steering to your own destination in the race and the course offers the next mark at the next rounding, the button for it sits in the Go-To line next to the ×; your navigation stays. In the pre-start and race your own destination appears as a narrow line without a blue area below the race bar (“GO-TO Name 266° · 30.1 nm”), and above the mark name of the race bar it says COURSE — so it is clear at a glance what you are steering to; a tap expands the line as usual. The distance to the next mark is shown in whole metres below 0.5 nm (or 1 km), as with the Go-To. An alarm — CPA warning, anchor watch, MOB — counts as one of the two bars outside the race and stands below the route band, race or destination bar, ahead of all the others; in the race it is added below the race bar. The CPA warning and anchor watch share one line: next to the CPA warning the anchor watch becomes a chip, a tap on it opens it with “Anchor up”; if an MOB alarm is running, both appear as chips above its banner. While an MOB alarm is running, whatever is neither alarm nor navigation rests at the top: the quiet notices, the course offer and the destination bar — in an emergency they are ballast. Warnings — Low Power Mode, missing position, offline without a package, map not loading — always stand. If a voyage is running, the Go-To line carries destination name, bearing, distance and arrival time; if the Go-To is the voyage destination or the voyage has none, the destination bar above it is dropped — previously both showed the same distance. If the Go-To is steering somewhere else, the destination harbour stands above it, as long as there is room at the top. If the width is not enough, a course error of up to 5° gives way first; if you are more than 5° off, the name is shortened first, and without an arrival time (the boat is not closing) the warning always stays. With large text, bearing ends up above distance, without name and arrival time; the course error moves below as a third line (above 5° as a warning), and in the race the course button gives way if necessary (the × leads to the same destination). The line including the × stays in view; in the three largest accessibility text sizes it grows no further. The “Area not saved” notice only appears while no voyage is running — in the harbour, where you can still load; underway “Seamarks & depths not loaded here” only lets you know, you can load in the harbour. For now the map does not load seamarks and depths by itself: “not loaded here” means that no area is saved for the section or part of it (or its download failed); “Loading seamarks & depths …” only appears briefly after launch, while the app reads in its saved areas. If “Load map online” is off, it says “Offline maps only …” instead (C3, “Load online”).

At the bottom right the large round button: without a voyage green “Cast off” (see below), with a running voyage the Logbook button with the logbook actions as a radial menu. “Running” means: a voyage is open somewhere – even on another boat, without a boat or while you are viewing the map of an old voyage. The entry then goes into the running voyage – the one being recorded, otherwise the one shown, otherwise the one started most recently, as with MOB –, and “Cast off” only appears again when no voyage is open any more. If the entry does not go into the voyage the map is currently showing, the undo bar names the target too – for example “Engine on logged · Ansegeln NSV” –, and VoiceOver says it on the Logbook button. If the map writes into the voyage shown, the bar names only the entry. If there is no boat at all, Radio, Trim and Entry are missing from the radial menu; instead it offers “Add boat”. “Map centre” is also in the radial menu; without a voyage the long press remains for this. The Cast off and Logbook buttons sit in the same place so that your thumb does not have to learn the spot again. MOB and anchor watch keep their own buttons in a fixed place — they must be hit blind.

The green button is called “Cast off” and does what the button in the logbook does: one tap starts the voyage and recording immediately, with no form and no dialog, using the engine or sail choice from the logbook. For six seconds the undo bar “Voyage started” appears at the bottom — a tap on it reverses an accidental touch. You create races and training sessions via the menu at the top right of the logbook, destinations at the map centre without a voyage via the long press. Only with the focus “Regatta” (Settings → Display) does the button first ask “Race …” or “Cast off”. If no boat has been created yet, the button does not cast off but opens the “New boat” form — a voyage needs a boat, otherwise it would have no logbook and no “Mooring”. After saving, the next tap casts off.

“Mooring” when stationary. When the boat is stationary — recording pauses after 30 minutes below 1.5 kn —, a second round button with the chequered flag appears to the left of the Logbook button. One tap closes the voyage like “Mooring” in the logbook: mooring entry, backdated to roughly the time the boat came to a stop, voyage closed, recording off. For six seconds the undo bar “Voyage closed” reverses this. A voyage over several days asks, as in the logbook, whether only a stage ends or the whole voyage. Underway the button is not there — an accidental tap in a swell does not close a voyage —, during a race only after the finish. A voyage without recording knows no standstill; you close it in the logbook. If a voyage without a boat is running that the logbook does not show, this button closes it.

Landscape: the map gets the space

In landscape the map is wide and flat — every band across the full width costs more there than in portrait. That is why the screen looks different in landscape: the tab bar is a capsule with four symbols without text. When you rotate into landscape it is collapsed straight away — rotating is the statement “I want map area”. The handle at the bottom edge brings it out; it then stays for ten seconds and goes immediately as soon as you touch the map. The map buttons move up while the capsule is showing, so that they do not end up underneath it. The undo bar and data card leave room on the right for the button column; the column no longer rises around the undo bar. While the bar or card is showing, the handle waits instead of lying over “Undo”. If a widened instruments column leaves too little room next to the left row of buttons, the undo bar sits above it instead of next to it, so that “Undo” stays legible. In landscape the anchor banner also ends before the button column – “+” does not lie over “Anchor up”. This applies as long as the banner has enough width next to it: if the instruments column is set wide, it runs across the full width – otherwise only the first letters of the state “Awaiting GPS” and of “End” in the MOB banner would remain –, and the button column starts below it. If the column does not fit in the height together with a data card, the + and − buttons give way until there is room again; zooming then works with two fingers, in also by double-tap, out with a short tap with two fingers at once. In landscape the data card takes up at most just under half the height; whatever does not fit, you scroll within the card. It sits next to the left row of buttons or, if there is too little room beside it, above it – like the undo bar. If it still gets tight, the track legend is the first thing to drop out of the column. If the banner runs across the full width with a very wide column, data card and button column then sit side by side in landscape: the column starts at the bottom, the data card may reach up to below the banner and notice, and the track legend gives way while the data card is open. With very large text, the anchor banner puts the settings button and “Anchor up” on a line of their own below state and distance – in portrait as in landscape –, and in landscape the MOB banner also puts the crosshair and “End” below bearing and distance; this keeps both unshortened. In the MOB banner this no longer depends on the text size: if the header does not fit on one line, the crosshair and “End” go below it – in every orientation, in landscape next to a wide instruments column even at the standard text size, and bearing and distance stay at full size. The banner gets taller for this; in landscape the “Area not saved” notice gives way while the banner is showing. With the instruments column set wide, the data card in landscape then only gets the rest below the banner: while the tab bar is showing, from the second of the larger text sizes its header with name and ✕ is also cut off; once the bar is collapsed again, the data card gets that height back. The location button always stays; the round button only gives way during an MOB alarm, when the banner lies over the column and there is no other room (see Zoom buttons above). If the banner lies over the column, the capsule only lifts the column as far as there is room up to the banner – the column stays at least 8 pt below it, “Anchor up” and MAYDAY stay clear, even with very large text. If there is not enough room, the column rises less, and the capsule lies in front of the lowest button of the column for its ten seconds. The instruments column sits on the right next to the map and is narrow by default: SOG and COG, two tiles one above the other, large digits. You make it wider in the layers menu (“Change column width”, three steps), and remove it completely with the same button you used to bring it up.

No mode survives an app launch: whoever opens the app at night gets the navigation map, never a tool from yesterday.

↑ Back to contents

B3Free and Pro — and the demo tier

Two tiers, one rule. In the beta you can try out both.

Everyday use on board is free. Pro is the analysis afterwards — what a start, a manoeuvre, a rounding cost you — and, on the onboard network, the sailing compass. Up to Build 42 there was a third tier in between, Plus; its on-board features are now free, its analyses belong to Pro.

  1. Safety is always free — MOB, SOS, AIS warning; the anchor alarm and depth alarm are currently hidden (C13).
  2. Free on board, Pro afterwards: everything that runs during the session — recording, navigation, instruments from the iPhone and from the onboard network, warnings, live values in the race — is free. What a race leaves behind in facts also stays free: times at the marks and per leg, distance at the gun, tacks and gybes, SOG history, boards, zones, the side at a passing mark. Pro passes judgement: against the polar, against other sessions or as a loss. On board, everyday use is free and anything further is Pro: sailing compass, custom instrument pages, Smart fields and the Regatta page with VMG against the polar; the four standard pages with all values from the onboard network stay free. Locking happens in the page manager, never on deck.
  3. No gate on the way out — export, report and sharing are free, including the map image in the report; analysis pages in the report contain what your tier has.
TierWhat it contains
Free Everyday use on board: logbook with Auto-Tracking, trim diary, harbour guide, safety, workshop, calendar; map with seamarks, depths and offline packages without limit, routes, AIS with CPA warning, laylines; NMEA connection (2000 and 0183) with dashboard and standard pages; races with countdown, line bias and live values in the race. Afterwards: key figures and facts of the race analysis, measured runs with duration, polar diagrams, PDF report with map image, voyage story, GPX export.
Pro The analysis after the session. From the iPhone GPS: race map, time-loss waterfall, start summary, manoeuvres with cost (without a wind sensor with estimated tack), leg summary, rounding quality and finish approach, form curve and learning curve, figures per measured run with A/B comparison. With wind from the onboard network as well: polar performance, VMG, wind & shifts, sailed polar, sail crossover, tack asymmetry and the other boat analyses. On the onboard network: sailing compass, custom instrument pages, Smart fields and the Regatta page.

Pro is available as a subscription: yearly (€24.99 with a 14-day free trial) or monthly (€4.99). You can try everything beforehand in demo mode (A4). If the subscription expires, logbook, voyages, tracks, trim entries, offline maps and safety remain usable; only the Pro features are locked, nothing is deleted.

Locked analyses never disappear silently — and they do not stand individually between the free rows either. Per screen they are gathered in one collapsed row at the end of the list, with a counter: “6 more analyses with Pro”, below it a sentence on what Pro brings here and where the values come from. A tap expands it, each row leads to the Pro overview. In the analysis hub the page is long — there the row stands at the end of each category’s section (“2 more with Pro”), not once at the end of the page. On the live screens — map, dashboard, pre-start, anchor alarm — there is no tier: what may be missing there is data, and the screen says so with the source, for example “needs NMEA” on an instrument tile.

A Pro notice in the app always says where the values come from: “From GPS: start, manoeuvres (port or starboard estimated), legs and roundings. Polar, VMG, sailing compass and shifts need wind from the onboard network.” If a single analysis needs a specific sensor, it is named on the row, for example “Pro · wind sensor (NMEA)”. If you have Pro and only the data is missing, the row says so without a tier: “Needs wind from the onboard network at the track points.” In this handbook Pro appears as a badge after the heading; whatever carries no badge is free.

In the beta Under Settings → Tier & demo, with the demo running, you can choose the demo tier: Pro or Free. This lets you try out both tiers without buying anything — including tier rows and the Pro overview. That is exactly what it is for: in the Free version, do you miss anything on board? And after the race, do you understand what Pro would tell you beyond the facts?

Part C · Reference

Every area of the app in one article: what it does, how to use it, what you can check while testing. Screenshots with numbered highlights — the list next to them explains the numbers.

C1Logbook & voyage

From casting off to the finished voyage report: how a voyage is created, what you record underway with one tap, how recording runs in the background and how you rework track and entries at leisure in the harbour.

In the harbour: “Before departure”

The logbook has two faces. When the boat is moored, the top shows what matters at the pontoon. First the answer to “Can I go?”, large and with a symbol: “Ready to cast off” with a tick, otherwise “One item open” or for example “3 items open” with a warning triangle (with a question mark if only unresolved items are open) – counted as in Planning; a notice with “i” does not count. Below that the items of the departure checklist that are still open, then the choice cast off under engine or sail, the berth (name from the harbour guide, otherwise the place name) and the GPS row with the age of the last fix (under one minute “Fix just now”, after that in whole minutes that keep counting without a new fix, orange from two minutes on). Up to Build 43, berth and fix age (row “Last fix”) are at the top and the choice of engine or sail at the bottom, and “Ready to cast off” only appears when nothing is open. Below the header – and below the “Current harbour” block when you are in a harbour – is the same row as in Planning: “Next event: title · day · time”; with no event in the next seven days, “No event in 7 days — open calendar”; a tap opens the event as in the calendar, or the calendar (C15). Before a boat’s first voyage, “Today” with “Regatta” and “Training” and this row appear below the “No voyages” notice — so the first training session works without the menu (⋯). The departure checklist is only here – before the first voyage as a block above the “No voyages” notice –, and it only shows what concerns you today: offline map for the area, location “Always”, maintenance due, Low Power Mode; with an upcoming race the course, with a set-up NMEA gateway (or with Pro) missing polar data, with a set-up gateway the remaining capacity of the raw recording — the data source decides, not the tier. In Free without a race and without a gateway that is at most four open items. If equipment or a sail is being serviced or is lent out, “Off the boat” is added (“Liferaft with Seaservice Kiel”, “2 items off the boat”) and leads to the equipment — an item that only your own entry creates and that “Back” resolves with one tap (C10). The Planning view in the context slot now only gives the number of open items and leads here with one tap. If you have never answered the question about the iCloud backup, you also see a notice with “i” instead of a warning triangle, which does not count as an open item; a tap leads to Data & privacy. If you have deliberately switched iCloud off, you see no row – that is a choice, not an omission. Cast off, Anchor and MOB sit fixed at the bottom edge in the thumb zone and do not scroll away — even before a boat’s very first voyage. With very large text all three stay fully in view: the labels of Cast off and Anchor stop growing beyond the second-largest standard size, at the larger text sizes from the second step on Anchor and MOB sit one above the other – MOB on the right below, 60 pt as always –, and the “No voyages” notice before the first voyage can be scrolled when it no longer fits above the bar. If an MOB alarm is running, its banner – bearing, distance, time, “End” and “Radio” – appears at the very top of the logbook even before the first voyage, above the “No voyages” notice. If iCloud is still loading your boat’s voyages after a reinstall, Anchor and MOB are at the bottom – casting off is then only possible via “Cast off anyway” in the notice, so that no second open voyage is created next to the synced one. Without a boat created – for example after “Continue from iCloud”, before your boats have arrived – Anchor and MOB are at the bottom, without Cast off: a voyage needs a boat, Anchor and MOB do not. When the anchor is down, the tile shows its state, and a tap on it opens the anchor alarm; the anchor banner then appears on the map. When a voyage is running, the map carousel takes the place of the header, and the quick buttons are below it. Their labels also stop growing beyond the second-largest standard size: at the larger text sizes no word breaks in the middle any more, the rows stay flatter, and Entry and Radio move up. The arrangement stays – MOB as a red circle at the top right, 60 pt as always. The undo bar after casting off likewise only grows up to this size. Equally capped are the buttons of a running race (pings, sync, ±5 min, recall, marker, event, engine, sail, trim), the run button in training and the status line, for example “Auto-Tracking active” with the number of track points – up to Build 35 words broke in the middle there at the larger text sizes. Under “Recent entries” – and likewise in a voyage’s entry list – at the larger text sizes the date sits below the entry type instead of to its right, and speed, course and wind are stacked; up to Build 36 words and times broke in the middle there.

The map of the last voyage is no longer on the start page: in the harbour the track of a closed voyage answers no question. It lives in the voyage list under “All voyages” – there every voyage carries the shape of its track as a small image, next to it date, duration and miles. The shape is recognised faster than the name: the zigzag of a beat, the triangle of a race course.

Casting off – one tap or none at all

One tap on Logbook → Cast off is enough (on the map it is the green Cast off button with the same single tap, see B2). The app immediately creates the voyage and the first entry of type “Cast off”. No form opens, everything else happens by itself:

  • GPS position, course and speed are captured in the background and filled in.
  • The voyage is named after the nearest place (e.g. “Voyage Kühlungsborn – 08/08/2026”), the entry is then called “Departure: Kühlungsborn”.
  • The sails last set on the previous voyage are carried over – without downwind sails, nobody casts off with a gennaker.
  • Auto-Tracking starts immediately.
  • Under sail, a “Sails” row appears in the checklist with the combination that was last up (downwind sails stay out). A tap opens the same sail list as the sail change underway – with wind fit, if the wind is known. The choice goes into the cast-off entry so that sail comparison and sailed polar later know what was up.
  • Casting off under engine (segment in the checklist above, default engine) additionally creates an “Engine on” entry: the harbour exit counts as engine distance from the first second (yellow on the map, engine hours in the statistics), and the engine button then stands at “Engine off” for the moment the sails go up. The choice applies equally in the entry form (type “Cast off”) and for automatically detected casting off; the app remembers it.

You can add weather, images or notes at any time later via the entry under “Recent entries”. A voyage closed by mistake can be reopened in the voyage details – important for multi-day voyages. “Reopen” removes the end date and brings recording back to this voyage – provided no other voyage is being recorded and the last entry is not a mooring (after a mooring things continue as always with the next “Cast off”); 6 s undo as when closing.

Motion autostart. Even if you tap nothing, you get a logbook: if Auto-Tracking is active but there is no cast-off entry yet (“Waiting for departure”), the app watches the GPS. If the boat makes way continuously for more than a minute (above 1.5 kn), a cast-off entry is created automatically – backdated to the start of the movement – and recording begins. The minute’s grace filters out warping in the harbour and GPS outliers. A notification on the lock screen confirms: “Departure detected – auto-tracking is now recording.”

NoteAutostart requires the app to be receiving NMEA data at that moment (a real onboard network or demo mode). Only then is it certain that the phone is moving with the boat – phone GPS alone cannot tell a car or bike ride from a boat trip. Without NMEA data the status line shows “Waiting for departure entry”, and the voyage starts via the Cast off button or a manual entry.

Underway: the quick buttons

As soon as a voyage is open, the map runs edge to edge at the top of the logbook and scrolls along when you scroll the page. It is sized so that the large action buttons are ready without scrolling even on small devices; its footer shows the voyage name, speed and course. A tap takes you to the voyage details. Underway you can also reach the same actions directly on the map via the logbook button at the bottom right (B2).

Size tells you how often you reach for it. Quick Log, Sails and Engine are large in the first row — these are the every-few-minutes controls —, with MOB to the right as a red circle. Below, flatter and outlined, what is needed less often: Capture trim, Anchor, Mooring, Entry, Radio. Colour now only carries state: Engine turns yellow while it is running, the anchor gets a green edge when it is set and a red fill when it is dragging. At 25 knots at night you find the right one by its position — under the red filter colours make no difference anyway, and in the sun they wash out.

  • Quick Log: one tap, one position entry with the current GPS position, course and speed. Sails and the weather free text come from the last entry; wind only fresh from the NMEA network (at most 60 s old), otherwise the field stays empty – old wind values are never carried forward, and that also applies to sail changes and the entry form. No form, haptics confirm.
  • Sails: tap the current wardrobe, done. Every sail row can be tapped across its full width (tick on the left); with five sails or more the sheet opens straight to full height instead of half. Creates an event entry with the new sail combination. Afterwards a bar “Capture trim too” is offered for ten seconds: a tap creates a trim draft with the new sails and opens the form – change first, then trim. After “Cancel” no bar appears.
  • Entry: the full form for an event entry – type, time (can be backdated), title, GPS (automatic, can be corrected manually), sails, weather/wind, images from the gallery or camera, and notes.
  • Engine on / Engine off (yellow/grey): one tap immediately creates the matching engine entry, no form. The button always shows the next action and glows yellow while the engine is running. The stretch between “Engine on” and “Engine off” is yellow on the voyage map.
  • Radio: opens the entry form with the type “Radio call” – station, channel and what was agreed go into the note. Radio contacts belong in the logbook; in a dispute, the record is what counts.
  • Mooring: the finish with one tap. On a day sail the app closes immediately (arrival entry, backdated to when the boat stopped, an undo bar instead of a confirmation). On a multi-day voyage – open stages or a start before today – a dialog asks: “Mooring (stage)”, the voyage stays open, or “Close voyage”.

MOB and Anchor are also in the button group, in a fixed place – both are described in C13. “Trim” captures the current trim and belongs to the trim diary.

Wind as a suggestion. When you create an entry, a grey suggestion appears in the empty wind and wind direction fields – the current value of the nearest station (DWD, DMI; in Sweden the SMHI, in the USA the NOAA buoys and coastal stations, there at measurement height, not converted to 10 m), otherwise from the GFS weather model, the same one as “Wind now” at the top of the map (C3); below the section it says where it comes from. It only goes into the entry with “Apply” in the header of the Weather section – nothing is saved before that. Without a network the last fetched value is shown as long as it is at most three hours old, and the line below the section then gives its age; otherwise the fields stay empty. The app no longer queries Apple Weather (up to Build 43 the suggestion came from there, with gusts, pressure and weather conditions). Without an onboard network, gusts, visibility, temperature and weather conditions are manual entries; pressure is pre-filled by the sensor in the iPhone (see below). With onboard electronics on the NMEA network your own instruments take precedence: bus wind and bus barometer fill the fields, and when the voyage is closed the wind of the departure and arrival entries is averaged from the first and last ten minutes of the recording respectively – the momentary value in the harbour is gusts and shelter, the average out on the water is the honest wind. Wind entered by hand always stays as it is.

Pressure from the iPhone. If no barometer on the onboard network is supplying fresh pressure, the app pre-fills “Pressure (hPa)” with the sensor in the iPhone – also in the short form, which saves the value with it. Next to it is the label “iPhone” as long as the pre-filled value is unchanged in the field; the onboard barometer gets no label. A value from the onboard barometer counts as fresh for 10 minutes after it arrives – pressure changes over hours, and the 1–2 hPa offset from the iPhone would be larger than what happens in that time; a short gap in the Wi-Fi therefore does not switch to the iPhone, but disconnecting does. In the Weather section (behind “More”), new entries then show the “Tendency” line – arrow, word and number, for example “↓ falling quickly · −1.4 hPa/h” – and the curve of the measured time, at most three hours. The tendency applies from one hour of measurement; before that the line says how much longer. The word follows the number shown: below 0.2 steady, up to 1.0 slowly, up to 2.0 quickly, above that very quickly (hPa per hour). Tendency and curve always come from one source, onboard barometer or iPhone, never mixed. Measurements are only taken while the app is open or a recording is running, every five minutes. The sensor can differ from the onboard barometer by 1–2 hPa, and 8 m of height difference makes about 1 hPa – the tendency is reliable as long as the iPhone stays at the same height. iOS asks for the “Motion & Fitness” permission at the first new entry without pressure from the onboard network; if it has been denied, the footer says where to switch it on. When editing an entry there is no pre-fill, tendency or curve – they belong to “now”. Only the value in the field is saved, pre-filled in whole hPa – no tendency, no curve.

Photos are stored entirely in the app: each image is scaled to screen size when saved and stored locally – it is sharp even without a network and is kept if the photo library is tidied up later. Etmar does not access the other photos on the device; photos taken with the camera are also saved to the library. With several images in one entry: touch and hold an image and choose “As preview image” (star) – this image appears as a photo pin on the map.

Mis-taps without consequences. After every one-tap action – Quick Log, Engine on/off, Cast off, Close voyage, pinging the start or finish line – a large undo bar appears at the bottom edge for 6 seconds; the whole area can be tapped. A tap reverses the action: entry deleted, voyage open again and tracking back on, ping back to its previous state. There are deliberately no confirmation dialogs before the action – they cost glance time on deck; undoing afterwards is the better protection. In the dialog “Complete open voyage?” (for a regatta “End running regatta?”) closing is deliberately not red — as everywhere, the undo bar afterwards carries the weight.

Forms protect what you enter. Swiping down or “Cancel” asks for confirmation in every form as soon as there really is something unsaved in it – and only then; unchanged forms close with one tap. Number fields accept both comma and point. And saving never fails silently: if a database save goes wrong, a red bar “Saving failed — the change may be lost” appears on every screen, tap to dismiss, otherwise visible for 8 seconds.

All entries can be edited afterwards. That is the intended way: only the essentials at sea, fill in the rest at leisure in the harbour.

Auto-Tracking

Where the position comes from. If the boat is on the NMEA network, the app records the position of the boat, not of the phone — an antenna on deck or at the masthead sees more sky than a phone in an oilskin pocket. If the gateway fails, the phone takes over writing immediately; the track never breaks off. Map, voyage header, instruments, MOB and the watch all show the same source — previously the voyage header could show “0.0 kn” while the tiles showed five knots. The raw data export states for each point where it came from.

Auto-Tracking records the route in the background as GPS track points – even with the screen locked and after an app restart. It is activated automatically when you cast off (in the entry form, too, the switch is always “on” for a new departure entry – important on multi-day voyages, because arrival stops tracking) and works economically with adaptive intervals:

  • Underway (above 1.5 kn): one point every 30 seconds, every 5 seconds in races.
  • When stationary: every 5 minutes. After 30 minutes without movement tracking pauses automatically (one point every 10 minutes) and resumes immediately once the boat is moving.
  • Saving is done in batches of 10 points – which saves the battery further.

The status line in the logbook says what is happening: “Active” (green, with track point counter), “Waiting for departure — starts automatically when underway”, “Paused” or, in red, “No location permission — nothing is being recorded” with a direct jump to the iOS settings. Storage errors and active Low Power Mode are shown too. With an NMEA connection and a wind sensor, the true wind and the tack (starboard/port) are also stored with every track point – the basis for tack colouring and race analysis. The position always comes from the iPhone GPS: if the NMEA connection drops, recording continues seamlessly; for tack colouring and analysis the last known wind data is used for up to 30 minutes, after which the points stay without wind rather than guessing with outdated values.

Robustness and diagnostics. While recording, a significant-location anchor runs: if iOS terminates the app (memory pressure, crash), a significant change of location wakes it up again, and tracking is restored when the process starts – not only the next time you open it. If there is no GPS fix for 5 minutes, a notification warns you. All state changes, app launches, foreground/background, GPS permission, GPS silence and storage errors end up in the log under Settings → Tracking diagnostics – a text file, shareable, without positions. This lets gaps be explained afterwards.

ImportantRed in the status line means: nothing is being recorded. With “No location permission” the jump leads straight to the iOS settings; active Low Power Mode is shown too. Check the status line once after casting off – one glance before the phone goes into your pocket.

End of voyage: stage or final

When mooring, the app distinguishes two entry types: “Mooring (stage)” for an intermediate stop – recording stops, the voyage stays open, and the next morning you carry on without detours – and “Arrival” for the final mooring, which closes the voyage. Both can also be chosen in the entry form; a manually created “Arrival” also closes the voyage.

Most important rule: every automatically detected arrival is a stage mooring. The automation never closes a voyage by accident, and nobody has to reopen it. It only becomes final automatically at the final destination:

  • Final destination: In the voyage form (Voyage details → Edit) you set a destination harbour – usually the harbour you started from – with an arrival radius, optionally with a “Return from/until” period. If the boat arrives there after having left the radius once (and, if set, within the period), the closing “Arrival” is created and the voyage is closed.
  • Stage detection: If the boat reaches the arrival radius of the chosen stage destination or of the tracking destination, a “Mooring (stage)” is created automatically and recording stops. The radius is enough for this, even at full speed. Only the place where you cast off counts here, too, only after you have left it: if yesterday’s destination is still in the voyage on the second morning of a multi-day voyage, the first fix does not stop recording. If you cast off via the form of a new departure entry, a line below the destination says that you are inside its radius. Harbour guide destinations, on the other hand, only count if the boat enters slowly (below 1.5 kn): sailing quickly past a harbour entrance triggers nothing, and the departure harbour only counts after you have left it once.
  • Standstill suggestion: If the boat has been stationary for more than two hours, the app suggests closing the voyage – as a button in the logbook (“Arrived? Complete trip”) and as a notification on the lock screen. Closing creates an arrival entry backdated to the moment the boat came to a stop. The suggestion stays as a highlight on the Mooring button. While the anchor watch is set, the app does not ask: the notification and button stay off, and the logbook shows a quiet line instead; after “Anchor up” the two hours start again. “Mooring” on the map remains.
  • Choosing a destination: For the final destination, the day’s destination and stages, the harbours, marinas and anchorages from the chart data are available alongside your own harbour guide and the place search – offline, sorted by distance, tappable directly on the map. A harbour chosen this way goes into the harbour guide with its category, VHF/berth note and a suitable arrival radius (anchorage: 150 m instead of 90 m). Unnamed anchorages are simply called “Anchorage”; areas marked as no-anchoring are not offered at all.

Lock screen: Live Activity and notifications

While recording, a Live Activity runs on the lock screen and in the Dynamic Island: sailing boat symbol, voyage name, the distance sailed since the last cast-off (in nm, large on the right) and the average speed since casting off in the status line (“Ø 5.2 kn since casting off”). As long as no distance has accumulated yet, the track point counter is shown on the right. In races it shows the countdown to the starting gun to the second and, after the start, the running race time. Put the phone down, a glance is enough – and the glance is honest: if there is no update for 15 minutes (app crashed or terminated by iOS), the display dims and says “No current data — open the app” instead of continuing to claim “Recording”; the compact island symbol becomes a grey exclamation mark instead of the green recording dot.

So that you can trust the phone in your pocket, the app reports important events with local notifications (no server, no data sharing):

  • Departure detected / arrival detected (with destination name).
  • Tracking problems: track points cannot be saved; GPS permission was revoked; no GPS fix for 5 minutes.
  • “Finish trip?” after two hours stationary – works even if iOS has terminated the app.
  • Regatta: start of the pre-start phase and the starting gun. In addition, a notification one minute before the gun, and the pre-start notification gives the chosen pre-start duration instead of always 15 minutes.

Permission for notifications is only requested when tracking or a regatta is activated – not at app launch.

Voyage details: export, map and post-editing

You reach the voyage details from the carousel (tapping the preview opens the voyage map, “Back” leads to the details), via “All voyages” or via the “Voyage” context tab. It brings together everything about a voyage: summary (status, period, duration, distance, avg SOG, engine time, max wind, sail combinations used), the stage plan, the Auto-Tracking switch, the map, the daily report, pressure and all entries in chronological order. “Edit” opens the voyage form, in which you set the final destination and the return period.

Export via the share menu in the voyage details – everything goes through the system share sheet:

  • Logbook as text (.txt) – all entries as plain text.
  • Track as GPX – the recorded track as a GPX file.
  • Raw data (CSV) – every track point as a table row: time, position, SOG/COG and, where an NMEA bus was recording, STW, HDG, wind, heel; gaps stay empty rather than invented. For races additionally Mark times (CSV) with the stamped roundings and leg times. Comma-separated with a decimal point, for Excel, Numbers or Python.
  • PDF voyage report – A4; page 1 with a map image of the track, key figures (distance, duration, avg/max SOG, sailed, engine), a regatta line and notes; from page 2 all entries in chronological order with position, SOG/COG, wind, pressure, weather, sails, note and up to three photo thumbnails. The map image is on page 1 in every tier — offline from the saved map packages, otherwise from the network; if it cannot be loaded, a track sketch with a north arrow appears there instead. The Wind section on page 1 shows the wind from the NMEA network where it was recorded, otherwise the wind from your logbook entries — averaged, with the maximum, the direction where noted and the number of entries —, and without either, a sentence explaining why it is empty. If the track carries no wind and the voyage has hourly wind from a station or the model, that comes before the entries — in the same order of precedence as the daily report, with its header (“Wind from station”, “Wind from model”) and its caveat including the source note: the average over the hours, the mean direction and, only with hours from a station, “to” with its strongest value — not a gust; what it measures is stated in the caveat. If you have entered the wind for every hour yourself in the logbook, the wind from the entries is kept.
  • Voyage story (image) – a 1080 × 1350 portrait image: map at the top, a dark panel with name, date, distance/duration/max SOG, for sharing in messengers or social media.

The report and story are deliberately free – they carry the voyage to the crew and the club.

Share report. Once the voyage is closed, the row “Share report” appears at the very top of its details, with “PDF voyage report or story image” below it. A tap opens the choice of “Voyage report (PDF)” – for a regatta “Race report (PDF)” – or “Story (image)”, and the choice opens the share sheet; while the map image is loading (up to 10 s), “Creating report — map image loading …” appears at the bottom. With an open voyage the row is missing: the report comes once the voyage is closed – on a day sail with “Mooring”. Without opening the details, it works under Logbook → All voyages: touch and hold the voyage – the “Share” menu offers the same two choices. The share menu at the top right stays as it is.

The voyage map opens zoomed so that the complete track is in view, with numbered pins for each entry (a tap opens the details including photos). For a running voyage underway it opens on the boat instead, with follow mode already on (B2); the voyage story still shows the whole voyage. The pins scale with the zoom: close in, large with a number; zoomed far out, small dots in the type colour. Only types for which colour means something carry it – Cast off and start green, Arrival and Mooring (stage) amber, course mark yellow, Engine grey, man overboard as the only red; position, event, radio, tack and gybe are neutral grey, at night light with a black number. The symbols in the entry list carry the same colours. Entries with photos show their preview image instead of the dot when you zoom in, with number and outline in the event colour; further out they remain dots with a camera symbol. Direction arrows along the track show which way the boat was sailing. Engine stretches are always yellow. With wind data the track is coloured by tack (pastel shades; default: red = wind from starboard, green = wind from port; can be inverted via “Invert tack colouring” in the voyage details, and the legend always shows the current assignment) – beats and gybes can be seen at a glance. If the track stays one colour, the legend – where there is one – says that this voyage did not record any wind; without a legend the sentence appears in the voyage details. In addition, a small arrow sits at every change of tack; when you zoom out the markers disappear again, otherwise the beat would be buried under them. What a manoeuvre cost and whether it was a tack or a gybe is in the race analysis – the map only shows the where. The daily report shows the true wind for each hour with a direction arrow and avg/max speed, grouped by day on multi-day voyages. Above the chart it says where the wind comes from — “Wind from the onboard network”, “Wind from station”, “Wind from model” or “Wind from the logbook”. Without a wind sensor, for new voyages the app fetches the hourly wind of the nearest station (Deutscher Wetterdienst, DMI; in Sweden the SMHI, for voyages in the last 120 days, in the USA NOAA buoys and coastal stations, for voyages in the last 45 days) or of the GFS weather model as soon as it is opened with a network after the voyage; the top then says “Wind from station”, below each hour the station and distance (“Pelzerhaken · 2 km”) or “Model”, and the source in the footer. Wind you have written into the logbook by hand takes precedence. The fetch can be switched off under Tools → Settings → Data & privacy with “Wind from open sources”. The image below shows the daily report of the demo evening race. With an open voyage, the open stage destinations appear as turquoise flags on the map; closed voyages show no destinations. Event pins can be edited directly from the map sheet (“Edit” button) – also on closed voyages. Harbours, seamarks, your own boat and all map controls behave exactly as on the main map (B2, C3).

Pressure. After the map and the daily report, the “Pressure” section shows the barograph of the voyage: one point per logbook entry with pressure, joined by straight lines – between two entries nobody knows how the pressure went. The steepest fall is drawn thick and labelled; below it are “Steepest fall” with times and hPa per hour, and “Over the voyage” with the first and last value. The app calculates the fall over at least 30 minutes, so that 1 hPa between two entries in quick succession does not become a storm; if the pressure only rises or stays the same, it says “no fall”. No entry, no point: the curve is as dense as your logbook. With fewer than two values the section stays in place and says where the pressure comes from. The demo voyage “Kühlungsborn – Gedser – Kühlungsborn” contains demo values.

The button at the top right switches to the voyage story: a single-colour track, events as photos with numbers, direction arrows – the tidy view for remembering and sharing (e.g. by screenshot), without legend, stage destinations and harbours; inspecting track points with a tap remains. The choice is remembered per voyage, and the map opens next time in the view last chosen; the default for voyages without their own choice is set by “Map opens with” in the settings.

Post-editing. The principle “only the essentials at sea, finish the rest at leisure in the harbour” also applies to the track itself:

  • Track point inspection: Tap anywhere on the track – the nearest track point shows a small boat pointing in the direction of travel, next to it a discreet arrow from the direction the wind was coming onto the boat at that moment. The data card at the bottom of the map gives time, SOG, COG, true wind and tack (wind from stbd/port). “Create entry here” turns it into a logbook entry after the fact – time, position and speed data of the track point are pre-filled.
  • Redraw track: The drawing map also shows seamarks, depths and – for a race – the course with its dashed connecting line, and the existing track is fully visible – without both you would be drawing blind, and the straight line across a gap is almost never the route actually sailed. If a section is missing (tracking forgotten, no GPS), “Redraw track” in the voyage details finds the gaps automatically: time jumps of more than 10 minutes as well as missing pieces before the first and after the last point. Only what also covered distance counts as a gap (at least 250 m) – time spent in harbour or at anchor does not show up as pseudo-gaps. Choose a gap, set points with a long press; they join up in the order they were drawn.
