Times and speeds are calculated back from the drawn distance and the speed of the edge points (fallback 5 kn, capped at the duration of the gap – gaps with time moored do not produce crawls lasting days), the wind of the edge points is interpolated and provides the tack colouring; a wind direction entered by hand takes precedence. If the gap contains time moored, the sailing time is placed at the matching edge and the seam is closed with a stationary point – no remaining gap is left. Redrawn sections stay recognisable: thinner and paler on the map, marked as “reconstructed” in the track point card.
  • Gap list with suggestion: Gaps of the kind “Probably sailed continuously” (course before and after the gap fits the connection – up to 45° each side –, plausible speed of 1.5–12 kn, line not into the wind) you fill after a visual check with one tap on “Apply connection” – without setting points. The connection is not a hard straight line: the course vectors before and after the gap are extended until they meet, and the intersection becomes a curve – the track looks as if it had been drawn through (without course data still a straight line). “Redraw manually” (mooring and casting-off manoeuvres, course changes in the gap, beating against the wind, breaks) remains handwork; the reason is given at each gap.
  • Gap caused by casting off: If the movement autostart only began recording once the boat was underway, the short stretch from the berth is missing – recognisable by the departure entry in the gap window. “Correct departure & fill in” moves the departure entry to the berth (the mooring position before it) and fills the stretch with a straight line – timed from the moment of casting off, so the time at the berth does not turn into a crawl. If more than about 2 km of distance is missing, the gap deliberately stays with “Redraw manually”. A departure entry moved afterwards is also detected as a gap; when filling in, the times are calculated backwards and the entry’s time is moved along with it.
  • Choose a section freely: Tap two points on the track and redraw the stretch between them – for instance to replace GPS outliers or zigzag. The existing points between the edges are only replaced after explicit confirmation; the edges themselves are kept.
  • Move an entry’s position: In the edit form of an entry, “Choose Position on Map” opens a map with the voyage track as a reference. A long press sets the new position (pressing again overwrites it), “Use this position” confirms – more precise and quicker than typing in latitude and longitude.
  • Coordinates by hand: Under “Edit manually” you type latitude and longitude as an absolute value in decimal degrees (comma or point) and choose the hemisphere next to it – N or S, E or W. This also lets you enter western longitudes and southern latitudes, for example in the English Channel. The hemisphere preselected is that of the position entered; if the field is empty, that of the most recent entry with a position in the voyage, otherwise N and E.
NoteTo try it without a gap of your own: the demo voyage Kühlungsborn–Gedser contains a 29-minute dropout on the downwind board – ideal for playing through “Apply connection” and manual redrawing once.
Test
  • Cast off once with the button and once just by setting off (NMEA has to be running for this): are the departure time, place name and the sails carried over correct? Did the notification “Departure detected” arrive?
  • Phone in your pocket, sail for a few hours, then look at the track: are there gaps? If so, please share Settings → Tracking diagnostics straight away and say whether the phone was locked, in Low Power Mode or without a network in between.
  • At the destination: did stage detection or the standstill suggestion hit the right moment – and did the automation never close the voyage without asking?
↑ Back to contents

C2Stage plan

On the voyage, the stage plan answers the question “Where to next?” — planning is done at home, the decision is made at sea with one tap.

Where to find it

The stage plan belongs to a voyage. You open it via Voyage details → Stage plan; the row carries a progress indicator, e.g. “2/2”. With a running voyage, the “Voyage” context field also shows the next open stage directly — more on that below.

Planning ashore

A stage plan is a sequence of stages in a fixed order. Each stage can have one or more destination options — that is the core: “With a westerly to Gedser, with an easterly to Klintholm.” Which option applies you only decide on the day itself.

  1. Create a stage via the + at the top right. The stages are numbered consecutively and form the order of the voyage.
  2. Choose a destination: “Add destination” leads to the search or into the harbour guide. A harbour from the harbour guide brings its arrival radius with it — the app needs it later to detect the arrival.
  3. Optionally set a wind condition: a direction sector from–to in eight compass points (also across north, e.g. NW to NE) and a maximum wind speed. “Max 25 kn” means: from 25 knots this option is no longer a recommendation. Add notes too, for instance on the course or the approach.
  4. Create further options with “Add alternative” — each with its own destination and its own wind condition.

Via the … menu of a stage you move it up or down, tick it off by hand, reopen a ticked-off stage or delete it.

At sea: one tap

During the voyage the “Voyage” context field shows the next open stage. If it has several options, the app asks “Where to today?” — one tap chooses the day’s destination. If wind data is available (NMEA or demo), the app marks the option that fits the current wind with “matches the wind”. The marking helps with the choice; you still tap by hand.

Linked to Go-To and arrival

“Set destination” means navigating, not just booking. Choosing an option therefore does two things at once:

  • Go-To active. The map shows the course line to the destination; the plotter tiles provide BRG, DTW, VMC and TTG.
  • Destination for Auto-Tracking. The app now knows the destination harbour including its arrival radius. When you sail in, it detects the arrival, ticks off the stage and makes the next stage active — without you having to do anything in the stage plan.

Changing your mind has no consequences: if the wind shifts or the plan changes, you simply tap a different option. Go-To and destination follow.

Stage destinations on the map

With an open voyage, the voyage map shows the open stage destinations as turquoise flags: the chosen destination bold, alternatives muted. Completed stages and closed voyages no longer show destinations — the planning is done, the track tells the history.

Stages can also be created directly on the voyage map, without going through the list:

  • Tap a harbour → As stage destination.
  • Long press on a free spot → Stage. The app fetches the place name by geocoding, and the harbour guide is updated automatically. A stage set this way gets a 300 m arrival radius.
TestIn demo mode or on a voyage of your own, create a stage with two options and different wind sectors: is the matching option marked “matches the wind” in the “Voyage” context field? Does a tap on the option immediately set the Go-To on the map? And is the stage ticked off by itself after you sail into the arrival radius, so that the next stage becomes active?
↑ Back to contents

C3Map, seamarks & offline

What layers and styles the main map offers, and how to save your cruising area so that the map, buoys and depth contours are there at sea even without a network.

ImportantEtmar is no substitute for a nautical chart or a chartplotter — it records what has happened. The map is based on OpenStreetMap: seamarks are community data, the depth contours come from a model with a grid of about 100 m (EMODnet). Neither is an official source, and neither is corrected according to Notices to Mariners. Dashed depth contours marked ≈ are approximate — close to the shore, in poorly surveyed or unassessed areas and where the grid is coarse. The app shows this notice once when the map is first opened (“Got it”); it is permanently available under Settings → About & legal and in the “Main map” tutorial.
ImportantAIS warning and MOB help — they are no substitute for a watch or a lookout. They can fail: battery flat, iOS terminates the app, no GPS, onboard network gone, device out of earshot. The AIS warning only sees vessels that transmit AIS. (from the next build) The anchor watch, depth and wind alarm are hidden; up to version 1.0 the notice named them too. The app shows this safety notice once at first launch, directly after the chart notice, and once on its own after the update; it is permanently available under Settings → About & legal and in the “Limits of the alarms” tutorial.

Wind now

At the top left of the map, in every situation – in the harbour, underway, in the pre-start and in the race – there is a small capsule “Wind now”: an arrow that flies with the wind, plus direction (“from”, true, with the compass point small next to it, for example “220° SW”) and speed in knots, large enough for a glance. With very large text the compass point is dropped and the degrees remain. Below that, quietly, the source and, from 20 minutes on, the age of the value. With very large text, direction and knots take priority: the source then gives whole kilometres (“Falsterbo · 9 km”) and wraps into a second line if necessary, and the capsule stays entirely in view. The order of precedence: onboard network, if a wind sensor is supplying fresh values; otherwise your set wind (valid for one hour, direction only); otherwise the nearest station (DWD or DMI, in Sweden the SMHI, for example “Falsterbo · 9 km”, in the USA and on the Great Lakes a NOAA buoy or coastal station, for example “44013 Boston · 12 km”; name and distance are shown); otherwise the GFS weather model. The app queries station and model every 15 minutes when online – only the station ID or grid cell goes out, no position, and none of it is saved. Without a network the last value stays with its age; after three hours the capsule shows “—”. A station counts when fetched as long as its latest value is at most an hour and a half old – otherwise the app asks the second-nearest station and then the model. Stations that only report once or twice an hour (all of the SMHI’s, some NOAA coastal stations) count for two hours: their new value only appears a while after the full hour (at the SMHI a good 20 minutes after it), and until then the capsule stays with the station instead of briefly jumping to the model. A tap opens a sheet with source, distance, time of reading and the caveat: DWD and DMI stations measure at 10 m height on land, NOAA stations at their measurement height (buoys 3–4 m), the model calculates on a grid – general mean wind, no shifts, no wind shadow. If the sheet shows a station, its gust in knots appears below the speed, for example “Gusts 17 kn” – the highest up to the time of reading: at DWD and DMI from the last 30 minutes, at NOAA the peak of the reports within it, at the SMHI from the last full hour. If the gust has a different time of reading from the mean – for example at the SMHI, when it appears later –, that time is shown with it (“Gusts as of”); if the station reports none, the line is missing. If the sheet shows the onboard network, “Gusts” is the highest 3-second mean of the true wind from your wind sensor in the last 10 minutes – the gust as defined by the WMO, without a time of its own; the line only appears once at least 5 of those 10 minutes have been received. The model and the set wind have no gust. The model brings its calculated gust with it: if the sheet shows the model, it is under “Gusts”; alongside another source it is under “For comparison” as “Model gusts” – an estimate for the model hour, not a measurement. If it is gusty at a station or on the onboard network, the capsule also writes the gust next to the wind, for example “14/22 kn” – for a station measured against its mean, for the onboard network against the ten-minute mean of your wind sensor, by the same rule as “mean/gust” at the stations of the wind layer (below); the number before the slash remains the current value. With the model and the set wind it stays at one number. In demo mode the onboard network blows a few gusts every 30 minutes; after a good five minutes the gust usually stays in the capsule for a while (with very little base wind the margin is not enough). In the sheet the speed is also given in Beaufort, for example “9 kn (3 Bft)” – the capsule stays with the one number in knots. If the capsule shows the onboard network, set wind or a station, below it “For comparison” shows the GFS model at the same place, for example “GFS model here · 190° · 11 kn (4 Bft)” with “00 UTC run · grid mean wind” below it: this lets you see whether the station matches the general situation or whether land is sheltering it. The model value is only fetched when the sheet is opened, and only once per model run, hour and grid cell (only the grid cell goes out); until it arrives it says “Fetching model …”, and without a network, in Low Data Mode, without a position or with “Wind from open sources” off, the reason is given. If the capsule already shows the model, the line does not exist – it would be duplicated. In demo mode a demo value is shown with “(demo)”, without fetching. That is also where “Set wind” is, the wind rose from the pre-start. The sheet is ordered by glance time: values, “For comparison”, “On the map”, without an onboard network directly afterwards “Set wind” with its footnote. The caveat below is a line that expands and is collapsed when opened – “General mean wind, not at the boat — more”, with the onboard network (once there are gusts) “Highest gust in the last 10 min — more”; expanded, the sentence reads as before. If a value is missing, the sheet says why (switch off, no network, no GPS fix, no station in range). The switch is now called “Wind from open sources” (Settings → Data & privacy) and applies to the capsule, the wind suggestion in the entry and the hourly wind after the voyage.

Wind layer

For planning – where is it blowing more, how does the wind shift across the bay? – the lighthouse menu has “Wind” with two switches that you turn on separately: “Model · from ≈ 30 km” for the arrows of the weather model and “Stations · every zoom level” for the measured values; by default both are off. The same two switches are also in the sheet of the “Wind now” capsule under “On the map” – a tap on the capsule, then the switch; whatever is on there carries the tick in the menu. If “Wind from open sources” is off, the way to Data & privacy appears there instead of the switches. From about 30 km map width, at every grid point of the GFS weather model (every 0.25°, in the Baltic about 28 × 16 km) there is an arrow that flies with the wind, with the whole number of knots to its right; below 2 kn a circle. Zoomed further out, there is only an arrow at every 2nd, 4th, 8th or 16th grid point (every 0.5°, 1°, 2° or 4°), with the value of exactly that point, not averaged – so the arrows stay about a centimetre apart on the screen, right out to the whole Baltic, in portrait as in landscape; further out still they move closer together again. Coastal stations of DWD and DMI (also those of the SMHI in Sweden, in the USA NOAA buoys and coastal stations) in the view (at most ten; as far apart from one another as the model arrows – if two are too close, the one nearer the centre of the screen stays) carry an arrow, a dot and knots above, and displace model points right next to them. They appear at every zoom level, even zoomed in off the harbour, and if a measured value is 20 minutes old or older, its age appears below it (“27 min ago”, from two hours “2 h ago”). Model and stations are grey below 17 kn and above that carry the colour of their base wind, at night both amber – the dot on the marker tells the station apart even without colour; see “Colour and symbols”. Below the “Wind now” capsule a line says what the arrows are: “Wind · GFS model, 06 UTC run”, from 20 minutes with the age, in demo mode “Wind · demo field”; without a field it gives the reason (switch off, no network, no model run reachable), and zoomed in it says nothing – “from ≈ 30 km” is already said by the switch. If the grid is thinned out, the line gives the spacing of the arrows (“· Arrows every ≈ 30 km”, east–west in the centre of the map, rounded to 5 km) – that is the closer spacing: north–south the arrows stand further apart, because a degree of latitude is longer than a degree of longitude. If one of the two layers is on but “Wind from open sources” is off, it says at every zoom level “Wind layer: “Wind from open sources” is off — Settings → Data & privacy”, and a tap on it opens Data & privacy. A tap on an arrow or a station opens the sheet with direction, speed, source, time of reading and caveat – titled “Wind at the grid point” or the name of the station, without “Set wind”, because it is the wind there, not at the boat. If a station is in a harbour, at another destination or next to a seamark, the tap opens the station; anchor, AIS targets, route waypoints, course marks and logbook entries take precedence over it. The speed is also given in Beaufort there, and the sheet of a station compares “For comparison” with the GFS model at the station itself. If the station reports gusts, they appear there as in the “Wind now” sheet (“Gusts 17 kn”, with a different time of reading with “Gusts as of”). Data is only fetched while the map is open and “Wind from open sources” is on, not in Low Data Mode: the model field at most once per hour and view, each station at most every 15 minutes, nothing is saved. Without a network the field stays until its model hour is three hours in the past; if you switch “Wind from open sources” off, it is gone immediately. In demo mode, without a network, the layer shows a built-in field around the Bay of Mecklenburg and three stations (Rostock-Warnemünde, Gedser, Lolland Falster) with invented values – the field with “Model”, the stations with “Stations”, see “Colour and symbols”; the sheet then shows “(demo)” after the source.

Wind layer: colour and symbols

The number on the arrow comes first – in sunlight, at night under amber and with colour vision deficiency the layer says the same without colour. By day and in the dark appearance, model arrows and stations also carry the colour of their mean wind by Beaufort, as you know it from wind maps – only from 17 kn; below that they are grey: yellow 17–21 kn (Bft 5), orange 22–27 kn (Bft 6), dark red 28–33 kn (Bft 7), magenta from 34 kn (Bft 8 and above). So colour means: from Bft 5 (in Build 46 seven bands, including blue-violet up to 6 kn, blue 7–10 kn and green 11–16 kn). The colour follows the whole number on the arrow – “16” is never yellow –; a station goes by its mean. Every symbol has the same fine edge, almost black by day, almost white in the dark appearance – so every symbol stands out against water and land (in Build 46 the edge switched between light and dark per band). Signal red does not appear: the orange is redder than that of the warnings, the dark red darker than the alarm red. The symbols are slimmer so that the planning layer is not louder than seamarks and boat, and the number is neutral: black by day, white in the dark appearance, amber at night – equally legible in every band (in Build 46 it carried the colour of its band). You recognise a station by its shape: by the dot at the end of its arrow – the arrow itself is as long as a model arrow –, both in the colour of its mean; without a direction, just the dot (in Build 46 a circle with a dark edge in the middle of the arrow). If it is gusty there – the gust reaches at least 1.4 times the mean and 6 kn more; both conditions, because the factor alone triggers in a calm (4/6 kn) and the difference alone in strong wind (26/32 kn is steady wind) –, the marker shows “mean/gust” instead of “17 kn”, for example “17/24”, calculated with exactly these whole numbers; if the station reports no gust, it stays with the mean. There is no longer a wreath of rings; the second number tells you the gustiness (in Build 46 the gusty station carried one ring, the very gusty one two). The gust only goes away below 1.3 times or a 4 kn difference, so that it does not flicker with the rhythm of the measurements – which is why “12/17” can stand on the marker. At night everything is amber: then the number alone tells you strength and gust. A tap on a symbol opens its sheet; it registers within 28 pt of its centre. Zoomed out with “Model”, the line under “Wind now” carries, except at night, a grey field “below 17” and four colour fields with the start of each colour band (“17 · 22 · 28 · 34 kn”; in Build 46 seven fields from 0 kn); a tap on the line opens the “Wind layer” legend with bands, Beaufort, station, gust, rule and caveat. The sheet of a station with gusts explains “mean/gust” at every zoom level (under “Gust on the marker”). Caveat: gusty at a land station in offshore wind mainly shows the station’s location, not the water – in one measurement week in October, land stations such as Fehmarn and Darßer Ort were gusty or more 67 to 71 per cent of the time, the sea station Drogden just under 10 per cent. The gust per station comes in the same 15-minute cycle as the mean, with the same station identifier; the model brings its calculated gust in the same request, and in the sheet of a grid point it is under “Gusts”. In demo mode the built-in field, zoomed out, ranges from a calm in the south to 35 kn in the north, and three stations with invented values show the gust: Rostock-Warnemünde calm (“12 kn”), Gedser gusty (“17/24”), the Lolland Falster airfield inland very gusty (“10/21”).

Layers menu (lighthouse)

The lighthouse button in the map’s left button column opens a menu with checkmarks: Seamarks, OSM harbours (harbours and anchorages from OpenStreetMap), Depth contours, Harbour guide, AIS targets, plus Races. You switch each layer individually — so you can, for example, hide the dense Danish club harbours without losing the buoys. The state stays saved; the symbol is filled as long as at least one layer is on.

Depth contours are off at first, also after the update. Anyone who switches them on — in the lighthouse menu or via “All layers on” under Settings → Map — gets a half sheet every time: the lines come from a model with a grid of around 100 m, are not an official source and are not intended for navigation. “Cancel” leaves them off, “Show” switches them on. In demo mode they are on, without a sheet; your own setting is not affected.

The menu is shorter: ten entries — Races, Voyages, the five layers, “Hide tab bar”, “Set anchor” (as long as no anchor is down; hidden from the next build) and at the very bottom, right above your thumb, Night mode; only with the instrument column open (landscape or on the iPad) is “Change column width” added. With “Wind (model)” there are six layers and eleven entries. In its place is “Wind”, with the switches for Model and Stations as a submenu – one entry, the menu stays at eleven. Without a draught on the boat, “Depth contours” carries the hint as a second line. With thirteen entries it stuck out of the top of the screen in portrait. Voyages and races sit together in the submenu “Voyages & races” (with one each for voyages and for races inside), and “Tab bar off” fits on one line: ten entries; in portrait, with normal text size, the menu fits fully on screen again from 6.3 inches (iPhone 17 and larger); one size larger, only “Voyages & races” at the top sits behind a swipe. In landscape the menu is taller than the screen — swipe down there, then voyages and races appear; the actions at the bottom are always available without swiping. “All layers on/off” has moved to Tools → Settings → Map; you save the visible area in the harbour via the hint “Area not saved” or under Settings → Map → Offline maps. “Map centre” now sits below the logbook actions on the round button at the bottom right, only with a voyage open (see B2).

Map style: Contrast

The map uses the “Contrast” style: land and roads almost colourless on neutral grey, so that track, course, laylines and AIS targets are the only elements carrying colour. In glaring sun on a wet display, all that remains of the base style is the coastline — exactly what you need then. The style recolours the tiles at runtime; saved offline packs remain valid. It is the only style: the “Chart” option under Settings → Map is gone, and anyone who had chosen it now also sees “Contrast”.

“Contrast” shows roads only from harbour zoom and their names only when you zoom in further; road signs (A 20, B 105) and the colourful symbols for shops and car parks disappear completely, buildings vanish into the land colour. So on a beat there are no more motorway signs between the buoys. The calm depth contours are drawn much stronger in “Contrast” by day and in the dark appearance — before, they were barely visible, and only the numbers carried the information. With night mode they stay deliberately subdued; at night what counts is the safety contour in amber: the line for your draught, without a draught the 2 m line.

Dark map. If the iPhone is set to the dark appearance, the map becomes a night chart: water in pure black (on OLED displays the pixels there are off — maximum contrast, minimum consumption, night vision preserved), land dark grey, roads and borders heavily dimmed, labels muted. The red filter of night mode is laid over it as well. This applies to all maps, including previews. In the dark, road signs and POI symbols also leave the picture, and buildings recede — under the filter they glowed brighter than everything else.

Seamarks

Buoys, beacons and lights from OpenStreetMap appear with their chart shapes: lateral buoys red (can) and green (cone), cardinal buoys yellow-black with double-cone topmarks, special marks yellow, safe water marks red-white, lights as a magenta star. Names only appear at close zoom. In night mode the map draws the seamarks in night colours: red stays red, green, yellow and the white edges become amber. This also applies to the topmarks, the star of the lights (instead of magenta) and the anchor of harbours and anchorages, which at night sits on a black disc. So at night the green conical buoy is an amber cone — you tell them apart by shape, conical or can. At night the safe water mark is striped red and black, the special mark a solid amber disc.

The display is graded by relevance: lighthouses, cardinal, isolated danger and safe water marks already appear in the area overview — small, so that far out they are not a blob of colour, and they grow as you zoom in. Lateral buoys join from area zoom, special marks and beacons only close in. There is deliberately no filter for this — no further menu on deck. The layer is on by default.

Pier lights appear only from area zoom, together with the lateral buoys, and as a smaller star — in the overview only the lighthouse remains, not every harbour entrance. Lighthouses, cardinal and danger marks are larger in the overview than before, so that you can tell a north cardinal from a south cardinal by its topmark.

The seamarks are loaded automatically with every offline pack: one download per area, stored locally, available offline. The same download brings harbours, marinas and anchorages onto the map as indigo anchors. Demo mode includes real seamarks of the Bay of Mecklenburg.

By day, red and green buoys carry the colours of the chart — a deeper red and green that stand out better against the bright water in the sun —, and harbours appear as an indigo ring with an anchor on a light disc, so that next to the buoys they are not the loudest symbols. On the dark map the strong colours and the solid indigo disc remain; at night, the night set.

Depth contours and safety contour

Depth contours at 2, 5, 10 and 20 m — subtle, smoothed, each line with an upright depth label as on the chart. Before drawing, the model grid is scaled up to double density and smoothed twice: the lines run more calmly, the jags at the grid cells are smaller. They do not become more accurate — their position remains that of the model. Depths are a separate layer in the lighthouse menu, independent of the seamarks, and like these are loaded with every offline pack and are then available offline. (from the next build) They come as ready-made files, see “How fine the depths are” below. The model covers European waters and part of the Caribbean; outside, the area line says so, without a retry button, and map and seamarks are saved anyway. For now the map does not load either of them by itself (see “Loading online — off for now” under Offline packs): they only come with a saved area. Demo mode includes the depths of the Bay of Mecklenburg.

Checked against the map’s water. Lines and soundings only exist where the depth model and the map’s water areas agree — no line runs across land; only at the shore itself can one still lie up to one grid cell beside the map’s shoreline. In rivers and harbour basins narrower than around 800 m (seven grid cells), the map shows no depth contours or soundings at all — whichever direction the water runs. The 800 m apply to the 120 m cells of a normal area; in large areas with a coarser grid the limit is correspondingly higher. So there are no depth contours in the Schlutuper Bucht and on the narrow stretch of the Trave up to the Pötenitzer Wiek, nor in the Warnemünde sea canal and on the Unterwarnow up to Rostock; in front of the entrance and in the wide harbour basins they remain. This is intentional, not a fault: there the chart with its channel and line of buoys applies, and a model line would lie within its own error. So that such water does not look like deep water, it is finely hatched in grey, as are inland lakes and harbour basins the model does not know; larger areas carry the note “No depths – use chart” from zoom 10. The hatching only lies on the map’s water, up to the shore, not on mole and quay; in night mode it is only very faint, without a bright area. Areas from an earlier build only get it, like the shading, after reloading. Wider stretches of water such as the Pötenitzer Wiek or the Dassower See keep their lines. If the app could not fetch the map’s water while loading, the area line under Settings → Map → Offline maps says so, with “Try again” below it.

Safety contour. If a draught is stored on the boat (Tools → Boat), the map emphasises exactly one line: the shallowest contour that still lies deeper than draught plus buffer (at least 1 m, with a deep keel half the draught — so with a draught of 1.65 m, the 5 m line). All other contours are subdued, because on the plotter the one line that counts is the one you do not cross.

Without a draught, the 2 m line is the default: it is emphasised and carries the label “2 m (default)”; in the layers menu, “Depth contours” carries a second line telling you where to enter the draught (Tools → Boat). The number of the safety contour takes precedence over seamark names and soundings — before, a buoy name or a sounding could push it aside. It does give way to the seamarks themselves, so that it does not hide a topmark; the names sit below their buoy. Depth figures follow the unit under Settings → Units: on the lines with unit (“16.4 ft”), the soundings in the same unit without.

Shallow water shaded. Water shallower than the safety contour is lightly shaded — like the shallow water on a paper chart and the shallow-water shading of a plotter: where it gets tight for your boat, you see it at a glance, without reading numbers. The shading only appears where the depth model knows something. It ends at the safety contour, at the edge of the downloaded area, in front of inland lakes the model does not know, and in front of rivers and harbour basins narrower than around 800 m — where there are no depth contours either; that water is hatched grey instead (see above). Towards the shore it ends where the model sees the coast; that is not exactly the map’s shoreline, so depending on the spot a narrow strip stays unshaded or a little shading lies on land. Without a draught on the boat it reaches to the 2 m line. The safety contour is somewhat narrower than before, because the shading helps carry it, and remains the strongest line. In the dark appearance the shading is a dark blue; in night mode it disappears, so that no bright area costs you your night vision — then the amber safety contour carries it alone. Areas you downloaded with an earlier build only show the shading once their depths have been reloaded: if “Load map online” is on, the app reloads them quietly the first time the map is opened after an app start, if there is a connection then — if the connection only comes later, it catches up then, and in iOS Low Data Mode it waits until a normal connection is back. If the switch is off, their lines stay without shading until you refresh the depths under Settings → Map → Offline maps with the arrow in the pack row. An area’s depths need more space for this, three times as much for the demo area (about 860 instead of 280 KB).

How fine the depths are. (from the next build) The depths come from EMODnet, delivered as ready-made files via Cloudflare: one file per 1° × 1°, already calculated and checked against the water of the map. Nothing is calculated on the device any more, and every area loaded this way is equally fine: the grid is around 120 m, the resolution of the EMODnet model. The lines are simplified during calculation; no omitted point lies more than 10 m from the line. For very large extents the app declines the depths before loading instead of loading them coarser: the area line gives the size, for example “Depths for this area: about 85 MB — choose a smaller area.”; the app loads map and seamarks independently of that. Large areas you have already saved stay as coarse as they were loaded until their depths are reloaded — by hand with the arrow in the pack row under Settings → Map → Offline maps. If the source cannot be reached, the app calculates from EMODnet itself as before: up to an area of around 80 × 80 nm the grid is then about 120 m, larger extents are capped and therefore coarser. If the source answers but not all files arrive, the app saves nothing; the area line says so, with “Try again” below. Model data — for planning, not for anchoring to the metre.

Dashed means approximate. A depth contour whose position the model does not give precisely is dashed — that is how the chart draws an approximate depth contour — and carries “≈” before the number: “≈ 2 m”, for the default contour “≈ 2 m (default)”. This happens in three places — close to the shore, with a coarse grid, and where EMODnet rates the survey as low or does not rate it (paragraph “Survey quality” below). Close to the shore: where a line runs over a grid cell that borders land or is land itself, its position is only accurate to about one cell, so around 120 m in a normal area; only that stretch is dashed. Coarse grid: in an area whose grid is coarser than 200 m — that starts at around 60,000 km², about 130 × 130 nm —, every line is dashed, and the area line under Settings → Map → Offline maps says “coarser than 200 m — lines dashed”. (from the next build) Areas loaded from the ready-made files always stay below that; so this only affects large areas from earlier builds and those the app had to calculate itself. Colour and weight stay as they are: even dashed, the safety contour is the strongest line, amber at night. Strong and dashed means: “It gets shallow here — exactly where, the model does not know.” The chart notice above says it in one sentence.

Survey quality. The depth model is assembled from surveys by different agencies and decades, and EMODnet rates each with a quality index from 0 to 100 that combines accuracy, age and purpose of the survey. It comes with the area when downloading, and the app draws accordingly: where EMODnet rates the survey as low (below around 46) or does not rate it at all, the line is dashed and carries “≈” — the safety contour too. Contiguous unrated water areas of 5 km² and more carry the note “Quality unknown” from area zoom — in the calm colour of the depth contours, amber at night, several times on large areas, at least around 8 km apart, without fill and without hatching. The note does not say “unsurveyed”, because EMODnet does not rate some sources at all — for example the Danish depth model, which covers large areas in the South Funen Archipelago: soundings were taken there, only the index does not say how accurately. The soundings therefore remain, and likewise with low quality. The demo bay shows all classes: off Kühlungsborn and Warnemünde the lines are solid except for stretches close to the shore, north of there and off Gedser dashed, with “Quality unknown” in between.

The area line under Settings → Map → Offline maps tells you how much of the water area is of good (index from around 62), medium, low or unknown quality, for example “Water area quality: good 62 % · medium 1 % · low 12 % · quality unknown 24 %” — counted grid cells, each class rounded to whole per cent; water for which the index says nothing at all counts towards no class. Below that is the source of the depths at one point in the area, for example “Source GERMANY-BALTIC-SEA-DTM 2024” in the Bay of Mecklenburg. If the app could not fetch the quality index while downloading, the line says so in orange, with “Try again” below it; the lines are then not dashed by quality.

Offline packs

The base map (OpenFreeMap/OpenStreetMap) only works at sea if you have saved the area beforehand — ideally on the harbour Wi-Fi. Packs come without a limit and at every level of detail, including “Fine”. The way there: Settings → Map → Offline maps or, as long as no voyage is running, the hint “Area not saved” on the map (see below). Tools → Prepare → Offline area also leads there without a detour through Settings. The tile names the most recently saved pack with name and date, for example “Kieler Bucht · As of 27 Oct 2026”, or says “no area saved” in orange — one glance before casting off tells you whether there is a map on board at all. Whether it covers today’s area, the tile does not say; the cast-off checklist in the logbook does.

  1. Move the map at the top onto the area.
  2. Choose the level of detail: Coarse = overview, Medium = area, Fine = harbour approach. The size estimate is shown next to it before you download.
  3. Tap “Save visible area”. The app rejects extents that are too large instead of downloading half of Europe.

The pack list shows name (automatically after the place in the centre), progress and size; delete with a swipe and confirmation.

Load seamarks & depths only. The second button fetches buoys, harbours and depth contours for the extent, but no map tiles — (from the next build) a few MB depending on the extent, up to Build 57 a few hundred KB, instead of hundreds of MB. Offline, the base map then stays empty, but shallow water and seamarks are there. Included in all tiers: shallow water is safety information, the Free limit only caps map tiles. For now the map does not fetch this data without a button press (next paragraph): this button and saving an area are the ways to seamarks and depths.

Loading online — off for now. Loading seamarks, harbours and depths on the fly is switched off until it works reliably: they only come from areas you save or fetch with “Load seamarks & depths only”. “Load map online” applies only to the base map — its tiles come from the network for the extent. What the map fetched by itself in Build 33 stays saved and is drawn; the “Auto-downloaded” row (below) remains so that you can clear it. Outside saved areas, the chip at the top says “Seamarks & depths not loaded here” (without a running voyage a tap leads to Offline maps), with the switch off “Offline maps only …”. The next paragraph describes how loading on the fly worked in Build 33.

Where the downloaded data ends. A thin dashed blue-grey line — information, not a warning, amber at night — labelled “End of depths and seamarks” outlines the area in which depths and seamarks are both on the device: your saved areas and what the map fetched by itself in Build 33, in demo mode the demo area. Like the chart boundary on a plotter, it shows the edge before the view shows the gap. Areas that adjoin or overlap get a shared border without a middle edge; outside, buoys, depth contours or both are missing, the base map continues. The line appears as long as seamarks or depth contours are shown, from the overview down to the harbour. It is finely dashed and pale: background information that does not drown out buoys, wind layer and track — whoever looks for it finds it, and what it says the map shows anyway: beyond it, buoys and depth contours end. The label only appears zoomed in, from about the scale at which a bay like the Kieler Förde fills the screen (scale around 1 nm), and only on edges that are at least as long on screen as half the width of an iPhone — in the middle of the edge, on a long edge also twice in the picture; close to a very long edge it can lie outside the picture. You cannot tap the line — a long press on it opens the radial menu, as everywhere. In all tiers.

Loading online, as it worked in Build 33. If Settings → Data & privacy → Load map online is on, the map fetches not only the tiles for the extent but also seamarks, harbours and depths by itself — in all tiers and also under way, as soon as there is a connection and you are not looking at an overview. The view the map opens with in the harbour is enough for this; on the iPad it takes one more zoom level. Loading happens in fixed blocks of 0.5° × 0.5° — in the western Baltic around 32 × 56 km —, each block once; after that it is saved and available at sea without a connection. A block costs a measured download of around 1 MB (0.7 to 1.3 MB for five blocks between Flensburg and the Bay of Mecklenburg, most of it the depth grid) and takes up to around 400 KB on the device (measured on ten blocks around Flensburg and Kiel, depending on the coast and the number of seamarks). At 6 kn, a new block is added every three to five hours by calculation — that is how long the boat takes to cross one. While a block in view is loading, the top says “Loading seamarks & depths …” — except in the harbour with your own boat in view, where “Area not saved” takes precedence; usually depths and buoys are there within a few seconds; if the seamark server throttles, it can take two minutes. Seamarks and depths load separately, so a throttled seamark server does not hold up the depths. If a download fails, the map retries the same block with this service after ten minutes at the earliest; after two failures in a row the service rests for ten minutes entirely — the “Auto-downloaded” row below gives the reason. If the switch is off, the map loads nothing by itself, and the top says, as before, “Offline maps only — this area is missing” (without a saved pack “… none saved yet”). The demo loads nothing on the fly; it brings its bay with it.

What you downloaded by hand takes precedence. If an automatically downloaded block overlaps an area you saved or fetched with “Load seamarks & depths only”, the depth contours and soundings of the area apply there: the block’s lines end at the area’s edge, nothing is drawn twice. Seamarks appear on the map only once anyway.

Two areas overlapping. If two areas you downloaded overlap, the one with the finer grid applies there — with the same grid, the one downloaded last. The lines and soundings of the other end at its edge, nothing appears twice. In the area list the other one carries a grey line “overlaps … — … applies there”; if it lies entirely within the finer one, it says “lies entirely within …”.

Download approaches — a fine map (zoom 14) only within a radius of three nautical miles around each destination in the visible extent, all destinations in one pack. You need “Fine” where it gets tight (fairways, pier heads, approach buoys), not on a beat: a good megabyte per destination instead of around 50 MB for the same zoom level across the whole area. You download the area itself alongside in “Medium”.

How long it takes. A small extent is there in seconds. A large area is split into blocks, and the button counts along (“Seamark 7 of 36”). “Cancel” below it costs nothing: downloaded blocks remain, a later attempt adds the missing ones. The seamarks come from separate files built once a week from OpenStreetMap; “As of” in the area line is their data date, and if it is older than three weeks when downloading, the line says so. If a block fails, the app retries in several rounds; whatever is missing at the end is shown in the area line, also after a restart, and “Try again” adds it.

Downloaded areas, map storage, hint on the map

Downloaded areas is the separate list below the packs: name, number of seamarks and harbours, depth contours, size and date; “Refresh” fetches the extent again. Seamarks and depths are stored separately from the map tiles. If you delete a pack whose extent does not match exactly, they stay and carry the marker “no map package” — swiping left deletes them individually. If a depth download fails, the reason appears in red in the row, along with “Try again”, also after an app restart. If an area’s grid is coarser than 200 m, “coarser than 200 m — lines dashed” appears below the depth contours: on the map, every one of its depth contours is then dashed. Below the depth contours there are also the water area quality in per cent and the source (see “Survey quality” in C3); if the quality index was missing when downloading, an orange line says so.

What the map loaded by itself in Build 33 is not listed individually but as one row “Auto-downloaded” at the very top — only as long as such blocks are on the device: number of blocks, storage used, most recent date and the sentence that for now the map does not load new blocks by itself. Swipe left → “Clear” removes them all after a confirmation; after that nothing comes back by itself — if you need the area, save it. Anyone who wants to manage an area permanently and individually downloads it by hand.

Map storage shows how much space offline packs and tiles viewed along the way take up together — one combined number, because both live in the same file. “Clear the cache” discards only the viewed tiles; packs, seamarks and depths remain.

“Area not saved”. With a connection, this hint appears at the top of the map as soon as your boat is in view and no pack covers the extent — a tap leads straight to saving. When you are just moving the map around, it stays quiet. Without a connection, a chip shows “Offline: <pack>” instead, or in orange “no pack for this area”. The hint is meant to come while you can still download — not only once the connection is gone. It only appears as long as no voyage is running: you download an offline pack in the harbour, not at six knots on a beat. Under way, a hint “Seamarks & depths not loaded here” only says that the map shows no buoys or depths here — an empty chart does not mean “no dangers”; you can download them in the harbour. The hint means: no area is saved here — for now the map does not load seamarks and depths by itself (see “Loading online — off for now” above). If you hold the iPhone in landscape with a very large text size set, the hint gives way while the anchor banner is at the top — its buttons then need a line of their own (see B2). Afterwards it returns if it still applies; during the MOB alarm it rests anyway.

Test
  • Save your home area in “Medium”, switch on flight mode and open the map: are tiles, buoys and depth contours all there? Does the number under “Downloaded areas” match what you see on the map?
  • Enter your draught under Tools → Boat (⋯ → Edit boat, section “Dimensions”): is the emphasised safety contour the line you would choose for your boat yourself?
  • Read the map in sunshine on deck: does it stay legible — and is there any information you miss in the Contrast style?
↑ Back to contents

C4Navigating: Go-To & routes

Steer for a destination, follow a sequence of waypoints and keep an eye on other vessels — all on the main map, with numbers that stay calm on a beat and in a seaway.

Go-To: steering for a destination

Go-To is the simplest form of navigation: a bearing to a point. You start it in three ways:

  • A long press on any point on the map, then Go-To in the radial menu.
  • The Go-To button in the point card of a harbour, a course mark or a seamark. Seamarks can be tapped and show name, type, bearing and distance.
  • The destination choice of the next open stage (“Where to today?” — a tap chooses the day’s destination) in the “Voyage” context field: “Set destination” means navigating, not just recording — the course line appears on the map, and the plotter tiles BRG/DTW/VMC/TTG run along.

On the map, the Go-To appears as one blue line at the top left: bearing, distance, course error and the × to end it — the glance on the way to the buoy needs no more, and the map stays clear. Before the number is the word GO-TO — under the crosshair, or above the destination name while a voyage is running —, and the course error carries the abbreviation COG: “COG ▶ 12°” means your course over ground lies 12° to the left of the destination — the arrow points to the side on which the destination lies; VoiceOver says it in words. A tap on the line expands the full tile, another collapses it again: bearing and distance large, below them the course error (COG against bearing as ◀ or ▶ with degrees, above 5° orange with a warning triangle — by day as an orange tag with black text, because orange directly on the blue was too weak in the sun; at night amber on a black capsule, see C16; in the dark appearance orange on a black capsule, because orange there could hardly be read on the light blue), the ETA and the deviation from the starting line (XTE) — how far you are to the side of the original course line boat → destination. While a voyage is running, the line also carries the destination name and arrival time (B2); if the width is not enough, a course error up to 5° drops out of the line first — it remains in the expanded tile. If you are more than 5° off, the name is shortened first; if there is no arrival time because the boat is not getting closer, the warning always stays in the line. On the map, the course line from boat to destination is dashed – the solid one is the COG vector. The destination stays active until you end it with “×” — even across an app restart. Bearing, distance and the COG comparison are free.

In a running race, the Go-To follows the course (see C5): at the starting gun the course takes over the destination, even one of your own from the pre-start – “Undo” brings it back. A destination you set yourself during the race stays until you end it with ×. When it ends — with × on the map, with “End” in the routes or because a route of your own has finished —, the app immediately follows the course again.

ImportantGo-To is a bearing, not routing: the course line to the destination and every leg of a route run straight, even across land and shoals. Whether a leg is clear, you check yourself on the chart.

ETA: from the approach, not from the momentary speed

The ETA is calculated from the averaged approach over the last few minutes — how fast the remaining distance is actually shrinking. The momentary VMC is no good for this: on a beat, the momentary speed towards the mark is meaningless, and in a seaway such a display jumped between twenty minutes and two hours. If you are moving away from the destination or lying still, you see no ETA instead of an invented number — the line and the expanded tile leave it out; “--” is then shown only by the ETA and TTG tiles on the “Navigation” page.

Laylines

If wind data is available (NMEA network or demo), the map also draws the laylines from the mark: green the starboard layline, red the port layline. The angle comes from your boat’s polar optima; without polar data, 45° and 150° apply. Whether it is an upwind or a downwind leg, the app detects by itself.

Planning routes

A route is a sequence of waypoints that you create ashore under Tools → Routes. The most direct way is the route mode of the main map:

  1. Press and hold on the map and choose Route. A blue band at the top says what a press does now, and has exactly one exit: Done.
  2. Each further long press adds a waypoint — blue line, numbered pins, in the band a counter and Undo.
  3. Done opens the route form with the points you set.

While route mode is on, a running Go-To route is hidden. MOB and anchor watch stay in their place in every mode, and no mode survives an app start: if you open the app at night, you get the navigation map, never yesterday’s tool.

You bring existing routes back onto the map with Edit on the map in the form; Done saves back once, and an undo bar takes it back. Waypoints also come from the harbour guide, the waypoint library, the seamarks or from your own position. For each waypoint you set a name and an arrival radius (50–500 m); you change the order by touching and holding a waypoint and dragging it, and for a saved route also via Edit at the top right. The list shows the bearing and distance of each leg as well as the total length. Planning is free.

Following a route

Start route makes the first waypoint the Go-To destination. As soon as you are within the arrival radius, the route moves on by itself — with haptics and the notification “Waypoint reached”. The waypoint reached becomes the base of the new intended course line, so the XTE always refers to the current leg. The banner shows “WP 2/5”, the name of the waypoint and, after Σ, the remaining distance to the end of the route. A large Next button moves on by hand, for example when you deliberately skip a buoy; an undo bar brings the waypoint back. On the map, legs already sailed are grey, upcoming ones in the accent colour, the waypoints numbered. The plotter tiles BRG, DTW, XTE, VMC, ETA and TTG always refer to the active waypoint.

In demo mode the route “Kühlungsborn – Warnemünde” with four waypoints is ready.

AIS: other vessels

If your NMEA network provides AIS data (NMEA 2000 or NMEA 0183; multi-part sentences are assembled), the map shows other vessels with a 6-minute vector, and with names when zoomed in close. Class A is dark blue, Class B turquoise; after 3 minutes without a new position a target counts as stale (see below), after 6 minutes it disappears. You switch the layer in the layers menu. The length of the vector follows the same time as for your own boat — six minutes, unless you choose otherwise under Settings → Map → COG vector.

From area zoom, each target appears as on a plotter as a filled triangle with its tip in the direction of travel (course over ground), Class A somewhat larger than Class B; the vector grows from the tip. A circle instead of the triangle means: no current course. Without a vector, the vessel is not making way or not sending a course — or it is no longer reporting, in which case it appears dashed (see below); with a vector, its last course report is more than a minute old — for small craft under 2 kn that is normal, they then only transmit every three minutes —, and the vector shows the last reported course. If a target falls below the warning threshold, it is red with a ring around it. At night both classes are light grey, the names amber on black; the dangerous target stays red, its name black on red. To tap, you do not have to hit the triangle exactly — a tap just beside it opens the point card too.

How far you zoom in determines how much a target shows: in the overview it is a small dot with its vector, from area zoom the triangle, and the name only appears close in — before, the vessel names piled up over the harbour name in the overview. A target below the warning threshold shows symbol and name at every zoom.

For each target the app calculates CPA and TCPA against your own vessel. If a target falls below the warning threshold — default 0.5 nm CPA and 20 min TCPA, adjustable under Settings → Map in the range 0.25–2 nm and 10–30 min —, it turns red, and a red warning button appears at the top: “CPA 0.3 nm” large, name and TCPA as a second line. The warning button is never shortened: if it shares the line with the Go-To – which happens above the MOB banner while an alarm is running –, the name of the Go-To destination is shortened first, and if not even its bearing fits beside it, the Go-To chip gives way entirely. If, with a very large text size, the width is not enough even for the warning button alone, it wraps onto another line. At the same time the app reports the danger as a time-sensitive notification — per target on entry, after that at most every 5 minutes — even with the phone in your pocket. A tap on a target opens the point card with vessel type, MMSI, call sign, speed and course, CPA/TCPA, length and age of the data.

Without a fresh course of your own. If no course has come from the onboard network for more than 10 seconds — many sensors leave the course field empty at low speed —, the approach is calculated with the phone’s course; if that is not fresh either, with your own boat standing still. A target heading towards you then keeps warning, and in the point card “without own course” appears after CPA/TCPA.

Target without a new report. If no new position comes from a target for more than 3 minutes, it appears as on a plotter as a “lost target” at its last position, without a vector: from area zoom as a dashed symbol without fill, by day in the colour of its class, at night in light red — you recognise “stale” by the shape, not the colour; in the overview it is a hollow dot, a ring without fill. The app does not continue calculating CPA and TCPA from the old position: the red warning button disappears, and instead of “CPA … · TCPA …” the point card names the last report. Directly under the name, in semibold in a dashed capsule with a clock symbol, it then says “STALE · 4 min” — even before bearing and speed, which are thus to be read as old. Below that, smaller, “CPA then 0.3 nm” — the CPA that applied when the last report arrived; if the target was already moving away then, it says “was moving away then”. The word appears exactly when the symbol on the map is dashed. If the target was dangerous at its last report, it stays red with ring and name until it leaves the map after 6 minutes: a vessel whose AIS cuts out close to you is very probably still there.

For CPA and TCPA the app uses your measured own course and measured speed, not the display values smoothed by damping: after a tack, the warning calculates with the new course instead of one that is still lagging behind. Warning button, red targets and point card calculate with the same values as the notification.

Demo mode shows three targets: a ferry, a freighter and a sailing yacht that converges and triggers the warning. The freighter falls silent after about two minutes, and on purpose: this way you see what a target that has gone quiet looks like. After about three minutes it appears as a circle instead of a triangle, the vector still shows its last course. After five minutes it is stale — dashed without fill and without vector —, after eight it disappears. After that only the ferry and the sailing yacht remain on the map. If you restart the demo or come back after briefly switching to another app, the sequence starts from the beginning.

TestSet a Go-To on a buoy and beat towards it: does the ETA stay steady, and does it show “--” as soon as you move away? In the demo (any demo tier), sail the route “Kühlungsborn – Warnemünde”: does the route advance within the arrival radius, does the notification arrive, and does the undo bar bring the waypoint back after Next? If you have AIS on board: does the CPA warning match what your plotter shows, and does the notification reach you with the phone in your pocket?
↑ Back to contents

C5Regatta: pre-start & race

A race is a voyage with a starting gun: once it is created, pre-start, countdown, tracking and — with a finish line set up — finish detection run on their own — you only operate what is really needed on the water.

Creating a race

A race is a special voyage type with a start time, countdown and its own analysis. There are three ways in:

  • Map → Cast off → Race … — only with the “Regatta” focus; otherwise the green button creates a voyage straight away.
  • Logbook → Menu (⋯) → New race — the same form.
  • Tools → Calendar — for a regatta event, a “Start” button appears on race day (with a confirmation). It creates the race together with the stored course template. The same button is also at the bottom of the opened event — for example when you open it via “Next event” in the logbook.

The form asks for name, starting gun time, course, scoring and notes. The course row offers your saved course templates — marks, lines and time rules come along when you save. If there is a calendar event with a course for today, it is already filled in. Also in the form: the length of the pre-start phase, selectable as 5, 6, 10 or 15 minutes (5 for 5-4-1 starts).

If notifications are off for Etmar, a row with a crossed-out bell appears below the pre-start length: pre-start and starting gun then won’t alert you on the locked phone. Tapping the row opens the notification settings. The countdown in the app still runs.

Below the pre-start length you choose the Signals: “Sound + voice”, “Sound only” or “Off” — like the length, a default for the next races. Below that, Preview signals plays a compressed sequence (double tone, “One minute”, three ticks, gun): listen once ashore to check the volume is enough. The row about notifications being off then adds that sound and voice still play as long as the app is running.

Special case, pursuit start: if a course carries a start procedure in which not everyone starts at the same time (kangaroo/pursuit start by Yardstick, group starts), the app asks for your own start time straight after you join such a course (for example a course from the community), as long as the course still has upcoming dates — to the second, with its own seconds field below the time, because the sailing instructions require starting to the second by radio or GPS clock. The time applies to all dates of the course; you change individual race days in the calendar. The bundled Wednesday race has no upcoming dates any more (the dates in September 2026 are over) — so the app doesn’t ask for it.

Wind against the course

Which leg is upwind, which close reach, which broad reach, which downwind — and on which tack? Headsail, spinnaker and the work before the windward mark depend on it. Before the start, at the top of the Race details sits the row Wind against the course: wind at the start and at the finish from the GFS forecast, the borderline cases with a diamond, and how many legs are upwind or downwind. During the race it sits below the analysis, after the finish as a way into the briefing, without a forecast. Until the gun, a row below the race buttons in the logbook leads straight there (“1st leg beam reach · 2 borderline”).

A tap opens the table — at the standard text size without scrolling. The row and the logbook row open the race’s briefing (C6). The separate screen is gone: table and sketch are in the briefing itself. It opens with the map, “Map · Sketch” below switches to the sketch, “At start time” is set to the start time before the gun, and instead of the angle to the wind the table gives the course — true, both tacks on upwind and downwind legs — and there the distance sailed. The angle to the wind is still in the sketch and in the borderline note. The point of sail before the start is calculated against the stored polar: closer than its tacking angle means beating, deeper than its gybing angle means tacking downwind, in between close reach up to 70°, beam reach up to 110°, broad reach above that. This is deliberately not the threshold of the analysis, which counts every sailed board up to 75° to the wind as upwind.

Times only with a polar. The app calculates the legs from the boat’s stored polar: upwind and downwind via VMG, the other courses via the speed on the course, each leg with the wind of its estimated departure time. It does not use the sailed polar for this; measured values get in via “Transfer into the stored polar”. Without a polar, the wind at the start time applies to all legs, upwind and downwind start from an assumed 45° and 150°, and instead of the times it says “Times per leg need a polar.” The app does not calculate extension legs. The forecast is fetched as soon as you choose “At start time” in the briefing; upwind and downwind are calculated over the boards at the tacking or gybing angle, without tacking losses, and without a polar it says “No polar, no times” with the link to the polar data.

Empty doesn’t mean gone. Without a course the row says “No legs without a course.” and leads to the course; if the start is more than 5 days away, it names the day from which the forecast is available; with no connection and no saved data, “The forecast needs a connection once.” In the demo, the race of “Nordwind” carries a made-up forecast — the source then says “(demo)”.

Pre-start: runs on its own

Once the race is created you don’t need to trigger anything; the app also survives a restart:

  • When the pre-start phase begins, the pre-start entry is created automatically, GPS tracking starts, and a notification sits on the lock screen. In addition, RAW data recording starts if an NMEA network is connected.
  • Even with the iPhone in your pocket: from 2 hours before the start the app stays ready in the background, and the timers fire on time without the app open. If the race was created earlier, a significant location change — typically the drive to the harbour — wakes the app up again, even if iOS has terminated it.
  • The countdown is shown large in the logbook; in the last minute the orange capsule “LAST MINUTE” sits above it, and after the gun the race time runs in green. The phases: waiting → pre-start (yellow) → race (green). Before the gun the countdown rounds up: the digits say how many full seconds remain at most, the last second reads -0:01, at the gun the logbook shows +0:00 and the tile GO; before the gun, map, logbook and tile show the same second. During the race the app records a track point every 5 s instead of every 30 s, so that tacks and VMG can be analysed cleanly.
  • In the pre-start and race, the voyage’s map preview is dropped from the logbook — your own track answers no question there — and the controls sit directly below the countdown or race time. On the right of the first row, next to “Ping pin”, “Ping boat” and “Sync” or the finish pings and “Recall”, is MOB (hold for 0.6 s) — the same place as in a voyage. With the large accessibility text sizes, two controls and MOB stay on top, and the third sits full width below. Engine, sails and trim are at the same height. At night, “Race in progress”, the race time and pinged finish ends are shown in amber instead of green.
  • The same figures appear at the top of the map in the context bar: countdown, distance to the line, time-to-burn and bias above the picture in which you see your boat relative to the line. A tap on the bar opens the Regatta tab.
  • At the starting gun, the start entry is created exactly at the start time, with a strong haptic pulse and a notification. With the app open, the pre-start, one-minute and starting-gun notifications don’t lay themselves over the countdown as banners and don’t sound in addition to the signal — they land silently in Notification Centre. If the signals in the race form are set to “Off”, or you have muted them in the pre-start (it then says “Silent” there), the open app stays without sound but still vibrates at the fixed marks (pre-start start, 1:00, 0:30, 0:10); at the gun comes the strong haptic pulse of the start entry. Below the header of the pre-start card it then says “Signals off — vibrates at 1:00, 0:30, 0:10”.

Pre-start signals. In the pre-start the app makes itself heard, so that in the last minutes your eyes stay on the line and the committee boat: a double tone and “Pre-start” at the beginning, from 5:00 a tone with a call-out every full minute (“Four minutes”), at 1:00 a double tone and “One minute”, at 30 and 20 seconds a high tone, from ten a tone every second and a long one at the gun. Above 5:00 it stays quiet — counting starts from the warning signal, not from the start of the app’s pre-start. With the app open, the iPhone vibrates at the start of the pre-start, 1:00, 0:30 and 0:10 — even when the signals are set to “Off” or “Silent”. At the top right of the pre-start card in the logbook, Sound mutes the signals for this race (it then says “Silent” there; only tone and call-out, the vibration stays), with undo in the bar; Sync and ±5 min leave that as it is, and after an app restart the sound is back on. The signals also play with the ringer muted; the volume buttons control them. And they come out of your pocket: with the screen locked and with the app in the background, as long as it is running — in the pre-start the background GPS keeps it awake (location permission “Always”); music is turned down while this happens, not paused. If iOS has terminated the app, the notification one minute before the gun remains. Limits: only in the pre-start — never while waiting, not after a recall before the warning signal; a phone call interrupts them, and they don’t make up missed marks.

Apple Watch. The watch app is not included in version 1.0.

At the gun, “Sync” doesn’t turn into “Engine on” straight away: for ten seconds the same place shows “Race”, dimmed, and only then the engine button. A Sync press that comes a second too late therefore doesn’t write an engine entry into the race.

ImportantBackground readiness requires the location permission “Always” — check it in the iOS Settings before your first race.

Sync and postponement

Sync: tap once at the race officer’s signal — the starting gun is rounded to the next full minute, and the countdown is then right to the second. The button sits where it is needed: in the pre-start block of the logbook next to the pin/boat pings, on the map in the pre-start bar to the right of the ping buttons, in the same place throughout the pre-start.

Postponement (AP): “+5 min” moves the gun by a full five minutes; timers, countdown and notifications are rescheduled. Tapping several times adds up, “−5 min” takes it back. No date picker at sea. ±5 min is folded away behind “Postpone the start” in the logbook — rarely used things stay off the map.

Undo: Sync and ±5 min change the start time immediately, without confirmation. A mis-tap can be reversed for six seconds with “Undo” in the bar at the bottom (Sync on the map too); start time, countdown and signals are then back as before. “−5 min” is locked as soon as less than 5:10 remains — otherwise the gun would fall while the bar is still showing.

−5 min before the pre-start: as long as the race still shows “Waiting for pre-start”, “−5 min” never moves the gun so that the pre-start would begin immediately or within the next ten seconds. Instead of jumping, the bar then says “−5 min would start the pre-start — tap “Sync” at the signal”; nothing has changed. An undo couldn’t reverse that: recording and the pre-start entry begin with the pre-start. If you expand “Postpone the start” in the pre-start, the row with ±5 min moves up so that the undo bar doesn’t cover it after the first tap; if you collapse it again, the list is as before, with pin, boat and Sync in view. Every pre-start begins collapsed, even if the row was open in the race before. Create a race with a 15 min pre-start and a start in 30 minutes, tap “−5 min” three times: do the first two postpone, and does the third show the note without the pre-start beginning?

Recall after the gun: after a general recall (First Substitute) or an abandonment (flag N), the “Recall” button appears in the logbook next to the finish pings for the first ten minutes — tap it at the recall signal. The race is back in the pre-start; you don’t have to finish it and create it again. There is no countdown yet: the warning signal only comes one minute after the flag is lowered, and how long it stays up only the race committee knows. The status card therefore says “RECALL — new start”, the map bar and countdown tile show “RECALL”, the lock screen “Recall · new start”; time-to-burn is dropped, and no gun is fired. At the warning signal you tap “Warning” — the button sits in the logbook where Sync otherwise is. The start is then exactly five minutes ahead, and countdown, Sync, ±5 min, gun and starting-gun notification run as in the first pre-start. Until then, Sync in the map bar points to “Warning”. Pings, manual wind, course and recording stay, and the old start remains in the logbook as an ordinary “Start (recall)” entry. Instead of a confirmation, the bar with “Undo” appears — after “Recall” for 30 seconds, because after that there is no way back into the race; after “Warning” for 6 seconds. Only the device on which you tap “Recall” knows the waiting state: if Etmar is running on an iPad with the same iCloud at the same time, it counts there to the earliest possible start time and sets a start there.

Start line, bias, time-to-burn

Pinging the line: in the pre-start, sail past the pin end and the committee boat and tap “Ping pin” or “Ping boat” — the start line is set from your GPS position. Pinging again simply overwrites; a mis-touch has no consequences. A ping without a GPS fix tells you so; the ping ticks survive an app restart.

Distance to the line & time-to-burn: as soon as pin and committee boat are pinged, the pre-start block shows the perpendicular distance to the start line and time-to-burn — countdown minus the time to the line at the current approach. Red means: too early. Both are based on GPS and are free.

Line bias: once the line is in place (pinged or from the course), the pre-start block shows which end is to windward — “Pin +7° · 12 m” or “Boat +7° · 12 m”. The metre value is the windward advantage (line length × sin bias); below 3° it says “Line square”. You set the wind direction via “Set wind”: a wind rose that you turn with your thumb; it clicks every 5° with haptics, plus ±5° buttons and “Head to wind — take the course”, which makes your heading (HDG, otherwise COG) the wind direction. The direction stays saved but only applies for one hour — see below. With a wind sensor on the NMEA network or in demo mode it comes in automatically as TWD; the button shows the source. The row carries no tier badge — in the pre-start, the countdown is what counts.

A manual wind applies for one hour. For that long “manual” appears beside it, from ten minutes on with the age (“manual · 25 min ago”) — in the logbook’s bias row below the wind direction, in the bar on the map below the bias, in the sail choice after the wind direction as soon as a course is set. After that the app no longer calculates with it: no bias, no course side, no wind angle for the sail choice and no wind in the start entry at the gun. The bias row and sail choice instead say “Wind at 18:05 — stale” (from another day, with the date), and a tap on it opens the wind rose; the bar on the map says “Wind stale” in place of the bias with the time below it, as long as there is room for that next to countdown and distance. A wind from yesterday on a course that lies differently today is worse than none. “Clear manual wind” in the wind rose removes it immediately; ending the demo clears it too. If the wind is stale, the wind rose starts empty (or with the wind from the bus) and says below the rose what you had – “Last 215° at 18:05 – stale, take a new bearing”. “Done” thus only confirms a freshly taken wind, not the old one.

Pinging the finish line: “Ping finish stbd” and “Ping finish port” are available during the race, from the gun — starboard and port ends as seen when crossing the finish. Once both ends are set, the app detects the finish automatically. In the course editor there is also “Use current position” for each end — no typing in coordinates.

During the race

During the race there are deliberately only two buttons. Marker sets a GPS-tagged point with one tap for moments you want to look at later: a bad start, a shift, a near-collision. Event opens the full form. You don’t need to log tacks, gybes and mark roundings — the analysis detects them afterwards from the track. On the map, only what is needed at the helm remains in this phase: pinging, as long as the line is incomplete, and Sync.

Live analysis: during the race, four equally sized tiles in fixed places sit at the top of the Regatta tab, one figure each. Next mark: the name on one line, the side as a word and colour next to the label (“gate” at a gate), the bearing in large type, the distance beside it — the same calculation as the bar and the logbook. VMG vs. polar Pro: VMG against the polar as a delta (“+0.3 kn” / “−0.4 kn”), below it the target (“Target 4.1 kn”); if you are more than 0.2 kn below, it also says “below target” — orange as text alone doesn’t hold up in the sun. Without Pro — and without wind from the onboard network — this place shows VMC to mark with “Progress to mark · GPS”, without a target and without colour; there is no lock during the race. Tacking angle: the average of the last tacks from the track (“Avg of 3 tacks · GPS”; botched tacks are left out, and a gybe is not a tack — only changes of tack upwind count). To the layline: the distance to the layline of the set mark; with no polar for the boat, “assumed 45°” or “150°” is shown with it. Deliberately nothing more: a tack counter, time on the wrong tack and distance totals need a comparison over time and belong in the analysis after the finish — anyone reading a table on the beat isn’t sailing.

Every tile is always there. If the value is missing, it shows “—” and the reason below, for example “No polar for this boat”, “No target when reaching” (between 60° and 120° to the wind the polar gives no VMG target), “No tack yet” or “No GPS fix”. Without wind from the onboard network the second tile is called VMC to mark: progress towards the next rounding from GPS, without a target and without colour, below it “Progress to mark · GPS”; negative means the mark is astern. On reaching and downwind legs that is the figure to steer by; on the beat it is no measure of pointing — whoever maximises the figure there pinches or bears away towards the mark. With Go-To the same figure is called VMC. The app detects tacks from the wind from the onboard network; without wind it reads the boards from the courses over ground of the last 50 minutes or so — two courses 70–110° apart, between which the boat switches on the same leg. The gun and mark roundings separate the legs, so bearing away at the mark doesn’t count as a tack, and the angle is shown from the first tack of the beat; on reaching and downwind legs the one from the last beat remains. Until then it says “From the first tack · GPS”, after that “Over ground from 3 tacks · GPS”. The layline needs the wind and a set mark — without wind it says “No layline without wind”, because a guessed figure that you steer by is worse than none; with no polar for the boat it calculates with 45° upwind and 150° downwind and says so below the figure.
