The mark is a Go-To destination you have set yourself, otherwise the next mark of the course. If the course expects a gate or the finish line, it says “Gate/finish: no layline”; if the mark has been missed and lies astern, “Mark missed”. Without an onboard network, then, mark, VMC and tacking angle show values from the iPhone GPS; only the layline gives the reason.

Finish and guest boat

You finish manually via “Finish race” (immediately, without confirmation; for six seconds “Undo” brings the race back) — or automatically: if a finish line or finish area is set up in the course, the app detects the crossing of the line. The finish entry is then created, the countdown stops, and the notification “Finish line crossed!” appears. Recording only stops at this point on courses without a possible extension; if the course has one (time rule or marked extra leg), it continues at the voyage rate until you are in the harbour – the finish ends the race, not the voyage. For this, every new race gets its departure point as its destination when it is created (300 m radius): returning there ends the recording, otherwise two hours of standstill do. In the technical view of the voyage map this way home is drawn thinner and paler, legend “After the finish” – the race course stays readable, and the miles still count. If the race is abandoned or you retire, the scoring window ends at the last scored mark rounding, not at the pontoon. For the first 60 seconds after the start there is a grace period, because the start and finish lines are often close together. As a safety net, after two hours of standstill the app asks by notification whether the race is over – and if the finish has already been scored while recording is still running, it doesn’t ask but ends the recording: a mooring entry at the time of the standstill, race completed, notification “Recording ended”; undo in the voyage detail via “Reopen”. The “Arrived?” button in the logbook now also appears for races. If the anchor watch is on, both wait until two hours after “Anchor up”; if the boat then stays put, the mooring entry is placed half an hour before “Anchor up”, not at the moment the anchor was dropped (the entry can be edited).

Finished too early? If the automatic detection triggered at a line that will be crossed again, open the race in the voyage detail with “Reopen”: countdown, course on the map and recording come back. The wrong finish stays in the logbook as an ordinary “Finish (discarded)” entry until you delete it – with time, position and anything you noted there. The next finish crossing counts again; 6 s of undo, as when finishing.

The course on the map. As long as the race is running, it sits on the main map without you doing anything – you don’t have to show it or open the voyage first. The next mark is larger and carries a ring and its name; the other marks are subdued but fully opaque. Deliberately subdued rather than transparent: on wet glass in the sun a semi-transparent mark disappears instead of being discreet – size and shape survive sun, wet and zooming out. After the finish the course is gone again.

The bar during the race. After the starting gun, the bar at the top of the map shows which mark is next: name, the side to leave it on (port or stbd as a word and colour, “gate” for a gate), the bearing in large type, then the distance and the race time in small type. After each rounding it moves on. If the app has not detected a rounding, tap Next on the right – the mark shown counts as done, and “Undo” in the bar at the bottom reverses it. A calmer way is via the map: tap the mark, Continue here. If a mark already lies astern without having been rounded, a warning sign appears in front of the name; it is never skipped automatically. If no side is shown, it can’t be read from the course – then choose it by hand in the course mark. Without a course the bar stays on the race time. As long as it carries the Go-To destination, there is no second Go-To row below it. Above the mark name is the word COURSE; if you are sailing to your own destination, it sits below as a narrow row with the word GO-TO and no blue background – you steer by the bar. If space gets tight — portrait on the iPhone, with the side capsule —, the race time goes first, then the name is shortened; the distance to the mark stays. In portrait, the side first moves next to COURSE, and the name gets the row below to itself; only if it doesn’t fit there either is it shortened – at the end (“Windward m…”), no longer in the middle. In landscape on the iPhone the bar stays on one line.

Next mark in the logbook, course in the Regatta tab. During the race the same mark also appears in the logbook, below the race time: name and side (“Windward mark · Port”), below that, in large type, bearing and distance from your position — the same calculation as in the bar; logbook and map never give two figures for one mark. Without a GPS fix it shows “—”, an old fix is dimmed and struck through, and a mark astern that hasn’t been rounded carries the warning triangle; without a course it says “No course set”. In the Regatta tab it appears as the “Next mark” tile; below that only After that: the completed roundings as one quiet row (“✓ 1–4 done”, on the right the mark that is next), then at most three open ones, the rest behind “+n more”. At the very bottom are three equally sized controls, 60 pt high: Course (opens the course), Analysis and Details (the race details). Before the gun, the tab shows the course as a list, one row per rounding (“2 · Windward mark · Port”, on laps “Windward mark (2nd)”), at most ten; “+n more” opens the course — for learning the course. Without a course it says “No course set — add one in Race details”.

The Go-To follows the course. After the starting gun the app sets the first waypoint, after each rounding the next, and at the end the finish line – there on the point closest to you, not the middle. In the pre-start it stays off: the start line is a line, not a point; that is what the line bias is for. If you set a destination yourself during the race, it is not overwritten – the app asks again at each rounding whether it should take over (with a button with a finish flag and the mark name in the Go-To row, next to the ×). A destination that is still left over from the pre-start at the gun is taken over by the course – “Undo” brings it back. If you end your own destination during the race with the X, the app goes straight back to the course. The same applies to “End” for routes and to your own route once it has finished; “Undo” after the takeover at the gun also appears in the logbook if you are there. You can switch this off under Settings → Regatta. After the finish the app clears its own Go-To; one you set yourself stays. Gates are not yet stamped individually; at the leeward gate the Go-To points to the mark behind it. A mark with “Pass only” counts as done as soon as it was abeam; a narrowly missed mark as soon as it lies a few hundred metres behind you.

The course must have been sailed. On many courses the finish line is the same line as the start, and in the pre-start you sail across it several times in both directions – the 60-second grace period alone isn’t enough against that. That is why a finish crossing only counts once the boat has, since the starting gun, been closer to the first course mark than to the start line at least once. It is measured against the first mark of the course, not any mark: otherwise a leeward mark just above the line would release the finish while still in the start area, and on a windward-leeward course with a shared start and finish line the race would end shortly after the gun. This condition comes from your course, not from a setting: a large course has large distances, a small one small ones. It also needs no detected rounding, just a single track point on the way to the mark – with patchy recording, the points at the mark are otherwise exactly the ones missing.

If the course has no course marks at all – for example because the race was started without a course by mistake –, there is nothing to compare. The finish then counts as soon as the boat has, since the starting gun, been far enough away from the finish line once; you set the distance under Tools → Settings → Regatta (100 to 2000 m, default 1000 m). “Finish race” works in every case.

Sailed on someone else’s boat? Via Edit voyage → Boat you can assign a race (or any voyage) to another of your boats afterwards — or mark it as “Sailed on another boat” with the name of the guest boat. Such voyages stay in the logbook (blue badge with the boat name) but don’t count towards the mileage and engine statistics of your own boat; in the statistics you can add them with a switch. In the demo, the calendar event “Club race — crewing” on the guest boat “Freya” shows this.

In demo mode

You can try out the pre-start without a boat and without a race: Tools → Help & demo → Start demo, then Regatta and Pre-start (C16). The demo shows the NSV Wednesday race with the real course from the library (C6, here with “Finish (short)” as the finish) on the demo boat “Nordwind”: both line ends pinged, the gun in 20 minutes. Until one minute before the gun the boat hovers behind the line, then it gets going. After that it sails the whole course in real time according to the Albin Express polar: upwind at the tacking angle with tacks at the layline, downwind with gybes, each mark on its side. In 12 kn of wind this takes about an hour and a half by calculation; tacks and gybes are then in the track. Race running, the second choice, sets the gun 40 minutes back: the track so far is already there, and the boat is in the middle of the course. You switch between the two while the regatta demo is running under Tools → Settings → Tier & demo → “Regatta demo”; the demo then restarts.

The regatta demo’s wind. With “Wind from open sources” and a connection, the demo fetches the current wind from the nearest station and the GFS forecast for Neustadt Bay — not for your position. “Wind against the course” then says “Wind: real (GFS/station) · Boat: simulated”, and the simulated wind sensor follows this wind, so row, sketch and instruments match. Without a connection or with the switch off, the demo sails with built-in wind (south-west 12 kn, slowly veering), and the source shows “(demo)”. The run knows nothing of current, land, depths or other boats; the demo’s seamarks and depths lie off Kühlungsborn, and in Neustadt Bay the map says “Seamarks & depths not loaded here”.

Test
  • Start the regatta demo with “Pre-start” and let it run to the end: does the demo boat round every mark on the correct side, does the app write the finish entry after about an hour and a half, and does the analysis then find the tacks and gybes?
  • Create a race with the starting gun in 20 minutes, lock the iPhone and put it away: do the pre-start and start notifications arrive on time without you opening the app?
  • Tap “Sync” at the signal: is the countdown then right to the second with the race officer’s clock?
  • Listen to the signals in the pre-start, phone in the mount and app in front: do tone and call-out come on time with the race officer’s clock, and are they loud enough against wind and water? And with the phone locked in your pocket, put away before the pre-start begins: do tone and call-out arrive there too — including the double tone at the start of the pre-start —, and does your music carry on at full volume afterwards?
  • Ping both finish ends: does the app detect the finish crossing, and do tracking and countdown stop on their own?
↑ Back to contents

C6Race course step by step: NSV Wednesday race

From the bundled club course to the running countdown — the complete route, using the Wednesday race of the Neustädter Segler-Verein as the example: join, understand the course in the editor, start on race day.

In Etmar a course is the race track as a data record: start line and finish line from two points each, course marks with detection radius and rounding side, and if needed gates, zones, info points and the time rules of the notice of race (shortening, extension, time limit per mark). On the map: start line green, finish line dark grey, marks yellow, course sequence dashed. If the finish line is the same line as the start – both ends at most 30 m apart, as on many windward-leeward courses –, a single label “Start / finish” appears there instead of two on top of each other. If two lines cross or their centres lie close together, there are still two labels, the finish’s one row lower.

It comes in two forms with the same controls: the course template, which you maintain ashore under Tools → Courses & waypoints and which applies every week, and the course profile of a voyage, which is copied from the template when you start and applies only to that race. The app calculates all times from the track — so a course can also be created or corrected after the race.

The quickest way in is a course from the community: sent in by other sailors, shipped with the app update — with organiser, area, dates and notes on the course. “Join” creates your own copy and enters the dates as a series in the calendar. You don’t have to place a single mark yourself.

Part 1: Getting the course

  1. Tools → Courses & waypoints opens the course library. It sits under “Prepare” next to the calendar: courses are created here, their dates there.

  2. At the top, a segment switches between “Courses” and “Waypoints”. Without a course of your own there is an empty state, below it “Courses from the community”. The “NSV Wednesday race” card names organiser and area, the next date (or, once the season is over, that the next ones will come with an update), the number of course marks and the version. The note “Pursuit start” warns in advance that the date is only the first start. Below it the notice of race appears on three lines, followed by “More” — so “Join” stays close instead of disappearing under a wall of text.

  3. “More” expands the notice of race in the row, “Less” collapses it again. It contains the sailing instructions word for word — course sequence, rounding sides, extension and shortening rule, time limit, shallow patch. Check it against the organiser’s documents. “Join” creates the copy. If the club changes the course later, “New version available” appears here: switch (old copy gone, dates move across, your own changes are lost) or keep both.

  4. In a kangaroo start (pursuit start) not everyone starts at the same time: each boat starts staggered according to its Yardstick, the calendar date is only the first start, and your time is in the notice of race or on the notice board. If such a course still has upcoming dates, the app asks for your start time straight after you join — to the second, with its own seconds field below the time, typed or set with the arrows, because the iOS time picker can’t do seconds but the sailing instructions require starting to the second by radio or GPS clock. “Take the start time” saves the time for all dates of this course; you change individual race days in the calendar. “Later” leaves the first start in place — the countdown would run out too early. With a new version the app asks again, because the rating changes from season to season.

    The bundled dates of the Wednesday race (September 2026) are over — so the app doesn’t ask for the start time when you join, and the course goes straight into your courses (next step). In the demo the race days still appear in the calendar: the demo creates the Wednesday race as its own weekly series.

  5. Back in the community card, below “Show course on the map”, is the green row “Joined — the course is in your courses”. The button draws the course on the main map.

  6. The “Courses” list now shows “NSV Wednesday race” at the top with “9 course marks” — your copy. From here on it is your course: editable, deletable, selectable as a template in the calendar and when starting. The community entry below it stays for switching to a new version. “Join” enters future dates in the calendar as a weekly series (Part 3) — for the bundled course only once the club has fixed the next season and it comes with an update.

Part 2: Understanding and adjusting the course

  1. A tap on your own course first opens its briefing — the course against the current wind: at the top first the map, each leg drawn by point of sail (upwind dashed, downwind dotted, otherwise solid) and, where there is room, labelled “number point of sail” — long legs first; whatever is crowded at the start is in the table. The names of the marks are not shown there but in the table — the map shows course and points of sail. Below it the wind choice Now · At start time · Assumed wind, a row with wind, source and age, the total time and for each leg the point of sail, the course true — on upwind and downwind legs both tack courses, the long one on top —, there the distance sailed with “≈” and the direct line small below it (the total row gives both), plus the time from the polar of your active boat. One tap further, the sketch: it is north-up like the map, with the wind as an arrow in it; “Map · Sketch” switches (C5, “Wind against the course”). Without wind there is no point of sail and therefore no sketch — it then says why. A diamond ◆ marks a borderline case; the footer says why. Without a polar there are no times, and upwind and downwind are classified against an assumed 45° or 150° to the wind; without wind, length and bearing are shown, and the sheet says what is missing.

    Now is the value of “Wind now” on the map, fixed for the whole course, without shifts; if the course is more than 15 km away from you, the briefing fetches station or model at the course and says so in the row — without a connection and in the demo it stays with the wind from here, and a second row gives the distance. At start time calculates with the GFS forecast for each leg at its departure time: − and + step hour by hour from the next full hour up to 5 days ahead (held down, they keep counting); the row gives the run and age of the forecast. Without a connection, with “Wind from open sources” switched off or in the demo, the sheet says why there is no forecast. Assumed wind you set in steps of 10° and 2 kn, starting from the last wind shown: “what if it veers 20°”. It is called “assumed”, applies fixed over the whole course and is not saved. At the very bottom, under “Races on this course”, are the races that were created from this course — date, wind (mean and direction, plus the word for the source), race duration and place; a tap opens the race. No verdict or comparison is given there; that stays in the analysis.

    Briefing of a race. Every race has the same view, with its own marks: in the Race details via the “Wind against the course” row (“Briefing” on the right) — before, during and after the race —, until the gun also via the same row in the logbook and via “Briefing” below the course list in the Regatta tab. “Edit” there opens the race’s course (Marks & lines), not the template; without a course it says “No legs without a course.” with “Choose course”. At start time begins at the race’s start time before the gun and calculates with its forecast, from one hour before the start with the local wind — at the start time the same points of sail and borderline cases appear as in “Wind against the course”. Table and sketch are in the briefing itself (C5, “Wind against the course”). Under “Races on this course” are the other races from the same template; if the race was created without a template, the sheet says so. In the regatta demo, “NSV Wednesday race (demo)” shows its briefing; “At start time” there calculates with the built-in forecast (“(demo)” at the source) or, with a connection and “Wind from open sources”, with real GFS and the note “Wind: real (GFS/station) · Boat: simulated”.

    Tapping the sketch, and full screen. Small in the briefing, the sketch shows only lines, numbers, marks and the wind at the start. Tapping a leg highlights it — wider, with a halo, its number filled (a diamond gets a ring), the others thinner and paler —, names the tack on it and shows its row from the table at the bottom of the sketch: point of sail, true course with the longer board first, distance and time. You don’t have to hit the line exactly; a thumb next to it is enough. A tap on empty space or on the same leg clears the selection. The button to the right of “Map · Sketch” opens the sketch full screen, portrait and landscape: the course fills the area, top right there is a panel with the wind at the start (solid) and at the finish (dashed), bottom left the legend and scale, plus mark names and the tack on the legs. No label covers another — when space gets tight, a name goes first, then a tack, and last the number of the shorter leg; zoomed in, they reappear. Double-tap zooms around that spot, + and − in steps up to six times, “Whole course” goes back, pinching with two fingers works too; zoomed in, you move the sketch with one finger. With −10° / +10° and −2 kn / +2 kn you turn the wind or change its speed; held down, the buttons keep counting: the first press switches to “Assumed wind · set by you”, starting from the wind currently shown, and sketch, row and table recalculate immediately; the view stays where it is. It is the same assumed wind as in the briefing, and it is not saved. In landscape, the row and buttons sit to the right of the sketch.

    “Edit” at the top right of the briefing opens the course editor as a full-screen map. On the right the tool palette (Mark, Gate, Info, Zone, Start, Finish), at the bottom the strip with the course sequence. With the “Mark” tool: tapping a buoy or a waypoint adds it to the course, tapping open water creates a new mark there, and tapping a course mark again means “round it again” (laps). The tool stays active; only after the second end of a line does the palette jump back to “Mark”. The arrow at the top undoes the last step on the marks, one step at a time. “?” shows a short help on how to use it, “Done” closes.

  2. “List” opens “Order & basic data”: name, start procedure (class start, pursuit start or group starts) and the course order. “Edit” enables dragging. The chips “Stbd” and “Port” are the rounding sides: derived from the order — a left turn around the buoy = to port (red), a right turn = to starboard (green) —, and changeable by hand by tapping the row.

  3. Further down are the two finishes “Finish (short)” and “Finish” — course N has an extension. Then the info points, here “Shallow patch” east of Pelzerhaken-S. An info point is a note pinned to a spot in the area and deliberately not a course element: not in the course order, never timestamped. On the map it is yellow with an “i”; a tap opens the text. It comes with the course into the race on the main map — edit it in the editor, during the race read only.

  4. At the end of the list is “Course change”. Shortening and extending are interventions by the race committee with a fixed time in the notice of race — which is why they belong to the course. “Shorten course from time” (here 20:50): anyone who reaches a rounding mark only from then on is scored there. “Extend course if finishing before” (here 20:30): if the first finish is crossed before this time, the fleet sails the extra leg; which marks belong to it you set on a course mark (step 13). On the row of the first finish sits the switch “Treat as a gate when extended”: off means the first finish is no longer part of the course when extended; on means it becomes a gate with a passage time. This is only scored in the analysis: the app merely suggests the shortening, and it only scores the extension when it is beyond doubt.

    The editor checks along. Two finish lines, but neither marked as the extension? Then a warning appears below the course order with the button “Mark second finish line as extension” – it marks the second finish and everything in between; Undo works as for every step. An extension time without an extension mark gets a note on the time rule that it has no effect, and the analysis says so too (“Extension: not applicable”) instead of staying silent. Without the marking, every race ends at the first finish. The same check is in the voyage’s course: Regatta → Course → List shows the warning and button in the same way — anyone who only creates the course on race day, or touches it up there, now sees it as well. In the analysis, the note appears directly below the extension time you set, not just in the footnote underneath.

  5. Tapping a mark in the course sequence opens the “Mark” sheet, here Pelzerhaken-S. At the top, name and position; the symbol in front shows the seamark type (inflatable buoy, lateral buoy, cardinal buoy, beacon, ship) that the map draws when you zoom in. “Rounding side” is set to “to port”; “Pass only” is for buoys that are to be left on one side without being rounded. The detection radius (default 60 m, 20–200 m) is only the tolerance within which the buoy counts as reached; the timestamp is the point of closest approach. “Time limit at this mark” is the earlier limit for this rounding mark, here 20:30: it is inherited backwards by earlier buoys without their own limit, never applies to the start, and with laps only to that one passage.

    If you switch on “Pass only”, the detection radius disappears from the sheet — it does not apply to such a mark. It gets its time at the moment it is abeam, even at a distance of one or two nautical miles (up to 5 nm, at most one leg length); in place of the radius, a sentence explains this. For that it needs its side: if the course runs straight past it, Etmar takes the side of the course line on which it lies. If the course bends at it or it lies on the line, Etmar does not set a side itself — at a headland the inside would be meant, at a boundary buoy the outside, and only the notice of race says which; a note then appears there, and the mark is saved anyway. And the other way round: if you missed the detection radius of an ordinary rounding mark because the buoy had been moved, the row no longer stays empty — the moment abeam applies here too, as long as it was within the narrow corridor around the buoy: ten times the radius, at least 500 m, so 600 m with the default radius.

  6. Further down in the same sheet: “Round again (another lap)” adds the same buoy to the course again — position, name and radius are maintained only once, and each passage gets its own rounding time and a lap badge. “The extension starts after that” sets the cut for the extra leg: everything after this row belongs to the extension, the row itself does not. On the NSV course the cut therefore belongs after the first finish (“Finish (short)”): the first finish triggers the extension and is never part of it; at Pelzerhaken-S the switch stays off.

  7. You meet the second editor on board: in the “Regatta” context tab, “Course” opens the course profile of the current voyage; in the Course chapter of the analysis, the way in is called “Adjust course”. Same full-screen map, same palette, same course sheet — only the scope is different: in the template you maintain what applies every week; here, what applies today. If the race committee moves a buoy at short notice, you correct it here without touching the template, and if needed save the changed course as a new template via the menu.

Part 3: From the event to the starting gun

  1. Tools → Calendar shows the series under “Upcoming”: every Wednesday “NSV Wednesday race” — with a pursuit start at your start time, not the first start; in the demo at 18:00. On race day you tap “Start” in the event. The training event “Forestay trim training II” on Tuesday also carries “Start”: it creates the training, without a countdown. With “+” you create your own regatta events, with the row “Course template”. Series can be edited together: “Apply to future events in the series” carries over name, time, boat, course and notes to all outstanding events.

  2. The other way is via the logbook: with no voyage open, the menu at the top right (⋯) offers “New voyage”, “New race” and “New training”; “New race” opens the same form as Map → Cast off → Race …, which the map only offers with the focus “Regatta”. “Cast off” below it, on the other hand, starts a normal voyage without a countdown. With no voyage open, “Regatta” and “Training” also appear directly in the list, in the “Today” section above “Next event” — the same forms as in the menu.

  3. The form asks for name, starting gun (quick picks +5 to 1 h, or date, time and seconds), pre-start duration (5, 6, 10 or 15 minutes; 5 for 5-4-1 starts), course and scoring. The course row offers your templates — marks, lines and time rules come along. If there is a calendar event with a course today, it is pre-filled; the picture shows “None” because there was no event on the day it was taken. Without a course, the pre-start has no line, distance or bias. Tracking begins with the pre-start; “Start” creates the race.

  4. “Start” in the calendar asks first — “Create race”: “NSV Wednesday race” is created with course and countdown, in the demo “Start 18:00”. “Create now” creates the race with course profile, time rules and your start time; a mistaken tap on “Start” has no consequences.

  5. The context panel switches to “Regatta”: at the top the course as a list, mark by mark in course sequence, below it three handles — “Course” with the number of marks and lines (opens the editor on board, step 14), “Analysis” and “Details”. Until the gun, “Briefing” sits below the list: the race’s course against the wind (C6); during the race the row disappears. Countdown, distance to the line and bias appear at the top of the map during the pre-start, not here. After the scored finish the tab is called “Voyage” again and shows the voyage too. There, instead of the stage plan, it then shows “Race analysis” — a tap opens the same analysis as “Analysis” before the finish.

  6. “Race details” is the race’s voyage detail: Analysis, “Course” with Marks & lines, Map, Redraw track, Daily report (wind) and below that the summary with the starting gun to the second. Here you check before casting off whether starting gun and course are right. The row “Wind against the course” opens the race’s briefing (C6) — before the start at the very top, during the race and after the finish below the analysis.

  7. In the logbook the race appears as “Waiting for pre-start” with the countdown to 18:41:00. “Sync” rounds the starting gun to the full minute at the race officer’s signal; “−5 min” and “+5 min” move it when the start is postponed. “Set wind” supplies the wind direction for the line bias as long as there is no NMEA wind. Everything else — pre-start, pinging the line, race, finish — is in C5.

Your own course without the community

If your race is missing from the community list, you build the course yourself — via “+” in Tools → Courses & waypoints. You don’t have to type in the buoys of the area: under “Seamarks in the area” the waypoint library shows the named buoys from the loaded chart data, sorted by distance; a tap takes over name, position and type. Adopted seamarks are kept separate from your own waypoints; deleting removes only the copy.

Round course: Finish = start line. If the course ends where it begins – island races such as Silverrudder –, open the course under Tools → Courses & waypoints, tap “List” at the top and in the “Round course” section (directly below the name) switch on “Finish = start line”. In a race’s course, the same section is at the very top of the list. The line then appears twice in the course: at the front as the start, at the end with “as finish”. You enter coordinates and names only once. Without a start line the switch is locked (“Set a start line first.”); it replaces a separate finish line only after confirmation, and the undo arrow at the top brings it back. If you round the same buoy several times, in Etmar you are sailing “Laps” – “Round course” only means start = finish.

Either direction. If the course can be sailed anticlockwise or clockwise, switch on “Either direction (anticlockwise or clockwise)” in the same section. Before the start the app then asks for the direction; the course marks are reversed accordingly, rounding sides set by hand flip with them (the list shows them as “Port”/“Stbd”), and the start and finish lines stay where they are. Without the switch the question does not come – otherwise it would appear before every club triangle. A shared course keeps the switch. In demo mode, the template “Round Fehmarn” shows both.

The direction question. If a race is running on a course with two directions and the starting gun has not yet gone, a selection panel appears in the middle – whether you started from the calendar, from the logbook or not yourself at all. The app works out which direction is clockwise and names the first buoy for each choice. “Later” postpones it until the next time you open it; after the gun it no longer asks. If you sail the other way after all, the app notices this from the track after a few minutes and offers once to reverse the course.

Changing direction. The chosen direction is shown in the race’s course – Regatta tab → “Course” → “List”, “Round course” section – as “Direction: Clockwise — first …”, below it the large button “Change direction”. It also works after the gun and does not ask for confirmation: the same button turns it back. If nothing has been chosen yet, both directions are offered there. The course row in the Regatta tab states the direction as a word (“anticlockwise” or “Direction open”).

Start and finish lines are each made from two ends — a buoy from the area, a tap on the map, a pinged position or coordinates from the notice of race; the selection also offers the seamarks in the map view plus 5 nm around it. A gate is two buoys that you sail through; the passage is timestamped. Zones are areas you need to know about (prohibited area, nature reserve, shoal, note): “Zone” tool, tap at least three corners, “Done”, name and type. Neither a zone nor an info point is rounded.

Waypoints on the map and with a note. In the “Waypoints” segment there is a map at the top with all your own and adopted waypoints, as turquoise pins with names; tapping a pin opens the waypoint just like tapping its row. The map fits all waypoints into view, shows a single one in a 4 km view, and without waypoints your position. When you search, it shows only the matches. If waypoints lie close together, their names overlap – zooming in separates them. In a waypoint’s form there is a small location picture at the top with the seamarks around it; a tap on it opens the position picker like “Choose on map”. Further down, the “Note” field takes what you need to know about the spot (“lies further east in winter”). The list shows the note as a quiet line below the position, the search also finds text in it, and the GPX export includes it as the description. In demo mode, “4 · Neustädter Bucht” and the committee boat carry a note.

Images on the waypoint. In a waypoint’s form the “Images” section takes up to ten photos – “Choose from photos” or “Camera”, for example the buoy as it looks from seaward. A tap on an image shows it large, the × at its corner removes it; changes are only kept with “Save”, and “Cancel” discards the images too. With ten images both buttons are locked, and the line below says that one has to go first. The first image appears in the list on the left, in place of the seamark symbol. No images appear on the maps, and neither the GPX export nor a shared course includes them. In demo mode, “4 · Neustädter Bucht” carries a sample image.

Sharing

A course leaves the app in three ways, all free. Etmar share: touch and hold in the course list → “Share (Etmar share)” creates an .etmarshare file for AirDrop, Mail or a messenger; info points travel with it, and the recipient adopts it with one tap after a preview. Marks, info points and zones with invalid values (position or radius) are not adopted by the app — a line only with both ends, a zone only with all corners; the preview and the “Adopted” display say how many entries were left out. GPX for the plotter: “Share as GPX (plotter)” in the same place exports the course as a GPX 1.1 file for B&G, Garmin, Raymarine & co. — buoys become waypoints, the course order a GPX route. Send to Etmar: the same menu item as for feedback opens a ready-made email with an attachment; you send it, never the app, and what goes with it is stated in the email text along with a rights statement. No server, no account: it is checked by hand and delivered with the next update.

Test
  • Is the way Join → Calendar understandable without instructions? Where would you have got stuck?
  • Do the marks, rounding sides, time rules and events of the NSV course match the notice of race? Name every discrepancy.
  • Can you find the rounding side and the time limit of a single buoy in the editor without reading this article?
↑ Back to contents

C7Race analysis

After you cross the finish, Etmar breaks the race down into numbers. At the top an overview that answers “how did it go” — below it six chapters that answer “why”.

All analyses have one place: Tools → Analyses; from the boat page, an “Analyses” row leads there. The page sorts them by category — Races, Manoeuvres & helming, Boat & speed, Sails & trim, Training, Season & voyages — and shows each analysis as a card with a title and a key figure (“8 legs”, “5 races”); key figure and chart come from the same calculation as the detail, and if wind from the onboard network is missing for it, the card says what is missing. How the cards are built is described in the next paragraph. Races, trim and training are calculated by the page immediately; the analyses across all sessions (season, helming steadiness, polar, target angles, heel, tack asymmetry, crossover, statistics) show key figure and chart once you have opened their detail once — until then they read “Key figure and chart after you first open it”. The app calculates these cards itself, without holding up the controls: a few seconds after launch, after a change of boat and when a voyage is added, ends or is deleted — also when the demo starts. The cards fill one after another; until then they read “Calculating …”. While a voyage is being recorded it does not calculate, and the first opening still applies. After a correction it recalculates as soon as you are back on the page. What a card has shown once is there again after the app restarts, until a new voyage or a correction can change the number. That way the page stays instantly usable even with hundreds of sessions. A tap opens the detail; the race cards (Start, Course, Manoeuvres, Speed & trim, Wind) open their chapter of the latest race with a track directly — or of the chosen race: if the boat has two races with a track, the race picker sits above the race cards, like the boat picker at the top; the choice applies to all race cards and the pages behind them, and the large card is called “Race” instead of “Last race”. Without data the card says why it is empty and where you create it; without a race with a track, a line below the large race card names the other race cards. What only Pro shows sits in a collapsed row at the end of each category (“2 more with Pro”). Each analysis lives only here: boat, polar data and trim list say in one sentence where their analysis is and lead here with a row. The only direct ways to the same detail are the voyage detail of a race (“Analysis”) or training (“Measured runs”), the “Analysis” row in the context tab during a race, the crossover in the sail list and, in the trim entry, “Compare with …” together with its measured run. What each card shows, where its numbers come from and where it goes wrong is in C17.

A grid of equal-sized tiles. Under each category the cards sit as tiles, all the same height — two side by side on iPhone, three on iPad; one per row on iPhone from “Large text” upwards, so that no title is truncated, and everywhere with the larger accessibility text sizes. Every tile reads the same way: the title at the top, the arrow on the right; below it the key figure on one line; below that, in up to two lines, what it refers to (“7 tacks · 4 gybes” under “11 manoeuvres”, “missing: upwind below 8 kn …” under “53 %”); at the bottom exactly one thing — the detail’s chart in miniature, the coverage as a bar, the source of the values, the way to create it, or “Calculating …”. Coverage. Sailed polar, target angles, target heel, sail crossover, trim by wind speed and strengths profile carry a bar at the bottom, with the covered fields to its right (“8/15”: wind band per point of sail for which the analysis has enough data); where the values come from is stated in their detail. “53 %” as the polar’s key figure counts the same fields — it is not speed against the polar. If one of these tiles cannot fill at all without a particular source, such as heel without an attitude sensor, it names the source, and the bar stays empty (“0 %”). The tile carries no explanatory sentence, and “From demo data” no longer appears on every card: in demo mode the DEMO badge at the top says so, and only a tile whose source differs carries “estimated” (start line drawn instead of pinged) or “Demo” next to the title. When the numbers are from is stated once under the category heading (“12 sessions up to 31 Oct 2026”, “Season 2026 · 6 races up to …”); which race the race tiles show is stated by the race picker above them, or, with only one race with a track, by the line under “Races”. Only these have the full width: the large “Race” card (chart on the right), the boat and race pickers, the “Data quality” tile, an empty card with a way in, the “Practice courses” and “Collect data” rows — together in one panel — and the Pro row.

Which boat? If you have created more than one boat, the boat name sits in a row at the very top of Tools → Analyses — built like the race picker of the race analysis. A tap opens “Choose boat” with all boats in the order of the boat list; after that all cards calculate for the chosen boat. The active boat for instruments and logbook stays the same, and when opened again the page shows the active one. Voyages on a guest boat are not a boat of their own; they count towards the boat they are attached to. The row is always there, even with one boat — then without an arrow, and a tap opens nothing. Under the name it says quietly what is being calculated for: type, sail number and the sessions and races of the current season with the date of the latest (“12 sessions, 6 races · Season 2026 up to 31 Oct”) — counted, not rated; voyages on a guest boat are not counted there. If the page shows a boat other than the active one, “active boat: …” appears below. In demo mode, “Vertigo Express” and “Nordwind” sit side by side.

Data quality. Directly below the boat name there is always the “Data quality” tile: how many analyses have everything they need with your equipment — as a count, not as a percentage: “16 of 25 full”. The count is against the analyses that are possible at all with what is on board. Anything that needs a sensor your boat doesn’t have does not count against you and is listed separately (“With wind sensor: 8 more”). Without an onboard network, the tile therefore counts only what GPS can provide; what can only be done more coarsely from it, for example with an estimated tack, counts, but not as full. Below that, what is not full (“6 partly · 3 missing”, without an onboard network “5 coarser from GPS”), and in the footer the one next step that opens up the most analyses (“→ Sail downwind above 20 kn”, “→ Add trim entry”). On the right there is a dot for each analysis; the shape tells the state, even at night under the red filter: full (circle with a tick), partly (half circle — gaps in the wind grid or coarser from GPS, for example with an estimated tack), missing (dashed — something you can make up yourself: trim entry, polar, sails, training, course, start line, the first session, the next race) and sensor (struck through — needs hardware). Anything still calculating does not count anywhere; if there is no step yet, “Calculating …” appears in the footer.

A tap opens the “Data quality” page. At the top the next step: what it unlocks and a button to where you take it — “Collect data”, Polar data, Sails, “Check instruments” (if a gateway is set up but there is no wind on the track points) or the race details; trim entry, training, first session and next race are in other tabs, so the way there is given as a sentence. Below that, per category, each analysis with its state and reason — it is the same sentence its card says —, on the right “full”, “coarser”, “missing” or the coverage (“6/15 fields”). Analyses with the same reason share a row; only what leads to a step has an arrow; what needs a sensor sits collapsed at the end of the category. At the very bottom, under “Which sessions count”, there is now the row “Data basis”. Without Pro the tile counts the free analyses, and the second line says in advance: “Pro: 6 of 13 would work for you” — a prediction from what is on your track points (wind, log, heel), not a calculation.

Your performance. Pro Above the categories there are five tiles: Start delay, Per manoeuvre, Extra distance, Result and Speed vs polar. A sixth, “How it’s calculated”, gives the calculation in one sentence; it opens nothing. Each shows a level across your scored races of the current season — the calendar year of your most recent scored race: the first race sets it, and each further race pulls it a third of the way towards its own value — for extra distance, a long race pulls harder. Below it, it says whether the latest race made the level better or worse, and by how much; the upward arrow always means “better”, even where the number falls, and below a small threshold it says “unchanged”. The units do not depend on the length of the race: seconds after the gun, the median cost per manoeuvre, extra distance as a share of the distance sailed, the result as place divided by fleet size (“Top 20 %”), speed as polar performance. A level appears from three races with a value, the trend from the fourth; before that, the tile says how many races with a value there are so far (“2 so far”). Extra distance and speed need wind from the onboard network and a polar — without them the tile says so; start delay, manoeuvres and result work with GPS and your placing (with an estimated tack, “From GPS: port or starboard estimated” appears alongside). A tap on a tile opens the analysis with the value per race. If there is a previous season with a level, its level at the end of the season appears after the trend (“End of 2025: 11 s”). Without Pro, a row sits in this place.

How well covered. Six cards collect over many sessions into a grid of point of sail and wind band — Sailed polar, Target angles from measurement, Target heel, Sail crossover, Trim by wind speed and Strengths profile. Instead of a counter they say how much of it is covered (“53 % covered”), and in a line of its own what is missing (“missing: upwind below 8 kn, broad reach and one more”). On the tile the share appears briefly as “53 %”, with what is missing below it. 100 % means every wind band (per point of sail the analysis calculates) — not just those in your area. For polar, crossover and trim the share is the key figure; for target angles, heel and strengths profile it appears below the finding. If a card cannot fill at all without a particular source — wind sensor, attitude sensor, stored polar —, it names the source instead of a number. What “covered” means for each card is in C17.

The analysis has two levels. Level 1 is the overview: race sketch, race card, key figures and the time-loss waterfall — the page that opens after you cross the finish, readable in one go. Level 2 is six chapters, each on its own page with its own help button: Start, Course, Speed & trim, Manoeuvres, Wind and Scoring. Previously, all of it was in one single long list; anyone who wanted to see the start distance scrolled past everything else.

Which race? At the very top is the row with the name and date of the race you are looking at — likewise above a race chapter you opened from Tools → Analyses. It has two lines: the name, below it the date and gun (“31 Oct 2026 · Gun 18:00”). A tap on it opens the list of this boat’s races with a track, newest first, with placing, the one shown with a tick; the choice recalculates the whole page, and you stay in the same chapter. From the analyses, the latest race with a track is preselected — or the one you chose there above the race cards —; from the voyage detail and the context tab, the one you came from.

Where the values come from. Every analysis that reads values from the onboard network says so in a quiet line — on its card in Tools → Analyses and at the end of the chapter: measured, “Onboard network: TWA · AWA”; without a wind sensor, the substitute, such as “From GPS: port or starboard estimated (wind entered by hand at the start)” or “Wind from the logbook”. Without a log, the app calculates true wind over ground; Speed & trim says so in its line instead of claiming STW: “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG), no log”. Without a wind sensor, the analysis counts tacks, gybes and boards from the GPS track; overview and chapter then carry the estimate caveat under “Where the values come from”. Without any tack, the racing tally shows “–” instead of 0. In demo mode, the “Evening race without wind sensor” shows this — via the race picker at the very top. On the tile in Tools → Analyses the line sits at the bottom: a substitute always, even instead of the chart; measured values where the tile carries no chart (the performance tiles name only the substitute) — otherwise the chapter says it.

The chapters are cut by the question you ask, not by the data source. That is why the SOG trace sits with the manoeuvres — there you tap a manoeuvre and it is highlighted in the trace. The zones sit with the start, because you open them when you want to know whether you were clean before the gun. And the course change sits with scoring: it writes the same field as the race outcome — kept separately, contradictions would arise.

Level 1: the overview

Race sketch — directly below the race picker the race appears as a picture: the course as in the briefing, north up, straight legs with their numbers, start and finish as squares, marks as circles; on top of it your track from the gun to the finish crossing (without a finish, to the end of the recording), blue by day, amber at night. A leg is always selected — on opening, the one with the most extra distance, i.e. the one the race card names; without a polar, leg 1. Its stretch of track is drawn wide, and its row appears below: at the top in semibold, point of sail, time and distance sailed (“Upwind · 35:21 · 2.8 nm”), below it extra distance and manoeuvres (“Extra distance +8 % · 7 manoeuvres”, without a polar the raw “Detour”) Pro; in the free version it reads “Extra distance and manoeuvres with Pro”. Without wind from the onboard network the point of sail is estimated from the track — it then carries an asterisk, and below it says quietly “* estimated from the track”. You change the leg with a tap on the line or with ‹ › to the right of the row (60 pt each; after the last comes the first again) — the safe way in a seaway, because in a tight corner of the course two lines lie under one finger. “Full screen” shows the same sketch with zoom (double-tap, + and −, “Whole course”), portrait and landscape, with the same row. If the race has no course, the sketch shows only the track with start and finish and says where to create the course. The sketch needs no network and no chart packs. If you have scored an extension, the track runs beyond the drawn course: the sketch draws the course up to the first finish, and the legs of the extension have no number.

Race card Pro — three sentences at the top of the overview: what the start cost, the leg with the most extra distance, the best and worst tack (with wind from the onboard network, the upwind performance against the polar instead). The single glance — and at the same time the way down: every sentence can be tapped and opens the chapter that explains it (start sentence → Start, extra-distance sentence → Course, third sentence → Manoeuvres or Speed & trim). If a finishing position has been entered, the card shows “3 of 14” with a Change button next to it; without the fleet size “Place 3”, without an entry the prompt to enter it. Both routes lead to the Scoring chapter.

Key figures — free from the iPhone GPS: race duration, distance in the race, avg and max SOG, speed at the starting gun (with a log on the NMEA network the speed through the water, otherwise over ground — polars apply through the water, so the log takes precedence). Only the race phase from crossing the start line to the finish is counted — the pre-start and the way back to the harbour would distort distance, avg SOG and tack balance. Next to it is the official time from the starting gun, and the difference as start loss. Only what was sailed and measured is scored: reconstructed sections and engine phases according to the logbook are left out of distance, avg and max SOG, tack balance and manoeuvre costs; mark and manoeuvre times remain. An orange line below the distance says how much time this affected (“11 min reconstructed — not scored”); if it is almost the whole race — usually a forgotten “engine off” —, a sentence there shows the way into the logbook.

Directly below sits “Times wrong? Correct window & gun”. The button leads into the Start chapter and opens the editor there straight away — and it is there in every tier, because all the key figures depend on this window. Putting a correction behind a lock would be the wrong kind of economy.

No analysis window. If the starting gun lies after the end of the recording — the race was ended in the pre-start or after a recall without a warning signal —, there is no race to calculate over. Instead of “No track data”, the analysis then gives both times, the stored gun and the last track point, and offers the gun correction directly below — the same as in the Start chapter. If there are at least two track points between the corrected gun and the end of the recording, the analysis appears immediately; otherwise the correction stays in the same place. Or you leave the race unscored; the recording is kept as a voyage. If start and end of the window have been set the wrong way round or too close together by hand, “Reset window correction” resets the correction. If a correction in a chapter dissolves the window — such as a window start after the finish in the Start chapter —, the chapter shows the same reason and way out on the spot instead of an empty page; once the window is back, the chapter is back too.

Time-loss waterfall Pro — it breaks your deficit down into items: race duration, reference time, + start, + manoeuvres (n × avg), + extra distance (nm), + speed & the rest. The total adds up exactly; whatever the measured items don’t explain is shown as the rest. If the start can’t be quantified — no start line in the course, no crossing of the start line found or course side cannot be determined —, a muted sentence with the reason takes its place, on screen as in the PDF report, and its time goes into the rest; a start at the gun is called “Start without loss”. This sets priorities: if extra distance costs eight minutes and manoeuvres 1:39, tacking practice is the wrong investment. From GPS alone the reference is your best own race on the same course: the same course template or the same marks, each no more than 100 m from its counterpart; shortened, extended, abandoned and retired races don’t count. With wind from the onboard network it is the polar-optimal time for the course, leg by leg with the wind that actually blew there: upwind via the beat VMG (the necessary zigzag is included in the reference), downwind via the run VMG, reaching against the polar target speed for the true wind angle sailed. Extra distance then only counts the distance beyond what that point of sail requires. Against your best race, its name appears in the reference row, the items are simply called “+ start”, “+ manoeuvres” and “+ extra distance”, and “there” in each sub-line says against what. Extra distance then counts the distance you sailed more or less than there on each leg — the necessary zigzag is included in both races. A leg only counts if both races recorded it completely from mark to mark; otherwise its time is in the rest. Without a wind sensor the number doesn’t say whether it was wind or tactics. Start and manoeuvres are also the difference to that race: the start with both times underneath, the manoeuvres with both figures, for example “20 × avg 7 s, there 16 × avg 6 s, from the speed dip”; over the line earlier or lower costs than there are shown with a minus. The manoeuvres are measured the same way on both sides: from the speed dip (the time lost from the drop in speed in the GPS) as soon as either race lacks wind from the onboard network, from the VMG loss only if every manoeuvre of both races has it. Tapping an item leads into its chapter, and there the comparison sits where the number arises: in the Course chapter under each leg the distance there and the difference (“there 2.3 nm · +0.1 nm”, below 0.1 nm in metres; if the leg is missing there or not fully recorded, “there — (not fully counted)”, if a piece is missing here, “there — (not fully counted here)”), in the Start chapter under “Line crossed +15 s” the line “There: line crossed +11 s”, in the Manoeuvres chapter at the very top both figures as under the item. The name of the race appears once in each of these chapters. The same applies if you open Course, Start or Manoeuvres directly from the hub: the chapter first shows your values, and those from there are added as soon as the reference race has been found. If either race lacks the start, a sentence gives the reason, for the reference race with its name, and its time is in the rest. The last item is called “speed & the rest” — besides speed it also contains the inaccuracy of the reference, because it is a model calculation.

The items of the waterfall are also the table of contents. Tapping “+ start” leads into the Start chapter, “+ manoeuvres 1:39” to the manoeuvres, “+ extra distance” into the course, “+ speed & the rest” to Speed & trim. Race duration and reference are reference values and therefore carry no chevron — they lead nowhere. If you lack the tier for the target chapter, the tier appears as a word on the item — “speed & the rest · Pro · NMEA” — and tapping opens the tier overview instead of an empty page.

Below it is the chapter list — and at the very end, after everything free, a single collapsed row for what your tier doesn’t calculate: “6 more analyses with Pro”, below it the sentence “Pro rates start, course and manoeuvres from the GPS track — with wind from the onboard network also polar, point-of-sail balance and wind shifts.” and where the values come from. Tapping expands the rows — race card and time loss, start summary, extra distance, roundings and final approach, speed & trim, manoeuvre costs and best board, wind & shifts —; each leads to the Pro overview.

Level 2: the six chapters

ChapterWhat’s in itTier
StartStart summary, analysis window & starting gun, zonesmixed: window and zones free, start summary Pro
CourseMarks & times, rounding quality, legs, final approachmixed: marks, times and legs (duration, distance, SOG) free; VMC, extra distance, rounding quality and final approach Pro
Speed & trimPoint-of-sail balance, tack balance, polar matrix, groove, heel, over ground vs. through the water, current & leewayPro
ManoeuvresTacks & gybes, boards, SOG over timemixed: number of tacks and gybes, boards and SOG over time free; cost per manoeuvre, VMG per board and best board Pro
WindWind & shifts, wind over timePro
ScoringResult and finishing position, scoring, course changefree

No chapter ever disappears. If data is missing, the subtitle says what is missing — “Needs a course set up with marks”, “Needs wind from the onboard network at the track points”. If, on the other hand, the tier is missing, the chapter does not stay as a lock between the open rows: it moves into the collapsed row at the end of the list, together with everything else that is locked. This only affects the two chapters that are entirely Pro — Speed & trim and Wind —, and tapping opens the Pro overview. At the top of the list there are therefore only chapters you can actually open: in the free version Start, Course, Manoeuvres and Scoring. The mixed chapters never carry a tier in their subtitle: they contain the facts of the race next to Pro content, and the row says what you get there without Pro — for the start “Correct times and the starting gun”. Locking them away would close correction routes that are open.

Start chapter

Analysis window and starting gun — free in every tier. The section names the origin of both limits: line crossing, log entry, starting gun or — marked orange — only the end of the track. Under “Correct the limits” you set both by hand or reset them to automatic. That is also where the starting gun is — in case it was wrong in the voyage, for example with a pursuit start with your own start time. Either directly as a time or via the route you really have on board: “I was X seconds late over the line”. The OCS verdict, delay, speed at the gun and the official time you compare with the results list all depend on it. The window is most reliable with a start and finish line set up.

Start summary Pro — distance to the line at the gun, delay until the crossing and the position on the line (pin third, middle, boat) as a marker between pin and boat. With the manual wind entered in the pre-start, also the line bias at the gun and whether the favoured end was taken, plus the speed build-up from 30 s before to 60 s after the gun. If the boat was over the line at the gun, this is shown in orange with a warning triangle — but only if the distance is greater than the accuracy of the line: pinged ±5 m, drawn on the map ±25 m. And only if it stayed that way: anyone over at the gun has to return and start properly (RRS 29.1) — if you cross again in the starting direction before the first mark, it shows “over the line — returned” with a return arrow instead of the warning triangle, and your start time is the second crossing. The app doesn’t know which flag was flown; under I, Z, U or black, stricter rules apply. On a drawn line, 20 m is a drawing error, not an early start; the summary then says so and points to pinging. Which side is the course side comes from the course (on which side the first mark lies) and only as a fallback from the wind — for a start that doesn’t go to windward, the windward side isn’t defined at all. If it can’t be determined from either, the summary doesn’t guess but says so: “Course side cannot be determined”, together with the way to add the first mark in the race details under “Course” — the same sentence appears in the PDF report. In the race card the line then reads “Line crossed +x s (course side unknown)” instead of “Start +x s”, and the value doesn’t go into avg start delay or the time-loss waterfall at all: without a checked direction, the crossing could also be the way back of an early starter who only starts properly afterwards. Live in the pre-start, the context bar and logbook then show “OVER x m” instead of distance and time-to-burn; both disappear as soon as the GPS fix is older than 10 s — otherwise a frozen fix costs an early start. The live warning itself is free, unlike the start summary above it: it depends only on the pinged ends, the course and the GPS, and an early-start warning doesn’t belong behind a tier. Since Build 13 the same derivation applies live: if the course side can’t be determined — no first mark clearly off the line and the wind is in line with it —, the app doesn’t warn at all and keeps showing distance and time-to-burn. A false red “OVER” in the last minute costs the start; a missing one costs nothing you wouldn’t have had without the app. The race details show distance and crossing at the gun in every tier at the top under “Analysis” (“Start: ≈ 12 m to the line · crossed +8 s”).

With wind from the onboard network the same summary adds the line bias from the NMEA wind, the speed at the gun as a percentage of the polar target and the single figure “Against the ideal start −38 m”: the deficit after 2 minutes compared with a start exactly at the gun at the favoured end — position, timing and speed in one figure. If the first leg goes to windward, it is measured against the beat VMG of the polar; if it goes elsewhere, against progress towards the first mark — a windward deficit would be the wrong question on a reaching start.

Zones — if the course has zones (prohibited area, nature reserve, shoal, notice), the app checks the track to see whether you touched one: with time, duration and distance sailed inside it. If you were in none, it says so explicitly (“No zone touched”). It is calculated without tolerance — a prohibited area is a hard edge, and a smoothed figure would round away exactly the cases you ask the question for.

Course chapter

Marks and times — with a course set up, the rounding time at each mark plus the leg time since the previous mark, with marks rounded several times stamped separately for each lap. At a gate, next to the time through it, it shows which of the two marks you rounded — “left mark” or “right mark”, as seen in your direction of travel through the gate, not on the map; over several laps you can see whether your gate choice paid off. If you sailed through the middle without rounding either mark, nothing is shown there — claiming a side would be a guess. “Adjust course” leads into the course list; after a correction the analysis recalculates.

Marks that are only to be passed carry a second line: on which side you left them and how far off — “left to port · 1.4 nm”. If it was the wrong side, the requirement is in the text (“· required: stbd”) and the line is red; never just the colour. If the app recognised the side from the course rather than you setting it in the editor, “(auto)” follows (“required: stbd (auto)”) — then it’s worth checking whether the detection understood the course correctly. Below 30 m offset it names no side, only “abeam · 7 m” — that close, the side is GPS noise. Below a turning mark whose detection radius you missed, it only shows “abeam · 288 m”: the distance to the entered position, without a side — if the mark had been moved, the side would say nothing. Rounded turning marks don’t get this line: there the distance is a GPS question, here a rules question.

Rounding quality Pro — every scored mark rounding from the SOG profile ±60 s: speed dip at the mark, recovery time and the cost against the approach speed (over several laps with Δ to your best own rounding of the same mark), plus the turn before and after the mark as a shape figure. For rounded marks, rounding side and distance are deliberately left out, both are below GPS accuracy; marks stamped abeam don’t appear here at all — their time is the moment abeam, not the rounding.

Leg summary Pro — per leg the time, distance sailed, extra distance and VMC, the progress towards the next mark: pointing, speed and extra distance in one figure, without wind data. Extra distance only counts what goes beyond what that point of sail requires: the zigzag of a beat is already included in the half tacking angle of your polar, so +0 % means as short as it gets. A good beat ends up at a few per cent. If a leg shows “below close-hauled norm −21 %” instead of “extra distance”, you were shorter than the polar-optimal zigzag: pointed higher or laid the mark — distance saved, speed paid for. Without a polar, the raw detour (sailed ÷ straight line) appears instead, which upwind never drops below about 130 %. If a leg is missing a reconstructed section or one sailed under engine, neither extra distance nor detour is shown there: the scored distance against the whole straight line would come out too short. If sail changes were logged in the race, each leg shows the sail set, with wind from the onboard network also the leg’s polar % — “was the code zero right here?”. Without logged changes nothing is guessed. Without Pro, each leg shows duration, distance and avg SOG — the facts, without a verdict.

Final approach Pro — the last 200 m: chosen end of the line with position on the line, avg speed and the distance as a percentage of the straight line. 100 % means dead straight; extra distance in the last few metres is almost always wasted time.

Speed & trim chapter Pro

The whole chapter depends on wind and log data from the NMEA network — or on demo mode, which simulates them.

  • Point-of-sail balance — VMG upwind and downwind, the points of sail detected automatically from the true wind angle, plus the avg performance against the polar. It is calculated against the speed through the water as soon as a log is on the bus; without a log the analysis flags this in orange — then the current is in the percentage, in a different direction on each board.
  • Tack balance — time, distance and avg SOG per tack as well as VMG and avg TWA upwind per tack (colours per Settings → Map → Tack colouring). If one tack consistently gives more than 4 % more VMG, the recommendation next to it is to check the masthead offset of the wind sensor (Boat → Calibration) — a consistent difference is almost never skill, but trim or wind sensor. Without a log, the true wind is calculated over ground; then it can also be current, and instead of the recommendation it says “Current or sensor — can’t be told apart without a log” (also in the PDF report).
  • Polar performance by wind and course — instead of one percentage over the whole race, a matrix of wind band × point of sail (upwind/reaching/downwind), time-weighted with the measuring time per cell, only cells from one minute. This gives a training goal like “upwind above 16 kn”, not just “be faster”.
  • Groove consistency — the upwind points as a cloud of |TWA| × speed over the stored polar curve: tight means consistently in the groove, left below the line too high and too slow (pinching), right above it too low and fast. Plus σ(TWA) and σ(speed) — 2° of scatter is a different kind of sailing from 6°. Needs at least 30 upwind points with a wind angle.
  • Heel (EXPERIMENTAL) — avg upwind and downwind from the attitude sensor, with a target heel set (Boat → Calibration) the share of upwind time above it: “22 % above 28°” means reefed too late. Experimental here means: the calculation has not yet been checked against real onboard data, sensor offsets are in the value unfiltered.
  • Over ground vs. through the water — SOG and STW as two curves on top of each other, the band between them green when the current is pushing, orange when it is holding you back. Drawn as a moving three-minute average so that not every wave shows up as current. Tapping the curve sets a marker, gives the set at that point (“+0.4 kn along course (pushing)”) and offers “Show on the map” below. Three caveats belong with it, and the app states them: the difference is the current component along the course, not the current as a vector — with a cross-current the sign flips at every tack. A log error goes in 1:1. And the leeway from sailing is included too. If you want the vector, read “Current & leeway” below.
  • Current & leeway (EXPERIMENTAL) — the set as a vector (over ground minus through the water, needs STW and HDG), always with scatter; if the average lies within the scatter, the app says “no clear set detectable”. Log and compass errors are included in the value.

Manoeuvres chapter

Where the tack comes from — measured with a wind sensor; without one, the analysis estimates it from the course over ground against an estimated wind direction (manual wind at the start, logbook, your upwind boards, station, model or the bearing to the first mark). A course over ground frozen for minutes is replaced by the bearing from the positions. Whether your upwind boards were a beat or a zigzag downwind is decided by the hourly wind from station or model before the bearing to the first mark — on a round course whose first mark is not a windward mark, the bearing would otherwise sometimes choose the wrong reading. At the end of the chapter, under “Where the values come from”, the caveat then appears: gybes dead downwind are uncertain, costs only in seconds. When measured, it says “Onboard network: TWA · AWA”.

Tacks and gybes — without Pro the number of tacks, gybes and other course changes; with Pro each one individually with cost in seconds, speed dip in per cent and recovery time to 95 % of the speed before the manoeuvre. Whether a change of tack was a tack or a gybe is decided by the wind angle immediately before and after: in a tack the bow goes through the wind, in a gybe the stern. Manoeuvres right at a mark are thus assigned correctly, even if the leg before or after was already on average a reach. Best, average and worst tack at the top, average cost per wind band below (at 20 kn a 12-s tack is normal, at 8 kn it isn’t), after that the expandable list of all manoeuvres with time and wind band. Needs the 5-s race recording; individual values scatter in gusts and waves, over many manoeuvres they are reliable. With wind from the onboard network, the estimated manoeuvre loss in metres is added, measured against the VMG of the neighbouring boards over the whole window until recovery.

Boards — the list cuts a board not only at a change of tack but also at every rounded course mark. If you go round the mark on the same tack and then sail a completely different course, you would otherwise get upwind and downwind averaged in one board — and thus a VMG that never existed. Each row gives tack, point of sail, duration and speed; with Pro VMG and polar performance per board are added — how much height the board really gained and how well it went against your polar. Without Pro the row shows the speed over ground. Without a wind sensor the point of sail is shown estimated and with an asterisk (“Upwind*”, “Reaching*”, “Downwind*”): from the course over ground against the same wind direction the analysis uses to estimate the tack — manual wind at the start, logbook, station, model, your upwind boards or the bearing to the first mark. The limits are the same as when measured (upwind up to 75°, downwind from 135°); a board under 30 s shows “–”. Dead downwind, both sides of a gybe count as Downwind*, because the angle to the wind is averaged without its side. Below the list it says where the wind direction came from and that current distorts the point of sail, with station or model also shifts. If you set the wind by hand at the start and your upwind boards — without them the hourly wind from station or model — are more than 30° off, the footnote also gives their direction (“Your upwind boards give 272°.”); the calculation still uses the manual wind. The estimated point of sail only appears in the row: it doesn’t make a best or worst board and goes into no summary. The same point of sail* appears without a wind sensor on the legs in the Course chapter, with the same footnote below; extra distance, polar norm and leg times are calculated without it. In demo mode, the “Evening race without wind sensor” shows this.

Best and worst board Pro — marked in the list so you don’t have to compare twelve VMG figures. Separated into upwind and downwind: 5.5 kn VMG downwind is not “better” than 3.6 kn upwind, it is something different. The marker only appears from three boards on the same point of sail — with two, one would inevitably be the best and the other the worst. Reaching boards stay unmarked: there the VMG to the wind says nothing about the quality of the board.

SOG over time — the speed over ground over the whole race as a curve. It sits in this chapter and not under Speed & trim because you read it together with the manoeuvres: tapping a manoeuvre opens the map view (see below) and at the same time highlights the manoeuvre in red in SOG over time — the list stays put instead of scrolling away under your finger, and the highlight is still there after you close the map view.

Wind chapter Pro

The whole chapter depends on the wind sensor on the NMEA network — or on demo mode, which simulates it.

Wind & shifts — from the recorded wind direction: oscillating, persistently shifting or steady, with amplitude, period or total shift, plus the conditions with wind range, gustiness and direction spread. “On the lifted tack” gives the share of upwind time on whichever tack was more favourable — measured against the moving wind average of the last 12 minutes —, the response to headers, how long you sailed in the header before you tacked. The footnote gives the source of the true wind: from the bus, calculated from apparent wind and log, or from apparent wind and SOG — then the current is in direction and strength. A wind figure with a source is one you can believe.

Below it is the matching rule of thumb: in an oscillating wind, stay on the lifted tack and tack in the header; in a persistent shift the other way round — get early to the side it is shifting towards. With a steady direction, the side of the course is decided by current and pressure, not by shifts.

Wind Over Time (TWS) — the true wind speed over the race as a curve. It answers whether the gust you remember really was one — and shows when the breeze freshened.

Scoring chapter

Scoring — free in every tier, because it changes the key figures. It covers races without a finish crossing: “Race abandoned” (the race committee abandoned, flag N) or “Retired” (your own boat dropped out) — kept separate because they are two different things. The calculation then runs to the end of the recording or to the limit from the analysis window; the warning “from the end of the track” is dropped, because here the end of the track is the right answer. Performance, boards and marks reached are still fully analysed — an abandoned race is just as useful as training.

Result and finishing position — also free. This is where you enter finishing position and fleet size, and where the switch “Exclude from the overall analysis” sits. Class, series and rating (Yardstick/ORC) you enter when creating the race in the race form. The way from the top is short: “Enter finishing position” or “Change” in the race card jumps straight here.

Course change — the app never scores a shortened course automatically: whether the course was shortened depends on when the leader of the fleet reached the mark, and your device can’t see that. It makes a suggestion (“Shortening applies from 16:00. First mark after that: Mark 4 at 16:04. Score there?”); if you confirm, that passage becomes the finish crossing, and everything after it is greyed out in the course. The app only scores an extension when it is beyond doubt — in exactly two cases: you yourself went through the first finish before the stored time, or the marks of the extra leg are in your track. If you yourself were too late, that doesn’t mean “no extension”, the leader may still have been through before the time; the analysis then asks and scores the short course until you answer. Overriding always works in both directions.
