Your answer moves the finish entry to the crossing of the finish line that then counts — with an extension, that of the extra leg; analysis, logbook and voyage map follow. If the extension is already established from the track but the finish entry is still at the first finish (that is where automatic finish detection sets it), the app doesn’t move it on its own: under “Course extension” there is then a sentence with both times and the button “Move finish” — a tap sets the entry to the long line, “Undo” puts it back. The same offer comes when the extension is established from your own finish time — you were at the first finish before the deadline —; the sentence then gives this reason instead of the track. What happens to the first finish is governed by the switch “Treat as a gate when extended” on the course: off — it appears unscored in the analysis; on — it counts as a gate with the time through it and the mark taken. Without an extension it remains the normal finish crossing. The course’s time rules are in the same section and can be corrected per race.

Map view: where it happened

A number in a list says what, not where. That is why, across all chapters, every row that refers to a moment or a section of the race has a map view: manoeuvres, boards, course marks, legs, start and final approach — plus the point you tap in the “Over ground vs. through the water” curve. Tapping the row opens it as a sheet from the bottom — the whole track grey, the relevant part in colour, for a moment (tack, rounding, gun) a circle round the spot and two minutes of track around it. The map can be panned and zoomed with + and −; it uses the same offline packages as the main map, so it also works without a connection. Below it the figures of the row appear again: time, duration, distance, speed.

Enlarging charts

Below every chart in the analysis is “Enlarge”. In the list a chart is about 350 pt wide — in a two-hour race that is one pixel per twenty seconds: enough for the trend, not enough for the question “what happened at 19:12?”. The button opens the same chart in full screen. There you pinch the time axis open with two fingers — lower limit two minutes, below that the curve shows noise and no information —, pan through time with one finger and bring back the whole race with “Show all”. Held in landscape, the chart takes the full width: a time series needs width, not height.

Tapping the curve sets the red marker in full screen too — and the line below gives time and measured value and offers “Show on the map”. If you tap it, the full screen closes and the map opens: you wanted to go to the map, after all, not back to the chart.

Pinching open, panning and “Show all” apply to the three long time series: SOG over time, over ground vs. through the water and wind over time. The tap also applies to the speed build-up around the gun — that one can’t be pinched open, it only shows a minute and a half anyway and the zoom’s lower limit is two. There the axis counts seconds around the gun, so the line below the chart gives “−12 s” instead of a time, and the map shows the approach with the start line. The marker stays until you tap somewhere else: a mis-tap in waves costs nothing.

The groove is the exception — it has no time axis. It also opens in full screen, but can neither be zoomed nor tapped there: there is no moment a marker could point to.

Beyond the single race

Sail crossover Pro (wind sensor on the NMEA network — the measured actual comes from TWA, TWS and polar percentage; entering the range is free) — under Tools → Analyses → Sails & trim → Sail crossover, even while no sail has been created yet; the sail list under Boat opens the same screen with one row — per sail the stated against the measured wind range: where a sail set with this sail delivered the best polar % of all those carried (per wind band × point of sail, from 5 min measuring time per cell, across all scored sessions — since 09/2026 also voyages, see C11 “Which sessions count”). “Your A2 really carries up to 19 kn, not 16” — with an arrow as the action. Below it the zone field: wind speed vertical, wind angle horizontal, and for each sail set the range in which it was not demonstrably worse than the best — not the range in which it won. Where colours overlap, it is a draw: there you don’t need to change. Tap a sail set to highlight its zone. Empty spots are not extrapolated — the measuring time is missing there (at least three minutes per sail set and five per field). Below that, as before, the measured crossover table with the best sail set per wind band and point of sail. Needs clean logging of sail changes; empty cells are not extrapolated.

The racing tally under Tools → Analyses → Racing tally adds up the boat’s races: races, race hours, race miles, manoeuvres, best and average finishing position. No silent filter: excluded races are listed with a reason — by hand (can be included again) or automatically (under 10 min / 0.5 nm). If more than 90 % of the race time was under engine according to the logbook, the race is also left out, with the reason “mostly under engine according to the logbook — check engine on/off” instead of “too short”. At the top is the season — the calendar year of the starting gun. Counters and curves show the most recent season with a scored race; tapping the season row opens the earlier years, and the footer of the counters names the one shown (“Season 2026 · calendar year of the starting gun”). Nothing is deleted, and there is no longer a manual cut-off date: races before a cut-off date set earlier count again in their year. Under “Reset” there remains “Reset the sailed polar (new sails)” — it only sets the cut-off date of the sailed polar.

From two scored races on, the long-term curves are added. All of them respect the exclusions and show the selected season; each says what it still lacks instead of disappearing empty:

  • Form trend Pro — from GPS as an avg SOG trend (the trend is right, the level is not normalised), with wind from the onboard network as polar performance per race, separated into upwind and downwind with a 100 % reference.
  • Manoeuvre learning curve Pro — median manoeuvre cost per race, from GPS in seconds from the speed dip (without a wind sensor with an estimated tack), with wind from the onboard network in metres.
  • Start history Pro — avg distance at the gun, avg delay, distribution of end choice pin/middle/boat, hit rate “took the favoured end” and, as soon as finishing positions are recorded, “your best results came from the pin”.
  • Strengths by wind band Pro — avg polar performance and finishing position per wind band; the wind band comes from the wind sensor on the NMEA network, without one from the wind details in the logbook — then only the finishing position counts, labelled “Wind from the logbook”. If the session carries wind from open sources, the wind band comes from there, labelled “Wind from station” or “Wind from model”.

This season against last. After the curves, a section compares the season shown with the previous season — the last year before it with a scored race; if there is a year without races in between, a line says so. In every tier the count for both seasons is shown (“2026: 9 races · 14 h · 54 nm” and 2025 below), without an arrow — it is a fact. Pro also compares each topic in one line, “value against value”, with an arrow (up means better, below a small threshold “unchanged”): avg SOG only per wind band with at least two races in both seasons — otherwise it says “not comparable” with the most frequent band of both years, because avg SOG would otherwise measure the wind, not the form —, with wind from the onboard network in both seasons the polar performance upwind and downwind, metres per manoeuvre and extra distance instead; the seconds per manoeuvre, distance and delay at the start, the rate for “favoured end” (“4 of 6”), the result as top % (place divided by fleet size) and the strengths profile per band (with wind from the onboard network as polar performance, otherwise also as top %). At the top is the wind of both years (“2026 mostly 8–12 kn · 2025 mostly 12–16 kn”), on the line its source where it isn’t GPS or your own entry (onboard network, tack estimated, wind from the logbook). If only one season had wind from the onboard network, a sentence appears there instead of a number. With fewer than two scored races in a season there is no comparison, with a reason; in the first year it says “No previous season yet — the comparison comes with the first counted race next year.” Without Pro, a line in that place says what Pro compares here.

Sailed polar Pro (needs the wind angle from the wind sensor on the NMEA network — without it there is no angle axis) — under Tools → Analyses → Boat & speed → Sailed polar — only there; the polar data says in one sentence where it is and leads into the analyses with one row — shows what the boat really does: as points over the stored polar, one view per wind band, angles in 10° steps (both tacks together), per cell the 90th percentile of boat speed — not the maximum, not the mean. The cells fill themselves when a race is completed, strictly filtered: engine phases, manoeuvre windows ±20 s, high rate of turn, gaps over 60 s and reconstructed tracks are thrown out; races on someone else’s boat never count, measured runs from training are included. Under 5 min measuring time a cell stays pale and without a figure, from 30 min it is full; without STW the app measures over ground (SOG, marked with *) — then the current is in the value. The “New sails” cut-off date and the tally’s exclusions apply here too. Applying to the stored polar only happens on request, cell by cell from 30 min measuring time, with old and new value per cell, deselectable — never automatically, so that a badly sailed season doesn’t lower the targets.

The polar says what it doesn’t score. Below the chart it says: engine phases according to the logbook, reconstructed distance and manoeuvres — fast turns, and in a race also 20 s before to 20 s after every detected tack, gybe or course change. If the polar stays empty, the empty state gives a possible reason: a race or training session that is under engine throughout in the logbook (such as an “engine on” without an “engine off”) yields no cells. The fix is in the session’s logbook: delete the engine entries or correct their time.

Test
  • After crossing the finish, can you find out in under a minute what cost you the most — and does the waterfall item take you to the right chapter?
  • Are you missing a figure that used to be on the one long page? Name it and say in which chapter you looked for it.
  • Is the analysis window right on its own after a race (both limits “from line crossing”), or did you have to help under “Correct the limits”? The way there is the button directly below the key figures.
  • Do distance, delay and position on the line in the start summary match what you experienced at the start? If you started without a first mark and the wind was in line with the line: does the summary say “Course side cannot be determined” — instead of claiming a side? Does the manoeuvre list detect all tacks and gybes — or do manoeuvres show up that you never sailed?
  • With a log on the bus: does the band between SOG and STW match the current you expected — and does the sign flip at the tack the way the caveat announces?
  • Set the demo tier to Free: does the collapsed row at the end of the list say clearly what Pro would rate there — and is a fact you look for after the race missing in the open chapters? And does “Enlarge” work with cold, stiff fingers?
↑ Back to contents

C8Training with measured runs

The same stretch twice, one trim change in between — and numbers instead of gut feeling on whether it brought speed.

In a race, wind and waves change all the time, and you repeat nothing. For the question “does this give more speed?” a race is therefore the worst data source. Training is the best: the same conditions, one change, the same stretch twice. That is exactly what the training voyage type with measured runs is for.

Creating a training session

A training session is a voyage with its own type. You create it via Logbook → Menu (⋯) with “New training”, or you choose the type (Voyage/Training) in the voyage form. At the pontoon it works without the menu: “Training” in the “Today” section above “Next event” opens the same form. And in the calendar, a training event of its own carries “Start” — in the row and at the bottom of the opened event; after the confirmation the training session is created, without a countdown, and you start runs and the practice course in the logbook. The map doesn’t offer training: the green button there is called “Cast off” and creates a voyage immediately. In the voyage list a training session carries its own badge so you can tell it apart from normal voyages later. Creating and logging are free — that is logbook, not a gate.

Starting and ending runs

A measured run is deliberately a one-button affair on deck. During a training session the logbook shows a full-width button that switches between “Start run” and “End run · m:ss” — no form, no input, one thumb.

  1. Bring the boat onto course, set the trim, then tap Start run. From now on the button shows the elapsed time.
  2. Sail the stretch. While the run is open, tracking records a point every second while under way.
  3. At the end of the stretch tap End run. The run automatically gets a title (“Run 1”, “Run 2” …).
  4. Change the trim, go back to the start of the stretch, start the next run.

You change the title afterwards in the log entry, for example to “Forestay +2” — then you see straight away in the comparison what the run tested. A mis-tap creates at most one run too many, and you take that back with the undo bar. Runs are normal log entries: a start and an end entry, paired like engine on/off. That is why they also appear in the voyage’s entry list.

Capturing trim on a run

In training you capture the trim as in any voyage via Logbook → Trim. The trim entry is assigned to the run in which it was captured — or up to 2 min after it. So you can end the run, take a breath and then enter what was sailed; the assignment is still right. Ideally you capture a trim for every run: then the run list shows on each run which setting it measured.

Collect data

Between runs, below “Start run”, there is a second, outlined button Collect data. It opens a sheet: at the top the manoeuvre series, below it one row each for the analyses that fill up over many sessions — sailed polar, sail crossover, target angles from measurement, target heel. Each of these rows shows its coverage like its card in the analyses (“53 % covered”, “missing: upwind below 8 kn …”) and a start as a measured run whose title names the gap: “Polar: upwind below 8 kn” is a run upwind in less than 8 kn of wind. If the analysis lacks its source, such as the wind sensor, the row says so and offers no start — a run that can’t fill anything would be wasted time. If everything is covered, there is no start either. Coverage and start of these four rows belong to Pro; without Pro a sentence appears there.

The manoeuvre series works with GPS alone and starts in every tier. During the run the target “10 tacks · 6 gybes” is shown large above “End run”. Nothing is counted — the target is the agreement with yourself. It is ended like any run, after which the series appears as a measured run in the list. In the manoeuvre learning curve it appears as its own dashed “Training” series next to the races; the races’ curve stays as it is.

In the pre-start and race and while a run is open, the button isn’t there. The coverage is the state of the last calculation under Tools → Analyses; under way the app doesn’t recalculate it, and after a restart under way it may say “No coverage yet.” there. The same list, without a start, is under Tools → Analyses in the Training section.

Wind task on deck

While a wind task is running (“Polar: upwind 12–16 kn”), what you should sail is shown above “End run”: large the target TWA, next to it the time left until the minimum duration from which the analysis covers the field (polar and crossover 5 min, target angles and heel 10 min), below it the sail set. The target TWA is a target, not a measured value: upwind and downwind the best angle of your stored polar at this wind, on a reach and without a polar a guide value for the course — the line below says which. If the onboard network delivers no true wind, it says “No wind sensor” there instead of a number. You don’t have to operate anything; it is stopped with “End run”, and the run is kept.

A crossover run sets, at the start, the sail whose stated range of use (Boat → Sails) covers the gap, with the mainsail that is set; the undo message names it. If none explicitly fits, the current sail set stays. You can change it as always via “Sails” — the change then also applies to the run from that point on. The title of each wind task takes the gap whose wind band is closest to the current wind: from the onboard network, otherwise “Wind now” from station or weather model (the foot of the sheet says which). If the coverage is based on demo sessions, it says “From demo data.” there.

Hint when the wind fits. If the wind from the onboard network stays for ten minutes in the same band that an analysis is missing, a capsule “Wind fits “Polar: upwind 12–16 kn” — Logbook › Collect data” appears above the map. Only in an open training session between runs, once per gap and session, never in the pre-start or race, never during an alarm, no notification when the app is closed. Tapping it hides it; switch it off under Settings → Racing & training → Training. Because the gaps come from the coverage, the hint is only available with Pro.

Practice course

Under “Collect data” there is a third, outlined button, Practice course — with the same conditions: in an open training session, between runs, not during pre-start or a race. It opens a sheet; nothing is written until you tap “Go”, and “Cancel” leaves everything as it was. The app lays a windward-leeward course of virtual marks into the wind: windward and leeward mark, below them the start line, 60 m across the wind. The start line lies at the boat, 100 m below the leeward mark; the finish is the start line.

Wind. At the top is the wind the course is laid on, and where it comes from: onboard network, station with distance, weather model or manual. If the direction does not come from your own wind sensor, “±20°” appears next to it — no station and no model gets the direction on the water any more precisely. It gets more precise with “More precise: close-hauled”: sail close-hauled, then tap — the app takes the course over ground plus or minus half the tacking angle from the stored polar (without a polar 45°), and the side from the suggestion. If there is no suggestion, there are two buttons, one per tack. Without boat speed (under 1.5 kn) this option is not available.

Course. Length in three fixed steps, 0.4, 0.6 or 0.9 nm (windward to leeward mark), plus 1 to 3 laps. The course type is windward-leeward.

Preview. “Next” shows the course on a map, zoomed to it, with the hatched diamond between the laylines — that is where you beat, and that is where it is checked: draught plus 2 m of water everywhere according to the depth model, at least 150 m from the shore, and a depth value at every check point. The three rows Depth · Land · Data give the result. If the course does not fit, the app shortens it (0.9 → 0.6 → 0.4 nm) or moves it across the wind, up to 0.5 nm, and says so in one sentence (“Shortened to 0.6 nm”, “Moved 200 m east”); it is never rotated. If nothing fits: “No course fits here — sail a little further out.” The depths come from the downloaded offline depths; the “Depth” row gives the lower limit by depth contours (“At least 5 m everywhere”). If no draught is set for the boat, the app calculates with 2.0 m, says so in the same row (“No draught set for the boat — calculated with 2.0 m.”) and offers “Add ›” below: this opens the boat form, and after saving, the preview checks again with the draught you entered. Without depth data at the spot it says “No depth data here” with a way to the offline maps, and “Go” stays locked and appears faded. Axis ±10° and shorter/longer change the course with large buttons; the sentence “Model data — keep a lookout” is always there — model depths are not the chart, and the app does not check fairways or prohibited areas.

Go. “Go” writes the marks to the training session and starts a measured run “Practice course windward-leeward 0.6 nm”; the undo message takes back run and marks. On the map the marks appear as a dashed ring with “V” — the shape tells you that there is nothing in the water — in amber at night. Free in every tier.

Sailing it. After “Go”, the course bar appears on the map and at the top of the logbook: first “Cross the start line”, after crossing towards the course the next mark with side, bearing and distance. The course time runs from crossing the start, not from “Go”; it runs along on the right of the bar if there is room — on the iPhone the width is usually not enough, in portrait or landscape — and at the finish it is always shown. A mark counts as rounded when you get inside its 30 m ring and leave it on the required side; side and distance come from the closest GPS point, and a tack inside the ring does no harm. Once you have left the ring, the phone vibrates, there is a short tone and the announcement “Windward rounded, next leeward, 210 degrees” (tone and speech like the start signals), and for 4 s the capsule “WINDWARD ROUNDED · 19 m” appears below the bar. On the wrong side it says “WINDWARD WRONG SIDE”, and the mark does not count. If you sail past without getting into the ring, it says “WINDWARD MISSED · 85 m” with two large buttons: “Back to the mark” or “Skip” — then the run does not count. The finish line is the start line: if you cross it after the last mark, the measured run ends and the course time appears at the bottom. “End course” in the bar’s menu (⋯) only ends the run, not the training session. Right after “Go”, the map zooms out once so far that, with the boat in the centre, every mark is in view — even if it is following the boat; after that it no longer moves by itself. The course is calculated on the recorded track: if recording is off, “Go switches recording on.” appears above “Go” — “Go” switches it on for the training session, as does “Start run”, even without a departure entry. If it is running on another voyage, it says instead “Go takes the recording over from “Baltic voyage”.” — from “Go” on, that voyage gets no more track. Undo restores the previous state. The result of the course is described below the picture; the demo shows a “Practice course training” with two courses sailed, two laps each. Does the announcement come shortly after you have left the ring — and is the side right?

Result. At the finish, the notice with the course time carries the Result button — on the map as in the logbook. The same sheet opens later from the row “Practice course result” under each practice course run in the measured runs, and under Tools → Analyses → Training → Practice courses you will find all courses of the boat, most recent first. At the top, in large type, the course time from start line to finish line — or “Not scored” with the reason: mark missed or skipped, wrong side, finish line not reached. Below that the facts, for everyone: distance sailed against course length, tacks · gybes, avg tacking angle over ground, the shift in degrees from the bisector of the tacking courses (last beat against first; for this each beat needs two tacks) and per mark distance and side, such as “rounded at 18 m to port”; over two laps with a number (Windward 1, Leeward 1, Windward 2, Leeward 2). If you round leeward before windward was taken, windward appears as “skipped · closest approach 180 m” and the verdict names the mark (“Not scored — Windward 1 skipped”); the windward rounding after that counts for the next lap. Sailing from the line through the leeward ring on the first beat is not a leeward rounding. At the very bottom, “Where the figures come from” says what is measured and what is assumed: times, distances and roundings from the GPS track, the wind direction from the course axis, the wind band from the onboard network or from the voyage’s wind series at the time of the course (station or model), in the demo “From demo data.”. In the result at the finish, as long as the training session is open, there are “Again” at the bottom — the practice course sheet with axis, length and laps of the last course, the line at the boat again — and “Other course” with an empty sheet. On the map the app switches to the logbook for this and opens the sheet there; “Go” and its undo are in the logbook. In the “Practice course training”, the demo shows two courses in the same wind band, the second one faster. Does the course time match your watch — and the rounding distances what you saw at the mark?

Practice course rating Pro — polar %: the target time from the boat’s stored polar at the middle of the wind band, divided by the course time, overall and per leg; without tacking losses, so rather strict. It only appears when polar and wind band are available and both upwind and downwind were sailed — otherwise a row says what is missing. In addition, the best in the wind band in min/nm across all scored practice courses of the boat, below it the last ten runs; for the first run in the band it says “first run in this band”. Without Pro, this spot shows a row “Polar %, best, history” with the way to the offer; the facts stay complete.

Planning ahead. Pro The course can be planned in advance at the kitchen table or on the pontoon — in the training event. In the Calendar, an event of type “Training” on your own boat carries the Practice course section with “Plan practice course …”. The sheet shows a map at the top: a tap sets the centre of the course (panning and zooming still work); the default is your last known position. The axis comes from the forecast (GFS) for the time of the event, with its source: “GFS for Tue 17:00, fetched Mon 14:10”. If the event is more than five days ahead, the sheet says from when the forecast is available, and you set the axis by hand with “Axis −10°/+10°”; if you open the event or sheet again later within five days, the app fetches the forecast and says so (“Forecast updated: 240° → 250°”). Length and laps as in training. Below that, the check at the planned spot — the same sentence and the same rows Depth · Land · Data as in the preview. If there are no depths there, Load depths for this spot downloads them as a separate region (“Practice course · event”, visible and deletable under Offline maps); without a connection, the plan stays “unchecked” and can still be applied. Outside the depth data (it covers European waters and part of the Caribbean) the button is not there: the sheet says that there are no depths for this area; the plan can be applied unchecked, and “Go” stays locked in the training session. If no course fits here, “Apply” stays locked. The event then shows “Windward-leeward 0.6 nm · 2 laps” and below it “Wind 240° · GFS Tue 17:00” (or “manual”, “(demo)”, “unchecked”); “Save” saves it. For a series, every event gets the same position; each event fetches the forecast for its own day when it is opened. Without Pro, a lock row appears there instead of “Plan practice course …” and instead of “Change”; a plan that was already saved stays visible, goes along when you tap “Start” and is sailed in the training session. Laying, checking and sailing the course at the boat stays free. Is the course where you tapped — and does the axis match the forecast you know?

Planning when creating. Pro The same works without an event: “New training” (for example via “Training” in the logbook at the pontoon) and “Edit training” carry the same Practice course section under the date. The sheet is the same, only it knows no time — the axis is the Wind now (onboard network, station or model), and it fetches no forecast; without a current wind it says “No wind now”, and you set the axis by hand. The form then shows “Wind 240° · now, Onboard network” (after 20 minutes with the time, for example “Station, as of Tue 14:10”), below it “Change” and “Remove”. If you planned the course in the event, “Start” takes it into the training session. After “Save”, no preview opens by itself. Without Pro the lock row appears here too; “Remove” stays. Does the row say where the wind came from?

Planned course in training. If the open training session carries a plan, the tile under “Start run” is called Planned course, with “Windward-leeward 0.6 nm · 2 laps” below it. The sheet takes length and laps from the plan, but the axis from the current wind — the plan was a plan. A sentence says how it compares with the plan: “Planned 240° (GFS) · now 255° from the onboard network — axis turned.” (same degrees: “as planned”; without a current wind the planned axis stays, and “close-hauled” works as always). The preview lays the course with its centre on the planned spot, checks it there and gives the distance and bearing from the boat to the start line, for example “Course 1.9 nm away · bearing 045°”. If you don’t want to sail there, Bring to the boat lays it at the boat with the start line as usual and checks it again there. Without a GPS fix, the distance and “Bring to the boat” are missing, but the check still runs. Do distance and bearing match what you see on the map?

Figures per run Pro

You will find the analysis under Voyage details → Measured runs, and as long as the training session is open, also in the context panel “Voyage” under “Measured runs”. For each run it shows duration, distance, avg and σ of speed, and avg COG. The spread σ is the figure you check a run by first: a steady run is a valid run; a twitchy run measures the steering, not the trim. Runs under 90 s are listed but not rated.

Tapping two runs opens the A/B comparison: figure against figure, with a difference column. Without wind measurement the app cannot tell whether a difference comes from the trim or from the wind — the view says so explicitly. From GPS alone you can therefore read “run B was faster”, not “the trim was better”. Without a wind comparison the difference therefore stays uncoloured, and below it says “Wind not compared” — with “one run without a wind sensor” if only one of the two had wind.

Rating with wind Pro

With wind data from the NMEA network, the comparison becomes a rating. Added to it are avg TWA, VMG, polar performance, avg heel and the “Conditions” row.

  • VMG is the main measure. Anyone who only looks at speed sees “lower and faster” as a gain — VMG takes pointing into account.
  • Polar puts the run in relation to your boat’s polar data: what percentage of the polar speed at this wind was reached.
  • Conditions is the conditions check. If TWS differs between the runs by more than 1.5 kn or TWD by more than 10°, the difference is greyed out: then the conditions have changed, not the trim. An A/B comparison that sells a wind shift as a trim success is worse than none.

The result also ends up in the trim entry: under “Measured” it shows what the run achieved, in the demo for example “101 % polar, VMG 3.9 kn, avg 5.5 kn ±0.11, at 11 kn TWS, from 4:00”. This turns the trim diary into a rated one — every setting carries its measured value. These entries are collected in the trim workbench under “Rated” (C9).

The training session as a PDF

On export (Voyage details → Export → PDF voyage report), a training session gets the usual voyage report followed by a separate page Measured runs — one run per row, in the order of the training session. Free are title, time span and duration; with Pro, distance, avg and σ of speed and COG are added, and if wind was recorded, also avg TWA, VMG and polar %. Unlike on screen, the wind columns don’t push anything out — on the page there is room for both sets of columns.

With Pro every row also carries the column avg wind: the mean true wind of the run, speed and direction in one, for example “12.4 kn · 214°”. These are exactly the two quantities by which the legend below the table decides comparability — if TWS differs by more than 1.5 kn or TWD by more than 10°, the wind has changed and not the trim. This way it can be checked on paper even without the app.

The page appears in every tier and says in one sentence what the tier holds back; a run without figures gives its reason (“open”, “too short to tell”, “too few track points”). Long run titles wrap in their column instead of being cut off at the end. Below the table it explains what σ means and where the speed values come from — log or GPS. For a training session, the cover page shows no stage plan, which a training session doesn’t have, and under “Sail changes” lists only real changes instead of every repetition of the same set.

In demo mode

The demo contains the training session “Forestay trim training” with three runs: baseline and +2 turns on the forestay in the same wind — the two are comparable, and the conditions check is green. The third run was sailed in 2.5 kn more wind; if you compare it with one of the first two, the gate kicks in and the difference is greyed out. Also set the demo tier to Free once and see what remains of the runs without Pro.

Test
  • Sail a real training session with at least two runs over 90 s on the same stretch. Do you hit the run button reliably with a wet thumb, and can you cleanly take back a false start via the undo bar?
  • Capture the trim once during and once shortly after a run. Is the entry attached to the right run in the list of runs?
  • Pro: trigger the conditions check with two runs in clearly different wind. Is the difference greyed out, and does the “Conditions” row show the deviating values?
  • Export your training session as a PDF and read the “Measured runs” page as someone would who doesn’t have the app next to them: are the same figures there as on screen, and does every column explain itself?
↑ Back to contents

C9Trim diary

The trim diary records per boat which settings worked in which wind, which sea state and which sail combination — on deck as a draft in two taps, ashore filled in at leisure.

How an entry is structured

You reach the diary via Tools → Trim. The list shows the entries of the active boat; “+” at the top right creates a new one. An entry consists of three parts:

  • Conditions — a wind range from–to in knots and the sea state: flat, moderate, rough or heavy.
  • Sail combination — which sails from your wardrobe were up.
  • Points of sail — for example close-hauled, broad reach, downwind — and within them the actual trim values. So an entry records how the traveller was set on the beat and how on a broad reach.

In addition there are images (from the gallery or straight from the camera) and notes. The list can be filtered by wind, point of sail, sea state and sail, and searched.

Photos on the trim field. You store the photo of a clutch, a track or the mark on the line once on the field (Tools → Boat → Trim fields → field). It then appears in every entry to the right of the value as a small tile, with “+2” if there are several photos; a tap shows all photos of the field large, with caption. In the form, too, a tap on the tile opens the photo. The “Images” section in the entry is there even without photos and says where they belong.

Customising trim fields

You decide yourself which trim values an entry asks for. Forestay, cunningham, traveller, kicker and other common fields are preinstalled; you can rename, delete, add and reorder them per boat — under Tools → Boat → Configuration → Trim fields. A sportsboat with runners needs different fields from a cruiser with in-mast furling, and both work without an app update.

Each field has a type that determines how you enter the value:

  • Text (default) — free description, such as “Top batten parallel to the boom”.
  • Number with unit — entered via the number pad, such as “22 Loos”. Numbers are comparable across entries and end up as numbers in the CSV export.
  • Scale 1–10 — large −/+ buttons, usable with wet hands.
  • Choice — fixed values such as “loose / medium / hard”: one tap instead of typing.

You set the type, unit and choice options in the detail view of each trim field. If you change the type later, the values already entered are kept. The demo shows forestay and shrouds as numbers and the mainsheet as a choice; you set the scale on a field of your own in the field detail. If a scale field still contains text from earlier, the row shows it on one line in grey until you tap −/+.

Taking over from the logbook

A logbook entry that contains sails and wind can be used directly as the starting point for a trim entry — you don’t have to enter conditions and sails by hand again. This is the way for the debrief ashore — when, going through the voyage, you notice that a stretch went well and you want to keep the setting.

Copying from an existing entry

When you create a trim entry – and in a draft – the row Copy from an existing entry appears below the sails. It shows the entries of this boat that carry trim values: same sails first, then the nearest wind range, then the most recent. A tap copies only the trim values and, if you haven’t chosen any yet, the sails. Title, wind, sea state, photos and notes stay empty – they belong to the day of the template. As soon as you have entered a value, the row disappears so that a mis-tap overwrites nothing. This is how a new combination comes about: take the light-air setup, backstay two turns tighter, save.

Capture trim — on deck

The moment the trim is right is straight after the manoeuvre — and that is exactly when you don’t have a hand free for eight form fields. That is why, while a voyage is running, the logbook tab has the Capture trim button; the same button sits in the radial menu of the pencil button at the bottom right of the map.

  1. Tap Capture trim. The app immediately creates a draft: wind range from the current TWS (±2 kn), point of sail from the TWA (close-hauled under 60°, beam reach under 110°, broad reach under 150°, otherwise downwind), the voyage’s sails that are set, plus time, position and SOG/COG as a note.
  2. The draft opens right away. Take a photo or enter one or two values — or tap Cancel and fill it in at leisure ashore.
  3. In the list the draft carries an orange Draft badge. Save turns it into a normal entry.

Two taps instead of eight form interactions. If it was a mis-touch, the undo bar deletes the draft again.

Wind without an onboard network. If no wind sensor supplies the TWS, then when creating an entry and in the draft – for example after “Capture trim too” at a sail change – the wind of the nearest station or the weather model appears in grey in “from” and “to”: ±2 kn around the value from the same source as “Wind now” on the map, below it the source and, from 20 minutes on, its age. It only goes into the entry with Apply at the top of the Conditions section; with wind from the onboard network there is no suggestion. Station and model give the mean wind without gusts, not the wind at the boat. Does the grey range match what you are sailing right now?

Add trim afterwards

Not every trim is captured on deck. In the voyage under “Trim on this voyage” — and in the regatta chapter Speed & trim under “Trim in this race” — there is the row Add trim afterwards, even if there is nothing there yet. It opens When?: first the sail changes from the logbook, then the other entries with sails or wind, below them Other time with a wheel within the voyage’s time span. A tap creates a draft with exactly this time and opens it as with “Capture trim”. The sails are those that were up at that time according to the logbook; speed, course and position come from the nearest track point at most five minutes away, the wind only from a track point with wind from the onboard network — never from station or model. Whatever is missing stays empty. The note begins with “Added afterwards for …” and gives for each row the source with the time of the track point, or why a value is missing. No logbook entry is created; the undo bar after the form deletes the draft. In the demo there is an added-afterwards draft at “Spinnaker up” in the Wednesday race. Do sails and wind in the draft match what you were sailing at that time?

“What fits now?”

As soon as wind is available — from the NMEA network or in demo mode — a chip with the current conditions appears at the top of the trim list: TWS ±3 kn, detected point of sail and sails set. A tap on it filters the list to the entries that match these conditions — no typing numbers, no menu. That is the question you ask on the beat when the boat isn’t going: what did I have last time at 16 knots close-hauled? The classic filters by wind, point of sail, sea state and sail remain alongside.

Workbench

The Workbench is the quiet place for trim knowledge — for the desk and the harbour, not for the deck. It is in the hub: Tools → Analyses → Sails & trim → Trim workbench; at the top of the trim list, below “What fits now?” or the wind chips, the Analyses row says so in one sentence and takes you there. There you find the Workbench row again with the sentence “What worked in which wind?”, and it opens the workbench directly — even without entries and without Pro, then with the duration instead of the figures. At the top your boat (with photo, otherwise in the trim colour) and three tiles: how many entries there are and how many of them are drafts, how many were captured in a measured run, and the best run. Below that the Rated row: every trim entry created during a measured run or up to two minutes after “End run”, next to the performance of that run — one entry per run, grouped by point of sail (close-hauled, beam reach, broad reach, downwind and “General” for entries without values for a point of sail). Each row shows in large type the wind range (without a range, the title), below it title and date and the run’s figures, with Pro with a bar; a tap opens the entry. Without Pro the row gives the duration of the run: “Duration 4:12 — figures with Pro”. Pro From GPS alone, the bar shows avg speed in relation to the fastest run of the group, ordered by date, and next to it it says “manual wind”: the only wind the row knows is the range you typed — a faster run may simply have had more wind. With wind from the NMEA network, the entries per point of sail are ordered by polar performance, the bar goes up to 120 % of the polar, and the row gives polar %, VMG, avg speed and the measured wind; if every polar value is missing, the bar measures speed as without wind and says “no polar value”. Without a measured run, the page says how to create one and leads to the training sessions. In the demo, the “Forestay trim training” brings three rated entries under “Close-hauled”.

Below “Rated” is the Wind axis row. It lines up all trim entries per point of sail across the wind from 0 to 30 kn — close-hauled, beam reach, broad reach, downwind and “General” for entries without values for a point of sail; the scale carries the same steps as the wind chips (4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 25 kn). Each entry is a bar across its wind range: if the entry only has “to”, it starts at 0; if it only has “from”, it reaches to 30 kn; an entry with values for two points of sail appears on both lanes. The stopwatch in the bar means “captured in a measured run”; dashed is a draft. Grey areas with a label are gaps: ranges between 4 and 25 kn at least 3 kn wide for which you don’t have a trim on this point of sail yet — drafts don’t fill any. Overlapping entries stack in up to three rows; anything beyond that is counted as “+n”; a tap on it lists the entries of that range, a tap on a bar opens the entry. Entries without a wind range are counted below the axis. The bars show what is in the entry — typed, or prefilled from the live wind when it was created, not a measurement. In the workbench, the row gives the number of gaps with an example. With very large text, each point of sail shows a list instead of the graphic. In the demo the “Base trim medium air” lies on close-hauled and downwind; the three entries of the “Forestay trim training” stack with it under “Close-hauled” at 10–14 kn, and whatever doesn’t fit in three rows is counted as “+n”.

The third row of the workbench is called Compare and puts two trim entries side by side — for the question of what was different on a day when it went well. There, A is the most recent entry (a draft only if there is no other). In the trim entry itself, the row Compare with … under “As Template for New Entry” leads to the same comparison, and then this entry is A. The comparison then also appears as the card “Compare trims” under Tools → Analyses → Sails & trim. You choose B from a list: at the top a suggestion — same point of sail, nearest wind range, preferably from a measured run, drafts last — below it all other entries by date; each row gives wind range, title, date, points of sail and whether the entry comes from a measured run. Nothing is preselected. The comparison shows two columns, A on the left, B on the right: at the top wind, sea state and sails, below them the trim values from “General” and the selected point of sail. The tab switcher applies to both columns; preselected is a point of sail both carry, otherwise the first tab. What is different is shown in bold in the trim colour with ⇄ in front of the field name, what is the same in grey. If a value is in only one of the two entries, the other side shows “–”, without highlighting — not noted is not different. Images and notes are not compared. Values over twelve characters are stacked; with very large text shorter ones too, as soon as they don’t fit side by side. Under “Measured” are the runs of both entries with their figures, per tier as in “Rated”. Pro With wind from the NMEA network, a sentence below says whether the runs are comparable — with the same limits as on the “Measured runs” page (1.5 kn TWS, 10° TWD), for example “Wind 2.6 kn apart — only partly comparable”. Without Pro it says that the measured wind decides this and that above only the wind range of the entries is shown. In the demo, you put “Forestay base” next to “Forestay +2 turns”: under “Close-hauled + General”, traveller, backstay and jib halyard are the same and grey, the forestay tension (8 against 10 Loos) is the one difference, and the runs are comparable.

NoteThe trim diary itself is free in every tier. The wind data for the chip and for the automatically detected wind range and point of sail when capturing come from the NMEA network — without instruments you can try both in demo mode.
Test
  • Create a trim field of your own with type Scale or Choice and enter a value with it: do you hit the buttons reliably even with wet hands or gloves?
  • During a voyage (or in the demo), capture a draft via “Capture trim”: do wind range, point of sail and sails match what you see right now — and can you find the draft in the list afterwards?
  • Tap “What fits now?”: does the filter show the entries you would expect, or too many or too few?
  • Sit down at the workbench with your own entries: can you see on the wind axis which wind you are missing a trim for — and does that match your feeling from last summer? Can you find in the comparison within a minute what was set differently on a good day?
↑ Back to contents

C10Boat, sails & polar

Everything that belongs to the boat — basic data, sails, equipment, polar and the miles/engine hours statistics: maintained ashore, used on every trip.

Basic data and several boats

You will find the boat under Tools → Boat. That is where the basic data are: name, type, photo, sail number, call sign, MMSI and home port. The SOS radio procedure cards insert the call sign and MMSI into the radio calls — enter them before you need them.

Below the boat name, type and draught appear once entered — this way you see the draught without opening the form; the map’s safety contour is based on it. Analysis does not happen here: instead of statistics and racing tally, an Analyses row leads to Tools → Analyses (C7).

You can create several boats. The active boat determines which voyages, sails and trim entries you see in the app — if you switch between your own boat and a club boat, this is where you switch.

Sails: wardrobe with images and wind range

The sails have a page of their own under “Configuration”, grouped by Mainsail, Headsail and Downwind; “+” at the top right creates a new sail. For each sail you enter name, category, manufacturer, year and notes. The category controls when a sail appears in forms.

Each sail has images: the shape upwind, the label with the measurements, the damage before the repair — the material for the conversation with the sailmaker. The first image appears as a preview in the list, further ones with a counter next to it.

Then there is the wind range: wind range, wind angle (for downwind sails) and the source, according to the sailmaker’s crossover chart. That is four figures per sail, typed in for the whole wardrobe in two minutes; you can store the chart PDF as an image on the sail. At a sail change in the logbook, the app uses these values: the sails that match the current wind are at the top and carry a tick — the wind comes from the NMEA network or from the manual wind you entered in the pre-start.

A tap on a sail first opens the detail view: crossover chart large (tap for full screen), wind range as values, “Edit” in the toolbar — the same navigation as for equipment and trim.

Under “Where is it”, “Send away …” records whether a sail is at the sailmaker or lent out — with whom, since when; the sails page shows it under the name, and “Back” brings it back (without a maintenance log, sails don’t have one).

CautionDeleting only works after a confirmation, also from the detail view and the form. A deleted sail disappears from all logbook and trim entries in which it was entered.

Equipment with maintenance reminder

The equipment is an inventory list with images and notes (maintenance, serial numbers). For each item you can set a maintenance reminder: an interval in months and/or engine hours, counted from “Last service”. The engine hours come from the boat’s voyages, so you don’t count anything by hand.

  • Due: orange with warning triangle, with a note such as “overdue by 3 wks” or “12 h overdue” — the same warning colour as overdue items in the Workshop; red is reserved for alarms.
  • Due soon: the status in grey with a clock symbol; the bar (see below) turns orange from “soon”.
  • The boat overview shows a counter such as “2 due” — also orange with warning triangle.

There is deliberately no notification for this. In demo mode you see the outboard (12 months / 50 h, overdue), the liferaft (36 months, due soon — “due in 2 wks”) and the lifejackets. Outboard and liferaft carry a photo and a description there — in the list the photo appears as a thumbnail, in the detail and in the inventory PDF photo and description appear.

Maintenance cockpit. At the top of the equipment list, three tiles count the items with a maintenance interval: due, soon and OK — coloured only when there is something to count. Below them it says how many items don’t have an interval yet. As soon as an item has an interval, the list sorts by due date: due, soon, OK, then the items without an interval; in each group the tightest first (by days until due, then by engine hours). “By name” and “Order created” remain in the sort menu at the top right, and if you had chosen one of them, it stays. For each item a bar shows how much of the interval is used up — by time or engine hours, whichever share is larger: in the Workshop colour, orange from “soon”, full when exceeded; without “Last service” it stays empty. The bars are the same length in every row so that they can be compared with one another; on the iPad they appear with the status below in a column of their own on the right (with very large text as on the iPhone).

Send to Workshop. A part that is due becomes a task in the Workshop with a swipe to the left (next to the red “Delete”), with a long press, or with the button in the detail view. At the top the sheet shows the task, such as “Service Outboard”, with its status and, if it is still to come, when it falls due; below that you choose the project (preset to the most recent open one) and the category. The app suggests the category from the name of the part — outboard → “Engine”, liferaft → “Safety”, otherwise a “Maintenance” category — and you can tap any other. Above the button it says where the task will go. “Create” creates it with the part under “Concerns” and the status as a note; the toast offers “Undo”. If there is no open project, the button creates a “Maintenance” project with the season, such as “Maintenance 2026/27”, with the part as its first task. If you tick off the task later, the Workshop asks as usual whether the service should be set to today.

In a part’s detail view there is the “Maintenance” section: bar and status, interval, last and next service, the engine hours since the service (calculated from the engine times of the voyages, as it says underneath) and the “Send to Workshop” button.

Maintenance log. Every part keeps a maintenance log. In the detail view, in the “Maintenance” section above “Send to Workshop”, there is the Serviced button. It opens a sheet with date (preset to today), engine hour reading, note and amount; “Add” creates the entry, and “Last service” moves forward to this date if it is newer — an older entry added later leaves it as it is. The toast offers “Undo”: entry gone, “Last service” as before. The reading is preset with the total counter of the boat statistics up to that day (calculated from the engine times of the voyages, as it says under the field) and can be overwritten — before a reset of the total counter the field stays empty because the statistics don’t know the old reading, and likewise without a reset on a day before the first voyage under engine in the logbook; the field only appears if the boat has engine times or the part is serviced by engine hours. It is a meter reading — “Reading 212 h”, not “212 h since” — and stays as entered, even if you reset the total counter later; the reminder keeps counting from “Last service”. The Maintenance log section below shows the entries, newest first: date and amount, reading, note and, as a symbol, the origin — the hand for “Serviced”, the hammer for the Workshop. A tap opens the entry for editing; you can delete it there, with a swipe to the left or with a long press, always with a confirmation (“Last service” stays as it is). If you tick off a task with “Concerns” in the Workshop and confirm the question, the Workshop writes an entry as well: note “Task · Project”, the reading automatically as with “Serviced”, and underneath “Open project”: this opens the project the task is in, as long as task and project still exist. It stays one entry per task — if you tick it off again and confirm again, it moves forward to the new date. If “Last service” is only in the form, a line says “… — from the form, without an entry”; an entry never arises from it by itself. In the equipment list each part shows its last entry; the inventory PDF shows the last three. The boat’s statistics show, under “Workshop 2026/27”, the row Maintenance 2026/27: the amounts from the maintenance logs of this winter (July to June), and “no amounts entered” if there are none. In demo mode the outboard carries two entries without a reading — the one you create yourself with “Serviced” shows it — and the lifejackets one from this winter with an amount, which the statistics count under “Maintenance”. Below the maintenance log is “From the Workshop”: the completed Workshop tasks that concern the part, per winter with project and date — in demo mode “Winterise engine” on the outboard. The maintenance log is available in every tier.

Off the boat. The liferaft away for servicing, the radio lent to a fellow club member: under “Maintenance” in the detail view, “Send away …” sends a part off for servicing or lends it out. The sheet asks for the type, with whom or to whom, and since when; “Add” only works with a name. Then the top of the section reads “With Seaservice Kiel since 12 October · being serviced”, and “Back” is the one filled button — “Serviced” moves to a list row. “Back” doesn’t ask: from servicing it adds an entry to the maintenance log (today, “Service at Seaservice Kiel”, the reading calculated from the engine times as with “Serviced”); after lending it only clears the status; the toast undoes it for 10 s. In the list, “Off the boat · with …” takes the place of the last entry; bar and tiles stay — due stays due, even while it is being serviced. The inventory PDF names it for each part, and so does the “Ready to cast off” checklist (C1). In the demo the liferaft has been with “Seaservice Kiel” for three weeks.

Inventory as PDF. The share symbol at the top right of the equipment list turns the whole list into a PDF — for the insurer, a buyer or handing over the boat. At the top are the boat name, sail number, the date and the number of parts; for each part follow the first photo, manufacturer and model, serial number, purchase date, the description (at most three lines) and the maintenance: interval, last service and the status on that day, “due” as an orange area with a warning triangle, and below that the last three entries of the maintenance log with date, reading and note; if one carries a reading, the header says that it is counted from the engine times in the logbook and may differ from the hour meter on the engine. It is sorted by name, and it always contains all parts — even while you are searching. Without equipment the button stays; the PDF then says “No equipment entered yet.” The inventory is available in every tier.

Polar data: template, ORC certificate or your own file

The polar is the basis for the performance percentages in the instruments and in the race analysis. The app’s grid has 9 wind speeds × 8 angles, plus the beat and run optima. All the ways of filling it are free:

  • Load a template. Built in are Albin Express, Nordic Folkboat, H-boat, J/80, X-99, Dehler 34, Hallberg-Rassy 34, Bavaria 37 and First 36.7 — table plus beat/run optima, one tap instead of 108 numbers. The first launch no longer offers the template — it is only here. The values are guide values from ORC/VPP orders of magnitude, not official class polars, and can be changed cell by cell after applying them.
  • Read an ORC certificate as PDF. The quickest way to the right polar: the table “Rated boat velocities in knots” is text in the PDF and matches the app’s grid exactly, plus the beat and gybe angles — nothing is estimated or guessed by image recognition. Also taken over are class, yard, year built, length, beam, draught, displacement and the measured sails (main, jib, spinnaker with area). Name and sail number deliberately not — people often use a classmate’s certificate because the polar applies to the class; the confirmation before applying names only class, length, year built and yard. The way is via Boat → Polar data → Import file; the first launch no longer asks for it.
  • Your own polar file. CSV, .pol or .txt from the sailmaker, from the ORC certificate or from your own measured run — automatically converted to the app’s grid. Beforehand the import shows what the file has to look like (example, accepted separators and decimal separators), provides a sample file to save and, if it fails, says what was missing. A row with a value that cannot be one (∞, “nan”, a negative speed or one above 50 kn, an angle of 0° or above 180°) is left out entirely so that no value slips into the wrong wind speed; the confirmation before applying says how many rows that was.
  • By hand. Enter the ORC grid directly — always possible, but the long way.

Calibration

The two fields in the boat form are free — anyone may fill them in, but they can only have an effect where wind data flows (wind sensor and log on the NMEA network). They cover around 80 % of the real true-wind error. Masthead offset in degrees: if the wind vane is twisted, every angle is off by the same amount — head to wind, read the AWA, enter the difference from 0°. Log factor in per cent: compare the log against GPS in calm water; if it reads too low, enter more than 100 (fouling makes it worse). Both act at the source, so identically on display, true-wind calculation, polar targets and recording; calibrated wind values carry “cal.” in the tile. The values belong to the boat, because the error sits in the sensor, not in the app. The third field, the heel offset in degrees, is added to every heel reading from the attitude sensor — for a sensor that is mounted askew; for measuring and applying it see C11, “Check instruments”.

Your season: miles, days, wind & engine hours

The card is called Your season. At the top is the season row: a season is the calendar year of casting off, the default is the most recent year with a session of your own, and a tap opens the list of years. Below it the “Season 2026” section with seven rows covering all your own sessions of the year — voyage, race and training, without voyages on other boats: Nautical miles (sailed, engine, engine time), Sessions by type, Days underway (calendar days from casting off to mooring, an open voyage up to today), Longest stage (between casting off, stage destination and mooring; without a track as a straight line), Night hours (track time between dusk and dawn), Harbours & anchorages (mooring entries with a position, the most frequent by name) and the Wind profile: hours with track per wind band as bars with percentages, wind from the onboard network, otherwise from logbook, station or model in the order of the daily report; the row below counts the hours per source. If a row lacks its source, it shows “—” and the reason. The card in the hub shows the nautical miles of the current season.

Below the season is This season against last — the comparison is with the last year before it with a session of your own; if there is a year without sessions in between, a sentence says what it is compared with. Without a subscription the section shows the count for both seasons, one row per year (demo: “2026: 132 nm · 12 days”, below it “2025: 102 nm · 6 days”), and a row saying that Pro shows the comparison. Pro At the top the wind of both years (demo: “2026 mostly 12–16 kn, 2025 mostly 8–12 kn”) — a light summer means more engine hours. Below it, row by row, value against value: nautical miles, share sailed, engine hours per 100 nm, days underway, longest stage, night hours, harbours & anchorages. Only share sailed (higher = better) and engine hours per 100 nm (lower = better) carry a verdict: an arrow in a circle, pointing up means better. The other rows only show more or less with an arrow without a circle — more miles don’t make a better year. Below 5 % or one unit it says “unchanged”. At the bottom a trend sentence from the biggest difference, and the origin. Without a previous season the section stays and says that the comparison comes with the first session next year. The card in the hub gives the previous season’s nautical miles below the figure (demo: “2025: 102 nm”).

Share at the top right produces the season PDF, in every tier: page 1 with boat and year in the header, the seven rows, the wind profile as bars, and origin and caveats as a footnote; without a subscription it says there that Pro adds a second page. Pro Page 2 “Against the previous season” with the wind sentence, the comparison rows and the trend sentence.

Below that, unchanged: under Tools → Analyses → Your season the app collects the analyses for the active boat or for the one selected at the top of the analyses: total miles, miles sailed, miles under engine and engine hours. They are based on the track points and the “Engine on”/“Engine off” entries of the voyages; the timestamps are evaluated, so whatever you added or corrected afterwards also counts. The distance within the engine phases counts as engine, everything else as sailed; engine + sail = total. For each voyage the statistics show engine time, distance under engine and the avg speed; in the voyage detail the engine time is also shown in the summary.

  • Total counter: runs endlessly across all voyages like an hour meter. Reset only with a confirmation — the entries in the voyages remain unchanged, only the counter starts again.
  • Trip counter: the same values, but resettable with one tap — like the trip meter in a car, for example per season or until the next oil change. Each reset resets all values of the counter in question together.
  • Boat filter: with several boats you set the statistics to one boat, several or all; each boat counts from its own counter reset. Voyages on other boats (guest boat) never count towards the boat counters, but can be included in the total with a switch — for your personal season miles across everything.

Boat & speed: is my boat right?

Under Tools → Analyses → Boat & speed are the analyses that say something about your boat across all your sessions, each as its own card: Sailed polar, Speed by point of sail*, Target angles from measurement, Target heel, Tack asymmetry; Helm steadiness is under Manoeuvres & helming, the sail crossover under Sails & trim. The “Data basis” row at the bottom of the “Data quality” page (the tile at the very top of the analyses) opens the page with the switches for which sessions count. The analyses answer a different question from the race analysis: not “how did my race go?”, but “is my boat right?” — not over one day, but across all your sessions. The dividing line is simple: if the answer changes something about the boat or a setting that still applies tomorrow, it belongs here; if it tells how a particular day went, it belongs in the race analysis (C7).

Target heel Pro — at what heel does your boat go best? The analysis takes the upwind sections of your counted sessions, sorts them within one wind band by polar performance and looks at how the fastest quarter heeled: “At 12 kn you were fastest at 21°.” A button enters the value into the boat as the target heel — only on your action, with a confirmation, old and new value side by side. Why always only within one wind band: across bands you would measure that more heel and more speed coincide with more wind — and derive a target from that which makes the boat slower in light airs. It is sorted by polar performance rather than plain speed: whoever bears away gets faster and still makes worse progress. Without a stored polar there is therefore no suggestion. Entering the target heel is free as before — only the measurement that finds the value is tiered. The evidence chart shows one wind band at a time — chips to switch, as with the sailed polar: heel horizontal, polar performance vertical, each point an upwind section. An inverted U shape means there is an optimum; a flat cloud means no target follows from this band — and then exactly that is what it says at the top. Two vertical lines show your entered target and the suggestion, so that the real question is in the picture: are my fast sections where my target is? Deliberately without a fitted curve — with ten to twenty points per band it would suggest an optimum that the scatter doesn’t support. There are chips for every band with points, including those that are still too thin.

Tack asymmetry Pro — does your boat go the same on both tacks? The analysis takes all upwind sections of your counted sessions (which ones count, you set at the top in the data basis), separates them by tack and compares avg speed, avg pointing and avg VMG: “On port you sail 0.2 kn slower, at the same pointing.” A lasting difference is almost never skill, but trim — mast rake off-centre, shrouds uneven, the side you prefer to steer from — or a wind sensor that is askew, which you should rule out first. Important: a difference has to clear two hurdles before it stands as a finding. First the measurement: it must be larger than the uncertainty with which the two averages are known — and that gets smaller with every beat, so the analysis gets sharper rather than duller with every season. Second, seamanship: below 0.1 kn it says “detectable, but too small to change anything” — a different sentence from “in the noise”, and the more useful one, because it says that something was measured and there is nothing to do. A long phase doesn’t count like several short ones: 25 minutes in one go are one measurement, not five. It needs at least three upwind sections and five minutes of measuring time per tack — until then it says what is still missing. Below it the evidence chart: each point an upwind section, speed against pointing, coloured by tack. Starboard is shown as a circle, port as a square — at night, when both colours are red under the red filter, the shape carries the tack. If the two clouds lie on top of each other, the tacks go the same; if one sits lower at the same pointing, you see the finding as a picture.

Vane or boat? Pro — is in the same article below Tack asymmetry and answers the follow-up question. If one tack points higher than the other, there are three possible causes, and upwind they look the same: the wind vane is askew, the rig is asymmetric, or heel tilts the masthead unit. Downwind separates them. A mounting error is fixed in the boat and acts equally on every point of sail; heel and rig show through upwind and hardly at all downwind. If the same amount comes out on both points of sail, the app tells you the misalignment and offers to add the correction to your existing masthead offset (Boat → Calibration) — on your action, with the confirmation “Current … New …”, never by itself. Only what has been recorded since the last calibration change counts. If something else comes out, it explicitly does not say what it is: mast rake, uneven shrouds, your favourite side of the tiller and the heel error all look the same at this point, and picking one of them would be guessing. The calculation is done in apparent wind — only there is a misalignment the same amount on every point of sail; in true wind the same error would appear roughly twice as large upwind as downwind, and the cross-check would come out the wrong way round. If apparent wind was not recorded or there is no downwind sailing below 170°, it says so instead of calculating.

Target angles from measurement Pro — at what angle do you make the best progress? Not according to the stored polar, but according to your own sections. This matters more than it sounds: your target angles drive the sailing compass and laylines. If they come from a boat-type template rather than your certificate, you are sailing against someone else’s values — and never notice, because the compass shows them without any caveat. It is sorted by VMG, not by polar performance, and that is the difference from Target heel: whoever sails high and slow can fully reach the target speed for exactly that angle — 100 % — and still make worse progress. Sorted by polar percentage, the pincher’s angle would come out. A button enters angle, speed and VMG together into your polar — together, because a new angle next to the old target speed would be two values that silently contradict each other. If your best sections scatter by more than 6° in angle, there is no suggestion, but the sentence that it was too unsteady. The evidence chart shows angle against VMG, one band and one point of sail at a time: if the dashed line of your polar lies left of the peak of the cloud, your target angle is too high.

Helm steadiness Pro — how steadily do you hold the angle to the wind, per wind band and over the season? The figure is a subtraction: from the scatter of your wind angle, the amount the wind itself shifted in the same time is subtracted. If it shifts by eight degrees while the boat goes doggedly straight ahead, that is no unsteadiness on the tiller. In the chart, per band, the light bar stands for the raw scatter, the orange one for what remains after the subtraction; they stand side by side rather than stacked, because they add up quadratically. The season comparison only reports a trend when the difference is larger than the accuracy of the two monthly averages allows — and it doesn’t compare months that had clearly different wind; that would be comparing the weather, not the tiller. What the figure doesn’t know: the waves. A choppy day looks like an unsteady hand. And whoever deliberately steers to the shifts gets a value that is too good — never one that is too bad. As the only analysis in this area it does not end with a value you apply to the boat: it describes the helmsman, not the boat.

Speed by point of sail* Pro — what does your boat do without a wind sensor? The card sits directly below the Sailed polar and is its rough version for everyone without an onboard network: per point of sail* (upwind* up to 75°, downwind* from 135° between course over ground and wind direction, reaching* in between) and wind band (below 8, 8–12, 12–16, 16–20, above 20 kn) the average speed over ground and how long you sailed there. The wind comes from the GFS weather model: after a session without wind from the onboard network, “Wind from open sources” fetches the model value for every hour, in addition to the station, the next time the app is opened with a connection. The station never counts here — measured on land, it reads too low. Sessions with a wind sensor are not included in the card; for them the sailed polar applies, and under “Not in the table” a row leads there. It also says there when sessions lack the model wind, because their wind was fetched by an earlier build or because they were first opened with a connection more than ten days later — it can’t be fetched afterwards, the server only keeps it for ten days. Caveat: no wind at the boat. Current shifts course and speed over ground, and the model doesn’t know shifts within the hour — that is why the table goes into no other calculation (VMG, target angles, polar, crossover). It is a yardstick for the next voyage and a first template when there is no polar.

Which sessions count

The Data basis page has three switches: races, measured runs, voyages. Separate, so that “everything”, “race only” and “race and training” are all possible. All three are on, and the switches are there in every tier: what happens to your own sessions is a matter of data ownership, not a feature. You switch off a single session in its own detail view (Voyage → Analyses).

Voyage data doesn’t water down race data. Where races and measured runs provide the same sail set in a cell, they alone decide — voyage sections only step in where there would otherwise be nothing at all, and the cell then says that it is based on them. In any case, only genuinely steady stretches of one minute or more get through from a voyage; engine, manoeuvres and harbour drop out. A session on someone else’s keel never counts — and that can’t be switched on either, because someone else’s measurements in your own polar would simply be wrong figures.

Three of the analyses (Tack asymmetry, Target heel, Target angles from measurement) each end with a value you can apply; Helm steadiness describes you rather than the boat. Sailed polar and sail crossover are described in detail in C7; the boat’s polar data say in one sentence where the sailed polar is, and the sail list opens the crossover itself. Without Pro, the Pro cards of a category sit in one collapsed row at its end, which says what Pro shows with instruments on the NMEA network — the cards remain reachable in every tier, and they name the data source. Pro

TestLoad the template for your boat type or read in your ORC certificate as PDF: do the values land in the right grid, and are the sails taken over correct? Create a sail with a photo and range of use — do you find the picture again later in the list and in the detail view? And after a voyage with “Engine on”/“Engine off”: do the engine hours and engine miles in the statistics match what you really sailed and motored?

C11Instruments & NMEA

The instrument dashboard shows speed, course, wind and depth in large figures at the helm — from the iPhone GPS and, via an NMEA gateway, from the onboard network. No tier is needed for that.

The dashboard by data source

Tools → Instruments opens a full-screen display for the mount at the helm: black background, large monospace digits, readable in sunshine and at a glance. What it shows depends on which data arrive. The standard pages with all values from the onboard network are free; sailing compass, custom pages, smart fields and the regatta page belong to Pro — without Pro they don’t exist in the dashboard, and there is never a lock there.

  • With the iPhone GPS only it shows pages with GPS values: SOG, COG, position, bearing, distance and XTE to the Go-To destination, countdown, anchor distance. It only shows the “Connect NMEA” button once a gateway has been set up (Settings → NMEA connection, “Connect” once there). The first time it opens, the app creates two pages: “Plotter” (SOG and COG, two large tiles) and “Navigation” (BRG, DTW, XTE, VMC, ETA, TTG). In the field picker every field is available, including those from the onboard network: a tile that has never had a value from the bus says “needs NMEA” — whoever sets up the gateway later finds their page ready.
  • With the onboard network the “Wind” and “Boat” pages, wind, depth and log tiles and the RAW recording are added; with Pro the sailing compass fills — without an onboard network it is a page of its own and gives the reason — and the regatta page is created. This needs an NMEA gateway on the boat’s Wi-Fi (2000 or 0183) and the sensors on the bus; you can try it without a gateway in demo mode. A tile without a value gives the reason: “needs NMEA” as long as the bus delivers nothing; “no value from bus” when it is running and only this field is missing, such as the RPM with the engine stopped; on the “Regatta” page, what the value is waiting for (tack angle “no tack yet”, layline “no mark”, TWD trend “needs 5 min of wind”).

Tiles, pages and smart fields

Instrument tiles show SOG, STW, TWS, TWA, TWD, depth and much more; you switch between several pages by swiping or with a tap on the left or right edge of the screen.

  • Go-To tiles: BRG, DTW and XTE to the destination, VMC (velocity made good towards the destination — not VMG, which would be towards the wind), ETA as a time of day, TTG as time remaining, plus race countdown (mm:ss → GO) and distance to the anchor.
  • Page manager Pro (Instruments → Manage pages): custom pages with grid, tile sizes and freely chosen values, swap tiles, delete pages. Without Pro it shows the list of pages, which can be reordered, and at the end a Pro row with sailing compass, custom pages and tiles, smart fields and regatta page; “+” opens the Pro overview. A page with VMG Δ, layline, TWD trend or performance is then marked “Pro” and is not in the dashboard — it stays saved.
  • Smart fields Pro (Instruments → Manage pages → Smart fields): rule-based tiles that show the right value for the situation — if a countdown is running, the countdown, otherwise SOG; with a wind sensor on the onboard network, VMG upwind and SOG on reaching courses. The rules are checked from top to bottom, the first that applies wins.
