In the editor, “Reorder” below the rules rearranges them (drag by the handle on the right) – the stricter condition belongs at the top, for example TWS > 25 before TWS > 15.
  • Sailing compass Pro: compass rose with heading, true and apparent wind arrow and green/red tack sectors — the pattern known from B&G. Needs a wind sensor and compass on the NMEA network.
  • Regatta page Pro (wind sensor and log on the NMEA network), created the first time it opens with Pro: VMG Δ (VMG sailed against the polar target, with sign), Tack ∠ (average tack angle of the last boards from the track), layline (distance to the layline of the mark you are steering for) and TWD trend (wind direction against the 10-minute average, arrow to the right = veer; only after five minutes of data), plus countdown and performance. All four fields are also in the page manager.
  • Pressure, pressure trend, log and trip — four values from the bus (barometer and paddlewheel on the NMEA network): “Pressure trend” shows the change in hPa per hour with sign, only after one hour of data — a fall of more than 1 hPa per hour means wind, more than 2 considerably more. Log and trip measure the distance through the water, not over ground; the difference from the GPS distance over the same time is the set caused by current. Without a barometer on the onboard network, “Pressure” and “Pressure trend” show the values of the sensor in the iPhone, and the line below the value then says “iPhone” (in the first hour “from 1 h · iPhone”); measurements are only taken while the app is open or a recording is running.

Data strip on the map

The round instruments button in the map’s left button column puts the values beside the map instead of over it: in portrait as a data strip at the bottom — a row of three tiles above the tab bar, like a plotter’s instrument bar; page through with the button on the left, full screen and close on the right, the first three fields of each page —, in landscape and on the iPad as a column on the right. The column is preset to narrow — SOG and COG one above the other, large digits; you set two further widths in the map’s layers menu (“Change column width”), and which fields appear in it you decide, as everywhere, in the page manager. The sailing compass now only appears in full screen: in a column just 210 pt wide its rose was smaller than the dial of a wristwatch and so no longer readable underway. Map, values and tab bar stay visible at the same time; tapping again hides it. The full-screen button in strip and column opens the dashboard behind it as “numbers only” mode; the Tools tile “Instruments” leads straight there.

Screen lock

The lock at the top right of the dashboard locks the display against accidental touches from spray. Locked means locked: swiping doesn’t change the page either, and a permanent lock notice shows the state. You unlock by holding for 3 s on an 88 pt area, with haptics as feedback.

NMEA connection

The app reads two formats over the boat’s Wi-Fi: NMEA 2000 as YDWG RAW and NMEA 0183 sentences from any Wi-Fi gateway or multiplexer (RMC, GGA, GLL, VTG, HDG, HDT, HDM, MWV, VWR, MWD, DPT, DBT, VHW, MTW, XDR; the checksum is checked) — via TCP (host:port) or via UDP, where the app listens on the port (typically 10110). Gateways that broadcast on the port are heard; nothing has to be set to the iPhone’s IP on the gateway. UDP, however, doesn’t repeat lost datagrams, and the app only notices whether the gateway is still sending after 20 s of silence; where TCP is possible, TCP is the calmer choice. The format is detected per line, and the settings show “Data format: NMEA 2000 (RAW)” or “NMEA 0183”. If the gateway only delivers apparent wind (AWA/AWS), the app calculates the true wind from apparent wind and STW or SOG.

Over UDP the app reads only one device. The first one from which data arrive after connecting delivers; if a second device on the same Wi-Fi sends to the port, the app discards its data and shows “Second source on the network (address) ignored” below the status — no alarm. A stranger’s transmitter on the harbour Wi-Fi thus no longer silently takes over depth, position or AIS targets. If the line shows the address of your gateway, the other device sent first after connecting — then disconnect and reconnect. If the gateway restarts, the app keeps reading it as long as it keeps its address; if it gets a new one, the app reconnects after a good 20 s of silence and then takes the first device that sends. The app remembers, per port, the device that delivered last: after every reconnect it reads only that one for 10 s, and any other one is shown in the line during that time; if it stays silent, the app again takes the first one that sends — so a gateway with a new address a good 10 s after reconnecting. “Disconnect” forgets the remembered device, which is why disconnecting and reconnecting helps when a stranger’s device has become the source. A gateway that sends from two addresses (Wi-Fi and Ethernet) shows the line constantly — that is harmless.

This is how you connect your onboard electronics:

  1. Join the iPhone to the gateway’s Wi-Fi (the boat’s Wi-Fi).
  2. Keep mobile data: the gateway Wi-Fi has no internet. So that the iPhone still stays online over the mobile network, in the iOS settings under Wi-Fi → ⓘ next to the boat’s Wi-Fi → “Configure IP” → Manual, enter a fixed address in the gateway’s network (for the YDWG-02N 192.168.4.20, subnet mask 255.255.255.0) and leave the Router field empty. The instructions are also in the app directly below the connection status.
  3. Open Tools → Settings → NMEA connection. Connecting and configuration are at the top; diagnostic log, PGN counters, damping and data recording are on the “Advanced” subpage, the demo switch at the very end. About damping: it smooths the display so that the tiles don’t jump — since Build 13 the recorded track always gets the measured values. Whoever needs 10 s of damping to be able to read anything at all no longer changes the measured values in the track by doing so. The same applies to course and speed in the MOB entry and the wind in the start entry of a race, and likewise to course and speed used by the AIS warning. The wind in an ordinary logbook entry, on the other hand, is deliberately taken by the app as it was on the display — the entry records what you read. The tack detection likewise deliberately stays with the display: with the wind damping turned up it detects the change of tack later, and the tack colouring in the track follows it. About data recording: if free storage falls below 500 MB, the app stops it by itself – with a notification and a note at the switch saying why it is off; the voyage recording continues. In a race, data recording ends with the scoring, not only at the pontoon. In front of it is now the range: the raw recording writes at the bus’s data rate, not at the boat’s speed — around 640 KB/min, so about 0.9 GB a day, whether you are doing 2 or 8 knots. The archive shows rate and range (“about 26 h left”); below six hours the same line appears in orange in the “Ready to cast off” checklist in the logbook. The calculation uses your boat’s most recently measured rate; as long as nothing has ever been recorded, it says “estimated”. Underway the app speaks up once as soon as less than an hour remains — you can tidy up at the pontoon, but no longer at sea. Tidying up is done in the archive below: every recording has a bin next to the share symbol, with a confirmation, and the swipe to the left stays alongside as a shortcut; “Delete all” clears the whole archive – except a running recording, which stays, and the confirmation says so. If you delete a voyage or its boat, the recordings of its race go with it – including one that is currently running; recordings started by hand stay in the archive. The archive is not part of the iOS device backup.
  4. Variation by hand: many compass sensors send the heading as magnetic but no variation (PGN 127258) – that otherwise comes from the plotter. If it is missing, there is no true heading: the true wind direction is then calculated from the magnetic heading, and “Head to wind” takes it over as the wind direction – off by the local variation, 4–5° in the western Baltic. That is why you enter it under “Advanced” under Variation: amount in degrees and next to it East or West, as shown on the chart (Kiel Bay about 4.5° East). If the bus sends one, the one from the bus applies. The setting applies to all boats – whoever changes cruising area changes it. Without either, the heading in the wind sheet and in the sail choice is called “HDG M” (magnetic) instead of “HDG”. The app does not recalculate voyages already recorded.
  5. Enter the gateway’s host and port — for UDP the port on which the gateway sends.
  6. Connect. The page shows the detected data format; in the live data list and under “Detected sensors” you see what arrives. Directly below, Check instruments opens the check per sensor (see below); without a connection it says “Connect, then check”.

After that you no longer need to go into the settings on board: Connect automatically (on by default) tries to connect when the app starts and every time it returns to the foreground, once a gateway has been set up — an address entered or connected by hand once; before that no attempt runs, which saves battery. Whoever connected automatically to the default address with Pro up to Build 42 stays that way: the app takes it over as set up at the first launch. A manual “Disconnect” keeps the automatic connection quiet until the next app start. The state is shown by the NMEA status chip at the top of the logbook — green connected, orange connecting, red error or data jam, grey off; a tap on it opens the NMEA settings. The same chip also appears in the settings list next to the “NMEA connection” entry and on the race event in the calendar, below time and boat: that is where the race is created, and whether wind and speed are recorded is decided at that moment. It is deliberately not next to “Start” — to the left of the start button, within thumb reach, there should be no second target. The chip’s words are then the same in all three places — “NMEA”, “NMEA connecting”, “NMEA stuck”, “NMEA error”, “NMEA off”, “DEMO”.

If no gateway can be reached, the app tries at growing intervals up to once a minute; when the instruments are opened, immediately.

The watchdog catches stalled connections: if TCP is connected but no more data are coming (typically when the iPhone silently drops off the boat’s Wi-Fi), the app reconnects automatically after 20 seconds. If the gateway accepts the new connection and stays silent, it tries again after around half a minute and then about every minute until data arrive again. After one minute without data, “NMEA data stopped” appears once on the lock screen, even if the app has reconnected in the meantime. In the dashboard the NMEA tiles then show “--”, and the NMEA chip in the logbook shows “NMEA stuck” in red — frozen values never look live. Reconnecting and the notification need an app that is awake: open, or in the background with a recording or anchor watch running. Without either, iOS puts the locked app to sleep after a few seconds, and the watchdog with it; the notification then only arrives as a banner when you open the app again. The app catches up on a missed tracking start as soon as it comes to the foreground.

If the network delivers nothing at the moment (gateway disconnected, error, frozen data), the dashboard shows its own pages with GPS values as a fallback; NMEA tiles show “--”, and at the bottom there is a large “Connect NMEA” button (while connecting, “Connecting to NMEA gateway…”). Why nothing is arriving is shown by the NMEA chip at the top of the logbook. As soon as data arrive again, the tiles fill by themselves. NMEA data recording under “Advanced” records the raw data (RAW logging with archive).

The archive has a budget: below the list of recordings it always says how much they take up and how much they may (“Archive 1.2 GB of 2 GB”); below that you choose 500 MB, 1 GB, 2 GB (preset), 5 GB or unlimited. If a recording ends over the budget, a notification comes once, and the archive line carries a warning symbol with a sentence. Only at the next start — including the automatic one for a race — do the oldest ones go until it fits again; the most recent always stays. Until then you have the choice: save (share symbol), delete or raise the budget. The notification only comes the first time; from then on it counts as consent, and at the start of the following races the app tidies up quietly. It only becomes loud if the most recent recording on its own is over the budget – then after every stop. If you change the budget or delete by hand, it speaks up again the next time. Without this notification no start deletes anything: even an archive that was already over the budget before this build loses nothing at the first start. During a recording the app deletes nothing, so a race is never cut short; the only brake underway remains the stop below 500 MB of free storage.

Sparse recording: for races over several days, storage is often not enough for a complete recording. That is why, below the “Record RAW data” switch, you turn on “Sparse recording” before casting off: the recording then writes only two minutes per quarter of an hour, around 13 % of the size. Which data are present stays visible; a bus dropout only if it falls into such a window. Every pause is a marker line in the file, the file name ends in “_sparsam”, the archive line says “sparse (2 min per 15)”, and the range in the settings and in the “Ready to cast off” checklist takes it into account and adds “sparse”. Underway the app doesn’t ask — you don’t change the format of a recording in the middle of a race. If you flip the switch during a recording anyway, it takes effect immediately; file name and archive line of the finished file only say so from the next recording, though.

NoteWithout a gateway of your own, demo mode shows the complete dashboard with onboard network data, simulated in a physically consistent way — sailing compass, wind, depth and regatta page included.

Phone GPS as fallback, calibration on the boat

The iPhone GPS is the fallback for everything — even with an onboard network. If the NMEA bus delivers no position, no COG or SOG (not connected, no GPS device on the network, silent for more than 5 s), the app feeds the phone GPS into the same data store: map, Go-To, MOB, race start line, instruments and logbook thus always have a position; when the bus comes back, it immediately takes priority again. The dashboard names the case honestly (“NMEA · position: iPhone GPS”) as long as the bus otherwise delivers data; without a connection or in a data jam it shows the GPS pages with the connect button. That applies in the foreground; in the background Auto-Tracking delivers. Wind, log, depth and compass have no substitute. And: phone GPS doesn’t count as “NMEA on” — a car journey still doesn’t start a voyage.

Calibration: the two fields in the boat form are free; they act on the values from the bus, so they need a wind sensor and log on the NMEA network. They cover around 80 % of the real true-wind error. Masthead offset in degrees: if the wind vane is twisted, every angle is off by the same amount — head to wind, read the AWA, enter the difference from 0°. Log factor in per cent: compare the log against GPS in calm water; if it reads too low, more than 100 (fouling makes it worse). Both act at the source, so identically on display, true-wind calculation, polar targets and recording; calibrated wind values carry “cal.” in the tile. The values belong to the boat, because the error sits in the sensor, not in the app. In addition there is a third field, the heel offset in degrees: it is added to every heel reading from the attitude sensor — for a sensor that is mounted askew.

Check instruments

In the dashboard’s menu (⋯) there is Check instruments: one row per sensor — wind sensor, log, depth sounder, compass, attitude sensor. “Now” is free and says whether the sensor is currently delivering, silent (from 10 s without a value) or stuck (3 min of exactly the same value at more than 2 kn of speed); plus three plausibility checks: wind above 80 kn, a log that shows speed while stationary, and jumps in depth. The watch only runs while the screen is open and sees the last 6 minutes. Findings from your sessions Pro: how long a sensor was missing or stuck on the last session (depth is not recorded, only the watch checks it), whether the wind vane is askew (the same cross-check as “Vane or boat?”), whether the attitude sensor is mounted askew (from at least six pairs of tacks in the same wind) and how far off the log is (from races and measured runs with opposite courses, subject to current — only a hint, you enter the factor by hand). For wind vane and attitude sensor there is a button: it adds the correction to the existing offset and asks first (“Current … New …”). Only what has been recorded since the last calibration change counts — older sessions carry different values. In Free, instead of the findings there is a row with a lock; without an onboard network the screen says that without sensors there is no sensor error, and names the way to connect. The same screen is also opened by the Check instruments row in Settings → NMEA connection, directly below “Detected sensors” — as a link as long as a connection is up or the demo is running; without a connection it says “Connect, then check”.

TestIs the dashboard readable at a glance in your mount, in sunshine and while heeling — which tile is too small or too crowded? With an onboard network: does the connection hold for a day’s sailing, does the status chip report a dropout before you notice it yourself, and did the iPhone GPS step in when the bus went quiet? Screen lock: did spray or a sleeve switch a page despite the lock, and could you release the lock with a wet hand in 3 s? Instrument check: does “Now” report a sensor as “stuck” when it is merely steady — say the compass or attitude under engine in flat water?
↑ Back to contents

C12Harbour guide

Your personal collection of harbours, anchorages and slipways — with arrival radius, practical info and POIs, linked to the map, logbook and stage plan.

Destinations: position, radius, practical fields

You’ll find the harbour guide under Tools → Harbour guide. Every destination has a position, a category (harbour, marina, anchorage …), an arrival radius, notes and practical fields such as toilet code, berthing fee and Wi-Fi access. You set the arrival radius with a slider between 20 and 5000 m; the map preview in the form shows the circle live as you drag. The radius isn’t decoration: it drives automatic arrival detection in the logbook — even when you haven’t set a destination at all. Destinations newly created when casting off, in voyage planning or by a long press on the map start at 300 m, like one created by hand. If the selection brings its own radius — a harbour or anchorage from the chart (90 or 150 m) —, that one still applies. Destinations already saved keep their radius.

You sort the list by name, distance or “Newest first”. The map symbol at the top jumps to the main map: the destination layer is switched on, and the camera frames all destinations. If the point card of a harbour, a buoy or an AIS target was still open there, it closes — it belonged to a different view. On the main map, as everywhere: tapping a harbour opens the data card, a long press creates one. Tapping a harbour zooms in with a smooth flight animation until the arrival circle takes up about two thirds of the screen width — shifted slightly upwards so the info card at the bottom doesn’t hide any of the harbour.

Adding harbours from OpenStreetMap

Marinas, harbours and anchorages from OpenStreetMap come with the same download as the seamarks: per offline package, stored locally, available offline. Commercial shipping (RoRo, ferry, tanker) is filtered out. In the map’s lighthouse menu they have their own layer, “OSM harbours”. On the map they appear as indigo anchors — anchorages as an open, dashed circle — already in the area overview. Tapping one shows name, type, berths, VHF channel and facilities (fuel, water, power, WC/showers, slipway, crane, pump-out …). Above the two buttons, across the full width of the map, it says what the circle is: the arrival radius — but arrival is only detected once the harbour is in the harbour guide.

Add to harbour guide opens the destination form pre-filled: name, position, category (harbour/marina/anchorage), plus phone, website and info as notes with the source. Only Save creates the record. The arrival radius is 150 m for an anchorage and 90 m for a harbour or marina – whether you add it from the map or from the harbour directory “Nearby”; the map shows the circle as soon as you tap. There is deliberately no bulk import — the harbour guide stays your selection. Harbours you have already added (closer than 250 m to a destination) are not shown twice on the map.

NoteCommunity data (ODbL): the position is usually the centre of the harbour area. That’s quite enough for arrival radius and bearing, but it’s no substitute for a pilot book. Demo mode brings the harbours of the Bay of Mecklenburg.

Harbour directory “Nearby”

The second page of the harbour guide, Nearby, lists the harbours, marinas and anchorages from the chart data — nearest first when the position is known, otherwise by name. The row gives type, berths and VHF channel, as far as OpenStreetMap knows them, with the distance on the right. Tapping the row shows the harbour on the main map: the “OSM harbours” layer is switched on, the map moves to the harbour and opens its data card with Go-To and Add to harbour guide — just as when you tap it on the map. Nothing is added in the process. The plus on the right of the row adds the harbour straight away as a destination (harbour and marina 90 m, anchorage 150 m); it moves to “My harbours”, and an undo bar takes back a mis-tap. On this page the map symbol at the top moves to the first harbours in the list — the nearest ones when the position is known —, also with the “OSM harbours” layer switched on.

Creating from the map, POIs per destination

On the main map and on every voyage map, a long press creates a new destination. After saving, its detail view opens straight away — you can add POIs, notes and berth info right then, without having to find the place first. On the voyage maps all destinations also appear as orange dots; there you can tap harbours (radius, POIs, info card) and use a long press to create destinations, anchorages and POIs — with a voyage open, stage destinations too.

POIs are the places around the harbour: harbour master, fuel berth, supermarket and so on — each with photos and a position on the destination’s detail map.

Choosing a destination: chart harbours and automatic destinations

Destinations appear in the destination search when casting off and in the stage plan. The destination picker (final stop, day’s destination, stages) shows, alongside your harbour guide and the place search, the harbours, marinas and anchorages from the chart data too — offline, sorted by distance, tappable directly on the map. A harbour chosen this way lands in the guide with category, VHF/berth note and a suitable arrival radius: anchorage 150 m instead of 90 m. Unnamed anchorages are simply called “Anchorage”; areas marked as no-anchoring zones are not offered at all.

The guide fills itself: if you pick a destination anywhere (casting off, voyage, stage plan) and no destination exists there yet, the app creates one automatically. If the area overlaps an existing destination, the existing one is used; places of the same name elsewhere are numbered (“Svendborg 2”).

Current harbour in the logbook

If the boat is within a destination’s area and not under way — no active tracking, below 1.5 kn —, the logbook shows the “Current harbour” card at the top with WC code, berthing fee, Wi-Fi and number of POIs. A tap opens the harbour guide detail. For the position the app uses the tracking, otherwise NMEA or demo GPS, otherwise the last track point (up to 48 h old).

Apple POIs and sharing by file

On a destination’s full-screen map, the pin button at the top right shows Apple’s points of interest: restaurants, shops, petrol stations, pharmacies and more around the harbour. This needs internet — which is why it is a harbour feature, not one for under way. Tapping an Apple POI shows an import card; Add to harbour guide turns it into one of the destination’s own POIs, with the category assigned automatically — after that it is available offline too.

Destinations can be passed on as a file: Destination detail → ⋯ → Share (Etmar share) creates an Etmar share file (.etmarshare) with position, radius, category, notes and harbour info, POIs and image thumbnails — via AirDrop, Mail or messenger. The same exists for race courses (Tools → Courses & waypoints, swipe right or long press) and routes (Tools → Routes). On the receiving side: tap the file → preview with map, details and a duplicate hint (“There is already … nearby”; with the same name a copy with a suffix is created) → Apply, one tap. Files from a newer version are cleanly rejected. No server, no account — the file is the way.

Test
  • Add an OSM harbour from your area to the guide: are position, category and the imported info correct, and does the indigo anchor then disappear from the map?
  • When your boat is in harbour: does the “Current harbour” card appear in the logbook with the right practical fields — even without tracking running?
  • Share a destination by AirDrop to a second device: does the preview come with a map, and are POIs and images complete after “Apply”?
↑ Back to contents

C13Safety: MOB, anchor alarm, SOS

Three tools for an emergency — man overboard, anchor watch and the radio distress call — all free, all without a network, all on your own device.

Currently unavailable(from the next build) From version 1.0.1, the anchor alarm, depth alarm and wind alarm are temporarily hidden: the app no longer shows any way to them, and an anchor set before the update is lifted afterwards. Saved anchorages and logbook entries remain. MOB, the AIS warning and the SOS card are unchanged. The sections “Anchor alarm” and “Anchoring from Tools” below describe the feature as it works in version 1.0.

Safety is always free in Etmar: none of the three functions asks for a tier. All three work with the iPhone GPS, the SOS cards additionally with boat name, call sign and MMSI from the boat data — none of them needs a mobile network. MOB and the anchor alarm also survive an app restart — what you have triggered stays active until you end it yourself. The exception is the demo (A3): an anchor set there ends with it, as does an MOB triggered there.

ImportantNone of the three functions alerts anyone outside your device: no DSC, no radio call, no message to third parties. That’s why you can safely run through MOB, the anchor alarm and the SOS cards at the pontoon. The anchor alarm’s sound breaks through the silent switch, the Sleep focus and the volume control — provided you allowed Critical Alerts when asked as you dropped anchor. If you declined, it stays at vibration; you can catch up under Settings → Notifications → Etmar.

MOB — man overboard

The red MOB button sits in three places: on the Map at the bottom left, in the action bar in the Logbook — also in the pre-start and race, where it is on the right in the first row of the race controls — and in the instrument dashboard under Tools → Instruments. You don’t trigger it with a tap; you hold it for 0.6 seconds — a ring fills as you do. Holding protects against false alarms when the boat slams into a wave or the phone is in your trouser pocket.

As soon as the ring is full, everything happens at once:

  • Position and time are fixed. If the last fix is older than ten seconds or has no measurement time, the point is provisional: the banner says on a line of its own “Refining position — fix 2 min before the tap” (black on orange), and the logbook entry carries the caveat as a note. A fix measured after triggering, accurate to 50 m or better, closer in time to the trigger than the old one and within three minutes, replaces the point once — in banner, map and entry, without averaging; the banner then says “Point refined — fix 4 s after the tap” for 30 seconds. If none comes, the old point stays, and the line “Point: fix 2 min before the tap” remains for as long as the alarm runs. “Refining position” only shows while a fix can still be closer to the trigger than the old one — if that was 40 seconds old, then for 40 seconds, at most three minutes. And the new point only counts as precise if it was measured no more than ten seconds after triggering; otherwise “Point: fix 2 min after the tap” stays permanently (black on orange), the radio card says “Position approximate”, and the note in the logbook gives the gap. The same applies when an MOB without a position only gets its first fix later.
  • With a voyage open, Etmar writes a “Man overboard” entry with position, course and speed to the logbook. Course and speed there are the measured values of that moment, not the smoothed ones of the display: if you have calmed the instruments with damping, that doesn’t shift the starting data for the search. If a value from the onboard network is older than 10 seconds, it stays empty in the entry rather than offering an old course as a basis for dead reckoning. Which voyage is the same on every route – map, logbook, instruments and watch: the one being recorded, otherwise the one the map or logbook is currently showing, otherwise the one started most recently, even one belonging to another boat. “MOB ended” is always in the same voyage as the alarm, wherever you end it.
  • A red banner shows the bearing and distance to the MOB position and the time since the alarm, counting by the second. If your own GPS fix is older than two minutes, it hides bearing and distance. It also says so on a line of its own above the radio button: “Bearing out of date · Fix 3 min ago” – by day black on orange, at night in large black type on the banner’s red. While the alarm runs, the quiet hints at the top of the map such as “Area not saved” pause; warnings stay, and the chips above the banner (destination, anchor watch, CPA warning) only grow up to the first level of accessibility text size. The buttons on the right move below the banner: if, with large text, it reaches down to them, + and − are dropped, and with very large text the round button too — Etmar writes the MOB entry itself when a voyage is open. The location button always stays (B2). If a CPA warning is running at the same time during the alarm and your own fix is out of date, the location button can sit on the right-hand end of the radio button at the two largest levels of accessibility text size; on the left and in the middle the radio button stays tappable.
  • On the map a lifebuoy appears at the position, with a red line from the boat to it.
  • While the alarm runs, the map keeps boat and person in view together: if either drifts to the edge or under the banner, it reframes them both – if they are close together, an iPhone in portrait then shows about 800 m across. If you have panned or zoomed the map or centred it on the point with the crosshair in the banner, it takes over again after 10 seconds; the location button brings both back at once. After “End”, the map follows the boat again if it did so before the alarm.

The alarm only ends via End — person recovered or false alarm. On the map and in the logbook a tap is enough; the “Undo” bar brings the alarm back for 6 seconds, map guidance included. If you end it there, the end is noted in the logbook when a voyage is open. In the instrument dashboard the app asks first. An app restart does not end the alarm. The only exception is an MOB from the running demo: it ends with the demo, without an “MOB ended” entry, and is gone after a restart (A3). If its trigger entry is in one of your own voyages rather than the demo voyage, the end of the demo adds “MOB ended” there — but not after a restart.

Apple Watch. The watch app is not included in version 1.0; you trigger the MOB on the iPhone.

Anchor alarm (currently unavailable)

You set the anchor via the Logbook → Anchor tile — it is there with and without an open voyage — or via “Set anchor” in the Layers menu of the Map; on top of that there is the dedicated anchor screen under Tools → Safety → Anchoring (below). One tap sets the anchor at the current position with the radius preset, which you change on the anchor screen with −10/+10 m (50 m to begin with); after a mis-tap an undo bar appears. The logbook then shows a banner such as “Anchor set · 23 m / 50 m · since 21:04”, and on the map you see the circle and the anchor pin. If the anchor drags, the pin turns red, grows larger and carries a warning triangle instead of the anchor — so even under the red filter, which makes red and amber look the same, it can be told apart from an anchor that is holding.

Without a GPS fix. If the iPhone has no position yet, the tap sets no anchor and opens no sheet either — an orange bar at the bottom (at night black with an orange edge) says what is missing, and where there is something to do, the bar itself is the button (recognisable by the arrow at the right-hand edge): if Etmar has never asked for location, it says “No GPS fix — allow location first”, and tapping it asks the iOS question. If you have denied location, it says “Location denied — Settings”, and a tap opens the iOS settings. If “Precise Location” is switched off for Etmar, it says ““Precise Location” off — anchor not set” — the anchor watch needs the precise position; a tap opens the settings too. This applies even when an approximate position is already available: Etmar no longer sets an anchor on it — the watch would see nothing there, yet would look like a watch until “WATCH BLIND” two minutes later. For the same reason “Anchor to current position” does not move the circle then either. If location is allowed but the GPS is still searching, it says “GPS still searching — anchor not set” — then wait a moment and tap again. This applies on the map as well as on the tile in the logbook.

If the boat leaves the circle, the banner turns red, the iPhone vibrates and sounds, and the notification “ANCHOR DRAGGING: 62 m from anchor” also reaches the locked phone in your bunk — as a critical alert, provided you have allowed it (see below), and again every 60 seconds for as long as the boat stays outside. This also applies if it holds again outside and stops moving — say because the anchor has reset. So that no false alarm occurs at the edge of the circle, Etmar allows a tolerance for GPS scatter: half the displayed accuracy. For the watch, a dedicated GPS monitor keeps running in the background (blue status indicator from iOS), independent of voyage tracking. So that iOS doesn’t freeze it when the phone lies untouched in the bunk for hours, Etmar explicitly registers the watch with iOS as ongoing background work — even without a recording running.

When the watch sees nothing. Until the first GPS fix after setting, the banner says “Awaiting GPS” and “— / 50 m” instead of a distance, and the anchor tile in the logbook also says “Awaiting GPS”. If no usable reading comes for two minutes — the iPhone delivers no fix at all, or its accuracy is 100 m or worse, say in the bunk below deck —, the banner turns orange: “WATCH BLIND”, below it a capsule with the age of the last fix (“Fix 3 min ago”, with no fix at all “No fix”) and the reason, for example “GPS too inaccurate (± 150 m)” or “No GPS reading”. At night the blind banner is black and only outlined, so that under the red filter it stands out from the red-filled “ANCHOR DRAGGING”. The last measured distance stays visible, dimmed; the anchor tile in the logbook turns orange, with “WATCH BLIND” in black type (at night only outlined). As with dragging, there is sound, vibration and the notification “ANCHOR WATCH BLIND”, on the locked phone as a critical alert, and again every 60 seconds for as long as the watch stays blind. With the app open, iOS no longer lays this notification over the screen as a banner while the anchor banner on the map, the anchor banner or anchor tile in the logbook, or the anchor screen is visible — they already show the state there; sound and vibration remain, and the notification is in Notification Centre. On other screens, over an open sheet and on the locked phone it arrives as before. “ANCHOR DRAGGING” always arrives as a banner, even at night, where it lies bright over the red filter — a dragging anchor is worth the loss of night vision. You stop the running sound by opening the anchor sheet or the anchor screen; the watch stays on and reports again one minute after the last alarm. When a usable reading comes again, the banner is dark again. If the banner was already on “ANCHOR DRAGGING” when the readings stopped, it stays red and shows the same capsule. The notification is then also called “ANCHOR DRAGGING — WATCH BLIND”, with the last measured distance; the anchor tile in the logbook says “Watch blind” below, the anchor chip on the map adds the age (“412/50 m · 4 min ago”), and the anchor screen gives the reason. A blind watch does not detect new dragging — checking only resumes with the next usable reading. If location is off for Etmar, the watch counts as blind at once (“Location off for Etmar”) — with sound and notification while the app is open; on the locked phone no warning comes without location. If location is off or only the approximate position is allowed, the sheet says so at the top with a crossed-out location symbol and takes you there with Open settings.

When iOS closes the app. If iOS needs memory overnight or the app crashes, the watch is gone with it. That’s why, when you set the anchor, Etmar asks for the location permission “Always” (iOS asks this question only once; if you already answered it when casting off, it won’t come again). With “Always”, iOS wakes the closed app again after a significant change of location — according to Apple after about 500 m at the earliest, without a mobile network considerably later too — and the watch carries on from the stored anchor; if the boat is then outside the circle, it reports the dragging. Drifting by less than this distance does not wake the app. If location is set only to “While Using the App”, the sheet says so at the top with a location symbol and Open settings: the watch then stays off until you open Etmar again. That is not “WATCH BLIND” — as long as the app is running, it watches as usual. In demo mode the position comes from the simulator: there Etmar doesn’t ask for the location permission when you set the anchor — not even if it has never been answered —, and the location warning lines in the anchor sheet stay away; you check both with the demo switched off. If you swipe the app away in the app switcher, iOS won’t wake it even with “Always”; leave it in the background at anchor.

You open the settings sheet via the anchor button or the symbol in the banner. The radius can be changed with large buttons in 10 m steps — rule of thumb: chain length + boat length + 10 m reserve. Every change takes effect at once; Done only closes the sheet. Important after anchoring: only once the anchor has dug in does the boat lie where it will stay — then tap Set anchor to current position once, otherwise the circle is centred on the point where you let go. Anchor up ends the watch; the state survives an app restart.

Depth alarm: the same sheet holds the depth alarm for the NMEA depth sounder. The suggestion is 2.0 m; draught + 0.5 m makes sense. Only three readings below it trigger sound and notification, so that a single false reading doesn’t wake you.

Every reading counts, even if the depth stays the same — so the alarm also comes in calm water with a steady display. If you start or end the demo, a standing depth alarm ends — it applied to the other data source. Switching it off ends it at once, not only with the next reading.

Without soundings the alarm ends after one minute. If you disconnect the NMEA connection or reconnect (also via Connect NMEA in the instrument dashboard), a standing depth alarm doesn’t end at once but one minute after the last reading — likewise if the connection drops by itself (Wi-Fi gone). If the depth comes back and it is still shallow, it reports again after three readings, with sound and notification. If it comes back within the minute and it is still shallow, it keeps standing without a new report. Up to Build 35 it stayed with no time limit and didn’t report a second time when the depth returned.

Wind alarm: the same sheet holds two switches, each of which also works on its own. Shifts alarms when the wind has shifted by the threshold compared with its direction when anchored (default 60°, 15° steps), Increase when it has picked up compared with the wind speed when anchored (default +10 kn, 2 kn steps). The reference is the first 3-minute mean after setting; if the wind only arrives later, the mean from then, and the line in the sheet gives the time. It only triggers once the 3-minute mean holds the threshold for two assessments in a row — a gust wakes nobody. Then “SHIFT 75°” or “WIND 27 kn” appears in orange on banner, tiles and screen, with sound and notification; Reset reference acknowledges (6 s undo), and the wind now then becomes the new reference. Without a wind sensor only the shift alarm watches, from the boat’s position relative to the anchor (GPS, reference after 10 minutes, from 15 m distance): in a current this shows the stream, not the wind. In demo mode the boat lies still at the set anchor, the wind shifts after just over a minute, the alarm comes in fast motion after around three and a half minutes, and after just over six minutes the anchor breaks out.

Shallow water is visible. If the depth alarm is standing while at anchor, the anchor banner turns red and says SHALLOW WATER with the last sounding and the threshold (“1.8 m · alarm below 2.0 m”); the anchor tile in the logbook and the Anchoring tile under Tools turn red as well, and the map’s anchor chip says “Shallow water”. If the anchor drags at the same time, ANCHOR DRAGGING takes precedence. On the anchor screen and at the top of the anchor sheet the Depth alarm off button then appears — on the screen with six seconds of “Undo”, in the sheet likewise. If the depth alarm is on and no sounding comes for a minute, the depth line in the sheet says “No depth from the onboard network — the depth alarm is not watching”, and the screen shows “no depth” — without sound. If the soundings stop while SHALLOW WATER is showing, the alarm ends after one minute without soundings; the banner then says “No depth — the depth alarm is not watching”, the tiles “No depth”, until soundings resume — also without sound. The depth alarm only watches while the anchor is down: Anchor up switches it off too, and in harbour it doesn’t go off. To try it in the demo (Pro): set the anchor and set the radius to 500 m (the demo boat keeps sailing, otherwise the anchor drags straight away), switch on the depth alarm in the sheet and set the threshold above the depth shown below it under “Current” — after three soundings the alarm comes.

With the app open, it always sounds. If the iPhone is lying on the table with the app open, Etmar plays its own alarm sound, which ignores the silent switch and needs no permission for critical alerts — and, provided notifications are allowed, shows the notification as a banner over the app. The sound plays until you acknowledge (anchor up, widen the circle or the boat is back inside the circle), for at most 30 seconds per round; as long as the boat stays outside, the alarm reports again every 60 seconds. The depth alarm sounds the same way, for at most 30 seconds, and ends as soon as the depth sounder again measures more than half a metre above the threshold (so at 2.0 m only above 2.5 m) or you switch it off — and one minute after the last reading, if none come any more.

When notifications are off. If notifications and critical alerts are both switched off for Etmar in the iOS settings, the alarm does not reach the locked phone — no banner, no alarm sound. The sheet then says so above “Set anchor to current position” in a warning line with a crossed-out bell, below it a large Open settings button that leads straight to Etmar’s notifications; the same note appears at the depth alarm. You can still set the anchor — with the app open it keeps watching. Critical alerts alone are enough: iOS delivers them even when the other notifications are off. When you come back from the settings, the warning line disappears as soon as something gets through again.

Mostly the anchor goes down without the sheet – via the tile in the logbook or “Set anchor” in the map’s Layers menu. Then the hint on setting says so (“Anchor set (50 m) – no alarm when phone is locked”), and the anchor banner in logbook and map carries, for as long as the anchor is down, a line with a crossed-out bell — as does the status area of the anchor screen (“No alarm when phone is locked”). If you create a race from the calendar while notifications are off, a hint says the same for the pre-start and starting gun: they then only alert you with the app open. In addition, the undo hints in the logbook at the pontoon now sit above the bar with “Cast off” and “Anchor” – before, they were behind it and couldn’t be seen.

Anchoring from Tools (currently unavailable)

At anchor you don’t want to operate the map, you want to know at a glance whether the anchor is holding. That’s why the anchor has its own screen: Tools → Safety → Anchoring — one number, one state, one button, free like the whole anchor alarm. Even the tile below SOS shows the state of the watch: neutral without an anchor, green edge with “Anchor set · 23 / 50 m”, orange area “WATCH BLIND” (at night black with an orange edge), red area “ANCHOR DRAGGING · 67 / 50 m”. The number only appears on the tile when it is a current measurement: if the watch is blind or the anchor is dragging after an old fix, only the word is shown there.

At the top the status area, like the banner, shows one large number: the measured distance to the anchor in metres, next to it, smaller, the radius. Until the first reading after setting it shows “—”. If the boat lies beyond the radius but still within the GPS tolerance — radius plus half the accuracy, the same limit at which the alarm decides —, the line “Within GPS margin — alarm from 52.5 m” appears below the number in the area’s type (at a 50 m radius and ± 5 m): the watch is working, “Anchor set” stays, and only beyond this limit does the anchor drag. Below the radius nothing appears there. Without an anchor it shows “—” and next to it “Preset 50 m” — the radius with which the next anchor will be set. Below that, a large button: Set anchor (the same route as logbook and map, without a GPS fix with the same hint) or, when the anchor is down, Anchor up — at once, without confirmation; for six seconds Undo brings the watch back. −10 m / +10 m change the radius at once with an anchor down, and without one the preset (10 to 500 m), which then also applies when setting from logbook and map. Anchor to current position (only with an anchor and a GPS fix) and Show on the map follow; if “Precise Location” is off, the first is dimmed, and a tap shows, instead of moving it, the bar ““Precise Location” off — anchor not moved” with the way into the settings; if the anchor is dragging, All good — widen circle appears above the button: the circle grows to the present distance plus 10 m, and for six seconds Undo restores the old circle — if the boat is still outside, the alarm reports again at once. With the anchor set, the Depth alarm row leads to the anchor sheet; if the anchor is dragging, it is missing — in an alarm what counts is acting, and the depth alarm itself keeps running. With standard text size the screen fits even the smallest iPhone without scrolling; with very large text it may scroll a little there, especially without an anchor, when “Before anchoring” sits below. Number, state and main button are always at the top.

Before anchoring. Without an anchor, the bottom shows whether notifications, critical alerts and location “Always” are allowed — tick, cross or an empty circle if iOS has never asked. Set up now asks the open iOS questions one after another, ideally while still ashore; if something is still missing afterwards, Open settings leads to the right page. The button only asks for location if it has never been answered — if it is set to “While Using the App”, you go through the settings. Merely opening the screen asks nothing. In demo mode the position comes from the simulator; the demo doesn’t preset an anchor, because the demo boat is sailing — you see “Anchor set” with the demo switched off and a real anchor.

SOS — radio procedure cards

The SOS page is the red tile at the top of Tools → SOS. It transmits nothing — it guides you word for word through the radio distress call, so that you don’t forget anything under stress. At the top is a scrollable row of chips with direct access to all eight cards: MAYDAY, PAN PAN and SÉCURITÉ first and in colour, followed by MAYDAY Acknowledgement, RELAY, SILENCE, SILENCE FINI and DSC false alert. Below that are the current GPS position — continuously updated and copyable — and the pre-written radio call, into which boat name, call sign and MMSI are inserted automatically from the boat data.

  • Situation: you choose the type of distress (flooding, fire, man overboard …) and persons on board in the form; both end up in the text of the radio call.
  • From the MOB banner: via “Radio” in the banner you come straight here, MAYDAY and “Man overboard” are preselected, and the position banner shows the MOB position with the addition “Spot of the alarm” instead of your own. With an approximate position the radio call says “my position is 54°22.27'N 010°09.20'E, position approximate”: the coordinates first, the caveat after. The position display and copy symbol keep the bare coordinates for DSC and plotter. With a provisional MOB point too (see MOB above) the radio call says “position approximate”; the cards show no warning area for it, the line about it is in the MOB banner. The cards now also show the warning area for the provisional and the approximate point — “Position approximate — fix 2 min before the tap” or “… after the tap” instead of “Spot of the alarm” —, and for a delayed press on the watch they give the age of the fix: “Position approximate — fix 5 min ago, pressed 6 min ago”.
  • Channel notes sit right by the card: channel 16 / 156.800 MHz, beforehand a DSC alert on channel 70.
  • PDF export via Share at the top right: A4, page by page — a cover sheet with vessel name, call sign, MMSI and persons on board in large monospace, then one page per card with a footer (boat, call sign/MMSI). Meant for laminating and hanging by the radio — paper needs no battery.
  • Boat data: you enter call sign and MMSI under Tools → Boat in the “Radio / SOS” section directly below the basic data, so that they appear in the radio call and in the PDF.
  • Without a boat created the cards are still there — from Tools as well as via “Radio” in the MOB banner. Boat name, call sign and MMSI then appear in the radio call as “---”, the position as always; above it an orange bar says “Boat name and call sign missing”, and a tap on it (“Add boat”) opens the form for a new boat. You then say the name from memory. The share symbol for the PDF only appears with a boat: a laminated sheet with dashes would stay hanging by the radio, even long after the boat has been created.
  • The red filter of night mode is deliberately off on this page — maximum readability under stress takes precedence over night vision here.
Test
  1. Anchor alarm at the pontoon: set the anchor, lock the iPhone and walk well over 50 m away with the phone in your pocket. Does the notification appear on the lock screen, does it repeat about every minute, and is it quiet after “All good — widen circle”?
  2. MOB with wet fingers or gloves: does holding trigger it reliably, and does a short tap stay without consequence?
  3. SOS PDF: are boat name, call sign and MMSI correct on the cover sheet and in every footer, and is each card on its own page?
↑ Back to contents

C14Workshop

The Workshop (called “Winter storage” until Build 19) organises work on the boat as a list of projects — winterising, refit, repair — with checklists, material, photos, dates and the weekly overview, so that nothing is forgotten in spring.

Projects and templates

You’ll find the module under Tools → Workshop. Each project is a checklist with items, photos and notes — a refit or winterising, for example. What runs the same way every year you create once as a template (such as “Winterising engine” or “Underwater hull”) and reuse it every season instead of typing the list again.

Several projects side by side Pro: one open project at a time is free, with everything this article describes; completed ones don’t count. With Pro you run as many open projects per boat as you like side by side — rig next to engine next to winter storage. If one is already open without Pro, “+” (also from a template) shows the Pro line instead of the fields, and below it the open project with a link to it; “Copy to another boat” only blocks boats that already have one open. What is open stays editable, including several from before, and “Send to Workshop” from maintenance still lands in the open project.

The start page shows the winter at a glance. At the top a header card with a photo from an open project (otherwise the boat photo, otherwise the Workshop colour), below it three tiles: how many tasks of the open projects are done (with a ring), how many days remain until the next Workshop date, and what the winter has cost so far — spent across all projects of the season, open and completed alike (July to June, the same figure as in the boat’s statistics; the tile names the season, such as “spent 2026/27”), below it the sum of the budgets, if any have been entered. Then two large handles for the shed and the DIY store: This week with the number of tasks and anything overdue in black on orange with a clock, and the Shopping list with items and planned total. The open projects are listed as cards below; tapping opens the project, swiping left or a long press deletes it after confirmation. Completed projects are listed per season with their balance: tasks, money spent and photos (both only if there are any) and the date. Without a project the page stays, and the header card says where to start. On the iPad the cards are in two columns; deleting there is via the long press.

Below the handles is the Maintenance row, such as “Maintenance · 1 due · 1 soon” — the state of this boat’s equipment, the same count as at the top of the equipment list; a tap opens the list. If nothing is due, it says “Maintenance · nothing due”; without equipment the row stays and says “Create equipment — under Boat”. A part comes from there, with “Send to Workshop”, as a task into a project (see C10).

Each task can have a due date (“Edit” in the task); from the day after, the task shows in orange as overdue — red is reserved for the MOB. The tree shows the date small next to the title; if the title doesn’t fit on one line beside it, date, trolley and photos go below, and the title keeps the full width. You create dates such as crane, mast crane or boatyard at the top of the project; they also appear in the calendar (in the Workshop colour) and move to the iOS calendar with a swipe to the right. The evening before, the app reminds you of the open tasks.

The project header shows the first project photo at the top (otherwise the Workshop colour with the symbol of the first category), below it a ring with “4 of 15 – tasks done” and the timeline: a band from the earliest to the latest day — start, end, the dates and today (for a completed project only while today still lies between its first and last day; the band then doesn’t extend beyond them). Dates appear as flags above the band, and tapping a flag opens the date; start, end and today are below it, and the past part is filled in. If markers are close together, the labels move out to the other side or one row further. With very large text — or if the labels still don’t fit side by side, say with many dates close together — the markers are shown as a list, with the gap in days. Without any date an empty band remains and says where period and dates are set. There is no separate “Period” section — you set start and end via “Edit”. A completed project shows the same picture, below it the completion sentence and View album. On the iPad and in landscape on the large iPhones (Plus, Pro Max) ring and number sit to the right of the photo; in the iPad’s narrow Split View and with very large text they sit below it, as on the iPhone.

This week — at the top of the Workshop list and in the project — collects, across all of the boat’s open projects, what is due by Sunday, plus everything you have marked by hand via “For this weekend”: grouped by project, with its material under each task (the trolley means still to buy, the tick means packed). Finish week clears the hand mark from all completed tasks; tasks with a date disappear on their own with Sunday. Reminders, at most three per week: Friday at 5 pm, what is coming up at the weekend and what still needs buying; the evening before a date, what is still open; once on the morning after a missed due date — can be switched off under Settings → Notifications. Photos exist on the project (craning, winter cover) and on every task (before/after).

Amounts and shopping list. An amount on material is optional (planned); Bought asks for the actual amount, pre-filled with the planned one — one tap if it’s right. The project shows the costs as a bar: spent solid, planned faint behind it, the budget from “Edit” as a vertical marker, next to it the amounts and what is still available; over budget the bar turns orange, and the budget line carries a warning triangle. Below that, per category, is what has been spent and planned — material counts towards the category of its top-level task, and “No category” comes last. The boat’s statistics state what the winter has cost. The Shopping list at the top of the Workshop list collects, across all open projects, what still needs buying — grouped by project, with amounts; tapping means bought, and Share sends it as text to whoever does the shopping. No conversion, no tax — the receipt is the truth, the app the overview.

Maintenance, completion and history. A task can concern a piece of equipment (“Edit” → Concerns). If you tick it off and the part has a maintenance interval, the app asks once whether the maintenance should be set to today — undo via the toast. The yes also writes an entry into the part’s maintenance log (C10). When creating from winterising or recommissioning, the Workshop suggests due and soon-due maintenance as tasks; that is the only place where it suggests anything by itself. Finish project under Planning takes a project out of “This week” and the reminders; it moves under “Finished · winter 2025/26” and stays readable — photos and completed tasks are the history. For projects from before whose tasks are all done, the app asks once on first opening whether they should be completed.

Who does it, and the work list for the yard. In “Edit” you choose Myself, Boatyard or Helper for each task, plus a name (“Müller Boatyard”, “Jens”). Myself is the default and isn’t marked anywhere; the tree shows Boatyard and Helper as a small icon in front of the date and trolley, and the segmented control above the list has a fourth view, “Boatyard”, with only the yard tasks. Yard tasks have no shopping/packing flow — the yard sources their material, and it goes on the work list instead of the shopping list. Share work list under Planning turns the tasks of one assignee into a PDF: a checkbox per task, due date, note, sub-tasks, material and the photos, plus boat name and date — via the share sheet, wherever you like. For every tier, because there is no gate on the way out.

In demo mode the project “Refit winter 2026/27” is ready with four categories, sub-tasks, material, a yard task with a photo and a helper task — so you can try everything below straight away. The task “Replace sacrificial anode” has a before and an after photo there, which brings the project’s album to four photos.

Categories: progress and collapsing

A refit quickly has forty items. As one long list that would be a wall of checkboxes, so the checklist is divided into categories — “Preparation”, “Hull”, “Rig”, “Engine”. Each category shows its progress in the header, for example “5/5”. In front of the name is an icon the app picks from the name — waves for Hull, a sailing boat for Rig, an engine block for Engine, a hammer if nothing fits. If it doesn’t fit, rename the category (menu with the three dots → Rename).

  • A tap on the header collapses the category. Whatever isn’t due in February is then one row instead of twelve.
  • Items with sub-items collapse the same way and then show how much of their branch is done.
  • Both states are saved — planning runs over weeks, and you shouldn’t have to collapse the list again every time you open it.

Adding tasks quickly. At the end of each category is the row New task: type the title, Return — the task is in the category, and the field is free again for the next one. That way you can type out a forty-item refit in one go; Return on an empty field closes the keyboard. Date, assignee, part and notes are available via the button with the three dots on the right of this row: it opens the form with the title you’ve already typed. To edit, use “Edit” in the task.

Changing the order

You change the order with a long press on an item or a category and then Move up / Move down. Items move among their siblings, categories among each other. There is deliberately no dragging: in a nested tree you rarely hit the right spot on a phone — let alone in a cold shed with numb fingers.

Task and material

Tasks and material are kept separate. In the checklist you plan the work: “Underwater hull”, below it “Pressure-wash the fouling”, “Sand the antifouling”. What you need for it, you enter in the task itself — a tap on the title opens it. That’s where the material list is, and each entry has only two states: need to buy or have it. If the tin of antifouling stood as an equal item between the work steps, the list would no longer tell you what needs doing.

The task also holds the sub-tasks and the photos — before/after, damaged areas. Next winter nobody remembers the details, but the photo does.

Back in the task list, a trolley counter on the row shows how much material for this task still has to be bought.

Album and before/after

Below the photos on the project is the row All photos with the number after it, in the demo “All photos (4)” — it counts the photos on the project and on all tasks. It opens the project’s album: first the photos on the project, then per category — with its icon — the task photos in checklist order, and finally tasks without a category. Below each task photo is the task it belongs to in grey, with its caption above that if it has one — those are the demo’s sample photos; your own photos have none. Below a photo on the project is only its caption, so below one of your own there is nothing. A tap shows the photo large, with caption and task at the bottom where they exist; for task photos, a long press → Go to task opens the task. On the iPhone there are three pictures in a row, on the iPad and in landscape on the large iPhones (Plus, Pro Max) five, in the narrow Split View on the iPad three again; with very large text one fewer. Without photos the album tells you where to add some. A finished project has its album too: View album sits directly below the note “Finished on …” — the winter in pictures.

Before / after. If a task has at least two photos, Compare before / after appears below its pictures. The view shows the first photo as “Before” and the last as “After” — on the iPhone in portrait one above the other, in landscape and on the iPad side by side, each whole and not cropped, with a caption (if the photo has one) below. Which photo is before depends on the order in which you added them; they can’t be reordered, and the footer of the view states this rule. So: take the before photo when you start, the after photo at the end.

Shopping and packing

Two more lists come out of the material with no extra work. You reach them via the segmented control above the checklist:

  • Shopping — the shopping list for the chandlery: everything that still has to be bought.
  • Packing — the list for packing at home: everything you already have and need to take to the boat.

Both views show all of the project’s entries in a flat list, each with the task it belongs to below it, and can be ticked off. Three views, because they belong in three places: the tasks at the boat, the list in the shop, the packing list in the garage.

ImportantDeleting asks first and names the consequences — for example “will be deleted with 4 subtasks”. With a category the whole branch hangs on it: all the tasks and sub-tasks in it. Read the confirmation before you confirm.
TestIn the demo project, collapse a category and an item with sub-items, leave the module and come back: are both states still as you left them? In a task, enter material as “need to buy” — is the trolley counter on the row right, and does the entry appear under “Shopping” with the correct task below it? And: can you change the order reliably with a long press even with gloves or cold fingers? Type three tasks in a row via “New task”: does the keyboard stay open between two Returns, and does each task end up in the right category? Can you hit the ticks and arrows in the tree with gloves too? On the start page, do progress, days until the date and costs match what’s in the projects? Can you hit the “This week” and “Shopping list” handles with gloves too? Take two photos on a task, first before, then after: does “Compare before / after” appear, and is the first photo under “Before”? Is the number in “All photos” right, and can you find every photo in the album under the right category?
↑ Back to contents

C15Sailing calendar

The calendar collects all sailing-related events in one place — and on race day it creates the race, course included, from a regatta event with one tap.

You’ll find it under Tools → Calendar. It is meant for planning ashore: what’s next, how full the month is, which course is sailed at the Wednesday race. On board you only need it for a single tap — the Start button.

If there is an event in the next seven days, the “Planning” context panel at the very bottom shows it too: “Next event: title · day”, followed by the time — so you can see whether an event today has already begun. A tap opens the event as it does here in the calendar. An event today that has begun stays there, as it does here under “Upcoming”, until midnight, even if its start was moved – unless another one follows today, in which case the line shows that one. With no event in the next seven days it says “No event in 7 days — open calendar”, and a tap brings you here. The same line also appears in the logbook as long as no voyage is open – under “Before departure” (C1).

Event types and series

There are four event types: Regatta, Training, Voyage and Other. You create a new event via the plus at the top right. Any event can also be created as a weekly series, for example “every Wednesday” for the club race — then each week has its own event in the calendar, which you can edit individually or all together (see below). If you aren’t sailing on your own boat, mark the event as Crewing on another boat.

List or month

At the top, a segmented control switches between two views; your choice stays until you change it.

  • List answers “What’s next?”: the Upcoming section shows the events ahead, and below it are the last ten past ones.
  • Month answers “How full is May?”: below each day are up to three dots in the colour of the event type — Regatta turquoise, Training indigo, Voyage blue, Workshop light blue, Other grey; red is reserved for danger. The selected day is filled, today is outlined.

A tap on a day in the grid shows its events below, with the same swipe actions as in the list. The arrows next to the month name page through the months; the day selection moves along with them so that the grid and the day list always match. A tap on the month name jumps back to today.

Regatta event: assigning a course template

For regatta events the form always contains the row Course template. Here you choose one of the courses you saved under Tools → Courses & waypoints. If you haven’t created a course yet, it says “none saved” with a note on where templates come from; the row stays visible so that you don’t have to look for the assignment later.

Starting on race day

On the day of a regatta event, a Start button appears on it. A tap on it — after a short confirmation — creates the race with the stored course template. The course is entered automatically — marks, lines and time rules come with it — and the times are tracked during the race. Countdown and analysis work as for any race (see C5 and C7).

If you create the race instead via Cast off → Race … on the map (only with focus “Regatta”) or via the menu (⋯) in the logbook, the calendar still helps: if there is a calendar event with a course today, the course row in the “New race” form is already filled in.

Editing a series all at once

If something changes in a weekly series — the start time moves half an hour earlier, the club sails a different course from now on — you don’t have to touch each event individually. Open an event in the series, change it and choose Apply to future events in the series. This applies name, time, boat, course and notes to all upcoming events in the series; anything already past stays unchanged.

Export to the iOS Calendar

You can export any event to the iOS Calendar with a swipe — in the list as well as in the day list below the month grid. That way it sits next to your other appointments and you see clashes straight away. The second swipe action is Delete.

TestCreate a weekly series (e.g. a Wednesday race), assign a course template to it and then change the time with “Apply to future events in the series”: are all future events right, and are past ones left untouched? On race day, tap “Start”: is the course entered in the race, and is the confirmation easy to understand? Switch between List and Month and page through the months: do the grid and the day list match, and is the chosen view still there after restarting the app?
↑ Back to contents

C16Settings & data backup

What you set once ashore — units, night mode, colours, focus — and how your data is backed up, exported and synced between your devices.

Layout: a list of sub-pages

New since 09/2026: Data & privacy. The place where you switch off whatever can leave the device. Three switches; the first launch asks about all three as well:

  • iCloud backup. On: voyages, trim and boat are also stored in your private iCloud and sync between your devices. They go to nobody else — Etmar has no server and doesn’t see them. Off: they stay on this device. Switching off does not delete anything already uploaded; it says so there too, with the way to get there (System Settings → Apple ID → iCloud → Manage Storage). The change takes effect the next time the app starts. “Off” means off. Up to Build 33 the app set up iCloud sync even with “Off”, because the system default is “mirror” — on a device signed in to an iCloud account, voyages, trim and boat could go to your private iCloud despite “No”. If that affects you and you don’t want it, delete the data there once by hand (path above); the app won’t create it again. If the backup is off, the departure checklist under “Planning” shows the line “iCloud backup off” – as a note with an “i”, not as a warning, and not in the logbook header “Before departure”; a tap brings you here.
  • Load map online. Why this is a privacy question: a tile request contains the area you are looking at — so it tells the map service where you are. The same applies to seamarks and harbour data. On: for now the switch only brings the map tiles of the visible area; the app fetches seamarks, harbours and depths only for areas you download. Off: only what you have downloaded under Settings → Map → Offline maps is shown; if an area is missing, it stays empty instead of quietly loading, and the chip on the map says “Offline maps only” instead of “Offline” — so that you look for the switch, not for a dead spot. Downloading an area still works: the rule is “not on the side”, not “never”.
  • Wind from open sources. On (default; the first launch asks with the cruising area, with “on” as the suggestion): without a wind sensor the app fetches “Wind now”, the wind suggestion and, after the session, the hourly wind from DWD, DMI, SMHI or NOAA — only the station ID or grid cell and the hour are sent, never your position, plus, as with any request, your IP address. Off: only the onboard network or manual wind applies.

No switch: place names. For the voyage name when casting off, the berth in the logbook, destinations, waypoints and tapped map points, the app asks Apple for the place name — with that one position. When casting off and for the berth, where you don’t tap anything, instead of your position only the centre of a grid cell of about 2 km goes to Apple, i.e. a position rounded to about 1 km. Exactly what goes where is set out in full in the privacy policy.

For new installations, iCloud backup is off and “Load map online” is on — the first launch asks about both, plus “Wind from open sources”, suggested on; for existing ones everything stays as it was — whoever syncs today keeps syncing. A third section, “Community”, is already there, but empty: if features are ever added for which data leaves the device, you’ll be asked there — individually for each purpose and with the option to switch it off.

You reach the settings via Tools → Settings — the gear tile in the “App” section, right next to Help & demo. They are a list of sub-pages, each fitting on one screen: Tier & demo · Profile · Display · Units · Map · Regatta · Notifications · NMEA connection · Data backup · Data & privacy, plus Help (Tutorials, “What’s new”, Tracking diagnostics, Feedback) and About & legal. The “Tier & demo” row shows the current state on the right: Free, Pro or “Demo running”.

Profile (Settings → Profile, directly below “Tier & demo”): here you choose what you use Etmar for — Racing & training, Cruising, Workshop; out of the box everything is on, and the row’s subtitle names what is selected. Each switch says in one sentence what the area covers and what goes away when it’s switched off. An area switched off loses its entry points for creating new things — so without “Racing & training” the Regatta sub-page also disappears from this list. What already exists stays where it is, a running race stays visible, and nothing is deleted; that’s why switching off doesn’t ask for confirmation. Always there are logbook, map, safety, boat, trim, instruments, harbour guide, calendar and analyses. The profile applies only on this device and is not a tier: it hides, it doesn’t lock. The demo always shows everything; afterwards your profile applies again. If you’re starting out, you choose the profile during onboarding, right after the boat (A3).

The section Set elsewhere names what deliberately isn’t in this list and links there without duplicating the setting: trim fields belong to the boat, instrument pages and smart fields to the instruments, the individual map layers to the map’s lighthouse menu. “All layers on/off”, on the other hand, is under Settings → Map itself.

Focus (Settings → Display): Automatic · Regatta · Voyage. If you use the app, for a period or in general, only for races or only for voyages, this highlights what fits. In Tools, with Regatta “Courses & waypoints” gets a border, with Voyage “Routes” — the tiles themselves always stay in their place. With Regatta, the green Cast off button on the map first asks “Race …” or “Cast off”; otherwise it casts off with one tap. Nothing disappears. If a race or a voyage is actually running, the app always follows the situation, whatever is set.

The onboarding at first launch (“Your data”, then boat, profile, sails, location, cruising area — with “Continue from iCloud” only location and cruising area) and the location permission are described in A3. In short: the location question comes exactly once, during onboarding ashore; if you chose “Later”, you can catch up on the map via the location button or the blue “Allow location” chip.

Display, units, map

Units (Settings → Units): distance nm/km, speed kn/km/h/m/s, depth m/ft, water temperature °C/°F. The choice applies in instruments, logbook row, Go-To, race key figures and reports. Wind always stays in knots, bearings true.

Night mode lays a red filter over the entire app to preserve your night vision. You switch it via the moon item in the menu on the map (lighthouse), logbook and instrument dashboard (⋯) — so directly from the live screen, without switching through bright tabs — or via the switch under Settings → Display. The from sunset automatic switches on according to the sun’s position at your location (civil twilight) and off again in the morning; a manual choice in between stays. The automatic is on from the start; if you have already flipped the switch once, your choice is kept. When it switches on automatically, a short note at the top names the automatic. On the SOS page the filter is deliberately suspended. It also rests while the photo picker is open — under the filter it wouldn’t accept any touch, not even “Cancel”. The brightness stays dimmed, and when you close it the red is back; the camera stays filtered. The same applies to the share sheet (every export, the backup, the shopping list) and the file picker (restoring a backup; importing GPX, a polar or a certificate). Buttons, links and switches change to a warm light red at night — the blue of the daytime version would be almost black under the filter. On the main map and the voyage map (and on the secondary maps, see below), your own layers also change to colours the filter doesn’t swallow: boat and COG vector, the course line to the Go-To target and the legs of a route in light red, the single-colour track in light orange, AIS targets of both classes light grey (their names amber on black, for the dangerous target black on red; an outdated target dashed in light red instead of grey), anchor, anchor circle as well as start line, finish line and gate in amber — the course lines also in the course editor and on the drawing map of “Redraw track”. Waypoint number and anchor arrow are light on a black disc. Under the filter everything is red, and shape tells things apart; at night the boat has a black edge. COG vector and course line are both red straight lines from the boat: the vector ends with a crossbar after the set time (default six minutes), the course line at the crosshair.
