The course line is dashed, by day and by night, the vector solid – so the shape tells the two apart along the whole length, not just at the end, and at the mark also the course line from the laylines. With a compass, the boat arrow points along the heading; the thinner heading line then continues it, reaching beyond the crossbar, and the vector with the crossbar branches off from it. If the two lie almost on top of each other, the Go-To row shows the deviation (◀/▶) as a number — during a voyage only if there is room next to the target name and arrival time, otherwise after a tap in the expanded tile. The round map buttons stay readable too — icon in light red on a slightly lighter disc, the entry and Cast off buttons light red with a black icon — and on the red alarm areas (MOB button and banner, “Anchor dragging”, CPA warning) the text is black at night, and the buttons in them become black capsules with red text. The green race time in the race bar runs in amber at night. The orange hint chips (Low Power Mode or battery low, location denied, offline without a pack) have black text by day and night. Anything filled in light red also has black text at night: the Quick Log and Sails tiles in the logbook, the Go-To row and the Go-To banner, the “Next mark” chip, the undo strip and filled buttons such as “Go-To” or “Arrived?”. Only the course error above 5° reverses this: warning triangle, ◀/▶ and degrees appear in the Go-To row and in the expanded tile in amber on a black capsule, so under the filter red on black in the otherwise black-on-red row. Likewise the dash over bearing and distance, when the fix is older than two minutes, is black instead of orange at night. The preview maps in the analysis and in sharing also draw line and points in light red instead of blue at night. The night colours also apply on the secondary maps: the track editor and the logbook carousel draw the track in light orange, the start and finish lines in the carousel preview and the arrival circle of a destination (search, detail, POI map) are amber; split tracks without tack colouring and the way home after the finish take the same light orange. In the pre-start panel and in the race bar, pinged line ends are amber, open ends and “Sync” light grey with black text. Pinged line ends are filled and carry the tick, open ones are only outlined — by day and by night, because under the filter amber and light grey are the same shade; “Sync” stays filled light grey. The label “Fix n min ago” at the boat and the “All good — widen circle” button are black on orange, the storage error notice and the SOS tile in Tools black on red. This also applies to the onboarding, for example when you end a demo started from it at night and land back in the onboarding: dark background, buttons, icons and progress bar in light red, the large “Next” button light red with black text as soon as a boat name has been entered; without a name it is disabled. Disabled light-red main buttons – such as “Next” without a boat name – carry dimmed light-red text on their dark surface at night. Green confirmations – ticks, “Position recorded”, “Auto-Tracking active”, the connected NMEA chip – and the “Cast off” tile are amber at night, the tile with black text; green would be almost black under the filter. The same goes for the analyses: whatever says “better” in green by day – a gain in the waterfall, the best rounding, the end taken, “carries longer”, the better difference in the A/B comparison – is amber at night. Whether better or worse is shown there by sign and word or an arrow, because amber and orange are the same shade under the filter. The Live Activity on the Lock Screen and in the Dynamic Island also follows night mode: light red and amber on black, no white area. There is no red filter over the Lock Screen, so the colours themselves are warm. If the automatic switches while the app isn’t recording anything in the background, the Live Activity only follows the next time you open the app. iOS notifications stay bright.

Track colours: the tack colouring of the track shows port and starboard tack in two colours, motoring stretches in a third — which tack you were on, the app only knows with the wind sensor on the NMEA network. The Colour-safe palette replaces green/red for the two tacks with blue/orange and yellow for engine with grey; in addition, the port tack is drawn thicker — a second channel besides colour. Intended for red-green colour blindness (around 8 % of men) and for the red night filter, under which green, red and yellow merge. Legends and analysis follow the chosen palette.

Regatta (Settings → Regatta) contains a single number, the Clearance if no marks. It only applies in the exceptional case: normally the course you built decides for itself when a finish crossing may count (see C5) – you set nothing for that. If the course has no course marks at all, there is nothing to compare; then the finish counts once you have been that far from the finish line at some point since the starting gun (100 to 2000 m, default 1000 m).

Two more defaults concern the map. Rounding colours: the colour of the arc around race course marks — default red = leave the mark to port, green = to starboard (navigation-light convention), reversible. Map opens with: the default main view of the voyage map, technical view or voyage story. If you switch in the map, that applies to this voyage and overrides the default.

Help where the question arises

Important screens have a ? in the toolbar that opens the matching tutorial topic directly as a sheet: the race analysis and each of its six chapters — each its own topic instead of one long one for all seven screens —, both course editors and the library under Tools → Courses & waypoints, Measured runs, Racing tally, Sailed polar, Sail crossover as well as dashboard and page manager. Empty states, for example under Analyses, offer “How it works — step by step”. You’ll find the full Tutorials under Settings → Help → Tutorials and under Tools → Help & demo: in-app guides to every feature, organised by area, some with interactive tours through the real interface.

How a topic is structured: first come the steps your tier has unlocked — in the order you need them, without any badge. What needs Pro is gathered after that in a collapsed group, for example “5 steps with Pro”, with the sentence saying where Pro gets its values from, and at the end of the group the way into the preview and to demo mode. A topic that is entirely Pro — such as “Race analysis: wind” — begins with a grey “Pro feature” card. If you have Pro, you don’t see a single badge; in the section lists, locked topics come last.

“What’s new” is under Settings → Help and under Tools → Help & demo next to the tutorials: for each release a few short sentences on what has changed, newest build at the top. Meant for the moment after an update when something looks different from your last voyage — the detailed version remains this page. After an update, the line “New in Build …” in the logbook at the pontoon also leads there until you tap it, for a week at most.

First-time hints: the first time you open a screen, a dark veil lies over it, the control in question is left clear and a sentence stands next to it — the long press on the map (harbour, anchorage, waypoint), the layers behind the lighthouse button, the tool in the course editor that stays active after the tap. A tap anywhere shows the next hint; after the last one the veil is gone (map two hints, course editor one). There is deliberately no close cross; it would end the whole series. During a voyage or a race nothing appears at all — a veil must never lie between you and the MOB button. Reset under Settings → Tutorials.

Pro overview: locked rows open it with the feature you came from at the very top: title, “With Pro” and, if the analysis needs a particular sensor, “For this feature: wind sensor on the bus.” Below that a card: “Logbook, map, safety and instruments are free. Pro is the analysis afterwards — and, on the onboard network, the sailing compass.” — what Pro calculates from GPS (race map, start summary, individual manoeuvres, form curve, run figures …) and what is added with wind from the onboard network (polar, wind & shifts, sail crossover …). At the bottom are both subscriptions with price, restore and the links to the privacy policy and terms of use, above them “Try in demo mode”. In the tutorials, the way at the end of the group is called “Unlock — or try it in demo mode”.

Send feedback (Settings → Help, also under Tools → Help & demo): choose the type (Error / Idea / Question), write, Next — the app opens a prepared email to the developer. The app has no server, hence the route via email; and nothing is sent silently: the text is in your mail app, and you send it yourself. Technical details (version, device, iOS, language, tier, demo on/off) are attached if you wish and are shown in plain text on screen beforehand — no position, no voyage or boat data. Without a mail app set up, the app offers to copy the address. Also under Help is Tracking diagnostics.

Data backup

Settings → Data backup begins with the iCloud status row: active, synced, error, no account or local only, plus the last sync. If you have switched iCloud off, that is exactly what it says under “Stored locally only”, without an error tone, with the link “Turn on iCloud backup”. With iCloud backup switched on, the app syncs all data between your devices via your private iCloud. That is convenience, not a backup — a mistake gets synced too. For emergencies there is the full backup as a file:

  1. Tap Save / share backup. The backup file (.etmarbackup) with all data including images is already prepared when you open the page, with its size next to it.
  2. Choose the destination: Files, iCloud Drive or AirDrop to another device.

Restore backup selects such a file, asks for explicit confirmation and replaces all data the next time the app starts; the previous state is set aside internally. The backup is deliberately a complete copy of the database instead of a separate exchange format: lossless, no server. It also takes the photos at full size. The dialog recognises a backup from an older version and says before restoring that it is missing large photos. A new backup only takes the photos that still occur in the app – those of a state displaced by a restore no longer travel with it. Prepared backups and exports older than a day are deleted by the app at launch.

Exports, one tap each: voyage as GPX (track plus entries as waypoints), harbour guide as GPX, regatta waypoints as GPX, trim diary as CSV (semicolon-separated, for Numbers or Excel — for crew and sailmaker). GPX import: waypoints end up in the harbour guide or in the regatta waypoints (duplicates are skipped), tracks become a new voyage with a departure and an arrival entry. Waypoints and track points without a valid position (such as “nan” or a latitude above 90°) are left out by the import; the message afterwards gives their number (“3 points without a valid position skipped”). In the trim CSV, no cell starts as a formula any more: before “=”, “+”, “-” or “@” at the start there is an apostrophe; plain numbers such as “-2” stay numbers.

Set-aside database: if the database can’t be loaded at launch for once (for example after an app downgrade), the app sets it aside instead of deleting it. A notice at launch says so; under Settings → Data backup → Set-aside versions you bring the state back with one tap. The current data is backed up in turn — the app deletes nothing on its own. The section also shows the state before each restore, each with date and size; Restore … works for both. Delete … removes a state permanently after a confirmation; large photos from it stay in the app’s storage but don’t go into any new backup.

ImportantiCloud is not a backup: whatever you accidentally delete or overwrite on one device is synced to all devices and is gone there too. Before trying out Restore backup and before every app update, save a backup (Files or iCloud Drive) — it is one tap and includes the images.

Change stamps and iCloud conflicts

Over iCloud, the last writer wins. If you edit the same entry on iPhone and iPad, you lose one version — and iCloud doesn’t report the conflict to the app. It can’t be undone, but it can be made visible: voyages, logbook entries, trim entries, courses, harbours, routes, instrument pages and winter projects remember when and on which device they were last changed. The voyage detail has the line “Last changed …”; if an open entry is overwritten from another device while you are editing it, an orange notice appears at the top. Derived data — track points, images, polar cells — deliberately carries no stamp.

NoteRule of thumb for two devices: only ever edit an entry on one device at a time, and give the other a moment to sync before you carry on there. If the orange notice appears, check the version before you save.

Demo mode and demo tier

Tools → Help & demo → Start demo. Demo mode simulates NMEA data and movement and creates sample data: the demo boat “Vertigo Express”, a voyage Kühlungsborn–Gedser with a motoring stretch and a radio call, an open day sail with the stage plan “Warnemünde or Poel?” (quick buttons, “Next stage” and stage destinations on the map included), the Wednesday race with full analysis, trim entries and calendar events. All Pro features can thus be fully tried out, even without instruments on board. A magenta “DEMO” capsule at the top centre shows the demo state; you end it with “Demo active” under Tools → Help & demo, and in landscape and on the iPad also with a tap on the capsule. Magenta in the app means “simulated” — orange is reserved for warnings. When you end it, all demo data is deleted.

Voyage or regatta. “Start demo” first asks what the demo should show — here, under Settings → Tier & demo and in the welcome (“Let me look around first — start the demo”). Voyage is the demo as above, with the simulator off Kühlungsborn. Regatta asks a further question: Pre-start (gun in 20 minutes) or Race running (start 40 minutes ago) — both with the NSV Wednesday race on the second demo boat “Nordwind”, which sails the course in real time (C5). In analyses and lists this race is called “NSV Wednesday race (demo)” — so you don’t confuse it with the analysed, genuinely recorded Wednesday race from the day before yesterday and the course from the library, which have the same name. The buttons span the full width; “Back” and “Cancel” are at the top, and it only starts with the last choice. Without a question, as a voyage, “View with sample data” from an empty area, “Try in demo mode” in the Pro preview and the NMEA settings start the demo. The item “Regatta demo” under Tier & demo now only exists while the regatta demo is running — to switch between pre-start and race running.

Demo tier: under “Demo active” you choose Pro or Free. The demo keeps simulating movement and sample data but behaves like the chosen tier — grouped tier rows, tier overview, no wind analysis; the capsule at the top shows “DEMO · FREE”. Instruments, tack colouring and laylines run in every demo tier with the simulated onboard network data. That way you see what remains without Pro: everything on board. The choice never locks below the tier you bought, only applies while the demo is running, and is kept across app restarts.

TestCreate a backup via Save / share backup and restore it on a second device via AirDrop: after the restart, is everything there — voyages, trim entries, images? The night mode automatic is on out of the box: does the red filter come on at your location at dusk, does a manual choice in between hold, and is it really off on the SOS page? With beta tier Free, set the demo tier to Free: are all Pro analyses locked, does the capsule at the top show “DEMO · FREE”, and is the choice kept after an app restart?

C17Analyses in detail

Every analysis in the app, in the order in which it appears under Tools → Analyses: what it shows, where the figures come from, how it calculates, what a sailor reads from it, where it goes wrong — and which tier it is in. The example figures come from demo mode (Tools → Help & demo → Start demo) and appear there on the card.

How the page is laid out — cards, Pro row, empty states — is described in C7. Here you’ll find, card by card, what lies behind each one, in the order of the page.

What all analyses have in common

Racing phase and official time. The race analysis calculates over the racing phase — from crossing the start line to the finish — not over the whole track. The official time runs from the starting gun. The difference is the start loss. That way neither the pre-start nor the trip home distorts distance or avg SOG.

What doesn’t count. Retraced sections and engine phases according to the logbook (“Engine on” to “Engine off”) don’t count towards distance, avg SOG or the performance values; they stay on the timeline. An “Engine on” without an “Engine off” turns the rest of the race into motoring — the analysis then says how much time that affected.

Where the tack comes from. The app detects tacks, gybes and boards from the tack of each track point. Measured, the logbook writes it while recording whenever a wind angle from the onboard network is available (true wind, otherwise apparent; as long as wind is coming in, the heel from the attitude sensor as a fallback). If the connection drops, the last wind remains valid for up to 30 minutes.

Without a wind sensor, the analysis estimates the tack afterwards from GPS — but only if no point of the race carries a tack or wind angle; measured and estimated are never mixed. For this it needs a wind direction: the manual wind on the start entry, otherwise the wind direction from the logbook, otherwise the bisector of your own upwind boards (per section between two marks), otherwise the bearing start line → first mark. If the session carries wind from open sources, the hourly direction of the station or model comes before the bearing, and pre-filled logbook directions no longer count. The bisector alone doesn’t say whether the boards were a beat or a zigzag downwind. That is decided first by the no-go zone (the reading in which the boat would sail noticeably longer closer than 25° to the wind loses), then by the hourly direction of the station or model if one reading lies no more than 60° from it, then likewise by the bearing to the first mark, and finally by the slower speed upwind — on a round-the-buoys course whose first mark isn’t a windward mark, the bearing alone can pick the wrong reading. The tack is then the windward side of the course over ground; a new tack applies after 30 s. A course over ground frozen for minutes is replaced by the bearing from the positions (at least two minutes of the same course at a speed of 1.5 kn or more, while the positions run more than 30° differently over at least 100 m). The estimate lives only in the analysis, never in the track or the raw data export, and every place that uses it says “From GPS: port or starboard estimated” with the wind source. Weaknesses: dead-downwind gybes are uncertain (±10° around 180° keep the tack), current shifts the course over ground, and costs are only available in seconds, not in metres — metres need the measured wind direction. From the same wind direction the board list estimates the point of sail per board, with an asterisk (see Manoeuvres). If manual wind is set, the analysis still calculates the bisector of the upwind boards — without it, the hourly direction of the station or model — averaged over the race. If it lies more than 30° from the manual wind, the footnote of the point of sail* adds its direction (“Your upwind boards give 272°.”, “The station’s hourly wind gives 280°.”, “The GFS weather model gives 258°.”). Tack and point of sail keep calculating with the manual wind — the figure is a prompt to check it, not a replacement.

Where the values come from. Every analysis whose figure reads values from the onboard network says so in a quiet line at the foot of its detail: measured “Onboard network: TWA · TWS” — the values this analysis reads —, without a sensor the substitute (“From GPS: port or starboard estimated (wind from tacking angles)”, “Wind from the logbook”, “Wind from station” / “Wind from model” (wind from open sources), “From GPS: avg SOG instead of polar performance”), mixed across several races with the count (“— in 1 of 6 races”). On the tile in the hub, a substitute instead of a sensor always appears in the footer, even ahead of the mini chart — an estimated figure without a caveat would be worse than no chart; measured sources appear where no chart needs the footer (the performance values carry only the substitute). The six summary tiles carry the coverage in their footer; their source is in the detail. If there is neither sensor nor substitute, the reason for the missing data appears. Tiles based on GPS and logbook alone carry no line; measured runs and the trim workbench only count entries on the tile and carry none there either. Without a log, the app calculates true wind from apparent wind and SOG — the current is then contained in TWA and TWS, and the line says so instead of naming an “STW” that didn’t exist: “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG), no log”. This applies to Speed & trim, Sailed polar, Tack asymmetry and Target angles, Target heel and Sail crossover; Wind in the race and Helm steadiness read no speed and only say “over ground (SOG)”. The basis comes from what was measured (majority of the upwind measuring time, of the race’s wind and speed samples, of the measuring time of the crossover matrix, or the cells of the polar), not from the catalogue.

Sections instead of single points. Pro The boat analyses (Helm steadiness, Target angles, Heel, Tack asymmetry, Sail crossover) don’t calculate over individual track points but over steady sections: same tack, same point of sail, at least 30 s in a race, 10 s in a measured run, 60 s on a voyage. A section ends at every point that doesn’t count — 20 s before and after a manoeuvre, when turning faster than 6° per second, below 1 kn of speed, under engine, retraced, or at a gap of more than 60 s. Point of sail here means: up to 75° true wind angle upwind, from 135° downwind, in between reaching. The log (STW) counts as speed if at least half of the points have it, otherwise SOG throughout — never mixed. All averages are time-weighted.

Which sessions count. Pro For the boat analyses, the sailed polar and the sail crossover, only sessions with your own boat count (never guest-boat voyages), whose “Include in analyses” switch is on, whose type the data basis allows (regatta, measured run, voyage — the row at the bottom of the page “Data quality”, the tile at the top of the hub) and which come after the polar cut-off date.

Wind bands. Pro Two grids, depending on the analysis: the polar’s support points (4 · 6 · 8 · 10 · 12 · 14 · 16 · 20 · 24 kn, a value belongs to the nearest) for the sailed polar, target angles, heel and helm steadiness; coarse bands (below 8 · 8–12 · 12–16 · 16–20 · above 20 kn) for manoeuvre costs, polar matrix, sail crossover and strengths profile.

Races

The race analyses show one regatta: from the hub, the most recent one with a track. At the top of the race analysis and in every race chapter opened from the hub, it says which one — name and date; a tap on it opens the list of this boat’s regattas with a track (newest first, with place), and your choice recalculates the whole page for that regatta. The same row appears in the hub above the race cards as soon as the boat has two regattas with a track — like the boat picker at the top. The choice applies to all race cards and the pages behind them, and vice versa: if you choose a different race in the overview or in a chapter, you’ll find it again in the hub. The default remains the most recent one; a different boat starts again with its most recent one.

Race

  • What it shows: How the race went — race duration, start loss, distance and scoring at a glance. The page behind it is the race analysis with all chapters.
  • Where from: GPS track of the regatta; starting gun and start line from the regatta; engine phases from the logbook; scoring and place from your input. With a log on the onboard network, the speed through the water at the gun. It shows the most recent regatta with a track — or the one you chose above the race cards; the card names date and name.
  • How: Key figures over the racing phase: race duration from the gun, racing phase from the line crossing, start loss (gun to crossing), distance in the race, avg SOG (only from one minute of scored time), max SOG, speed at the gun (nearest track point, no more than 90 s away). The card shows the race duration from the gun — without a gun the racing phase —, without a chart: the overview’s time-loss waterfall is rows of times, not a diagram. At the very top of the overview, below the race picker, the race sketch: the course as in the briefing (straight legs, north up, without wind), over it the track from the gun to the finish, thinned to at most 300 points, keeping the mark roundings. One leg is always selected — on opening, the one with the most extra distance (the same one the race card names), without a polar leg 1 —, its piece of track drawn wide, below it its row: point of sail · time · distance sailed, with Pro below that extra distance (without a polar “Detour”) · manoeuvres. These are the same values as in the leg list of the Course chapter, not a separate calculation. Tapping the line or ‹ › switches the leg; “Full screen” zooms. Without a course, only the track with start and finish.
  • Pro With Pro: At the top the race card — three sentences: what the start cost, the leg with the most extra distance, best and worst tack (with wind from the onboard network, the upwind performance against the polar instead). Below it the time-loss waterfall: the deficit against a reference (your own best race on the same course, with wind from the onboard network the polar time), broken down into start, manoeuvres, extra distance and “speed & the rest”; each item leads to its chapter. The same course means: the same course template or the same marks, each no more than 100 m from its counterpart; shortened, extended, abandoned and retired races don’t count. The reference race may have been sailed in different wind — the rest carries the difference. Against the polar time, extra distance counts the distance beyond what the point of sail requires; against a race, the distance you sailed more or less than there per leg, converted into time with your speed on the leg — the row is then called “+ extra distance”, and only the reference row above it carries the race’s name. A leg only counts if both races recorded it completely from mark to mark; otherwise its time is in the rest, and a sentence says so. Without a wind sensor, the figure doesn’t say whether wind or tactics made the difference. Against a race, start and manoeuvres are also the difference to there: “+ start” is your start delay minus the one there (both times below), “+ manoeuvres” your manoeuvre costs minus those there (both below, for example “20 × avg 7 s, 16 × avg 6 s there, from the speed dip”). The Course, Start and Manoeuvres chapters then show the values from there next to yours — per leg the distance there and the difference, the start delay there, the manoeuvres of both races —, with the race’s name once per chapter. Both sides are measured the same way: from the speed dip (the time lost from the drop in speed in the GPS) as soon as one of the two races lacks wind from the onboard network, from the VMG loss only if every manoeuvre of both races has it — the subline says which. A race’s manoeuvre list and polar waterfall keep their own method. Being over the line earlier or lower costs than there appear with a minus. If one of the two races lacks the start (no line, no crossing, course side open), it isn’t quantified, and a sentence gives the reason, for the reference race with its name; its time then counts towards the rest.
  • What you read from it: Race duration against racing phase separates the result from the boat speed: if you hang at the line for a minute after the gun, you see it as start loss and not as “sailed slowly”. On a short course, a minute is often more than the gap to the next place; on a long-distance race it weighs less.
  • Caveat: Everything over ground — current is contained in avg SOG and distance. The start loss needs a start line and a crossing in the track. A forgotten “Engine off” distorts distance and avg SOG; the analysis shows how much time under engine wasn’t scored. If the boundaries are wrong, you can correct them in the Start chapter — in every tier. Without wind from the onboard network, the point of sail in the sketch row is estimated from the track; it then carries an asterisk, and a quiet line below says “* estimated from the track”. The sketch doesn’t draw a scored extension: there the track runs beyond the course, and the extension’s legs have no number.
  • In demo mode: The card shows a race duration of “1:52:04” for the NSV Wednesday race — a race that was actually recorded.
  • Pro In demo mode with Pro: The sketch opens on leg 5 — the leg with the most extra distance, which the race card also names. The “Evening race without wind sensor” (race picker at the top) has no wind from the onboard network; its waterfall calculates against the Baltic Trophy, an earlier, faster race on the same course. The Wednesday race has wind from the onboard network and calculates against the polar.
  • Tier: partly free — key figures, analysis window and scoring free, as is the race sketch with point of sail, time and distance per leg; race card, time-loss waterfall and, in the sketch row, extra distance and manoeuvres Pro.

Start

  • What it shows: How the start went — were you clear before the gun, and when were you over the line?
  • Where from: GPS track, start line (pinged or from the course), starting gun; the zones from the course. Line (with Pro): “Onboard network: TWD” or “Wind direction entered by hand in the pre-start” for the line bias.
  • How: Free in the chapter are the analysis window — gun, line crossing and end, with correction of the boundaries — and the zones: were you in them, how long, how far. The card shows the start loss from the gun to the crossing. Distance at the gun and crossing also appear in every tier in the voyage details.
  • Pro With Pro: The start summary: distance to the line at the gun with a sign (on the course side = suspected premature start), first crossing to the course side and delay in seconds, where on the line (pin · middle · committee boat) and the speed build-up from 30 s before to 60 s after the gun. The course side comes from the first course mark, otherwise from the wind — if neither yields anything, the analysis makes no statement about the side. With wind (entered by hand in the pre-start or from the onboard network): line bias and whether the favoured end was taken. With a polar and wind from the onboard network: speed at the gun against the polar target and the deficit after two minutes against an ideal start. The card stays with the start loss and adds the reason below the figure, the distance to the line at the gun — “behind the line” or “over” only if the course side is established, otherwise “to the line” —, next to it the speed build-up as a chart. If the start loss is missing, the distance appears as a figure.
  • What you read from it: Whether the window is right, before you believe any figure — all key figures depend on it. After the race, the zones tell you whether you were in a restricted area before the gun, instead of reconstructing it from memory.
  • Caveat: GPS scatters by around 5 m — below that, “over the line” means nothing. A line drawn by hand is less accurate than a pinged one; pinging records the iPhone’s position, not the bow. Speed over ground includes current; with a log, the speed through the water counts. Bias and choice of end are only as good as the wind entered.
  • In demo mode: The card shows a start loss of “+0:12”.
  • Pro In demo mode with Pro: The card shows “+0:12”, below it “≈ 24 m to the line at the gun”.
  • Tier: partly free — window and zones free; start summary Pro, bias and choice of end Pro with wind entered by hand or from the onboard network, speed against polar target Pro with wind from the onboard network.

Course

  • What it shows: When each mark was rounded, how long each leg was, and whether it was rounded the right way.
  • Where from: GPS track and the regatta’s course (marks, gates, lines). With wind from the onboard network, line “Onboard network: TWA · TWS” (point of sail and polar reference of the legs).
  • How: Mark times from the track (closest approach and the side on which the mark was left, against the required side from the course), passing marks with side and distance — red if on the wrong side —, which mark of a gate was rounded, and per leg time, distance sailed, avg SOG and point of sail. The card shows the number of legs, without a chart (the chapter is a list). Without a wind sensor, the point of sail is estimated and marked with an asterisk (“Upwind*”, “Reaching*”, “Downwind*”) — by the same rule as in the board list (Manoeuvres chapter): course over ground against the estimated wind direction, averaged per point without side, up to 75° upwind, from 135° downwind, from 30 s; below that the same footnote with the source of the wind direction.
  • Pro With Pro: The leg summary with VMC (progress towards the mark), extra distance beyond what is needed (the zigzag of a beat at the polar’s tacking angle doesn’t count as a detour) and manoeuvres per leg; the rounding quality (dip, recovery, gap to the best rounding of the same mark) and the final approach; with logged sail changes the sail per leg, with wind from the onboard network the polar performance per leg.
  • What you read from it: Which leg was slow — and whether a mark was rounded the wrong way, before someone else notices.
  • Caveat: Without a course set up, the chapter is only a pointer to where to set one up. The mark times depend on the mark position in the course — a wrongly placed mark shifts rounding and legs. Everything over ground. The point of sail* of a leg is only as good as the course over ground it comes from: current sets it, and if the recording’s COG freezes, it reads the frozen course. It appears only in the row — extra distance, polar norm and leg times calculate without it.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “8 legs”. The “Evening race without wind sensor” (race picker at the top) shows the point of sail with an asterisk per leg, below it the footnote of the board list.
  • Tier: partly free — mark times, passing marks, gate choice and legs free; leg summary, rounding quality, final approach and sail per leg Pro.

Manoeuvres

  • What it shows: How many tacks and gybes, and the boards in between.
  • Where from: The tack at the track points — measured from wind or attitude data from the onboard network (line “Onboard network: TWA · AWA”), without a wind sensor estimated from GPS (line “From GPS: port or starboard estimated” with the wind source, see “Where the tack comes from”). The speed from the GPS track.
  • How: A board is a stretch on one tack between two changes of tack (or a mark); a manoeuvre is the change of tack between two boards. Whether it is a tack or a gybe is decided by the average wind angle 20 s before and after: both below 90° a tack, both above 90° a gybe, otherwise a change of course. Free are the number of tacks and gybes, the board list with tack, point of sail, duration and avg SOG, and the SOG trend. The point of sail comes, measured, from the true wind angle; without a wind sensor the list estimates it from the course over ground against the same wind direction from which the tack is estimated, and adds an asterisk (for example “Upwind*”) — with the same limits (up to 75° upwind, from 135° downwind, reaching in between, time-averaged, from 30 s per board; below that “–”). Averaged per point is the angle to the wind without its side: dead downwind, both sides of a gybe lie at around 175°, and both boards count as downwind. A footnote below the list names the source of the wind direction and the caveat.
  • Pro With Pro: Each manoeuvre individually with its costs from SOG — the reference is the speed over the 30 s before, the dip is the lowest value up to 60 s after, the recovery time lasts until 95 % of the speed is reached again (at most 180 s), the costs in seconds are the distance lost divided by the reference speed; only with dense recording (points no more than 15 s apart, in a race it is 5 s). In addition best, average and worst tack, the costs per wind band and the highlighting in the SOG trend. With wind from the onboard network, the loss in metres against the neighbouring boards — expectation and progress both over ground along the wind direction, so that current and a log error drop out of the difference — and the best and worst board per point of sail; the change of tack is dated to its beginning, not to the confirmation 30 seconds later with which the recording writes it.
  • What you read from it: How many manoeuvres the race needed — on a beat with wind shifts, that alone says something about the tactics.
  • Caveat: From GPS the tack is estimated: the wind direction comes from manual wind, logbook or your own upwind boards, and dead-downwind gybes are uncertain — chapter and analysis say so under “Where the values come from”. If no wind direction can be found, the chapter stays empty and says why. When measured, the recording only confirms a change of tack after a holding time; the analysis backdates it to the beginning of the manoeuvre. Costs from SOG include current and waves; in light wind the dip lasts longer by itself. The point of sail* is only as good as the course over ground and wind direction it comes from: current sets the course over ground, and with wind from a station or model, shifts come on top — the footnote says so. It appears only in the row and is used in no calculation: no best or worst board, no point-of-sail balance, and whether a change of tack was a tack or a gybe is still decided by the wind angle before and after. Without a wind sensor, the legs in the Course chapter carry the same point of sail*.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “10 manoeuvres”, below it “7 tacks · 3 gybes” (the change of tack 20 s after the windward mark is a change of course) and in the footer the chapter’s SOG trend with the manoeuvres as ticks. The “Evening race without wind sensor” (race picker at the top) shows the point of sail with an asterisk, below it the footnote “Point of sail* estimated from course over ground against the wind direction from the manual wind at the start; current distorts it.”
  • Tier: partly free — number of tacks and gybes, board list and SOG trend free, measured or estimated from GPS; costs per manoeuvre Pro, loss in metres and best board Pro with wind from the onboard network.

Speed & trim Pro

  • What it shows: How well the boat went against its polar — per point of sail, per tack, in the groove — and what the current added.
  • Where from: Wind, log and attitude from the onboard network at the track points; the stored polar; the race’s trim entries. Line “Onboard network: TWA · TWS · STW”; without a log “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG), no log”.
  • How: Point-of-sail balance upwind and downwind with VMG; tack balance (VMG per tack, with a note for a marked difference — with true wind through the water “check the masthead offset”, over ground “Current or sensor — can’t be told apart without a log”; likewise in the PDF report); polar matrix wind band × point of sail with measuring time; groove consistency (the beat as a scatter of wind angle × speed, with spread and polar reference); heel balance; speed over ground against through the water with a current band; current and leeway. The trim entries created during the race appear below. The card shows the average polar performance and the speed trend like the chapter: over ground as a line, through the water dashed, the area in between. With wind but without log and polar, it shows the partial value the chapter calculates: “VMG upwind 2.7 kn” (otherwise downwind) over ground, plus the SOG line. Without wind it stays empty: “Needs wind from the onboard network at the track points”.
  • What you read from it: The groove cloud shows whether you sailed too high and slow (pinching) or too low and fast, and how tightly the angle was held. The polar matrix tells you where training is lacking (“upwind above 16 kn”). A persistently weaker tack is rarely a matter of skill — more likely rig or wind sensor (see Tack asymmetry).
  • Caveat: Polar performance is only as good as the polar (a boat-type template isn’t your boat) and the true wind (masthead offset, log factor). Heel balance and current/leeway are experimental — leeway reacts strongly to errors in log and compass. Without a log, SOG stands in for STW; “against the polar” is then a comparison over ground.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “84 %” average polar performance.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network; speed through the water also needs the log.

Wind in the race Pro

  • What it shows: How did the wind behave — oscillating, shifting or steady — and were the shifts used?
  • Where from: True wind direction and speed from the onboard network at the track points. Line “Onboard network: TWS · TWD”; calculated over ground “Onboard network: TWS · TWD over ground (SOG)”.
  • How: A moving 12-minute average of the wind direction is the “centre”; what is measured is the deviation from it. Oscillating from 5° average deviation, shifting from 15° of shift over the race. A shift only counts from a 3° swing and no earlier than two minutes after the previous one. In addition period, lift share (share of upwind time on the lifted tack), response time (from the onset of a header to the tack), wind speed trend and conditions profile. The card shows the average wind speed and its trend.
  • What you read from it: Oscillating means: stay on the lifted tack, tack on the header — the lift share is the grade for that. Shifting means: go to the side it is shifting towards. The response time shows whether you sit out headers.
  • Caveat: The direction is only as good as compass and wind sensor; a sensor mounted askew shifts everything by a fixed amount. Nobody achieves 100 % lift share; the course forces boards on the other tack. Lift share and response time need detected tacks. Below two wind values nothing appears.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “avg 9.1 kn”.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network — wind from the logbook, station or model doesn’t replace it here (only in the daily report and in the strengths profile).

Manoeuvres & helming

Manoeuvre learning curve Pro

  • What it shows: Are the manoeuvres getting cheaper over the season?
  • Where from: The manoeuvres of every scored regatta (Manoeuvres chapter) — the tack measured from the onboard network or estimated from GPS (line “Onboard network: TWA · AWA · TWD”, “From GPS: port or starboard estimated” or mixed with the number of races). With wind, the losses in metres, otherwise the costs in seconds from the speed.
  • How: Per race the median of the manoeuvre costs. If metres are available from at least two races, the curve shows metres, otherwise seconds. The curve appears from two races with rated manoeuvres; which races count is decided by the racing tally (exclusions, season as the calendar year of the starting gun). The card shows the value of the last race of the current season; if there is a previous season, its value appears below in the same unit (“2025: 41 s” — the value of its last race), but not when comparing metres with seconds.
  • What you read from it: Downwards is training that works. The median survives the one botched tack at the windward mark.
  • Caveat: Seconds from the speed dip depend on the day’s wind — in light wind the dip lasts longer by itself, and a light-air part of the season looks like a step backwards. A manoeuvre that lost nothing against the neighbouring boards counts as zero metres, never less. Without a wind sensor, the manoeuvres estimated from GPS count, but only in seconds.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “2 m”: in the last race, the actually recorded Wednesday race, the average manoeuvre costs two metres against the neighbouring boards — the Express tacks cheaply; only the gybe after the start and a tack in a lull were expensive. The curve before it comes from the season’s demo regattas. Behind it is the previous season, the last race of the year before: “2025: 14 m”.
  • Tier: Pro; needs the tack from wind from the onboard network.

Helm steadiness Pro

  • What it shows: How steadily is the angle to the wind held — upwind and downwind, per wind band and over the season?
  • Where from: Sections with wind from the onboard network: true wind angle and true wind direction at the track points. Line “Onboard network: TWA · TWD · TWS”; calculated over ground “Onboard network: TWA · TWD · TWS over ground (SOG)”.
  • How: Per section the spread of the wind angle, minus what the shifting of the wind itself explains. Grouped by wind band and point of sail (upwind and downwind, not reaching). A band counts from 6 sections and 15 minutes; a month from 8 sections and 30 minutes. A trend only between months with no more than 4 kn difference in average wind, and only once the change is clearly above the noise and at least 0.5°. If the wind direction is missing, the raw spread remains, and the page says so. The card shows the steadiest band.
  • What you read from it: A small figure means a steady tiller. If you work the sheet single-handed or let the tiller pilot steer, you see it in the figure — a comparison you otherwise only make by feel.
  • Caveat: If you deliberately steer to the shifts, you break the calculation’s assumption — the figure then tends to come out too low. Waves and the sensor’s damping play a part. There is no established comparison measure from other devices; the figure is good for comparing yourself with yourself. The season trend needs at least two months with data.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “±2.7°” in the steadiest band, “16 kn upwind”.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network.

Boat & speed

Sailed polar Pro

  • What it shows: What does the boat actually do, per wind speed and wind angle — against the stored polar?
  • Source: Wind from the onboard network (wind angle and speed), boat speed (log, otherwise SOG) from scored races and measured runs — not from voyages. Line “Onboard network: TWA · TWS · STW”; if a cell carries an asterisk (measured without a log), “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG), no log” — the cell doesn’t know its wind source, but without a log the true wind can only have been calculated over ground.
  • How: Cells of wind speed (the polar’s grid points) × wind angle in 10° steps from 30° to 180°; a point sailed higher than 25° to the wind is dropped. Each cell counts the 90th percentile of boat speed — not the maximum (wave surfing, outliers), not the mean. Not counted are points that were reconstructed, under engine, around manoeuvres, during fast turns, below 1 kn and at gaps; in a race only the race phase, in training only the measured runs. Under 5 minutes of measured time a cell stays pale without a figure, up to 30 minutes muted, then full. Only you adopt values into the stored polar — cell by cell, from 30 minutes. Cells measured from SOG carry an asterisk. The card shows how much of the grid of point of sail and wind band is covered: a field counts as soon as a cell in it has 5 minutes of measured time — from then on the detail shows a figure. If no session has a cell, the card names the wind sensor.
  • What you learn from it: Where the template is too optimistic or too cautious — and with it, whether target angles, laylines and polar performance are right. A cell clearly above the polar when reaching is often waves or current, not the boat.
  • Caveat: Without a log (asterisk) the current is in the value. The true wind is only as good as the calibration. The 90th percentile describes good moments, not the average; a badly sailed season still lowers the values — which is why the app adopts nothing by itself.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “53 %”, below it what is missing, and in the footer the bar with “8/15”.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network.

Speed by point of sail* Pro

  • What it shows: What does the boat do over ground without a wind sensor — per point of sail* (Upwind*, Reaching*, Downwind*) and wind band (under 8, 8–12, 12–16, 16–20, over 20 kn)? The rough polar for those who have no onboard network.
  • Source: Only sessions without wind from the onboard network (no track point with TWS) that count in the analyses — races, training and, unlike the sailed polar, voyages too, depending on the boat’s switches; excluding voyages on other boats, from the polar’s reset point onwards. Course and speed over ground from the iPhone GPS, wind speed and direction from the hourly wind of the GFS weather model, which the app fetches after the session with “Wind from open sources” in addition to the station. The station never counts here — measured on land, it reads too low. Line “Wind from model”.
  • How: Point of sail* from the angle between course over ground and the model’s wind direction: up to 75° Upwind*, from 135° Downwind*, in between Reaching*. The band from the wind speed of that hour. Per field the average speed over ground, weighted by time, and how long was sailed. A stretch only counts from 30 seconds in the same field; not counted are points that were reconstructed, under engine, below 1 kn and at gaps over 60 seconds. Empty fields stay empty. The card shows the speed of the field with the most time, below it point of sail* and band. If sessions are missing, the detail says why: without wind from open sources (with the way to the switch), “Model wind not fetched for this voyage — from build 47” for sessions whose wind was fetched by an older build, “No model wind for this voyage: the first fetch with a connection came more than ten days after it …” for sessions that stayed without a connection that long, and “With an onboard network: sailed polar” with the way there. The model wind can’t be fetched later: the server keeps GFS for only ten days.
  • What you learn from it: Roughly how fast the boat goes upwind, reaching and downwind in which wind — a benchmark for the next voyage and a first template when there is no polar.
  • Caveat: Wind band from the model’s hourly wind, angle from the course over ground against the wind direction — no wind at the boat; current and shifts distort. That is why the table feeds into nothing: not VMG, target angles, polar or crossover.
  • In demo mode: The evening race and the practice course training without a wind sensor carry model wind (demo values); the card shows “4.5 kn” Upwind* at 8–12 kn (two practice courses).
  • Tier: Pro, needs no onboard network — GPS and wind from open sources.

Target angles from measurement Pro

  • What it shows: At which angle to the wind does the boat make the best progress — upwind and downwind, per wind band?
  • Source: Sections with wind from the onboard network from the sessions in the data basis (voyages included, if allowed); the stored polar for comparison. Line “Onboard network: TWA · TWS · STW”; without a log “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG), no log”.
  • How: Per wind band and course the sections sorted by VMG, the best quarter averaged (angle, speed, VMG). Only from 8 sections and 10 minutes per band and course; if the angle in the best quarter scatters by more than 6°, there is no suggestion. Adopt into the polar with a tap, after confirmation. The card shows the upwind angle in the most-sailed band, below it the coverage across upwind and downwind: a band counts as soon as it reaches the 8 sections and 10 minutes — even if the scatter rules out a suggestion. If no section has wind, the card names the wind sensor.
  • What you learn from it: Anyone sailing with a boat-type template is sailing to someone else’s target angles — and so to the wrong laylines. Sorting by VMG rather than polar performance prevents pinching from being rewarded.
  • Caveat: Current distorts VMG over ground; without a log the speed is SOG. A wind band from only one sailing area measures the area. The suggestion is an average over good sections, not a calculated optimum.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “40°” upwind in the “16 kn” band.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network.

Target heel Pro

  • What it shows: At what heel is the boat fastest — per wind band?
  • Source: Upwind sections with wind and heel from the onboard network (attitude sensor); the stored polar. Line “Onboard network: heel · TWA · TWS · STW”; without a log “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG) · heel, no log”.
  • How: Comparison only within a wind band, never across bands (otherwise you only measure: more wind, more heel, more speed). Per band the upwind sections sorted by polar performance; the average heel of the fastest quarter is the suggestion, next to it the band’s average. Only from 8 sections and 10 minutes per band; without a polar, no finding. Adopt as the boat’s target heel — it takes effect in the sailing compass. The card shows the suggestion in the most-sailed band, below it the upwind coverage: a band counts from 8 sections and 10 minutes. If wind, heel or (with wind) polar performance is missing in every section, the card names the wind sensor, attitude sensor or polar respectively.
  • What you learn from it: When to reef or twist off. If the fastest quarter and the band average are the same, heel isn’t the lever in that band.
  • Caveat: Without an attitude sensor on the onboard network there is no value — the iPhone in your pocket measures whoever carries it. Polar performance inherits every error of the polar and the true wind. The magnitude of heel is averaged; the side is dropped. The app doesn’t give a target heel for a boat type — it measures yours.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “20°” in the “16 kn” band.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind and an attitude sensor on the onboard network.

Tack asymmetry Pro

  • What it shows: Is one tack faster — and is it the boat or the wind vane?
  • Source: Upwind sections with tack and wind from the onboard network; the apparent wind angle for the cross-check. Line “Onboard network: TWA · STW”; if the true wind for most of the upwind measured time was calculated over ground, “Onboard network: TWA over ground (SOG), no log”.
  • How: The upwind sections split by tack, per tack the average speed, pointing and VMG. Only from 3 sections and 5 minutes per tack. A difference only counts if it is twice its uncertainty and above 0.1 kn or 1° — otherwise it reads “can’t be separated from the noise” or “measurable, but too small to act on”. Vane or boat? The cross-check compares the apparent wind upwind and downwind: a vane mounted askew twists it by the same amount on both courses; rig and steering have a much stronger effect upwind. The same amount on both courses means a mounting error, with a button that adds the correction to the existing masthead offset. Only what has been recorded since the last calibration change is used — the recording already carries the offset, the finding is the remainder. The demo shows 3.2° upwind here, but “undecided”: downwind is too imprecise, so no button. The card shows the speed difference between the tacks.
  • What you learn from it: A persistent difference between tacks is rarely skill — more likely mast rake or uneven shrouds, or the sensor. Check the vane first (adopt the correction), then the rig.
  • Caveat: Current across the beat favours one tack over ground — without a log that looks like asymmetry. Then the “Possible causes” section doesn’t point to the sensor but says “Current or sensor — can’t be told apart without a log”. The “Vane or boat?” cross-check remains: it works in apparent wind, and that is free of current. Sailing areas with one-sided wind distort the tack averages. The cross-check needs recorded apparent wind; if it is missing, the page says so.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “0.05 kn” — below the 0.1 kn threshold, so no finding to act on.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network.

Sails & trim

Sail crossover Pro

  • What it shows: Which sail or sail set is fastest in which wind and on which course — and does that match the wind range you entered for the sail?
  • Source: Wind from the onboard network, boat speed, the stored polar, the logged sail changes (entries with a sail list) and the wind range entered for the sail. The sessions in the data basis, voyages included, if allowed. Line “Onboard network: TWA · TWS · STW”; without a log “Onboard network: TWA · TWS over ground (SOG), no log”.
  • How: A track point’s sail set is the sail list of the last entry before it; points before the first entry are left out. Matrix of coarse wind band × course (upwind, reaching, downwind); in each cell the set with the best polar performance wins, from 5 minutes in the cell. If two sets are at most 2 percentage points apart (each from 3 minutes), both count — there’s no need to change there. Next to each sail, the entered against the measured range. The card shows how much of the matrix is covered: a field counts as soon as a set has 5 minutes in it and is therefore the winner — one sail is enough, a comparison of two isn’t needed for that. Without true wind at the points the card names the wind sensor, without a stored polar the polar.
  • What you learn from it: When to change from the genoa to the jib or from the gennaker to the spinnaker — from your own figures instead of the rule of thumb. If a sail was never carried above its range, the measurement is missing, not the performance — the app then claims nothing. The same in the top wind band: if the measured range reaches beyond 20 kn, the measurement knows no upper limit — the table shows “from … kn” and no “carries longer”.
  • Caveat: Stands or falls with cleanly logged sail changes; a forgotten entry credits minutes to the wrong sail. Polar performance inherits errors of the polar and the true wind. The 2-point margin is a setting, not a measured scatter.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “86 %”, below it what is missing.
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network. Entering the wind range and choosing sails by the current wind are free.

Trim workbench

  • What it shows: Which trim setting gained how much in a measured run?
  • Source: Trim entries captured during a measured run, and the run’s GPS track.
  • How: Exactly one trim entry per measured run — the earliest in the run, with two minutes’ grace after the end of the run (because “Capture trim” often comes only afterwards); each entry at most once, only completed runs. Grouped by course (Close-hauled · Beam reach · Broad reach · Downwind · General). Free: the duration of the run for each entry, sorted by date. The card shows how many entries were captured during measured runs.
  • Pro With Pro: Average and spread of speed per run and a bar relative to the fastest run in the group; with wind from the onboard network, polar performance, VMG, average wind and the ranking by polar performance, plus the “Best run” tile.
  • What you learn from it: Which setting has already been sailed under measured conditions — and which comes only from memory.
  • Caveat: The wind range on the entry is typed in or pre-filled from the onboard network when capturing, not measured wind. Without wind from the onboard network every comparison is one over ground — current and a few knots more wind beat any trim.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “7” entries captured during measured runs.
  • Tier: partly free — the workbench, its entries and the duration of the runs free; figures and bars Pro, polar performance, VMG and ranking Pro with wind from the onboard network.

Trim by wind speed

  • What it shows: For which wind is there already a trim — and where is the gap?
  • Source: The boat’s trim entries with their wind range (entered or pre-filled from the onboard network when capturing).
  • How: An axis from 0 to 30 kn, one lane per course plus “General” — all five are always shown. One bar per entry across its range (only “to”: from 0, only “from”: up to 30); overlapping bars stack up to three rows, beyond that “+n”. Entries without a wind range are counted, not hidden. A gap is anything 3 kn wide or more that is not covered. The card shows how much of the grid of course and wind band is covered, below it what is missing; the axis with each entry’s wind range as a bar is in the detail. An entry (not a draft) covers every wind band its range spans — Close-hauled the upwind, Beam reach and Broad reach the reaching, Downwind the downwind, “General” all three, because rig trim doesn’t depend on the course.
  • What you learn from it: Before the season or the next race: nothing yet for 16–20 kn upwind — then that’s the next training day.
  • Caveat: Shows what has been entered, not what has been measured.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “73 %”, below it what is missing.
  • Tier: Free.

Compare trims

  • What it shows: Two trim settings side by side — what is different?
  • Source: Two trim entries (wind, waves, sails, trim values); if both were in measured runs, their values.
  • How: A is fixed, you choose B (suggestion at the top, otherwise by date). One switch for both columns (General or a course); identical values quieter, differences in full. Below that the runs and whether they are comparable: with wind, two runs count as comparable if the wind speed differs by at most 1.5 kn and the wind direction by at most 10°. The card shows how many entries there are.
  • What you learn from it: What was different on the rig between the good day and the bad one — before blaming the sail.
  • Caveat: Without wind it can’t be judged whether the runs were comparable; the page says so. A difference in the values doesn’t prove that it made the speed.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “16 entries”.
  • Tier: Free; the measured values of the runs tiered as on the trim entry.

Training

Collect data. Below the card is the “Collect data” row: for each collecting analysis (Sailed polar, Sail crossover, Target angles from measurement, Target heel) the coverage and the gap as on its card, plus the manoeuvre series. Nothing is started here, but in the open training session in the logbook, between runs: as a measured run whose title names the gap (“Polar: upwind below 8 kn”; the one closest to the current wind). If the analysis lacks its source (such as the wind sensor), the sentence about it appears and no start. The manoeuvre series (target 10 tacks · 6 gybes, not counted) works with GPS alone and becomes a measured run; the manoeuvre learning curve doesn’t count it, it counts only races. Without Pro the four wind rows show a sentence instead of a gap.

Measured runs

  • What it shows: The runs of the last training session — how long, and which trim was captured during the run.
  • Source: Logbook (“Start run” / “End run”) and the GPS track during the run.
  • How: A run is the stretch between the start and end entries. Under 90 s or under three track points it appears in the list as “too short to tell”, without a score. Free: runs, duration and the captured trim. The card shows how many runs the last training session had.
  • Pro With Pro: Figures per run from the GPS — distance, average and spread of speed (log, otherwise SOG), average course over ground — and the A/B comparison of two runs. With wind from the onboard network also wind angle, VMG, polar performance and heel; A/B then only applies if wind speed differs by at most 1.5 kn and direction by at most 10°.
  • What you learn from it: Two runs of equal length on the same course in the same conditions are the most honest trim test without a training partner — the list shows whether you have them at all.
  • Caveat: Short runs are sensitive to gusts. Without wind, A/B compares speed and course over ground — current and wind shifts then look like a trim effect. That is why the difference stays uncoloured without a wind comparison (not green, not orange), and below it reads “Wind not compared — no wind sensor”, or “— one run without a wind sensor” if only one of the two runs has wind.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “3 runs” in the Forestay trim training.
  • Tier: partly free — training, runs, duration and capturing trim free; figures and A/B from the GPS Pro, A/B with wind Pro with wind from the onboard network.

Season & voyages

Racing tally

  • What it shows: How many races, hours, miles and manoeuvres in the season — and which places?
  • Source: The boat’s scored races (track, logbook), the place from what you entered in the race analysis. The manoeuvre counter carries the line “Onboard network: TWA · AWA” or “From GPS: port or starboard estimated”.
  • How: Counters across the scored races of a season — the calendar year of the starting gun in the local calendar; the default is the most recent one with a scored race, earlier years via the season row at the top: races, hours, miles, manoeuvres, races with a place, best and average place. Left out entirely are races you excluded, those run more than 90 % under engine, or whose race phase stayed under 10 minutes or 0.5 nm; every exclusion appears with its reason in the list (across all years) and can be undone. There is no longer a manual season cut-off date; what remains is the polar’s cut-off date (the sailed polar learns afresh, for example after new sails). The long-term curves (form trend, learning curve, start summary, strengths profile) show the same season, their cards and the performance levels the current one. The card shows the number of races in the current season, without a chart — its line “Season 2026 · 6 races up to …” counts the year’s races in the data —, below it the previous season (“2025: 8 races”), on every tier. The comparison by topic is in the tally’s “This season against last” section.
  • What you learn from it: The sober season tally — and the visible exclusions tell you why a figure is smaller than expected (usually a forgotten “Engine off”).
  • Caveat: The manoeuvre counter counts changes of tack — measured from the onboard network or estimated from the GPS (then “Manoeuvres: estimated from GPS” appears below). If no race has a tack, it shows “–” with the reason, not zero. Places only as far as entered.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “6 races”, below it “2025: 4 races”. The demo has two seasons: six races in the current year and four races on the same courses the year before, sailed worse. In the “This season against last” section every row with an arrow therefore shows better — upwind 89 % against 83 %, per manoeuvre 7 s against 10 s, distance at the gun 23 m against 44 m, result top 40 % against top 76 %, the strengths profile at 8–12 and 12–16 kn; the wind in both years mostly 8–12 kn. The races depend on today’s date: from 6 March to New Year’s Eve the six fall in the current season and the four in the previous year; in January and February the older demo races slip into the previous year, and count and comparison shift.
  • Tier: Free.

Form trend Pro

  • What it shows: Have you got faster over the races of the season?
  • Source: For each scored race the polar performance upwind and downwind (wind from the onboard network and polar), otherwise the avg SOG of the race phase from the GPS. Line “Onboard network: TWA · TWS” or “From GPS: avg SOG instead of polar performance”.
  • How: If polar values exist from at least two races, two curves (upwind, downwind) in percent of the polar; otherwise one curve of avg SOG per race. From two scored races. Tapping a point opens the race. The card shows the upwind performance of the last race; with a previous season, its last value in the same unit (“2025: 88 %”), never polar % against avg SOG.
  • What you learn from it: Upwind and downwind separately, because a single figure blurs exactly the progress — upwind improves, downwind stagnates. The GPS version shows a trend; the level depends on the day’s wind.
  • Caveat: The avg SOG is not normalised — a light summer looks like a loss of form, and current and course length feed in. The polar version is only as good as polar and calibration; changing the polar mid-season shifts the curve.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “87 %” upwind, followed by “2025: 85 %”.
  • Tier: Pro; the polar version with wind from the onboard network.

Start summary Pro

  • What it shows: How do you start over the season — distance, delay, which end, and did it matter?
  • Source: The start summary of every scored race (GPS, start line, gun); the favoured end with wind (by hand in the pre-start or from the onboard network); the place from what you entered.
  • How: Across the races with start data: average distance at the gun, average delay to the line (only crossings with a verified direction), the distribution of end choice pin · middle · committee boat, how often the favoured end was taken and — if places exist for at least two ends — from which end the best results came. From two races with a start line and gun. The card shows the average distance at the gun, with a previous season its average in the same unit after it (“2025: 31 m”).
  • What you learn from it: Whether you have a favourite end that the line doesn’t justify, and whether the cautious start in the second row costs places.
  • Caveat: The averages mix short and long lines, small fleets and large ones. End choice needs a crossing, “favoured” needs wind. Place per end is chance with few races.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “23 m” on average, followed by “2025: 44 m”.
  • Tier: Pro; the favoured end with wind by hand or from the onboard network.

Strengths profile Pro

  • What it shows: In which wind band do you sail best?
  • Source: For each race the average wind speed of the race phase (wind from the onboard network; without it the hours of the wind series in the race window in the same order of precedence as in the daily report, without a series the average of the wind entries in the logbook, from 15 minutes before the start), the polar performance (polar) and the place (entered). Line “Onboard network: TWS · TWA”, “Wind from station” (with “· n h from model”), “Wind from model”, “Wind from the logbook” or mixed, with the number of races.
  • How: The races sorted by average wind speed into the coarse bands; per band the number of races, the average polar performance (upwind and downwind averaged) and the average place. Shown as soon as two bands are covered or one band has two races. The card shows the band with the best polar performance; if the bands have none and a race carries wind without an onboard network (logbook, station or model), the one with the best place. Below it the coverage across the five bands: a band counts from two races. If no race has any wind, the card names all the ways to get it — wind sensor, logbook, station, weather model. With a previous season, its best band follows (“2025: 12–16 kn”).
  • What you learn from it: “Good in light wind, falls off from 16 kn” — with data instead of a feeling. From that follows where training or an earlier reefing point pays off.
  • Caveat: A race is an average — one with 6 kn at the start and 18 kn at the finish lands in a middle band. Averaging upwind and downwind hides a weak course (use the form trend for that). Few races per band. A wind band from the logbook is estimated by hand or pre-filled, from station or model it is the hourly wind nearby — neither measured on board; without an onboard network only the place counts.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “12–16 kn” as the strongest band, the same in the previous season (“2025: 12–16 kn”).
  • Tier: Pro, needs wind from the onboard network.

Your season

  • What it shows: How many nautical miles, sessions and days did the season have, how long was the longest stage, how many hours at night, in which harbours, in what wind — and how many engine hours?
  • Source: GPS track (distances, times, positions), logbook (Cast off, stage destination, mooring, “Engine on” / “Engine off”, wind), wind from the onboard network or from the session’s wind series (station, model); Workshop and maintenance log (amounts).
  • How: A season is the calendar year of casting off; all of the boat’s own sessions are counted — voyage, regatta, training —, no voyages on other boats. At the top the season row (default: the most recent year with a session of your own), below it seven lines: nautical miles and engine time per session as the counters, summed; sessions by type; days = calendar days from casting off to mooring, merged across sessions; longest stage = track miles between casting off, stage destination and mooring (without a stage destination the whole session, without a track the straight line); night hours = track time between two points at most ten minutes apart that both lie between dusk and dawn; harbours & anchorages = mooring entries with a position, distinct by 2 km cell; wind profile = hours with track per wind band, wind from the onboard network, otherwise from logbook, station or model in the daily report’s order of precedence, below it the hours per source. Below that, unchanged, the counters: engine phases from the times of the engine entries (a double “on” counts from the first, an open “on” ends at the end of the voyage), a stretch of track counts as engine if both points lie in an engine phase, otherwise as sailed; a total counter (reset after confirmation, like an hour meter) and a trip counter (freely resettable); engine per voyage; the costs of Workshop and maintenance over the winter. Boats selectable, voyages on other boats can be added for the counters. The card shows the nautical miles of the current season, without a chart, below it those of the previous season. The previous season is the last year before it with a session of your own (a gap is named in a sentence); “This season against last” counts both seasons (nautical miles, days). “Share” writes the season PDF: page 1 (seven lines, wind profile, sources, caveats).
  • Pro With Pro: “This season against last” holds both values in the same unit for each line: nautical miles, sailing share (sailed ÷ total), engine hours per 100 nm (engine time ÷ nautical miles × 100), days, longest stage, night hours, harbours. “Unchanged” below 5 % of the previous year or one unit (1 nm, 1 day, 1 h, 1 harbour, 1 percentage point, 0.1 h per 100 nm). Only sailing share (higher is better) and engine hours per 100 nm (lower is better) carry a verdict — an arrow in a circle, up = better; the other lines use an open arrow to show only more or less. At the top the wind sentence from the most frequent wind band of both years, at the bottom a trend sentence from the largest swing (relative to the previous year); if a season has one session, it names the sessions of both years. The season PDF has the comparison as page 2.
  • What you learn from it: The season’s miles and days for the cruising award entry, the summer’s wind profile and the engine hours for the oil change — without a scrap of paper at the chart table. Against the previous season: more sailing, less motoring? The wind sentence above says whether the summer was lighter — more miles aren’t a better year.
  • Caveat: The engine time is only as good as the entries: a forgotten “Engine off” turns the rest of the voyage into motoring. Distance over ground. Night hours need the track — a night at anchor without a track is not a night passage; harbours need mooring entries with a position. An open voyage counts up to today. If a line lacks its source, it shows “—” and the reason. The figure has nothing to do with the hour meter on the engine, unless you enter its reading in the maintenance log.
  • In demo mode: The card shows “132 nm” — the nautical miles of the current season, below it “2025: 102 nm”. The previous season is four races and two voyages: Kühlungsborn – Warnemünde – Kühlungsborn (out under engine) and Kühlungsborn – Klintholm (cast off at 04:00 in the dark) — 6 days, longest stage 53.7 nm to Klintholm, 3 harbours, wind mostly 8–12 kn; the current season blows mostly 12–16 kn, and the wind sentence names both bands. With Pro the sailing share is 98 % against 83 % and the engine hours per 100 nm 0.3 h against 3.0 h, both “better”; with demo tier Free it shows the count for both years and the line “Pro shows the comparison with the previous season”. The night hours depend on the date, because the demo sessions are placed relative to today: the Gedser voyage casts off at 06:00 — before sunrise in the winter half of the year, not in summer —, and casting off at 04:00 for Klintholm gives the previous season roughly one to four hours depending on the time of year. So the trend sentence also changes with the season. The current season grows while the demo voyage is running (one more nautical mile after a few minutes).
  • Tier: partly free — season, count of both seasons, previous season on the card and PDF page 1 free; the comparison against the previous season and PDF page 2 Pro.

Daily wind report

  • What it shows: How did it blow on the last voyage, hour by hour?
  • Source: True wind speed and direction from the onboard network at the track points (line “Onboard network: TWS · TWD”); if the track carries no wind, the wind series from open sources (line “Wind from station”, “Wind from model”), without a series the wind details of the logbook entries (line “Wind from the logbook”). The app fetches the series for new sessions without a wind sensor the next time it opens with a connection: for each hour the nearest station with data (Deutscher Wetterdienst, DMI, in Sweden the SMHI, available 120 days after the session, in the USA NOAA buoys and coastal stations, these available 45 days after the session; on open coasts and at buoys up to 25 km, in firths, lagoons, bays and harbours up to 10 km), otherwise the GFS weather model (available for about 10 days after the session). Can be switched off under Settings → Data & privacy → “Wind from open sources”.
  • How: The track points with wind speed grouped by hour, per hour the average speed, highest reading and average direction; divided by day. From the logbook an entry applies to its hour, the entry’s gust is the maximum, gaps stay gaps. With a series, per hour: logbook by hand before station before model before pre-filled logbook; below each hour its source (“Pelzerhaken · 2 km”, “Model”, “Logbook”). Station: hourly mean of the ten-minute values, “max” the strongest ten-minute mean (NOAA stations: hourly mean of their reports – buoys eight-minute, land stations two-minute means, some only hourly –, “max” the strongest report; SMHI stations: the single ten-minute mean per hour, “max” the same value); model: GFS on the hour, without “max”. The footer names only the sources that appear in the report.
