Above the hours is the trend — a chart over the whole voyage, even across several days: the line through the hourly means (each at the middle of the hour), a band up to the hour’s “max” (missing where the hour has no maximum: model, logbook in the order of precedence), the strongest hour by mean thick and labelled; the kn axis starts at 0 and extends beyond the strongest value of the voyage. Below the time axis, for each hour, the arrow showing where the wind blows to, with a three-digit bearing as long as each arrow has 34 pt of space; the time appears at every ⌈hours/5⌉th hour, and if it gets too tight for the arrows, they appear only there. Midnight is a solid line with the day on the axis. Under two hours no chart; a sentence says why. The source header sits above the trend, because it applies to both — following the same pattern for every source: “Wind from the onboard network”, “Wind from station”, “Wind from model” (mixed “Wind from station and model”) or “Wind from the logbook”. Hours from the logbook or entered by hand in a wind series are not named by the header, just like the line in the footer; they carry their mark at the hour, the footer their caveat. The footer says what the values are: from the onboard network “TWS · TWD”, from station and model the averaging and source attribution, from the logbook the caveat. The card takes the last voyage with wind and shows the highest value, without a chart; the report shows the trend.
  • What you learn from it: When the wind picked up or shifted — for the logbook, the debrief and as a check against the forecast for next time in the same area.
  • Caveat: “Gusts to” is the highest recorded value, not the gust — peaks are lost between two track points and through the sensor’s damping. Wind from the logbook is estimated by hand or pre-filled, not measured on board — whether by hand, from NMEA or from the weather forecast, the entry doesn’t know. Wind from station or model is the hourly wind nearby: it knows nothing of shifts, gusts and wind shadow at the boat. With none of these there is no report; the card and the report then say what is missing (nothing to fetch, will be fetched later, switch off).
  • In demo mode: The card shows “max 13.0 kn” for the Day sail Kühlungsborn; the evening race without a wind sensor carries a “Pelzerhaken (demo)” series with demo values, and the footer says “Demo values, not fetched”.
  • Tier: Free; wind from the onboard network, from open sources or from the logbook.
↑ Back to contents

Part D · While testing

Concrete test plans, what is known not to work yet, help with problems and the instrument abbreviations.

D1Test plans

Five runs to work through — from the first harbour trip to a night at anchor — with an expectation for every step, so you notice straight away where something is wrong.

The plans build on each other, but each can be run on its own. Each one says up front what you need and how long it takes; after every step it says what the app should do. If it does something else, that is exactly the feedback that counts.

NoteFor plans 1, 3 and 4, demo mode must be off — the demo simulates position and onboard network and would distort the results; when it ends, the demo data is deleted again. The easiest way to give feedback is straight from TestFlight: take a screenshot and share it — or write to support@etmar.app. MOB, anchor alarm and SOS are real functions but alert nobody outside the device; you can trigger them without risk.

Plan 1 — First voyage without onboard electronics

  • Duration: 45–90 minutes — a short board outside the harbour is enough, plus ten minutes on the pontoon for the report.
  • Requirements: boat created (onboarding), location allowed — when you cast off, the app also asks for “Always” for background recording. No onboard network, no mobile signal needed. Demo off.
  • Tier: everything free, including the map image in the PDF report and the voyage story.
  1. On the pontoon, go through the departure checklist in the logbook under “Before departure”. Expected: an active Low Power Mode appears there as a row — it throttles background recording, so switch it off beforehand.
  2. Map → one tap on the green “Cast off” button, no dialog. Expected: no form. The voyage and the departure entry are created immediately, and a large undo bar sits at the bottom for six seconds. The voyage is named after the nearest place (“Voyage Kühlungsborn – …”), the sails from the last voyage are carried over, Auto-Tracking is running. If “Depart under engine” (switch in the logbook below the Cast off button, on by default) is on, there is also an “Engine on” entry, and the engine button shows “Engine off”.
  3. Once through the app: the context slot is now called “Voyage”, the context bar sits at the top of the map, and the round pencil button at the bottom right. In the logbook the map has become flatter and carries speed and course in its footer; the status row says “Active” with a track point counter. Expected: the map shows your boat as a blue arrow pointing in the direction of travel, from 0.5 kn with a COG vector (6 minutes ahead); the arrow turns grey when the position is older than 2 minutes. The GPS accuracy turns orange as soon as it is worse than 30 m.
  4. Lock the phone. Expected: a Live Activity on the lock screen with voyage name, distance since casting off and avg speed — at first the track point counter, until some distance has built up.
  5. Hoist the sails, engine off: engine button in the logbook or pencil button → radial menu → Engine. Expected: an “Engine off” entry straight away, no form; the button turns grey and shows “Engine on” again. The way out of the harbour is yellow on the map.
  6. Tap “Sails” (large, first row) and pick the current wardrobe. Expected: every sail row can be tapped across its full width; an event entry with the new combination is created.
  7. Quick Log — the large tile at the top left of the logbook or in the radial menu on the map — twice: keep one, take the other back with the undo bar. Expected: a position entry with position, course and speed, confirmed by haptics. The wind field stays empty — without an onboard network an old value is never carried forward; that is not a bug. After undo, the second entry is gone.
  8. Photo: “Entry” (outlined, third row) → choose a type, take a picture with the camera, note, Save. Expected: with a signal, the Weather section shows a grey suggestion for wind, gusts, pressure and weather; “Apply” enters it — the function deliberately carries the experimental label, and empty fields are not a bug. A photo pin appears on the map, and when you zoom in the thumbnail replaces the dot.
  9. Phone in your pocket, screen off, sail for 15 minutes. Expected: the track point counter keeps growing — every 30 seconds at speeds above 1.5 kn, every 5 minutes when stationary. If there is no GPS fix for 5 minutes, a notification warns you.
  10. Outside the harbour, engine on, moor up, then “Mooring” (outlined, on the right in the second row). Expected: a day sail closes immediately: arrival entry backdated to when the boat stopped, undo bar instead of a confirmation. On a multi-day voyage a dialog asks for “Mooring (stage)” or “Close voyage”. If you tap nothing, after two hours stationary you get the suggestion “Arrived? Complete trip” — on the lock screen too.
  11. Open the voyage detail (carousel preview → map → “Back”). Expected: summary with duration, distance, avg SOG, engine time and the sail combinations used; map with numbered pins, yellow engine stretch and photo pin. A tap on the track shows the nearest track point with time, SOG and COG.
  12. Share menu → “PDF voyage report”. Expected: A4 via the system share sheet: page 1 with map image (if it cannot be loaded, a track sketch with a north arrow), key figures and notes; from page 2 all entries in chronological order with position, SOG/COG, weather, sails and up to three photo thumbnails. For comparison “Voyage story (image)”: a portrait image for sharing, likewise with the map image in every tier.
  13. Finally Tools → Analyses → Your season. Expected: at the top the season row and the seven season lines with your miles and days; below them the total and trip counters keep your miles, including the engine miles and engine hours from your engine entries (counting the timestamps from “Engine on” to “Engine off” or to mooring).

What to watch for: is the place name in the voyage title right? Are the sail changes and the engine stretch where they happened? Does the track have gaps — and does the log under Tools → Settings → Help → Tracking diagnostics explain them? How much battery did the hour cost?

What to report: gaps or outliers in the track (send the diagnostics file along; it contains no positions), a missing undo bar, a wrong place name, a report whose figures don’t match the voyage, battery use in per cent per hour.

Plan 2 — First race with a community course (or in the demo)

  • Duration: 60–120 minutes on the water; a dry run in demo mode at your desk about 20 minutes.
  • Requirements: location permission “Always” — otherwise the timers don’t fire with the phone in your pocket. A course: from the community — or, in the demo, put together from the supplied demo waypoints under Tools → Courses & waypoints. For the real run, a course you can sail in 30–60 minutes — a club race is ideal, a triangle you lay yourself will do too.
  • Tier: countdown, pings, distance to the line, line bias, live values in the race and key figures are free — VMG, tacking angle and layline need wind from the onboard network, next mark and VMC to the mark run from GPS; also free are the facts from the track (distance at the gun, mark times, legs, tacks and gybes, SOG trace, boards, zones, side at pass marks). Pro is what a start, a manoeuvre, a leg has cost — race card, waterfall, start summary, manoeuvre costs, tack balance.
  1. Get a course: Tools → Courses & waypoints → “Courses from the community” → open a course → “Join”. On the map the course is the chequered flag; tapping it draws the course straight away and offers “Join” in the sheet below — in landscape as a card on the map instead, so the course stays visible. Expected: your own copy is created, and the future dates appear as a series in the calendar. For a pursuit or group start the app asks straight away for your own start time — to the second, with its own seconds field. Without a suitable community course: start the demo (Tools → Help & demo) — it brings waypoints for the area, from which you lay your own course in the course library (start and finish line from two points each, tap the marks); there the boat is simulated, so the whole sequence can be played through at your desk. The finished demo race “NSV Wednesday race” — a real race that was actually sailed — shows you in advance what an analysis looks like.
  2. Create the race: Logbook → menu at the top right → “New race” (or on race day “Start” in the calendar, with a confirmation). Name, starting gun 15–20 minutes ahead, course from the course row, pre-start length (5/6/10/15 minutes), class and scoring. Expected: if there is a calendar event with a course today, it is preselected. After saving, the context slot is called “Regatta” (course, analysis), the course lies orange on the map — start line green, finish line red, course order dashed — and the countdown stands in the logbook in the “Waiting” phase.
  3. Put the phone away until the pre-start begins. Expected: on its own: pre-start entry, tracking starts, notification on the lock screen. The countdown runs large in the logbook (yellow, in the last minute with the orange “LAST MINUTE” capsule), in the context bar at the top of the map and to the second in the Live Activity.
  4. Ping the line: sail past the pin end → “Ping pin”, past the committee boat → “Ping boat” (pre-start bar on the map or in the logbook). Expected: a tick for each end; pinging again simply overwrites; undo bar. As soon as both ends are set, the context bar and logbook show the perpendicular distance to the line and time-to-burn (red = too early); if you are over the line, “OVER x m” appears; if the GPS fix is older than 10 seconds, both values disappear instead of staying frozen. A ping without a GPS fix reports it. Plus the line bias (“Pin +7° · 12 m”, below 3° “Line square”) — wind via “Set wind” with the rotating wind rose or “Head to wind — take the course”.
  5. At the race committee’s signal, tap “Sync” once — on the map to the right of the ping buttons. Expected: the starting gun rounds to the next full minute, and the countdown is right to the second. For AP: “Postpone the start” → “+5 min”, undo with “−5 min”; tapping several times adds up.
  6. Starting gun. Expected: start entry exactly at the start time, a strong haptic pulse, notification (with the app open without a banner and without its own sound, only in Notification Centre); the countdown becomes the green race time, and recording switches to one point every 5 seconds.
  7. During the race, tap “Marker” two or three times — after a bad manoeuvre, at a shift. Expected: one tap, one GPS-tagged point, no form. You don’t log tacks, gybes and roundings — the analysis takes them from the track. During the race the Regatta tab shows four tiles: next mark with side, bearing and distance, VMG against the polar as a delta (without wind from the onboard network, VMC to the mark from GPS), average tacking angle, distance to the layline — without a value, the tile states the reason.
  8. Finish: with a finish line set, just sail through. Expected: the app detects the crossing (60 seconds’ grace after the start, and only once you have been out on the course – crossing the line in the pre-start ends nothing), writes the finish entry, stops tracking and countdown and reports “Finish line crossed!”. Without a finish line, during the race “Ping finish stbd”/“Ping finish port” or “Finish race” (with confirmation). After two hours stationary the app asks by notification.
  9. Open the analysis: key figures in the voyage detail; a tap leads to the full analysis (also via Tools → Analyses). Expected: free: race duration, distance in the race, avg/max SOG, SOG at the gun; only the stretch from the start line to the finish is counted, and the start loss is shown separately. In the Start chapter, “Analysis window” says where both limits come from — orange if only the end of the track was available; the short way there is under the key figures. Also free: distance at the gun, rounding and leg time for each mark in Course, the tacks and gybes, the boards and the SOG trace through the manoeuvres. Pro: race card with three sentences at the top, below it the time-loss waterfall, whose items lead into the chapters — start summary (position on the line, speed build-up) in the Start chapter, rounding quality and leg summary in Course, every tack with its cost in seconds; with wind from the onboard network also tack balance, VMG, performance against the polar and SOG against STW under Speed & trim, wind & shifts in the Wind chapter.
  10. Correct the course: “Adjust course” → move a line or mark. Expected: all times, start distance and leg times are recalculated automatically. Finally, at the end of the analysis, “Enter finishing position” — the race then counts towards the racing tally under Boat.

What to watch for: did the pre-start begin on time even though the phone was in your pocket? Do the map and logbook show the same figures? Does the rounding time match your memory (the stamp is the closest approach, detection radius 60 m by default)? Did a mark set to “Pass only” get its time, and are the side and distance below it right — does the row turn red if you passed on the wrong side?

What to report: pre-start or starting gun without a notification, a countdown that is wrong after “Sync”, an undetected finish (with course), mark times that are off, an analysis window “from end of track” despite a line — and for community courses anything that is wrong about organisers, dates or start procedure.

Plan 3 — Offline test

  • Duration: 10–20 minutes downloading (on the harbour Wi-Fi), 20 minutes checking.
  • Requirements: a connection for downloading, then flight mode. A draught set on the boat (Tools → Boat), so that the emphasised safety contour matches your boat. Demo off — otherwise you see the Bay of Mecklenburg instead of your own area.
  • Tier: everything free — packs without limit, “Fine” and “Download approaches”. Seamarks and depths are included in all tiers.
  1. Open Tools → Settings → Map → Offline maps — or tap the “Area not saved” hint on the map, which appears with a connection as soon as your boat is in view and no pack covers the area (only without a running voyage). Move the map to your area, choose detail level “Medium”, “Save visible area”. Expected: a size estimate before the download; the app refuses areas that are too large. The pack appears with progress, size and the place name from the centre of the map.
  2. Watch the seamarks and depths follow. Expected: they are fetched automatically with the pack; a large area is split into up to 36 sections, and the button counts along (“Seamark 7 of 36”). “Cancel” keeps what has been downloaded. If a section fails (the free OpenStreetMap server throttles when busy), the reason appears in red in the area row with “Try again”.
  3. Look at the “Downloaded areas” list. Expected: name, number of seamarks and harbours, depth contours, size and date; plus the quality of the water area in per cent and the source.
  4. Flight mode on (Wi-Fi and mobile data off), close the app from the app switcher, restart it, open the map. Expected: at the top a chip “Offline: <pack>” — orange “no pack for this area” as soon as you move outside it. Within the pack the map stays sharp up to the chosen detail level.
  5. Lighthouse menu: switch Seamarks, Harbours & anchorages (OSM), Depth contours and My harbour guide on and off one by one. Expected: each layer switches on its own; the symbol is filled while one is on. Seamarks in chart shapes: lateral buoys red (can) and green (cone), cardinal buoys yellow-black with topmarks, special marks yellow, safe-water marks red-white, lights as a magenta star. Lighthouses, cardinal and safe-water marks already in the area overview, lateral buoys from area zoom, pier lights as a smaller star also only from area zoom, beacons only close in; names from close zoom.
  6. Tap a buoy. Expected: name, type, bearing and distance from your boat, plus “Go-To” — the blue banner with bearing, distance and course error works without a connection.
  7. Check the depth contours. Expected: contours at 2/5/10/20 m with upright labels, only on water. Water shallower than the emphasised line is lightly tinted; the tint ends at the line and at the edge of the area. With a draught set, exactly one line is emphasised — the shallowest that is still deeper than draught plus margin (with a 1.65 m draught, the 5 m line).
  8. Tap an OSM harbour (indigo anchor) → “Add to harbour guide”. Expected: name, type, berths, VHF channel and facilities in the card, and below them, above the buttons, the note about the circle fully readable; the destination form is prefilled, and only “Save” creates the record. If you leave the card open and go via Tools → Harbour guide to the map symbol, it is closed afterwards. The destination picker (e.g. final destination in the voyage form) also shows the chart harbours offline, sorted by distance.
  9. Flight mode off. Under Offline maps, look at “Map storage” and “Clear the cache”. Expected: only tiles viewed in passing disappear; packs, seamarks and depths remain.
ImportantSeamarks and harbours are community data from OpenStreetMap, the depths a model on a grid of about 100 m (EMODnet). Neither is an official source, neither is corrected by Notices to Mariners, and neither replaces a chart — for the test you check the app against your chart, not the other way round.

What to watch for: do the position and type of the buoys in your area match the chart? Is one missing that you know? Are the depth contours plausible (up to about 80 × 80 nm the grid is about 120 m, larger areas get coarser)? Can you find your home harbours, and are the VHF channel and number of berths right?

What to report: area, detail level and pack size; sections that failed to download (reason from the row); a map that stays empty offline despite a pack; misplaced or missing seamarks with name and position; harbour data that is wrong.

Plan 4 — With onboard electronics

  • Duration: 20 minutes in the harbour, then a board with at least two tacks — 30–60 minutes.
  • Requirements: an NMEA Wi-Fi gateway on board — NMEA 2000 in YDWG RAW format or NMEA 0183 via TCP (host:port) or UDP (typically port 10110) — and the iPhone on the boat’s Wi-Fi. No tier is needed; without a gateway the NMEA tiles say “needs NMEA”. Demo off.
  • Good to know: the position always comes from the iPhone GPS as well. If the bus delivers no position for more than 5 seconds, the phone GPS steps in, and the dashboard says “NMEA · position: iPhone GPS”. Wind, log, depth and compass have no substitute.
  1. Tools → Settings → NMEA connection: let the app search for the gateway on the Wi-Fi, or enter host, port and TCP/UDP, “Connect”. Expected: status connected; “Data format: NMEA 2000 (RAW)” or “NMEA 0183” is detected line by line. The live data list and “Detected sensors” fill up — also with pressure, pressure trend, log and trip if the bus supplies them. Diagnostics log, PGN counter, damping and data recording are on the “Advanced” subpage.
  2. Switch to the logbook. Expected: at the top an NMEA status chip: green connected, orange connecting, red error or data backlog, grey off; a tap opens the NMEA settings.
  3. Open Tools → Instruments and hold the values next to the onboard displays. Expected: sailing compass with heading, true and apparent wind arrow and tack sectors; tiles for SOG, STW, TWS, TWA, TWD, depth; pages by swiping or a tap at the edge. If the gateway only supplies apparent wind, the app calculates true wind from AWA/AWS and STW or SOG. The pressure trend only shows a figure after an hour of data, the TWD trend on the racing page only after five minutes.
  4. On the map, tap the round instruments button in the left button column. Expected: a data strip with three tiles above the tab bar, map and values visible at the same time; in landscape and on the iPad a column on the right. Tap again to hide it.
  5. Auto-connect: close the app from the app switcher and restart it, then send it to the background once and back. Expected: the connection comes back on its own both times (“Connect automatically”, on by default). A manual “Disconnect” keeps the automatic connection quiet until the next app launch.
  6. Provoke the watchdog — with the app open or a recording running (locked and without a recording, the app goes to sleep after a few seconds, and the watchdog with it): walk away from the boat with the phone until the Wi-Fi drops — the case the watchdog is built for (the connection still appears to be up, but no more data arrives). Expected: after 20 seconds without data the app reconnects automatically; after one minute “NMEA data stopped” appears once — on the lock screen when locked, as a banner with the app open; the NMEA chip in the logbook shows red “NMEA stuck”, and the NMEA tiles in the dashboard show “--” instead of frozen values. Back in range, the tiles fill up on their own. If, with the phone locked and no recording, the notification only arrives the next time you open the app, that is not a bug.
  7. Cast off (green Cast off button on the map) and sail a board with at least two tacks; take a Quick Log along the way. Expected: the Quick Log entry contains the wind from the bus — only if it is at most 60 seconds old. If you want to put it to the test, don’t tap Cast off: if the status row in the logbook shows “Waiting for departure — starts automatically when underway”, the app with an onboard network detects the departure itself after one minute of continuous speed above 1.5 kn, backdates the departure entry to when you got underway and reports “Departure detected” on the lock screen.
  8. Back in the harbour, open the voyage map. Expected: the track is coloured by tack — by default green = wind from port, red = wind from starboard; the legend shows the assignment (reversible under Settings → Map → Tack colouring), and engine stretches stay yellow. A tap on the track shows time, SOG, COG, true wind and tack for the track point, plus a small boat with a wind arrow. After the connection drops, the colouring keeps using the last wind for up to 30 minutes; after that the points stay without wind.
  9. Close the voyage and go to the voyage detail. Expected: the wind of the departure and arrival entries is averaged over the first and last ten minutes respectively; the daily report (wind) shows the true wind per hour with a direction arrow and avg/max. Share → “Raw data (CSV)” delivers STW, HDG, wind and heel for each track point — gaps stay empty.
  10. Optionally at anchor: set the anchor alarm and switch on the depth alarm in the sheet (threshold draught + 0.5 m). Expected: three readings below the threshold trigger a sound and a notification. With AIS on the bus: zoomed in close, other vessels with name and 6-minute vector; from area zoom as a filled triangle with its tip pointing in the direction of travel, from which the vector grows (class B smaller; a circle means no current course — without a vector no speed, with a vector the last course report is more than a minute old); in the overview only as a dot with a vector, names only close in — a target below the warning threshold always with symbol and name; below 0.5 nm CPA / 20 min TCPA the target turns red, with a ring around it, a warning button appears at the top, and a notification arrives even with the phone in your pocket.

What to watch for: which fields arrive, which are missing (“Detected sensors”)? Do STW, TWS or depth differ from the onboard displays — and by how much? Does the tack on the track match what you sailed? How long did the watchdog really take? Does the drive to the harbour by car not start a voyage?

What to report: gateway model, data format and TCP/UDP; fields that are missing or misinterpreted; deviations from the onboard instruments, with figures; tack colouring the wrong way round (with wind direction and course); the times at which the drop-out, the warning and the reconnection came. The diagnostics log from “Advanced” is worth its weight in gold for any connection problem.

Plan 5 — Night and wet

  • Duration: 30–45 minutes on the berth after sunset. You don’t need to sail for this; if you want to see values moving, start the demo (Tools → Help & demo).
  • Requirements: darkness, wet hands (a spray bottle will do), sailing gloves if you have them. Set the iPhone to dark appearance.
  • Tier: free, including tack colouring; the sailing compass belongs to Pro and needs a wind transducer and compass on the NMEA network.
  1. Switch on night mode via the moon item in the menu on the map, logbook or dashboard. Expected: a red filter over the whole app, without a detour through the settings. With dark appearance the map is a night chart — water black, land dark grey, labels dimmed — with the red filter on top. The automatic “from sunset” option (in the settings) switches according to the sun’s position at your location; a manual choice stays until the morning.
  2. Open the SOS page (red tile in Tools). Expected: here the red filter is deliberately off — the chip row with all eight radio cards and the pre-written message must be readable under stress. Back on the map, the filter is there again.
  3. Under the red filter, look at a voyage map with a track (in the demo: voyage Kühlungsborn–Gedser with tack colouring and an engine stretch). Expected: green, red and yellow merge under red — that is what the “Colour-safe” palette in the settings is for (blue/orange for the tacks, grey for engine, port tack thicker). Switch it on and check whether boards and engine stretches can now be told apart; legend and analysis follow the palette.
  4. Readability at a glance: map with context bar, logbook row with SOG/COG, dashboard, Live Activity on the locked phone. Expected: every value can be read in under two seconds, at arm’s length, without losing your night vision. Note every one where that doesn’t work.
  5. Lock the dashboard (Tools → Instruments), then put water on the display and swipe and tap with wet fingers. Expected: locked means locked: no page change, no tap gets through, a permanent lock notice is shown. Unlock only by holding the large area for 3 seconds, with haptics.
  6. The everyday actions wet and with gloves: Cast off (undo bar), Quick Log, Engine, sails row, Mooring; pencil radial menu on the map (a tap beside it closes it without consequence); location button and zoom buttons; “Map centre” instead of a long press (underway on the round button at the bottom right: logbook button → “Map centre”; without a voyage only the long press); anchor radius with the 10 m buttons. Expected: every action hits on the first try, every mis-tap can be taken back with the undo bar, no button triggers anything on the side. Pinch is unreliable with gloves — that is what the zoom buttons are for.
  7. MOB: hold the red button for 0.6 seconds (the ring fills) — once wet, once with a glove, plus a few short taps on it. Expected: short taps do not trigger it. When held: red banner with bearing, distance and seconds counter, lifebuoy and red line on the map, and with a voyage open an entry in the logbook; “End” asks for confirmation. Nobody outside the device is alerted.
  8. Anchor watch overnight: anchor button (radius 50 m, rule of thumb chain + boat length + 10 m), phone in the bunk. Expected: banner “Anchor set · … m / 50 m · since …”, circle and anchor pin on the map, blue status indicator of the GPS guard. In the morning the state is still there — even after an app restart. For the first few seconds it says “Awaiting GPS”. If reception in the bunk is not good enough because the accuracy is 100 m or worse, “WATCH BLIND” appears in the banner after two minutes, with sound and notification. That is intended: put the phone closer to a hatch or the companionway. Report it if “WATCH BLIND” comes although the phone has a clear view of the sky and the boat is lying quietly, with the time and the reason from the capsule (such as “GPS too inaccurate (± 150 m)” or “No GPS reading”).
ImportantThe anchor alarm now also rings with the silent switch on and the Sleep Focus active. That depends on a permission the app asks for once when you drop the anchor (Critical Alerts) — without it, with the phone locked, you only get vibration. With the app open it rings even without the permission, with its own sound and banner — third run: app open, silent, Sleep Focus on. The anchor alarm screen tells you what currently applies. Try it out once in the harbour before you rely on it at night.

What to watch for: which value could you not read at night or on a wet display? Where did you get stuck under the red filter because an element disappeared? Which button did you need twice with a wet thumb? Did a swipe trigger something by accident?

What to report: every button that missed or triggered the wrong thing, with screen and situation; every figure that was too small or too low in contrast — ideally as a screenshot straight from TestFlight; places where the red filter is missing or gets too dark; whether the anchor watch lasted through the night and whether a “WATCH BLIND” came without an obvious reason.

D2Known limitations

What is already known in this build — so you can place it and don’t report it twice.

Everything on this page is known: either it is on the work list, or it was built this way on purpose. If you come across one of these behaviours, you don’t need to report it. Do report it, though, if something behaves differently from what is described here, if a tip given here doesn’t work, or if a limitation affects you more on board than it sounds here.

Instruments and map

  • Wind shifts are only analysed after the race. The app detects oscillating and persistent shifts and analyses amplitude, period, share of lifts and your reaction time — but only in the race analysis. Live there is only the “TWD trend” tile: the current wind direction against the average of the last ten minutes, with an arrow for the direction of the shift, and only after five minutes of data. Whether the wind is oscillating or shifting persistently is still something you have to keep an eye on yourself during the race.
  • The units setting doesn’t apply everywhere. Distance, speed, depth and water temperature can be changed under Settings → Units and take effect in the instruments, the logbook, Go-To, the race key figures and the reports. In a few places it still stays at knots and nautical miles — for instance the VMG delta on the race face, the charts in the race analysis and the polar data. Wind speed stays in knots anyway; that is the convention among sailors and intended. The limitation concerns boat speed and distances.

Notifications and alarms

  • Anchor alarm: depends on a permission. The anchor alarm and depth alarm run as Critical Alerts — they break through the silent switch, Focus and the volume setting. The app asks for this once when you drop the anchor; if you decline, you only get vibration with the phone locked — with the app open the alarm still rings, with its own sound. To grant it later: Settings → Notifications → Etmar → Critical Alerts. If notifications are switched off entirely, no alert reaches the locked phone — the anchor sheet and the race form then say so and lead to the settings. All other alerts (race signals, AIS, recording) deliberately remain normal time-sensitive notifications — Etmar only overrides your silent switch where the boat is in danger.
  • No watch function. There is no recurring wake-up alarm (“every 20 minutes”). For single-handed offshore sailing that is the central safety tool; the anchor alarm (at anchor) and AIS (targets nearby) do not cover that case.
ImportantDon’t rely blindly on the anchor alarm at anchor at night just yet. Try it out once in the harbour — with Sleep Focus active and with your iPhone lying the way it really does at night — and report whether the sound and vibration come through.

Language, sync and accessibility

  • English interface. The app is fully bilingual German/English (British English). The demo’s sample data (voyage names, notes) is created in the iPhone’s language; only the marks of the recorded race keep their names, and the North Sails trim notes keep their Swedish technical terms, as in German (“Brante Slör”). No editor has read the English texts yet — an odd word, a line break in the middle of a word or a German leftover: please report whatever you notice.
  • iCloud: the last writer wins. In a conflict between two devices, the most recently saved version wins; iCloud does not report the conflict to the app, and nothing can be recovered. If you edit the same logbook entry offline on iPhone and iPad, you lose one of the two versions. At least it is visible: the voyage shows the row “Last changed” with time and device, and if an open entry is overwritten from another device while you are editing it, an orange notice appears at the top.
  • VoiceOver does not read out instrument values. The values on the instrument tiles are not announced by VoiceOver. That a value is stale is shown only through opacity and border colour — which cannot be perceived without sight. The polar diagram is not labelled for VoiceOver at all.

Other

  • The wind suggestion in the logbook is mean wind. Without an onboard network, the entry suggests wind and direction from the nearest station or the model — 10 m above ground (at NOAA buoys and coastal stations at measurement height, not converted to 10 m), without gusts and shifts, and without a connection up to three hours old (the age is then shown below the section). Without an onboard network, the app does not prefill gusts, pressure or weather. With NMEA on board, your own instruments take priority; when the voyage is closed, the wind of the departure and arrival entries is averaged over the first and last ten minutes respectively.

Intended

Not a flaw but a decision. You don’t need to report these — but do tell me if one of these points bothers you on board:

  • No server of its own, no account. Everything is stored locally on the device or in your private iCloud. The share file is the “currency”, not an account.
  • iCloud sync is not a backup. An error gets synced too. That is why there is also the backup file — and the app says so explicitly.
  • No routing around land. Go-To is a great-circle bearing to the destination, not a route calculation across the water. You check on the map whether there is land in between.
  • An MOB in the running demo points into the demo area. While the demo is running, the position comes from the simulator — for an MOB too, whether you trigger it on the map, in the logbook or in the instrument dashboard. Such an alarm points to a spot where nobody is in the water, and ends with the demo (A3). If someone really goes overboard during that time, it is of no help: end the demo and then trigger the MOB again, and the real position applies. So don’t leave the demo running when you are out on the water — it replaces all the real instruments anyway. It is different during its background pause: if you press MOB on the watch while the demo is paused in the background (for instance with the iPhone locked), the alarm is real, gets no point from the demo, and the demo ends when you come back (A3).
  • Red/green for port/starboard. Seamanlike convention; departing from it would be worse than the red-green problem. On the compass rose, the position separates them as well.
↑ Back to contents

D3When something gets stuck

The most common stumbling blocks in the beta — what you see, what causes it and what you can do before you report it.

NoteLook at the status displays first: the status row in the logbook, the chips at the top of the map, the NMEA status chip, the Live Activity. That is where the app says what is not working right now — and usually why, too.

Recording and track

  • No track, status row red “No location permission — nothing is being recorded”. Cause: location never allowed, denied or withdrawn again in iOS; for background recording the app needs “Always”. Fix: directly below the red row is the jump to the iOS settings; set location to “Always” there. If the permission is withdrawn underway, a notification also appears on the lock screen saying that recording has stopped.
  • Departure is not detected; it says “Waiting for departure entry”. Cause: automatic departure needs NMEA data (a real network or demo mode) — only that makes sure the phone is moving with the boat. Phone GPS alone cannot tell a car journey from a boat trip and deliberately does not count as “NMEA on”. Fix: press the Cast off button or create a manual entry. If the short stretch from the berth is then missing, “Correct departure & fill in” in “Redraw track” closes it — up to about 2 km; anything longer stays with “Redraw manually”.
  • The track has gaps. Cause: iOS terminated the app (memory pressure, crash), Low Power Mode throttles background recording, or the GPS had no fix. A significant change of location wakes the app again and recording continues — until then distance is missing. If there is no fix for 5 minutes, a notification warns you; when Low Power Mode is on, an orange chip appears on the map, below 20 % battery with the biggest lever (close the map). Fix: first look under Settings → Tracking diagnostics (“Help” section) — every state change, app launch, GPS silence and save error is listed there. Then in the voyage detail “Redraw track”: it finds time jumps of more than 10 minutes with at least 250 m of distance, as well as missing stretches before the first and after the last point (time lying at a berth is not a gap). For “Probably sailed continuously”, “Apply connection” is enough; otherwise set points with a long press. Redrawn sections remain recognisable as thinner and paler.
  • The Live Activity says “No current data — open app”. Cause: no update has come in for 15 minutes — the app crashed or was terminated by iOS. The display deliberately dims instead of continuing to claim “Recording”. Fix: open the app and recording is restored; the logbook status row shows with “Active” and the track point counter that it is continuing. Redraw the gap later, and put the tracking diagnostics in your feedback.

NMEA connection

  • NMEA does not connect. Cause: iPhone not on the boat’s Wi-Fi, wrong host or port, or “Disconnect” was pressed by hand — that keeps “Connect automatically” quiet until the next app launch. Fix: the NMEA status chip at the top of the logbook (green connected, orange connecting, red error, grey off) opens Settings → NMEA connection with a tap; tap “Connect” there — or the large “Connect NMEA” button right in the instrument dashboard. You don’t choose the format: the app detects NMEA 2000 (RAW) and NMEA 0183 line by line and shows it under “Data format”. With 0183 via UDP the app listens on the port (typically 10110); UDP, however, does not resend lost packets — where TCP (host:port) works, TCP is the calmer choice. Diagnostics log and PGN counter are on the “Advanced” subpage.
  • NMEA stuck: the chip in the logbook shows red “NMEA stuck”, the NMEA tiles in the dashboard show “--”. Cause: the connection is up, but no more data is arriving — typically when the iPhone silently drops off the boat’s Wi-Fi. Fix: usually do nothing. The watchdog reconnects automatically after 20 seconds without data; after one minute “NMEA data stopped” appears once on the lock screen — as long as the app is awake, i.e. open or with a recording or anchor watch running in the background. If the position is missing for more than 5 seconds, the phone GPS steps in: position, SOG and COG then come from the iPhone; wind, log, depth and compass have no substitute and show “--”. If you don’t want to wait: “Connect NMEA” at the bottom of the dashboard rebuilds the connection immediately. If it stays red: check the Wi-Fi on the iPhone, then “Disconnect” and “Connect”.

Map and depths

  • The map is empty offline. Cause: no offline pack covers the area — the orange chip “no pack for this area” appears at the top. Or you only used “Load seamarks & depths only”: then the base map deliberately stays empty offline, but buoys, harbours and depth contours are there. Fix: with a connection the map shows “Area not saved” as soon as your own boat is in view (only while no voyage is running) — a tap leads to saving. Otherwise, under Settings → Map → Offline maps, move the area into the map, choose a detail level, “Save visible area”. Packs are available without limit and at every detail level. Download on the harbour Wi-Fi before you head out.
  • Depths are missing in the area. Cause: no area has been downloaded for this section, or part of the download failed — a large area is split into up to 36 sections, and the free OpenStreetMap server throttles when busy. Fix: in “Downloaded areas” the reason is shown in the area row; “Try again” adds the missing sections — sections already downloaded stay saved, even after “Cancel”. Contours exist at 2/5/10/20 m, coarser for very large areas — for an approach, download the smaller area. Model data, not official depths.

After an update

  • At launch it says the database was set aside. Cause: it could not be loaded once, for example after installing an older build. The app does not delete it then but sets it aside. Fix: Settings → Data backup → Set-aside versions brings the version back with one tap; the current data is backed up in turn. The app never deletes anything on its own.
  • After the update the old icon or launch screen is still shown. Cause: iOS caches the icon and launch screen. Fix: delete the app and reinstall it from TestFlight. If something still jumps or stutters at launch after that, it is worth a report.
ImportantDeleting the app also deletes the local data. Beforehand, under Settings → Data backup, use “Save / share backup” to store a full backup (Files, iCloud Drive or AirDrop) — it contains all data including images.

Demo, location, saving, weather

  • After “End Demo” data is gone. Cause: intended. Demo mode creates sample data and simulates NMEA; when it ends, all demo data is deleted — and that includes everything you created yourself while the demo was running, including your own voyages and entries. The confirmation “End Demo?” says so once more. Anything created before or after the demo is left untouched. The magenta banner shows the whole time that the demo is running. Fix: create what you want to keep outside demo mode — never record real voyages while the demo is running.
  • You chose “Later” during setup and want to allow location afterwards. Cause: the app deliberately asks only once, on land during setup, never unprompted when you open the map. Fix: on the map, tap the location button or the blue “Allow location” chip, which appears as long as there is no position. When you cast off, the app also asks for “Always” for the background — iOS only allows this second step after the first. If location was denied, the button explains what is missing and leads to the iOS settings.
  • A red bar “Saving failed — the change may be lost” appears at the bottom. Cause: the database did not write a change. The bar appears on every screen, can be tapped to dismiss and otherwise disappears after 8 seconds. Fix: check whether the entry or change is there, otherwise do it again. Save errors also appear in the logbook status row and in the tracking diagnostics — please always report this case.
  • Weather fields in the entry stay empty. Cause: the wind suggestion (station or model) only appears greyed out in the Wind and Wind direction fields and only goes into the entry with “Apply” in the section header; without a connection (and without a value fetched in the last three hours), without a station in range or with the “Wind from open sources” switch off, there is no suggestion at all, and never for gusts, pressure and weather. Your own onboard instruments via the NMEA connection take priority; when the voyage is closed, the wind for the departure and arrival entries is averaged over the first and last ten minutes respectively. Fix: tap “Apply” or enter it by hand; visibility and temperature are always manual entries anyway. Not a bug you need to report.

What belongs in feedback

Settings → Send feedback (“Help” section) or Tools → Help & demo → Send feedback: choose a type (Error, Idea, Question), write, “Next” — the app opens a prepared email, and you send it yourself. Technical details (version, device, iOS, language, tier, demo on/off) are attached if you wish and are shown in plain text on the screen beforehand; position, voyage or boat data never. If no mail app is set up, the app offers to copy the address. Alternatively straight from TestFlight (take a screenshot and share it) or to support@etmar.app.

For anything concerning recording, gaps, saving or GPS, please send the file from Settings → Tracking diagnostics: a text file, directly shareable, without positions but with every state change and its time. Without it, a gap can only be guessed at after the fact. Add briefly: what did you expect, what was on the screen, and was the app in the foreground, in the background or the phone in your pocket?

If the app is sluggish — a tap takes noticeably long, a tab builds up hesitantly — that is in the same file too: if the app does not respond for 0.25 seconds or longer, it then writes a line with the duration, tab, recording state, GPS updates per second, points of the open voyage and iCloud import. Measuring only happens while the app is in the foreground, and only once it is ready after launch — if the launch itself takes long, please mention that. Under Settings → Tracking diagnostics, “Measured hangs since launch” shows how often this has happened since the app was last launched and how long the longest one lasted; the “Log hangs” switch is on by default. Then share the file and say what you were doing at the time.

Below that are the “Reports from iOS”: iOS itself writes metrics about the app at most once a day, for hangs and crashes often with the place in the code; Etmar keeps the last 14 on the device, and “Share reports from iOS” gives them to you as one zip file to send along — they do not leave the device in any other way.

↑ Back to contents

D4Abbreviations

What the abbreviations on tiles, banners and in the analysis mean and where Etmar uses them – for looking up when a figure on the display leaves you wondering.

The abbreviations mean the same in Etmar as on a B&G or Raymarine display. Two distinctions run through everything: over ground (GPS, current included) versus through the water (log), and apparent versus true wind. A badge marks the places where the function in the app is not free.

Speed and course

AbbreviationMeaningHow Etmar uses it
SOGSpeed over Ground – speed over the ground from GPS, current included.From the iPhone GPS, with an onboard network from the bus. In Quick Log and entries, in the logbook row when underway, as avg/max in the voyage and analysis, as a tile in the dashboard.
COGCourse over Ground – course over the ground, the actual direction of movement, not the direction of the bow.The boat arrow on the map carries the COG vector (6 minutes ahead, from 0.5 kn; with a crossbar at the end, the time also 3 or 12 minutes under Settings → Map → COG vector). The Go-To banner shows the course error COG against bearing, orange with a warning triangle above 5°.
STWSpeed through Water – speed through the water from the log. The difference from SOG is the current.Tile in the dashboard, from the log on the onboard network. Polar performance is calculated against STW as soon as a log is on the bus; without a log it is shown in orange, and the current is then included in the percentage.
HDGHeading – the heading from the compass, the direction of the bow.From the compass on the NMEA network. “Head to wind – take the course” under “Set wind” takes the HDG, without a bus the COG. On the map the boat arrow turns by the HDG, and underway a thin heading line lies under the COG vector, reaching beyond its crossbar. The angle between the two is leeway plus current. It is drawn as true: if the compass sends magnetic, the map needs the variation from the bus or entered by hand; if it is missing or the compass is silent for more than ten seconds, the arrow stays with the COG.

Wind

AbbreviationMeaningHow Etmar uses it
AWA / AWSApparent Wind Angle / Speed – apparent wind as the vane at the masthead measures it, including the wind from the boat’s own motion. AWA is the angle to the bow.Apparent wind arrow in the sailing compass, from the wind transducer on the onboard network. If the gateway only supplies AWA/AWS, Etmar calculates the true wind from apparent wind plus STW (otherwise SOG). Masthead offset under Boat → Calibration (free to enter, applies to the values from the bus): head to wind, read the AWA, enter the difference from 0°.
TWA / TWS / TWDTrue Wind Angle / Speed / Direction – true wind without the wind from the boat’s motion: TWA is the angle to the bow, TWS the strength, TWD the compass direction it blows from.Tiles in the dashboard (wind transducer on the onboard network), plus “TWD trend” against the 10-minute average. “Capture trim” turns the TWA into the point of sail (Close-hauled below 60°, Beam reach below 110°, Broad reach below 150°, otherwise Downwind) and the TWS into the wind band (±2 kn). In the measured-run comparison: more than 1.5 kn TWS or 10° TWD difference – the conditions changed, not the trim.
VMGVelocity Made Good – the component of speed directly against the wind (upwind) or directly with the wind (downwind). Decides whether “higher and slower” or “lower and faster” pays more.Live value in the race as a delta against the polar, “+0.3 kn”, with wind and speed from the onboard network. In the analysis, point-of-sail balance and VMG per board Pro, upwind or downwind detected automatically from the wind angle; in the measured-run comparison Pro the main measure.
NoteVMG and VMC measure against two different reference points. VMG is progress towards the wind: your speed projected onto the wind axis – regardless of where the mark is. VMC is progress towards the destination: the same projection onto the bearing to the mark. Only when the mark is dead to windward are the two the same. Etmar shows VMG where it is about wind (live in the race with wind from the onboard network; in point-of-sail balance and measured runs – Pro), and VMC where it is about getting there (Go-To tiles; leg summary – purely from GPS, Pro).

Navigation to the destination

AbbreviationMeaningHow Etmar uses it
VMCVelocity Made good on Course – the component of speed towards the destination or next mark. Combines pointing, speed and extra distance in one figure, needs no wind data.Go-To tile on the “Navigation” page and leg summary in the race analysis Pro: progress towards the next mark per leg.
BRGBearing – bearing to the destination in degrees.Large in the Go-To banner and in the MOB banner; seamarks and harbours show bearing and distance when tapped. Also as a tile.
DTWDistance to Waypoint – remaining distance to the destination.In the Go-To banner; also as a tile.
XTECross Track Error – lateral deviation from the original line that ran from the boat to the mark when the destination was set.In the Go-To banner below bearing and distance; also as a tile.
ETAEstimated Time of Arrival – expected time of arrival.Go-To banner; with a voyage running already in the row, as arrival time instead of remaining duration. Calculated from the averaged approach over the last few minutes, not from the momentary VMC; if you are moving away or lying still, you see no ETA — “--” appears only on the ETA and TTG tiles of the “Navigation” page.
TTGTime to Go – remaining time to the destination.Tile on the “Navigation” page.
CPA / TCPAClosest Point of Approach / Time to CPA – the smallest passing distance to another vessel if courses stay unchanged, and the time until then.For every AIS target. Below the warning threshold (default 0.5 nm / 20 min; under Settings → Map 0.25–2 nm and 10–30 min) the target turns red, a warning button shows “CPA 0.3 nm”, and a time-sensitive notification arrives even with the phone in your pocket.

Regatta

AbbreviationMeaningHow Etmar uses it
BiasLine bias – the angle by which the start line deviates from the perpendicular to the wind. The windward end is favoured.Pre-start block: “Pin +7° · 12 m” or “Boat +7° · 12 m” – angle and windward advantage (line length × sin bias); below 3° “Line square”. Wind by hand via the wind rose (“Set wind”) or automatically as TWD from the wind transducer on the NMEA network.
LaylineThe line on which you just fetch the mark on your current tack. Beyond it you sail extra distance; short of it you don’t make the mark.With Go-To and wind data, the map draws the laylines from the mark: green starboard, red port, angles from the polar optima (without a polar 45°/150°). In the race, the distance to the layline is one of the four tiles in the Regatta tab.
Time-to-burnTime you still have to “burn” before the gun: countdown minus time to the line at your current approach.In the pre-start block and in the context bar as soon as pin and boat have been pinged; red means too early. Over the line it shows “OVER x m”; if the GPS fix is older than 10 s, the value disappears.
Pin / boatThe two ends of the start line: the pin is the buoy or mark, the boat is the race committee’s committee boat.“Ping pin” and “Ping boat” as you sail past set the line from the GPS position; pinging again overwrites. The start summary Pro says where you crossed: pin third, middle or boat.
Tack (stbd / port)The side the wind comes from: starboard tack means wind from starboard (right of way under the COLREGs), port tack wind from port.Tack colouring of the track: Settings → Map → Tack colouring sets what green means – “Wind from port” (default) or “Wind from starboard” (COLREGs reading); the legend shows the current assignment. Plus the tack balance in the analysis.
PolarPolar diagram – the boat’s target speed for each wind speed and wind angle, plus the optimum angles for the beat and the run.Boat → Polar data: grid of 9 wind speeds × 8 angles plus beat/run optima; templates (Albin Express, Folkboat, J/80 …), ORC PDF or CSV/.pol import – all free. Basis for performance percentages, VMG delta and laylines — live without a tier, in the analysis Pro.
ORCOffshore Racing Congress – rating system whose certificate contains the boat speeds calculated by a velocity prediction program (VPP).Reading in an ORC certificate as a PDF provides the polar, beat/gybe angles and boat data (class, builder, dimensions, displacement, sails) from the text of the table – nothing guessed. Name and sail number are deliberately not carried over.
YardstickHandicap number that makes the elapsed times of different boat types comparable.Start procedure on the course: in a kangaroo start (pursuit start) each boat starts staggered according to its Yardstick – the calendar date is only the first start; your start time is in the notice of race.
APAnswering Pennant – flag signal of the race committee: the start is postponed.“+5 min” in the pre-start postpones the gun by a full five minutes, and timers and notifications are rescheduled; tapping several times adds up, “−5 min” takes it back.
RRS 32Rule 32 of the Racing Rules of Sailing: shortening or abandoning after the start (32.1 abandonment, flag N).Course editor → Course change: “Shorten course from time” and “Extend course if finishing before” belong to the course (free). The analysis never scores the shortening by itself – it suggests the first mark after the shortening time, and only when you confirm does its rounding become the finish; marks that were dropped stay greyed out in the course. The “Race abandoned” scoring is available in all tiers.

Data sources and maps

AbbreviationMeaningHow Etmar uses it
NMEA 2000 / 0183The two standards in which onboard instruments exchange data: NMEA 2000 is a bus with binary messages, NMEA 0183 sends text sentences (RMC, MWV, DPT …).Settings → NMEA connection: NMEA 2000 as RAW via a YDWG gateway, NMEA 0183 from any Wi-Fi gateway via TCP (host:port) or UDP (port, typically 10110); the app detects the format itself. Where TCP works, TCP is the calmer choice.
PGNParameter Group Number – number of a message type on the NMEA 2000 bus, e.g. 129038 for a class A AIS position.The PGN counter under Settings → NMEA connection → Advanced shows which messages arrive from the bus.
AIS class A / BAutomatic Identification System – vessels transmit position, course, speed and identification over VHF. Class A: commercial shipping, class B: leisure craft and small vessels.AIS targets on the map: with a 6-minute vector (the same time as for your own boat), with name when zoomed in close, as a triangle in the direction of travel, class A dark blue, class B turquoise; after 3 min without a position dashed, without fill and without vector (CPA/TCPA are no longer calculated), gone after 6 min. A tap on the target shows type, MMSI, speed/course and CPA/TCPA.
OSMOpenStreetMap – free map data maintained by a community.Base map (OpenFreeMap), seamarks and harbours & anchorages as layers in the lighthouse menu, named buoys as “Seamarks in the area” in the waypoint list. Community data, not corrected by Notices to Mariners – not a chart.
EMODnetEuropean Marine Observation and Data Network – European depth model compiled from merged surveys.Depth contours 2/5/10/20 m; grid about 120 m, (from the next build) equally fine in every area loaded from the ready-made files; up to Build 57 and when the app calculates itself, extents over about 80 × 80 nm get coarser. Model data, not official depths.

Safety and radio

AbbreviationMeaningHow Etmar uses it
MOBMan Over Board – man overboard.Red button on the map, in the logbook and in the dashboard: hold for 0.6 s. Fixes position and time, shows bearing, distance and time since the alarm, and on the map a lifebuoy and red line. Ends only via “End”. Always free.
DSCDigital Selective Calling – digital distress alert from the VHF radio via the distress button on channel 70, followed by voice on channel 16.The SOS radio procedure cards give the channels on every card (16 / 156.800 MHz, before that a DSC alert on channel 70) and include a “DSC false alert” card for cancelling an alert sent by mistake.
MMSIMaritime Mobile Service Identity – the vessel’s nine-digit identifier for DSC and AIS.In the boat form under “Radio / SOS” next to the call sign; the SOS cards insert it automatically into the radio call and onto the PDF cover sheet. AIS targets show their MMSI when tapped.
